# Landmark Medical AI Papers — Reading Queue

Curated backlog of landmark / field-defining papers in medical AI for the
daily decode. The daily run takes the next unchecked paper, unless a genuinely
major new medical-AI paper published that week deserves priority (in which
case it jumps the queue). Check off entries as they are decoded and added to
`index.md`.

- [x] Gulshan V, et al. Development and validation of a deep learning algorithm for detection of diabetic retinopathy in retinal fundus photographs. JAMA, 2016. (The paper that kicked off modern deep learning in medical imaging.) — decoded 2026-08-31

- [x] Berger J, et al. Human learning is an understudied but promising lever for boosting human-AI synergy. PNAS, 2026;123(39):e2536100123. (PMID 42766752, doi 10.1073/pnas.2536100123.) Reanalysis of all 74 studies from an earlier meta-analysis that found human-AI combinations do not on average beat the better agent alone; argues the pessimism comes from designs that give humans no chance to learn. Strongest recent match for this list's themes. **Decoded 2026-10-02** — a PubMed Central copy (PMC13624608) appeared on day 11 of waiting, vindicating the re-check habit yet again. Graded 4/5. See summaries/2026-10-02-berger-human-ai-synergy-learning.md.
- [x] Feng Y, et al. Accuracy of deep learning in detecting cerebral microbleeds: systematic review and meta-analysis. Journal of Medical Internet Research, 2026;28:e95041. (PMID 42767630, doi 10.2196/95041.) Prospectively registered in PROSPERO, QUADAS-2 appraisal, pooled sensitivity/specificity/likelihood ratios and diagnostic odds ratios — metrics this list has not yet taught. **Decoded 2026-10-08 from PMC13639419 (full text), which appeared overnight after sixteen days of daily checks — the sixth 're-check rather than abandon' entry to pay off. Graded 3/5.** Good process (prospective PROSPERO registration, PRISMA, QUADAS-2 with a well-argued high-risk rating on all 42 studies, screening kappa 0.891, a sensitivity analysis on its own table-reconstruction step, and an honest note that QUADAS-2 misses AI-specific risks). Over-reaching numbers: 33 of 42 studies could not supply extractable data, so the pooled estimates rest on 14 contingency tables and the patient-level claim on 415 patients; a DOR of 1738 with a 100-fold interval; no I-squared anywhere; publication-bias tests on 4-9 studies reported as reassuring. Best contribution is the lesion-level versus patient-level split (specificity 0.98 vs 0.86, a sevenfold false-alarm difference) with a correct mechanistic explanation. See summaries/2026-10-08-feng-deep-learning-cerebral-microbleeds-meta-analysis.md.

- [x] **DECODED 2026-10-17 (entry 50, ABSTRACT ONLY)** ~~Zhou J, et al. Large-scale esophageal cancer screening through noncontrast computed tomography and artificial intelligence. Nature Medicine, 2026. (PMID 42773211, doi 10.1038/s41591-026-04656-4. Trial registration ChiCTR2300074806.) The EAGLE model detects oesophageal precancer and cancer on ordinary chest CT — trained on 6,813 patients from 2 centres, validated across 12 centres in 3 countries on 80,612 patients. Reports 98.5% specificity with 90.0% sensitivity for cancer and 52.5% for precancerous lesions; a real-world calibration cohort (n=35,402) cut false positives 72.7%; prospective hospital validation (n=17,446) reached 42.2% positive predictive value. By far the largest and most consequential medical-AI paper seen in these scans. Would teach screening arithmetic at low prevalence, positive predictive value versus specificity, operating-point selection, and threshold calibration. **Highest-priority retry — check for a PMC copy every day.** *Re-checked 2026-10-03 (day 12): still no PMC copy.*~~

## Added 2026-09-29 — found while scanning, PMC copy confirmed

- [x] Little M. Explicit mechanistic causal analyses or interventional trials are required for objective, clinical, voice-based Parkinson disease characterization. Journal of Medical Internet Research, 2026;28:e111711. (PMID 42804162, doi 10.2196/111711, PMC13618403.) A commentary by the researcher who largely founded voice-based Parkinson detection, arguing that ad-hoc observational datasets carry spurious causal associations no purely statistical analysis can remove, and that causal inference plus diagnostic trials are required. Short, and it is a commentary rather than primary research, so it would be a format departure — but it is the cleanest available vehicle for teaching **confounding and causal inference**, which this list has circled (manufactured confounders on 09-13, association versus prediction on 09-23, mathematical coupling on 09-26) but never taught head-on. **Decoded 2026-09-30, paired exactly as hoped** — with the primary paper it responds to, Shukla et al., *Demographic confounding in voice-based Parkinson disease screening* (JMIR 2026;28:e95609, PMID 42623305, PMC13492483), which supplied the numbers the commentary argues about. Graded **5/5**. See summaries/2026-09-30-shukla-demographic-confounding-voice-parkinson.md.
- [x] Luque-Hernández MJ, et al. trIAje project: protocol for a retrospective cohort study to optimise AI-assisted telephone triage of time-sensitive conditions in emergency medical services. BMJ Open, 2026;16(9):e113242. (PMID 42805668, doi 10.1136/bmjopen-2025-113242. NCT07247669. **PMC copy confirmed 2026-10-03: PMC13630096 — now reachable, and the next queue entry.**) A published protocol rather than results, which would let this list teach how to read a study **before** it has findings: prespecification, the planned shadow-mode pilot, under- and over-triage as paired outcomes, and sex-stratified evaluation planned in advance. **Decoded 2026-10-04 from PMC13630096 (full text). Graded 4/5 — the grade is for the quality of the plan, since a protocol has no results.** Strong prespecification (TRIPOD+AI, STROBE-RECORD, voluntary registration NCT07247669, hold-out protected in writing, thresholds from validation and not test data, SHAP non-causality stated twice, shadow-mode pilot scoped out behind performance gates). Two design problems that could still undermine it: the outcome label is partly caused by the triage decision under study (ALS-on-scene and ICU admission both require having been dispatched), and a random 80/20 split across a 2020-2024 window containing the pandemic. See summaries/2026-10-04-luque-hernandez-triaje-ai-dispatch-protocol.md.

Re-checked 2026-09-30: the Nature Medicine EAGLE paper (PMID 42773211), Berger
(PNAS, PMID 42766752) and Feng (JMIR cerebral microbleeds, PMID 42767630) still
have no PubMed Central copy — days 8, 9 and 10.

Lesson recorded from today's run, worth repeating: when a queued entry is a
commentary or a letter, search for the paper it responds to and decode both
together. The commentary supplies the argument, the primary paper supplies the
numbers, and the pair is far stronger than either alone. The Little commentary
had been queued as a format-departure risk; paired with Shukla it produced the
list's clearest treatment of confounding.

## 2026-10-01 — Scite is now paywalled; retrieval options have narrowed

Checked today in the hope that Scite's monthly MCP allowance had reset (the
09-14 error said usage resets 2026-10-01). It has not reset — Scite now returns
"MCP tools require a paid scite plan or an active free trial." So the Scite
read_fulltext route to paywalled papers is closed for good unless a plan is
bought. Retrieval in this environment is therefore PubMed Central only, plus
WebSearch for metadata.

Useful discovery from today's run: PubMed's `pubmed pmc[sb]` filter restricts a
search to records that actually have a PubMed Central copy. Using it turns the
daily scan from "find candidates, then check each one's availability" into a
single query that only returns decodable papers. Use it from now on.

## Added 2026-10-01 — found while scanning, no PMC copy yet, re-check

- [x] Ye Z, et al. Development of an interpretable triage tool for colorectal polyp risk stratification within a population-based screening program: machine learning approach. JMIR Medical Informatics, 2026;14:e89422. (PMID 42815038, doi 10.2196/89422.) 4108 individuals aged 50-74 in Wenzhou, China. Worth decoding specifically because the reported performance is poor and the paper still proposes clinical deployment: XGBoost reached AUC 0.672 and LightGBM was selected for triage on recall 0.6503. It also used SMOTE on the training set only — a direct contrast with the Shimizu paper decoded today, which deliberately refused to rebalance in order to protect calibration. Pairing the two would make the rebalancing question concrete. **Decoded 2026-10-03 from PMC13626646 (full text). Graded 2/5.** The Shimizu pairing paid off exactly as hoped: Shimizu refused to rebalance and got near-perfect calibration; Ye used SMOTE (1981 of 2634 training cases synthetic, 75.2%) and its calibration claim is the weakest part of the paper. Also found a selection cliff not discussed by the authors — 93.3% of available polyp cases were excluded against 51.7% of controls, so the 19.9% training prevalence is an artefact. See summaries/2026-10-03-ye-colorectal-polyp-triage-smote.md.
- [ ] Lu J, et al. Detecting misspelled drug names using transformer-based language models: model development and external validation. JMIR Medical Informatics, 2026;14:e91151. (PMID 42809845, doi 10.2196/91151.) Clean internal-versus-external generalisation gap: F-score 0.859 on the held-out RxNorm test set falling to 0.696 on an external long-term-care database, and domain-specific BERT models beating GPT-4o internally but not externally. A tidy vehicle for teaching why external validation is the only validation that counts.

Retrieval lesson recorded from today: when a paper arrives in PubMed Central,
the rendering is not always complete. The Berger full text was served with every
Hedges' g point estimate and every Bayes factor stripped out of the inline text —
only study counts, credible-interval bounds and posterior probabilities survived.
Check for silently missing numbers before writing, and say in the summary exactly
which magnitudes could not be retrieved rather than working around the gap.

## 2026-10-03 — two reusable appraisal techniques, both proven today

**1. Deliberately pair papers that took opposite turns at the same
methodological fork.** Yesterday's Shimizu scorecard (entry 34) refused to
rebalance a rare outcome, explicitly to protect calibration, and its
calibration came out near-perfect. Today's Ye triage tool (entry 36) applied
SMOTE, and its calibration claim is the weakest thing in the paper — it
benchmarks its Brier score against 0.25, the no-information value for a
*balanced* set, when the no-information value at its real 19.9% prevalence is
0.159. Decoding the two back-to-back taught the rebalancing/calibration
trade-off far better than either alone could. **When a queued paper makes a
choice a recently-decoded paper refused, schedule it next and decode it as a
pair.** Keep a note of live forks worth pairing: rebalance vs refuse (done);
internal vs external validation (Lu J, entry pending, is the natural partner
for anything claiming internal performance); abstract screening vs full-text
screening in meta-analyses.

**2. Compute the trivial baseline from the paper's own Table 1, every time.**
For a binary rule, AUC = (sensitivity + specificity) / 2, and a baseline table
that reports a predictor by outcome group gives you both. Today "refer every
man" came to sensitivity 0.564, specificity 0.593, AUC 0.578 — against the
paper's nine-algorithm, twenty-one-predictor 0.672. The model is better, but
knowing by how much reframes the abstract entirely. This takes under a minute
and should be done before reading any Discussion section.

**3. Check where the decision threshold came from.** New failure mode seen
today, and distinct from the familiar feature-selection leak (which this paper
correctly avoided): Ye et al. state that "the optimal probability cutoffs were
derived from the validation dataset" — the same hold-out used to report the
headline recall. The threshold is a fitted parameter; tuning it on the test set
makes the reported sensitivity an upper bound. Look for the sentence naming the
threshold's provenance in every prediction-model paper from now on.

## Added 2026-10-04 — found while scanning, PMC copy confirmed

- [x] Akbal S, et al. CT radiomics showed no improvement beyond volume dynamics for early lesion-level size response to immunotherapy in metastatic melanoma. Cancer Imaging, 2026;26(1). (PMID 42823717, doi 10.1186/s40644-026-01135-4, PMC13632448 — reachable.) **High-value and the next queue entry.** A rare thing: a well-designed *negative* study that says so in its title. 158 patients, 1626 lesions, internal cohort n=129 with standardised protocols plus an external cohort n=29 with heterogeneous ones; models trained internally with patient-grouped cross-validation and applied **unchanged** to the external cohort. Internal AUC 0.84 (95% CI 0.78-0.88) for volumetrics versus 0.86 (0.81-0.90) for radiomics; externally both fell to 0.68 with CIs of 0.50-0.85 and 0.51-0.88 — on **12 progression events**. Would teach, all new to this list: **equivalence/non-inferiority testing against a prespecified margin** (they used ±0.05 on paired AUC differences), **patient-grouped cross-validation** (why 1626 lesions from 158 patients is not 1626 independent observations — the clustering lesson from Huang 2026-09-27 applied to splitting rather than pooling), **what a 12-event external cohort can and cannot settle**, and the discipline of reporting that an expensive new method adds nothing over a simple one. The median absolute correlation of 0.37 between radiomic features and lesion volume is a tidy worked example of why "independent predictor" claims need checking. **Decoded 2026-10-05 from PMC13632448 (full text). Graded 5/5 for conduct and reporting — the most carefully executed paper decoded so far.** It prespecified an equivalence hypothesis with a +/-0.05 margin, handled within-patient clustering three ways (patient-grouped CV, patient-level cluster bootstrap, equal-patient-weight sensitivity), applied the external model once unchanged with no centre indicator, took its threshold from internal out-of-fold predictions, self-scored RQS 18/36 and published it, and systematically tried to overturn its own null (volume residualisation, ComBat, distribution filtering, extended feature sets, alternative learners, IPW for survivor bias) and reported that it could not. Best single finding, buried in the supplement: raw early volume change with no model fitted beat the fitted volumetric model externally — the trivial-baseline check, run by the authors on themselves, and won by the trivial baseline. See summaries/2026-10-05-akbal-radiomics-no-gain-over-volume-melanoma.md.

- [x] Yu Y, et al. Development and validation of an AI-enhanced prediction model for 3-year visual decline in patients with diabetes using ophthalmic imaging: protocol for a real-world longitudinal cohort study. BMJ Open, 2026;16(9):e123521. (PMID 42816089, doi 10.1136/bmjopen-2026-123521, PMC13630008 — reachable.) A second protocol, and the natural partner to the trIAje decode: this one **does** plan external validation (Ningbo Eye Hospital, independent of the Peking University Third Hospital development cohort), uses a **time-to-event** primary outcome (time to first ≥0.2-logMAR decline within 3 years) rather than a binary label, and prespecifies discrimination, calibration, Brier score and decision curve analysis. Pairing the two would teach what separates a good protocol from a very good one, and would bring survival analysis into the list. **Decoded 2026-10-06 from PMC13630008 (full text). Graded 4/5 for the plan.** The pairing worked exactly as hoped, and in both directions. Where trIAje cited Riley and declined to apply it, this one applies it and the number reproduces to the unit (822.7 against a stated 823 from P=15, S=0.90, R2cs=0.15). Where trIAje registered an observational study voluntarily and added PPI, this one does neither. New weaknesses of its own: two large competing causes of the outcome go unaddressed (incident cataract, which causes exactly this measurement in diabetes, and death, which is informative censoring and is never mentioned); the 9.4% uplift from 823 to 900 eyes covers an inter-eye correlation of only about 0.1 in a bilateral systemic disease; the multimodal comparison has no sample size of its own; and the anticipated R2 of 0.15 is unsourced while halving it would roughly double the requirement. First time-to-event primary outcome in the list. See summaries/2026-10-06-yu-ai-visual-decline-prognostic-protocol.md.

- [x] Hojeij B, et al. Psoriatic arthritis digital phenotyping and inflammation drivers (PDPID) study: protocol for an international multicentre prospective cohort. BMJ Open, 2026;16(9):e115903. (PMID 42805667, doi 10.1136/bmjopen-2025-115903, PMC13629932 — reachable.) NCT06347237. Smartphone and smartwatch passive sensing plus patient-triggered flare reporting across four countries, 12 months. Would teach intensive longitudinal data, patient-initiated (and therefore non-random) measurement timing, and digital-biomarker validation. **Decoded 2026-10-09 from PMC13629932 (full text). Graded 4/5 for the plan.** Best measurement-science planning of the three protocols: it prespecifies test-retest reliability (ICC), minimal detectable change AND minimal important change by both anchor-based and distribution-based methods — the three numbers that decide whether a monitoring tool is usable, and which digital-health papers almost never report. Sample size of 554 reproduces exactly by both stated routes (138 flare cases for a 90% sensitivity at +/-5 points; 139 events over 13 parameters). Strongest PPI of the three (patients tested the app during design) and a prespecified skin-tone adjustment. Two real problems: data volume depends on the outcome (14 questionnaire observations per fortnight during a flare versus 1 outside it, plus flare-triggered photos), and the protocol was published ~24 months after recruitment began with no enrolment figure. See summaries/2026-10-09-hojeij-pdpid-digital-phenotyping-psoriatic-arthritis.md.

## 2026-10-05 — a fourth reusable technique, and a note on reading negative results

**4. Check what the simplest possible rule gets, and check whether the authors
checked.** This was recorded on 10-03 as a thing to compute yourself from the
baseline table. Today's paper did it internally and the trivial baseline **won**:
raw early volume change, with no model fitted, kept most of the Random Forest's
internal discrimination and exceeded it in the external cohort. A fitted model
can lose to its own raw input because fitting absorbs centre-specific noise that
does not travel; a raw number has nothing to overfit. **So the check has two
parts now: compute the trivial baseline, and look in the supplement for whether
the authors already did.** When they have and it won, that is the finding.

**On grading negative results.** Three 5/5 grades have now been given, all to
papers that refused to oversell: Yin (entry 31) for reporting a null honestly,
Shukla (entry 33) for systematically deflating its own positive finding, and
Akbal (entry 38) for prespecifying an equivalence margin and then failing to
overturn its own null across a dozen sensitivity analyses. The pattern worth
keeping: **the grade tracks whether the conclusion is entitled to the evidence,
not whether the finding is exciting.** Akbal's external cohort has 12 progression
events and settles nothing externally — and the authors say so rather than
citing their matching 0.68s as confirmation. That refusal is why it scores 5.

**New appraisal tool added to the list today:** the Radiomics Quality Score
(RQS), 36 points. Akbal et al. scored their own study 18/36 and published the
item-level detail. Worth looking for in any future radiomics paper — and worth
noting that most papers citing the RQS apply it to other people's work.

## Added 2026-10-06 — found while scanning, worth decoding later

- [ ] Holly L, Raeside R, Morton A, et al. The public health challenges of children's and adolescents' digital media use: applying the digital determinants of health as an interpretive framework. The Lancet, 2026. (PMID 42830095, doi 10.1016/S0140-6736(26)01611-9. No PMC copy yet — re-check.) Second paper in a Lancet Series. Not a medical-AI study, but an unusually good teaching target for a different family of statistics: it states that "illustrative modelling indicates the potential population burden is substantial, even as its causal basis remains uncertain", and criticises the field for having "privileged easily measured proxies, such as screen time". Would teach population attributable fraction, the gap between association and attributable burden, exposure misclassification from proxy measures, and how a modelled burden estimate inherits every assumption in the model — none of which this list has covered. Pairs naturally with the first paper in the Series (Mechael P, et al., Lancet Digital Health 2026, PMID 42833894, doi 10.1016/j.landig.2026.101078), which sets out the framework.

**EAGLE schedule change applied.** Per yesterday's note, EAGLE (PMID 42773211)
moves to weekly re-checks; it was last checked 2026-10-05 (day 14, no PMC copy),
so the next check is due around 2026-10-12. Feng (PMID 42767630) likewise. A
date-filtered scan of 2026-09-29 to 2026-10-06 across Nature, Nature Medicine,
Lancet, Lancet Digital Health, JAMA, NEJM and NEJM AI returned 14 records and no
landmark primary medical-AI study — only the two Lancet Series reviews queued
above, a Nature Medicine commentary, a JAMA news item and an unrelated genetics
paper.

**Protocol-pairing result, for the record.** Two protocols decoded 48 hours
apart turned out to be strong and weak in opposite places, which is a better
lesson than either alone:

- *Sample size.* trIAje cited Riley and skipped the calculation, justifying it by
  using the whole population (which answers the sampling question, not the
  overfitting one). Yu computed it correctly and checkably.
- *Registration.* trIAje registered on ClinicalTrials.gov voluntarily
  (NCT07247669) despite being observational. Yu states no registration anywhere.
- *External validation.* trIAje has none and says so. Yu plans an independent
  site with no refitting of any kind.
- *Outcome integrity.* trIAje's label is partly caused by the triage decision
  under study. Yu's outcome has two unaddressed competing causes (cataract,
  death).
- *Patient involvement.* trIAje added a committee including patients. Yu had
  none and says so plainly.
- *Both* protect the hold-out in writing, both follow TRIPOD+AI, and both state
  the limits of what they will be able to conclude.

So the general lesson for reading a protocol: **the quantitative plan and the
governance commitments are independent axes, and a paper can be excellent on one
and absent on the other.** Check both separately.

Today's decode was **not** from the queue. MedGemma (Sellergren A, et al., "An

**New retrieval route discovered, and it matters.** The Nature Medicine full
text is paywalled and nature.com is blocked; arxiv.org is blocked too. But the
**alphaXiv MCP server indexes arXiv directly and is reachable**, so the MedGemma
Technical Report (arXiv:2507.05201v4) could be read in full, including its result
tables. Use `mcp__alphaXiv__answer_pdf_queries` with a paper ID and a batch of
questions — it returns the most relevant pages, so put every question for a paper
into one call. **This re-opens a whole class of papers:** any Nature/Nature
Medicine/Lancet AI paper with an arXiv preprint is now decodable even when the
publisher is blocked. Worth retrying the four oldest deferred entries (Topol
2019, Beam and Kohane 2018, Moor 2023, Wu 2021) this way, though commentary and
review pieces are less likely to have preprints than technical reports are.

**Discipline that this requires.** The preprint and the published paper are
different documents. Today's summary states that distinction prominently,
attributes every number to the arXiv report rather than to Nature Medicine, and
notes one claim that was demonstrably softened between the two: the arXiv
abstract's "reducing errors in electronic health record information retrieval by
50%" became "can be more effective than fine-tuning the base Gemma 3 model…
particularly in the setting of limited training data" in Nature Medicine. That
is peer review working, and it is visible only because both documents were read.
**Always state which document a number came from.**

## 2026-10-08 — a fifth reusable technique, and the unit-of-analysis theme consolidating

**5. Check that every ratio's confidence interval is log-symmetric.** For a ratio
measure — odds ratio, risk ratio, hazard ratio, likelihood ratio, diagnostic odds
ratio — the point estimate should sit at the GEOMETRIC mean of its interval,
sqrt(low x high), because these intervals are computed on the log scale. Today
this caught a real error. Eleven of the twelve ratio intervals in Feng et al.
pass; the directly extracted positive likelihood ratio is printed as 40.2 (95% CI
9.3-79.9), where sqrt(9.3 x 79.9) = 27.3, off by 47% — far too much to be
rounding. Solving 40.2^2 / 79.9 gives 20.2, and sqrt(20.2 x 79.9) = 40.2 exactly,
so the lower bound is almost certainly 20.2 mis-transcribed as 9.3. (A second
tell: the printed upper bound 79.9 is identical to the row below it.) The four
negative-likelihood-ratio intervals appear to fail the same check but only because
they are printed to two decimals — allow for rounding before calling a mismatch.
**This takes thirty seconds per interval, needs only a square root, and catches
transcription errors, unit mix-ups and intervals computed on the wrong scale.**

**6. In a meta-analysis, find the number attached to each pooled estimate, not
the number in the title.** Feng et al. says "42 studies were incorporated in the
meta-analysis" in its Results and, several pages later in the risk-of-bias
section, "33 studies provided only limited outcome data and could not be directly
included." The pooled numbers come from 14 contingency tables from at most 9
studies, and the patient-level claim from 415 patients. Look for "k = " in the
forest plot or count its rows, then ask how many *patients* those studies hold.

**The unit-of-analysis theme has now appeared four days running, in four
different disguises, and is worth treating as a standing check.**
- Entry 38 (Akbal, 10-05): 1,626 lesions from 158 patients — a **clustering**
  problem, fixed with patient-grouped cross-validation and cluster bootstrap.
- Entry 39 (Yu, 10-06): two eyes per patient — a **sample-size** problem, where
  cluster-robust standard errors report precision honestly but cannot increase it.
- Entry 41 (Feng, 10-08): lesion-level versus patient-level — a **task-difficulty**
  problem, where specificity falls from 0.98 to 0.86 and false alarms rise
  sevenfold because a whole brain contains hundreds of chances to be wrong once.
- And entry 40 (MedGemma, 10-07) is the same idea again: per-question accuracy on
  multiple-choice benchmarks is not per-patient diagnostic accuracy.
**Standing question for every paper from now on: what is the unit the accuracy is
measured in, and is it the unit the clinical decision is made in?** It almost
never is, and the clinical unit is almost always the worse number.

## 2026-10-10 TOP PRIORITY — AMIE prospective clinic study, confirmed reachable

- [x] **Brodeur PG, Koshy JM, Palepu A, Saab K, ... Schaekermann M, Karthikesalingam A, Rodman A. Conversational diagnostic artificial intelligence in ambulatory primary care: a prospective feasibility study. The Lancet, 2026.** (PMID 42849491, doi 10.1016/S0140-6736(26)01535-7. NCT06911398. Funded by Alphabet.) **Decode this next.** This is the first prospective deployment of a conversational diagnostic AI with *real patients in real primary care appointments*, from the Google/DeepMind AMIE programme with Beth Israel Deaconess and Harvard. From the Lancet abstract: 114 patients enrolled April-November 2025, 98 completed both the AMIE interaction and the physician appointment; patients chatted with AMIE up to 5 days before a single-complaint urgent appointment; **physician safety supervisors monitored every interaction in real time** with predefined intervention criteria; **zero safety stops**, one hallucination noted, clinical information added in five interactions; conversations rated favourably in 87-100% of cases on 17 criteria by clinical evaluators but **48-96% on 16 criteria by patients**; patient attitudes to AI improved and stayed elevated; PCPs completed post-surveys in 60 of 98 cases and found AMIE helpful for visit preparation in 33 of 44 reviewed cases, with possible behaviour change in 25 of 44.

  **Retrieval plan, already checked today:** The Lancet full text is paywalled and there is no PMC copy, BUT an arXiv preprint exists and is reachable through the alphaXiv route discovered on 2026-10-07 — **arXiv:2603.08448** ("A prospective clinical feasibility study of a conversational diagnostic AI in an ambulatory primary care clinic"), with versions v1 and v3 indexed. So this can be decoded from full text, as a two-document analysis like MedGemma (entry 40).

  **Discrepancy already spotted, and exactly why the two-document discipline matters:** the preprint describes **100 participants** completing the interaction, while the Lancet abstract gives **114 enrolled and 98 completing both stages**. The numbers moved between preprint and publication. Attribute every figure to the document it came from, and treat the difference as a finding in its own right.

  Decode it alongside the accompanying Lancet commentary — Omar M, Nadkarni GN, "Conversational diagnostic AI in primary care: what happens after it speaks?" (PMID 42849492, doi 10.1016/S0140-6736(26)01763-0; abstract not available in PubMed) — using the commentary-pairing technique from 2026-09-30. Note that Omar and Nadkarni also wrote the Nature Medicine agentic-autonomy commentary seen in the 2026-10-03 scan, so they are tracking this area closely.

  **Why it was not decoded today.** It was published 2026-10-08 and found in today's scan, after PDPID had been retrieved in full and analysed. The established discipline is to prefer a complete full-text decode over a rushed abstract-only one — the same judgment that deferred EAGLE twice. PDPID was the legitimate next queue entry and was available in full, so it went first. AMIE is now confirmed reachable and is tomorrow's paper.

**Teaching value of the AMIE study, to prepare for:** it is the first paper in this list where an AI spoke to real patients, so it brings in a family of concepts none of the previous 42 have covered — single-arm feasibility design and what it can and cannot show; **safety supervision as a study instrument** (a human watching every conversation is both the safety net and a confound, since the intervention under test was never allowed to run unsupervised); zero events in 98 interactions and how to put a confidence interval on a zero (the rule of three: with 0 events in n trials the upper 95% bound is roughly 3/n, so **zero safety stops in 98 interactions is consistent with a true rate as high as about 3%**); the gap between expert ratings (87-100%) and patient ratings (48-96%) on nominally similar criteria; survey response as a denominator problem (60 of 98 PCP surveys, 44 with transcript review); and attitude change after exposure as an outcome that cannot be blinded.

## 2026-10-10 — AMIE decoded, and a correction to yesterday's note

Decoded as entry 43 from the Lancet abstract plus arXiv:2603.08448v3 in full,
graded **4/5** — on conduct the most carefully run AI deployment study in this
list.

**Correction to yesterday's queue note.** I recorded a "discrepancy already
spotted" between the preprint's 100 participants and the Lancet abstract's 114
enrolled / 98 completing. That was wrong: they are three different milestones of
one funnel — 114 initiated an AMIE encounter, 100 completed it (87.7%), and 98
completed both the AMIE encounter and the physician appointment. No conflict.
**There is a real between-document difference, but it is a different one:** the
Lancet abstract says supervisors "noted one hallucination and added clinical
information in five interactions," while the preprint says "on three occasions,
the AI supervisor made remarks to the patient." Three versus five. The
two-document discipline still paid off — it just caught a different thing than I
predicted, which is a reason to check rather than to assume.

**Techniques added to the toolkit from this decode:**

**7. With zero events, apply the rule of three before believing a safety claim.**
Zero events in n observations bounds the rate at roughly 3/n. Here zero safety
stops in 100 interactions bounds the true rate at 2.95%, i.e. up to about 3 in
100 — and ~3,000 supervised conversations would be needed to bound it below 1 in
1,000. Notably this paper computed binomial confidence intervals for its
diagnostic accuracy and its blinding check but **not** for its headline safety
result, which is the one everyone will quote.

**8. Check whether the blinding was verified, not just claimed.** The best method
in this paper: evaluators were asked to guess which output was AMIE's and which
the physician's. They were right 58 of 98 times, 59.18% (95% CI 49.45%-68.91%) —
an interval that contains 50%, so they were guessing and the blind held. Note the
inverted logic: a HIGH correct-identification rate would have been the bad
result. First paper in 43 to do this. From now on, for any "blinded assessment",
ask whether the assessors were asked to guess and what the interval was.

**9. Ask what k is in any top-k accuracy figure.** The abstract's "included the
final diagnosis in 90% of cases" is **top-seven**, unlabelled. Top-3 is 75% and
is labelled. Top-1 — the single best guess, which is what a patient would be
handed — is 55/98 = 56%, and appears in neither abstract.

**10. Watch for ceiling effects in rating rubrics.** "Seeking and addressing
concerns" scored very favourable in 98 of 98 cases; five more criteria sat within
three cases of the top. A saturated measure cannot discriminate and cannot detect
future regression. The informative criterion was the one that was NOT at the
ceiling: family history, where 43 of 98 were marked not applicable, leaving a real
denominator of 55 with 4 unfavourable (7.3%).

**11. Follow the denominator downhill in survey findings.** 98 encounters -> 60
physician surveys returned (61.2%) -> 44 who had actually read the transcript ->
33 who found it helpful. "75% found it helpful" is 33/44; across all encounters
it is 33/98 = 34%. Compare response rates within the same study: patients ~90%,
AI supervisors 100%, physicians 61.2%.

**A reference-standard contamination pathway worth watching for generally.** In
52 of 98 cases the "final diagnosis" was the physician's presumptive impression
with no confirmatory test — and that physician had read AMIE's transcript *and its
list of candidate diagnoses* before the visit. So in the majority of cases AMIE
may have shaped the standard it was scored against, which is the most
parsimonious explanation for the paper's own observation that accuracy trended
higher in exactly that subgroup. The authors flag the weaker standard and run the
subgroup analysis but do not name the pathway. **Generalised check: when a
reference standard is a human judgement, ask whether that human saw the model's
output first.** This is the fourth distinct flavour of reference-standard problem
in the list, after entry 37 (outcome partly caused by the decision under study),
entry 38 (gold standard only for transported patients) and entry 42 (gold
standard only when the patient attends).

**Next queue entry:** the two Lancet Series papers on the digital determinants of
health (Holly L, et al., PMID 42830095; Mechael P, et al., PMID 42833894) — check
for PMC copies, neither had one as of 2026-10-06. They would bring population
attributable fraction and proxy-exposure misclassification into the list. Failing
that, re-check Huang J (PMID 42765400, PMC13573861 with an empty full_text for
two consecutive days) and Lu J (PMID 42809845, no PMC copy). EAGLE (PMID
42773211) and the four oldest deferred entries are due their weekly re-check
around 2026-10-12.

**Also worth queueing when retrievable:** the accompanying Lancet commentary on
the AMIE study — Omar M, Nadkarni GN, "Conversational diagnostic AI in primary
care: what happens after it speaks?" (PMID 42849492, doi
10.1016/S0140-6736(26)01763-0). No abstract in PubMed and the full text is
paywalled, so it could not be paired with the study today. These two authors have
now appeared three times in these scans (this commentary, the Nature Medicine
agentic-autonomy piece from the 10-03 scan, and the MAGIC network letter), so
they are a useful tracker for where this field's critical commentary is going.

## 2026-10-11 run — Rauschenberg et al., AI4U micro-randomized trial

**Where today's paper came from.** The curated queue is exhausted of reachable
entries, so today's paper came from a scan rather than the backlog. Route used:
`pubmed pmc[sb]` on the standing medical-AI query (93 hits) narrowed by
`randomized controlled trial[PT]` (10 hits), then screened three by hand. This is
now the fallback whenever the queue has nothing retrievable — record it so the
provenance of each entry stays auditable.

**Reachability re-check (fourth consecutive day of the same three failures).**
Holly L (PMID 42830095), Mechael P (PMID 42833894) and Lu J (PMID 42809845) still
have no `pmc` field. Huang J (PMC13573861) still returns an **empty `full_text`**
despite a valid PMC identifier — third consecutive day, now on weekly checks only.
`convert_article_ids` returned RATE_LIMIT_EXCEEDED; `get_article_metadata`
returns the same `pmc` field and is not on that limit, so prefer it for
reachability checks.

**12. Back out an unreported sample size from a reported test statistic.** The
paper never says how many decision points entered the primary analysis — the
most consequential omission in it, because that is the effective sample size of
the headline result. But it reports a Wilcoxon rank-sum test on the
intervention-to-outcome interval: W = 114,075, p = 0.518. R's W is the U
statistic, with expectation n1*n2/2 under the null, and p = 0.518 puts W almost
exactly at that expectation. So n1*n2 ~= 228,150; with 1:1 randomisation each arm
is ~478 and the total is **~955 analysed intervals across 42 participants, ~23
per person** — against a per-person maximum of 210, so about 11%. The estimate is
robust to the split (40/60 gives ~975). "Up to 210 decision points each" and "~23
analysed decision points each" are very different studies. **Generalised check:
reported test statistics carry information about the n they were computed on;
when an author omits an effective sample size, try to recover it and label the
recovery clearly as your own.**

**13. The unit-of-analysis question in its newest costume.** The standing question
— "what is the unit the accuracy is measured in, and is it the unit the clinical
decision is made in?" — has a sibling for within-person designs: **what is the
unit the effect is measured in, and is it the unit the uncertainty is driven
by?** Here the effect is measured in decision points (hundreds) but the
uncertainty is driven by participants (42), because observations within a person
are correlated. That is why hundreds of randomisations still leave the study
under-powered. `wcls()` exists precisely to handle this.

**14. Recompute realised power when a study under-recruits.** Planned: 60
participants, ~87% power for a standardised proximal effect of 0.10 at one-sided
alpha 0.025. Work backwards: z_crit = 1.96, z_power = 1.126, so planned
non-centrality = 3.086. Non-centrality scales with sqrt(n), so at 42 analysed it
is 3.086 * sqrt(42/60) = 2.582 and power = Phi(2.582 - 1.96) ~= **73%** (80% at
49). Not catastrophic, but it means the two null findings are weak evidence of
absence. Caveat: MRT power also depends on the availability rate, which this
paper does not state, so treat the figure as an approximation.

**15. Forecasting performance is not policy value.** The authors' own phrase, and
the sharpest idea in the paper. The model was trained and tuned to minimise EMA
*prediction* error, then deployed to *choose* among eight interventions. Those
objectives come apart: predicting your mood well says nothing about correctly
ranking counterfactual interventions. The authors write that the prediction
objective "was used as a pragmatic proxy for model selection; it does not
establish that the forecasting objective is optimally aligned with the causal
effect of the assignment policy." So the MRT validated **the policy, not the
model** — the right thing to test, but a positive result does not validate the
forecasting and a null would not have invalidated it. **Generalised check: when
an AI system is trained on one objective and deployed to make a decision, ask
which one was actually measured.** This is the cleanest statement of the
discrimination-vs-decision gap the list has collected so far, and it came from
the authors, not from a critic.

**16. Check the CI against the p-value, and prefer the version with more decimal
places.** B = 0.147, CI 0.004-0.290. Midpoint = 0.147 (symmetric, as a normal
interval should be), half-width 0.143, SE = 0.143/1.96 = 0.0730, z = 2.015,
two-sided p = 0.0439 — reproduces the stated 0.044 exactly, so estimate, interval
and p all come from one calculation with nothing mistyped. The paper then gives
the interval two ways: abstract 0.004-0.290, body text 0.01-0.29. But 0.004 to
two decimals is 0.00, not 0.01, so the body version makes the lower bound read as
a real small positive rather than essentially zero. **When two parts of a paper
disagree on a number, read the one with more decimal places.**

**Funnel arithmetic that does not close.** The distal analysis reports 44 of 49
with 4 excluded for incomplete baseline, but 49 - 4 = 45. It reconciles only by
routing through the 48 who completed the post-assessment (48 - 4 = 44), which the
text does not spell out. All other funnel steps check out exactly (58 - 9 = 49;
49 - 6 - 1 = 42; 42/49 = 86%, 44/49 = 90%, 48/49 = 98%, 7/49 = 14%). Also: the
abstract gives distress d = -0.23, the body d = -0.24; and 38 female + 10 male =
48 of 49, leaving one participant unaccounted for in the retrieved text.

**An exemplar worth keeping for contrast.** Graded 3/5 — sound design, thin
result — but the limitations section pre-empts nearly every criticism I would
have made, in the authors' own words: forecasting vs policy value; the resilience
effect "cannot be unambiguously attributed to accurate personalization" with four
named alternatives; the uncontrolled distal changes "should not be interpreted as
causal"; the exclusion comparisons "exploratory and underpowered and should not be
interpreted as evidence of equivalence"; and an explicit statement that the model
should not be assumed to detect "deterioration trajectories, or crisis contexts
that were absent from the training data." **Keep this one as the reference example
of what honest reporting of a modest result looks like** — it is the calibration
point for noticing when a paper claiming far more has done far less. Note also
what the honesty does not cover: no multiplicity correction and no mention of
multiplicity, no designated primary outcome, no registration number anywhere in
the retrieved text, no a priori feasibility thresholds ("no formal a priori
pass/fail thresholds were specified"), and the unreported decision-point count.
Candour in the discussion is not a substitute for prespecification.

**Next queue entry:** ~~Li W et al., LLM-adapted music therapy pilot RCT (PMID
42853827, PMC13652252)~~ **— decoded 2026-10-12, entry 45** — a confounded three-arm design that the authors report
honestly; screened and held over from today's scan. Then re-check Huang J
(weekly), EAGLE (PMID 42773211) and the four oldest deferred entries (Topol 2019,
Beam & Kohane 2018, Moor 2023, Wu 2021), all due their weekly re-check now and
worth retrying via the alphaXiv route. Holly/Mechael/Lu move to weekly checks too.

## 2026-10-12 run — Li W et al., LLM-adapted music therapy pilot RCT

**Source:** the queue entry held over from the 10-11 scan. PLoS One 2026;21(10):e0360478,
PMID 42853827, PMC13652252, full text retrieved intact. Graded **2/5**.

**Reachability re-check (weekly).** EAGLE (PMID 42773211) — still **no `pmc` field**;
day 21 of daily checks, the single biggest loss in this list. Lu J (42809845), Holly
(42830095) and Mechael (42833894) — still no PMC copy. Huang J (PMC13573861) — **empty
`full_text` for the fourth time**. The four oldest deferred entries (Topol 2019, Beam &
Kohane 2018, Moor 2023, Wu 2021) remain unreachable by the same route; the alphaXiv
retry is still worth trying for any of these that have arXiv preprints, though all four
are journal commentaries unlikely to be on arXiv.

**17. OPEN THE REGISTRATION RECORD. This is the highest-yield check in the whole list.**
The paper carries an NCT number, so I pulled the ClinicalTrials.gov record (NCT07359950,
"EAST-MUSIC") with the Clinical_Trials MCP tool and compared it line by line against the
paper. Six mismatches, one of them decisive:
- The registry names **a single primary outcome: change in state anxiety (STAI-S)**. In
  the paper that outcome is **null** (F(2,109)=0.68, p=.511). The abstract's one positive
  sentence is about **negative affect — a registered secondary outcome** — and the paper
  never tells the reader which outcome was registered as primary. Textbook outcome-
  reporting shift, visible only from the registry.
- Registry enrolment **123**; paper analyses **113**. The number 123 appears nowhere in
  the paper.
- Registry defines every outcome as a **change score**; the paper analysed **baseline-
  adjusted post scores (ANCOVA)**. That is a statistical *upgrade*, but it is an
  undeclared deviation from the registered plan.
- Registry exclusions (diagnosed psychiatric disorders, psychotropic medication,
  participation in another study within 30 days) are **broader than the paper's stated
  exclusions** (severe hearing impairment, cardiovascular disease).
- Registry study start **2025-10-01** vs the paper's recruitment window **23–25 January
  2026**. (The registration *posting* date is not in the retrieved record, so whether
  registration was prospective could not be determined — not guessed.)
- Registry title: "A Randomized Controlled Trial." Paper: "a pilot randomized exploratory
  study." The softening arrives after a null primary outcome.
**Make this a standing step: whenever a paper gives a registration number, fetch the
record and compare the primary outcome, the enrolment, the analysis definition and the
eligibility criteria.** It needs no statistics, takes one tool call, and here it changed
the reading of the paper entirely. Note the inverse too: the 10-11 paper (Rauschenberg)
had *no* registration number at all, so this check was unavailable — which is itself why
its "one of three outcomes" could not be audited.

**18. Confounded multi-component arms: ask what single thing differs.** The three arms
differed in music (Tan Dun's *Hero* / a clinical therapy recording / Hisaishi's
*Kikujiro*), in narration (AI Eastern-imagery / conventional / none) **and** in duration
(26m19s / 29m56s / 25m35s — the longest 17% longer than the shortest). Nothing isolates
the LLM script the title is about. **Randomisation makes the *people* comparable; only
design makes the *treatments* comparable.** The fix is factorial: hold the music fixed,
vary only the words. The authors name the flaw and recommend exactly that — acknowledgement
does not create the missing arm. This is the same family as the AMIE/MedGemma "what is the
comparison actually isolating?" question and it is now the most common defect seen in these
scans.

**19. Check whether the outcomes point the same way.** Negative affect favoured the AI-script
arm; heart rate favoured the *standard therapy* arm, with the AI arm statistically
indistinguishable from silent piano music. When one supposed mechanism produces opposite
rankings on two outcomes, the simplest explanation is that different ingredients of a
confounded bundle are driving different outcomes.

**20. A null is a claim about your own sensitivity.** With N=113, 3 arms, 1 covariate, the
design had ~80% power to detect ηp² ≈ .081 (roughly medium). Achieved power for the observed
state-anxiety effect (ηp²=.012) was **~16%**. So the two nulls rule out medium-or-larger
differences only. Always restate a null as "we could rule out effects larger than X."
Computed with a noncentral-F power calculation; λ = f²N with f² = ηp²/(1−ηp²).

**21. Partial eta squared reproduces from F, and Holm's monotonicity step explains repeated
p-values.** ηp² = df1·F/(df1·F + df2) — all four of the paper's values and the sensitivity
analysis reproduce exactly. Separately, both significant negative-affect contrasts report
p = .026, which looks like a typo and is not: raw p's recovered from the CIs are .0084,
.0094, .895; Holm gives .0251, then .0188 — but **Holm enforces monotonicity**, so the
second is raised to .0251 and both round to .026. All six Holm adjustments across both
outcomes reproduce. **Note which family was corrected:** Holm was applied to the three
pairwise contrasts *within* each outcome, not across the four outcomes.

**22. ANCOVA beats change scores in a randomised trial — and check the slopes.** Two reasons:
fairness (a group starting worse has more room to improve) and, less obviously, **precision**
(a change score carries the noise of two measurements; ANCOVA uses baseline as a predictor
without importing its full error). The assumption to verify is **homogeneity of regression
slopes** (the group × baseline interaction). These authors tested it — non-significant in the
main models (p = .113–.709), but **significant (p = .010) in the state-anxiety sensitivity
analysis**, which they correctly flag. Watch out: ANCOVA is safe here *because of*
randomisation; in observational data "adjusting for baseline" can reverse or manufacture an
effect (Lord's paradox).

**Credit where due.** The statistical execution is better than average: complete data with no
dropout, every Cronbach's α ≥ 0.84 reported, homogeneity-of-slopes tested, Levene and
Shapiro–Wilk run, HC3 robust SEs as a cross-check, a pre-stated outlier rule with sensitivity
analysis, and Holm applied correctly. The authors also state the confound, decline to claim
efficacy, refuse to call the one-arm cultural-resonance rating a manipulation check, and
recommend the right follow-up design. The 2/5 is for the design and the registry shift, not
for sloppiness.

**Next queue entry:** nothing reachable remains in the curated backlog, so expect another
`pubmed pmc[sb]` scan tomorrow. Standing candidates on weekly re-check: EAGLE (42773211,
highest priority), Huang J (42765400), Lu J (42809845), Holly (42830095) / Mechael (42833894),
and the four oldest deferred reviews. Standing offers never taken up: upgrade the abstract-only
entries (Gulshan #2, Ardila #8, McKinney #10) if PDFs are supplied, and retrofit summary #1
(Vishwanath) with a Statistics Spotlight.

## 2026-10-13 run — Liu & Li, Vision Transformer synovitis grading

**Source:** a `pubmed pmc[sb]` scan, because the curated queue has nothing reachable left.
Query: medical-AI terms in title/abstract AND (diagnosis OR screening OR prognosis OR
clinical decision) AND PMC AND a 1–13 October 2026 date window — 479 hits, top 40 pulled,
8 screened by metadata, 2 full texts retrieved. Annals of Medicine 2026;58(1):2738281,
PMID 42820939, PMC13637757. Graded **3/5**.

**Daily reachability re-check.** EAGLE (PMID 42773211) — **still no `pmc` field on day 22**.
Lu J (42809845), Holly (42830095) and Mechael (42833894) unchanged. Huang J (PMC13573861)
remains an empty `full_text`.

**Rejected in screening, with reasons** (keeping these so the screen is auditable):
polyphenol quorum-sensing GNN (42828729) and a TNFSF10 bioinformatics study (42834715) —
not clinical medical AI; FTIR proteinuria ML (42857332) — n=51 total across four groups,
too thin to carry a decode; paediatric UTI random forest (42841462) — no external set;
gallstone body-composition ML (42841700) and COPD rapid-decline CatBoost (42817919) —
both decent, held as spares.

**HELD FOR TOMORROW, and it is the stronger paper of the two finalists:** Tong M, et al.,
"Artificial intelligence-powered renal pathology for glomerular disease classification: a
systematic review and meta-analysis," Renal Failure 2026;48(1):2729643 (PMID 42838512,
PMC13644454, full text retrieved and confirmed non-empty). Why it is worth a decode:
(a) it pools **accuracy and precision** across studies, and both are prevalence-dependent,
so pooling them is a genuine methodological error that the bivariate/HSROC literature exists
to avoid; (b) it reports a **pooled AUC of 1.00** for minimal change disease, which is a red
flag on its face; (c) its own abstract says "AI also showed higher accuracy than senior
pathologists" and then concludes "current head-to-head evidence is insufficient to establish
superiority over pathologists" — a self-contradiction inside one abstract, and exactly the
kind of sentence that gets cited loosely; (d) it describes its sample as "39,536 validation
sample units, not necessarily unique patients," the unit-of-analysis problem stated by the
authors themselves. Deferred only because the list already decoded a diagnostic meta-analysis
on 10-08 (Feng, cerebral microbleeds) and today's paper brought three concepts that were new.

**23. Weighted kappa, and always ask which weighting.** New to the list, and the right metric
for any ordered scale (cancer stage, NYHA class, biopsy grade, 0–3 synovitis). Plain kappa
corrects accuracy for chance agreement but treats all disagreements alike; **quadratic**
weighting penalises by the square of the distance (off by 1 → 1, by 2 → 4, by 3 → 9), so a
0-vs-3 blunder dominates a 1-vs-2 quibble. Two watch-outs: quadratic weights are **generous**,
so frequent off-by-one errors still score well (linear weights score the same predictions
lower) — a paper that says "weighted kappa" without naming the weighting is uncomparable; and
kappa's chance term depends on the **grade distribution**, so kappas from different populations
are not directly comparable.

**24. "The AI beat expert agreement" is usually two different measurements.** The sharpest
finding in this paper, and the paper does not notice it. Humans: quadratic-weighted kappa
**0.81**, raw pairwise, reader A vs reader B each working alone. Model: **0.842**, against the
**third-expert-adjudicated consensus** — a label with much of the individual noise removed,
i.e. an easier and more stable target. Like comparing two gymnastics judges to each other, then
comparing a fourth observer to the panel's agreed score. The fair comparison (model vs reader A,
alongside reader B vs reader A) was computable from data the authors already had and was not
reported. **Standing question: when a paper says the model matched or beat experts, ask what it
was compared against — one human, or a cleaned-up consensus of several?**

**25. The shared-labeller circularity, and the six-rung external-validation ladder.** The same
two sonographers labelled *every* image, the "external" set included, so the model was trained
to reproduce their habits and then scored against their habits. The rungs, weakest to strongest:
(1) random split; (2) temporal split; (3) different operators; (4) different devices;
(5) different institution; (6) **different labellers** — the only rung that breaks the
circularity. This paper's title claims rung 5 and delivers rung 2 plus parts of 3 and 4. Verify
it from the baseline table without reading the methods: **Overall 312 = Development 218 +
Internal 47 + External 47**, one consecutive cohort sliced three ways. **Tell: if the "external"
set is a neat fraction of the total cohort, it is a split, not an independent collection.** Note
also that a small internal→external drop (0.809 → 0.771) measured between two slices of one
hospital says little about the drop at a second hospital — the literature is full of models that
survived temporal splits and collapsed on geographic ones.

**26. Recompute one reported number from another.** Internal test: 152 correct + 30 off-by-one +
6 off-by-more = 188 ✓, and 152/188 = 0.8085 = the reported 0.809 ✓. Those same counts give
MAE = (30×1 + 6×2)/188 = **0.2234**, matching the reported 0.223 exactly — which independently
confirms the breakdown *and* pins every large error at exactly two grades. But within-one-grade
accuracy from those counts is 182/188 = **0.968**, while the paper reports **0.957** (= 180/188).
Those cannot both describe one confusion matrix; since the MAE matches, 0.957 looks like the slip.
Consequence-free here, but the habit is the point: a paper whose numbers reconcile has probably
been handled carefully.

**27. Credit the leakage precaution when you see it.** Splitting **at the patient level** so all
of one person's images stay in one subset is the most commonly botched step in imaging AI — two
views of the same knee landing on opposite sides lets the model recognise the knee rather than
the disease. These authors did it and said why. Worth noting as a positive marker, alongside
their reporting of inter-reader reliability (most papers omit it), their deliberate refusal to
harmonise across vendors, and their plain statement that no sample-size calculation was done.

**28. The missing analysis in every reader-assistance study: automation bias.** Juniors improved
(accuracy 0.702 → 0.782, weighted kappa 0.692 → 0.801) and read faster (21.4 → 15.8 s). Neither
answers the safety question: **how often did a reader who would have been right get pulled to the
model's wrong answer?** The data to answer it exist in the study (each reader's unassisted and
assisted grade per case, crossed with whether the model was right) and one table would settle it.
Also note faster reading with the answer displayed is partly anchoring, not comprehension. And
the reader study never reports **how many cases were read**, names no test, and gives only
"p < 0.05" — with 3 readers, whether the unit of analysis is readers or image-level readings
changes the p-value by orders of magnitude.

**Also unreconciled in this paper, worth remembering as a pattern:** the methods say "a senior
sonographer provided the reference standard" while the results score a "senior reference reader"
at accuracy 0.809 — a reader scored against their own labels would be 1.000, so these cannot be
the same person. And 27.6% of patients had grey-scale only with no Doppler, yet a Doppler-only
model is scored and a fused model is the headline, with no statement of how those ~30% were
handled.

**Next queue entry:** ~~the Tong et al. renal-pathology meta-analysis above (PMC13644454)~~
**— decoded 2026-10-14, entry 47.** Spares if needed: gallstone body composition (PMC13647361), COPD rapid decline
(PMC13637773). Standing daily/weekly re-checks unchanged: EAGLE highest priority, then Huang J,
Lu J, Holly/Mechael, and the four oldest deferred reviews (Topol 2019, Beam & Kohane 2018,
Moor 2023, Wu 2021). Standing offers never taken up: upgrade the abstract-only entries
(Gulshan #2, Ardila #8, McKinney #10) if PDFs are supplied; retrofit summary #1 (Vishwanath)
with a Statistics Spotlight.

## 2026-10-14 run — Tong et al., AI renal pathology meta-analysis

**Source:** the entry held over from the 10-13 screen. Renal Failure 2026;48(1):2729643,
PMID 42838512, PMC13644454. Full text retrieved; figures and all supplementary tables
unretrievable. Graded **3/5** — and the grade undersells the conduct: this is close to
4/5 reviewing of an evidence base that cannot support more than 3.

**Daily reachability re-check.** EAGLE (PMID 42773211) — **still no `pmc` field on day 23**.

**29. Accuracy for an uncommon disease starts high before anyone does any work. THE cleanest
published example this list has found.** The paper's own table: MsPGN pooled accuracy **0.96**
with pooled sensitivity **0.61**. Computed MsPGN prevalence from the validation sets the paper
itself lists for the largest included study (311/5330, 70/1226, 48/875, 48/933, 51/962,
52/1024 → 580/10,350 = **5.6%**). A model that says "not MsPGN" every single time scores
accuracy **0.944**. The reported 0.96 beats doing nothing by **1.6 percentage points** while
missing two in five real cases. Decompose with **accuracy = p·Se + (1−p)·Sp**: solving gives
implied specificity ≈ 0.98, so the number reflects what the models are good at (ruling out)
because 94% of slides need ruling out. **The structural tell needed no arithmetic: accuracy was
the highest of the four main metrics for ALL EIGHT diseases** (MN .96/.96, LN .79/.97,
DN .78/.96, MsPGN .61/.96, MPGN .60/.91 as Se/Acc). When one metric is the most flattering in
every single condition, it is measuring how the number is built, not how good the model is.
**Standing step: for any accuracy figure, get the prevalence and compute 1−p first.** This is
technique #4 (the trivial baseline) in its most quotable form yet.

**30. Confidence interval vs prediction interval — new to the list, and the single most useful
column in any meta-analysis.** CI = where the *average of past studies* sits. PI = what *the
next study* should expect. They diverge when heterogeneity is high, because the CI shrinks with
more studies while the PI must keep carrying the real between-study spread. Worked examples from
this paper: IgAN accuracy CI (0.92, 0.97) vs PI **(0.82, 1.00)** — 3.6× wider; IgAN precision CI
(0.84, 0.95) vs PI (0.62, 1.00); LN precision CI (0.78, 0.97) vs PI **(0.50, 1.00)**; MsPGN
sensitivity CI (0.45, 0.76) vs PI (0.19, 0.95); and MPGN precision 0.58 with CI **(0.00, 1.00)**
— two datasets, no information at all, and the most honest number in the paper. Context: median
I² across the 38 pooled estimates = **92.8%**, with 28/38 ≥ 75% and 24/38 ≥ 90%. **If a review
reports high I² and no prediction interval, you are being shown a precise-looking average of
things that disagree.** Note also what I² is not: not a measure of test quality, and not evidence
of bias — it means the estimate is *not transferable*. These authors say that in their methods.

**31. A pooled AUC of 1.00 is a red flag, and check whether each metric in a row shares a k.**
MCD AUC = **1.00 (95% CI 0.99–1.00) with I² = 0.0%** — perfect discrimination replicated
identically across six datasets, which real diagnostic tests do not do; it is the fingerprint of
small curated datasets. It also sits oddly beside MCD's own sensitivity of 0.92: AUC 1.00 means a
threshold existed catching every case with no false alarms, yet 8% were missed at the threshold
used. **The resolution is the k:** MCD sensitivity rests on 11 datasets, MCD AUC on **6**. Same
pattern elsewhere — MN sensitivity k=22 but AUC k=10; IgAN k=14 vs k=11; FSGS and MPGN have no
AUC row at all. Studies reporting AUC are a non-random subset (they need probability outputs and
a threshold sweep, which tracks with better-resourced work). **Reading across a row invites the
assumption that the metrics describe one body of evidence; verify the k on each.**

**32. Datasets are not studies, and "units" are not patients — a three-layer version of the
standing question.** (a) 15 studies produced ~26 validation datasets (16 'IV' + 10 'EV' tokens
in Table 1); MN alone appears in **22**. One study (Ma et al.) contributes six on its own —
IV + EV1..EV5 — all sharing one model, one training set, one architecture, one group. The authors
*did* guard against patient overlap and took only the main model where several were tested on one
cohort, which is better than most reviews; but non-overlapping patients ≠ independent evidence.
(b) 39,536 validation "units" decomposes to **16,223 patient-based + 9,505 image-based +
9,988 glomerulus-based + 3,820 lesion-based** (sums exactly), so **only 41% are patients**. (c)
The authors entered unit of analysis as a meta-regression covariate and **found it mattered** —
for IgAN, precision p=0.002, F1 p=0.001, AUC p=0.02 — then still reported one pooled estimate
across units. **Ask for a second column, "number of contributing studies," next to every k.**

**33. "Higher accuracy than senior pathologists" rested on ONE study.** Comparative data existed
in only 4 of 15 studies: one reporting junior pathologists, one senior, two unspecified. The
abstract says "AI also showed higher accuracy than senior pathologists for IgAN, MN, and MCD" —
three diseases, one study's senior readers, compared by **Z-test between pooled estimates from
different studies on different slides**, i.e. an indirect comparison, not an experiment. The body
text calls it exploratory, descriptive, hypothesis-generating and explicitly "not head-to-head"
— three hedges the abstract sentence will outrun. Pairs with technique #24 (the AI-beat-experts
comparison is usually two different measurements) and adds a new variant: **the comparison may
also be two different sets of patients.**

**34. When a paper's conclusion is more cautious than its abstract, believe the conclusion.**
Almost every criticism in this decode is one the authors made first: that the totals are analysis
units not patients; that **"a bivariate or HSROC model could not be applied"** and the pooled
metrics are "complementary reported performance measures rather than a complete paired
sensitivity-specificity diagnostic accuracy model"; that **specificity was not pooled and they
refused to impute it** because that "could introduce bias"; that 7/15 studies are at high risk of
bias on model evaluation; that GRADE certainty is moderate at best and "substantially lower" for
five of eight diseases; that all 15 studies are retrospective. Their conclusion — "current
head-to-head evidence is insufficient to establish superiority over pathologists" — is the
correct reading of their own data and the sentence to quote. **The gap between a conscientious
methods section and one quotable abstract sentence is where most misleading citations are born.**

**Also worth keeping as positive markers of a careful review:** prediction intervals for *every*
estimate; REML random effects with the Hartung-Knapp-Sidik-Jonkman adjustment (widens intervals
appropriately with few studies); a **prespecified 0.80 relevance threshold** with the summary
figure colour-coded by whether each CI excludes it; leave-one-out and Egger's restricted to k≥10
with the reason stated ("would be unstable and difficult to interpret"); duplicate screening with
agreement reported (kappa 0.87 / 0.89 / 0.91); PRISMA-DTA, PROBAST+AI and GRADE all applied and
their uncomfortable results printed.

**35. Registration numbers can be malformed — check the format, and say what you could not
verify.** The paper gives PROSPERO **CRD420261278316**, which has 12 digits. PROSPERO identifiers
follow CRD + '4' + YYYY + 6 digits = 11 digits (e.g. CRD42026123456); this reads as
4 + 2026 + 1278316, a seven-digit final group. One digit too many, and it defeats the entire
point of registering. **Recorded as a formatting discrepancy, NOT as a claim that the
registration does not exist** — the record could not be verified with the tools available, and no
link to it was published in the summary. Contrast 10-12 (Li et al.), where the registry *was*
reachable and changed the reading of the paper entirely: the lesson from #17 holds, but it
depends on the identifier being correct.

**Also noted in this paper:** the reference standard is not uniform — the inclusion criteria say
pathology confirmation, while the study table records roughly half the entries as "expert
consensus," which raises the same circularity question as #25 when the comparison is AI vs
humans. And the study table carries at least one stray full-width character ("EV：171"),
suggesting it was assembled by hand.

## 2026-10-15 run — Jerjes et al., elastic scattering spectroscopy for nodal metastasis

**Source:** a fresh `pubmed pmc[sb]` scan (medical-AI terms AND randomized/prospective/multicenter/
external validation/trial, 8–15 October window, 57 hits, top 30 pulled, 6 screened). This beat both
spares held from 10-13. JMIR Cancer 2026;12:e103960, PMID 42868592, PMC13661036. Graded **4/5**.

**Daily reachability re-check.** EAGLE (PMID 42773211) — **still no `pmc` field on day 24**, though
the record now carries full pagination (Nat Med 2026;32(10):3748–3759) and MeSH terms, so it is
being indexed; a PMC deposit may yet follow.

**Screened and rejected / deferred today:** AI air-quality implementation qualitative study
(42868279) — no statistics to teach; measles vaccination-defaulting TabNet ensembles (42867502,
PMC13660459) — decent SMOTE-NC/SHAP material, held as a spare; Mössbauer-spectroscopy ML
transferability (42866681) — **not medical**, so out of scope for this list, but worth recording
because it quantifies technique #25's ladder in another field: median R² fell from 0.670 under
random splitting to 0.322 holding out publications and 0.287 holding out experimental-provenance
groups. **HELD FOR TOMORROW:** Park YS et al., smartphone ECG app (ECG Buddy) for left-ventricular
dysfunction in sepsis, 425 patients across 3 hospitals, AUROC 0.901, beating NT-proBNP 0.911 vs
0.637 (PMID 42865282, PMC13659111, J Korean Med Sci). Two angles the list has never covered:
**author affiliations include the company that makes the device (ARPI Inc.)**, so conflicts of
interest become teachable; and the threshold (37.35) is described as "optimized," which is the
tuned-on-test-data question again.

**36. Nested cross-validation, and the leak it does NOT close.** Ordinary CV used for both tuning
and reporting is optimistic — you are quoting the maximum of many noisy numbers. Nested CV puts an
**inner** loop in charge of every choice (hyperparameters, components, calibration, threshold) and
touches the **outer** test fold once, so the estimate describes the *whole procedure*. This paper
did 10 repetitions × 5 outer folds = 50 splits with a 4-fold inner loop, and fitted smoothing,
scaling, SNV, PCA, component count, regularisation, class weighting, calibration **and** the
threshold inside the training folds. **But nested CV protects tuning within a family, not the
choice between families.** Five families were compared and the hybrid was carried forward "because
it had the highest pooled patient-grouped out-of-fold AUROC" — chosen on the same predictions then
reported. The authors say it in the abstract: the estimate "does not represent independent
validation of a prospectively fixed final model." **Tell to watch for: "we compared N models and
the best achieved X." X is the maximum of N noisy estimates; the bias is upward and unquantified.**

**37. THE LEAKAGE PENALTY, MEASURED INSIDE ONE PAPER. Keep this as the canonical number.** Same
model, same 274 nodes, two ways of splitting:
  - patient-grouped (correct): **AUROC 0.966**, TP 90 / **FN 6** / TN 170 / FP 8, Se 93.8 Sp 95.5 Acc 94.9
  - node-grouped only (the usual shortcut): **AUROC 0.989**, TP 93 / **FN 3** / TN 172 / FP 6, Se 96.9 Sp 96.6 Acc 96.7
**The shortcut buys +0.023 AUROC, +1.8 points of accuracy, and halves the missed cancers from 6 to
3.** Nothing about the model changed; only the bookkeeping. Every operating characteristic in both
rows reproduces exactly from the confusion matrices. This is the quantified version of technique
#27 (credit the patient-level split) and the sharpest answer yet to "does grouping really matter?"
Structure here was three deep: **45 patients → 280 nodes → 840 spectra**, median 6 nodes/patient.
Note also the aggregation gain for contrast: patient-grouped at *spectrum* level AUROC 0.944 →
node level 0.966, i.e. **+0.022 from legitimately averaging 3 readings — almost exactly the same
size as the +0.023 from cheating.** A real improvement and a bookkeeping error can move a number
identically; only one is earned.

**38. Clustering shows up in the ERRORS, not just the splits.** All **8 false positives came from
just 2 pathology-negative patients**, both with florid reactive change (follicular hyperplasia,
sinus histiocytosis). Treating nodes as independent would score that as 8 independent errors in
178 chances; really it is ~2 errors repeated across two people's nodes. Which is why this paper
also used **patient-clustered bootstrap** (2,000 resamples, whole patients with all their nodes)
and **within-patient permutation** (10,000 permutations preserving each patient's own positive/
negative mix). **Getting the splits right and then bootstrapping over observations undoes half the
work** — most papers that group their folds still compute intervals as if every node were a person.

**39. Calibration slope and calibration-in-the-large** (new to the list; Brier was covered 10-03).
Brier 0.063 against its prevalence reference p(1−p) = 0.350 × 0.650 = **0.228**, so ~72% reduction
in squared error — *a Brier score means nothing without that reference*. **Calibration-in-the-large
−0.03**: is the average predicted risk right? (0 = yes.) **Calibration slope 0.94**: is the spread
right? (1 = ideal; **<1 = predictions too extreme**, the signature of mild overfitting — and the
paper's own "modest overprediction at the highest probabilities" matches). Discrimination says the
ranking is good; calibration says the displayed numbers mean something. Calibration is also the
first property to break when a model moves hospital.

**40. Report the no-result rate.** 6 of 280 nodes (**2.1%**) produced no analysable reading and were
excluded from the denominator. STARD requires this and almost nobody reports it: a test that fails
to answer 2% of the time is a different proposition from one that always answers. Note the knock-on
— all 6 were pathology-negative, so removing them nudges prevalence from 34.3% to 35.0%.

**41. Keep this paper as the reference example of GOOD methodology**, the counterpart to 10-12
(registry-checking) and 10-14 (believe the conclusion over the abstract). Markers worth recognising:
QC rules prespecified and applied **blind to the label**; pathologists blinded to the spectra; nodes
selected for the index test **before** final pathology was known; 8 optical features prespecified;
robustness as real tests not decoration (leave-one-patient-out 0.961, leave-one-batch-out 0.952,
drop worst 10% 0.969, 2-spectra 0.951, 3-spectra-only 0.964, area-normalised 0.895, shape-focused
0.912 — the two that strip amplitude lose most, and the authors use that to flag amplitude as
handling-sensitive rather than burying it); error analysis naming *which* cases failed (4 of 6
misses were micrometastases 0.3–1.6 mm); TRIPOD+AI and STARD-AI followed; and plain statements that
no sample-size calculation was done, that the comparison was exploratory and unregistered, and that
the binary operating characteristics are "secondary descriptive" because the threshold was applied
after validation. **Here there is no gap between the methods and the abstract — the caution is in
both.** What still caps it at 4/5: no external validation, 45 patients, ex vivo only, acknowledged
selection optimism, and the hardest category (micrometastases) is where it fails.

**Still worth asking of this paper:** sensitivity split by deposit size (micro vs macro) is not
reported though the counts exist; one operator per node prevents any interoperator reproducibility
estimate, on a handheld probe where contact angle and pressure were not measured; funding and
competing interests do not appear in the retrieved text; and the median excision-to-reading
interval of 18 min (IQR 13–26) could be tested against signal drift from the recorded timestamps.

**Next queue entry:** ~~the smartphone-ECG sepsis study above (PMID 42865282, PMC13659111)~~
**— decoded 2026-10-16, entry 49.** Spares: measles TabNet (PMC13660459), gallstone body composition
(PMC13647361), COPD rapid decline (PMC13637773). Standing re-checks unchanged: EAGLE daily, then
Huang J (42765400, empty full text ×4), Lu J (42809845), Holly (42830095) / Mechael (42833894), and
the four oldest deferred reviews. Standing offers never taken up: upgrade the abstract-only entries
(Gulshan #2, Ardila #8, McKinney #10) if PDFs are supplied; retrofit summary #1 (Vishwanath) with a
Statistics Spotlight.
