## 2026-10-16 run — Park et al., smartphone ECG app for LV dysfunction in sepsis

**Source:** the entry held over from the 10-15 screen. J Korean Med Sci 2026;41(39):e217,
PMID 42865282, PMC13659111. Full text retrieved; figures and supplementary tables not.
Graded **3/5**. Sits directly opposite yesterday's Jerjes paper as a matched pair on
threshold discipline — same statistic, opposite practice, one day apart.

**Daily reachability re-check.** EAGLE (PMID 42773211) — **still no `pmc` field on day 25.**

**42. Sensitivity and specificity belong to the TEST; PPV and NPV belong to the POPULATION.**
Reconstructed the confusion matrix from the reported percentages: **TP 60, FN 7, TN 269, FP 89**
(sums to 425; every reported figure reproduces — Se 89.55%, Sp 75.14%, PPV 40.27%, NPV 97.46%,
accuracy 77.41%). Then held Se and Sp fixed and varied prevalence:
  30% → PPV 60.7 / NPV 94.4 · **15.8% (this study) → PPV 40.3 / NPV 97.5** · 10% → 28.6 / 98.5 ·
  5% → 15.9 / 99.3 · 2% → 6.8 / 99.7
Same test every time. **The abstract reports sensitivity, specificity and NPV, and omits PPV** —
the one unflattering operating characteristic, and the one saying 89 of 149 alarms were false.
Note which direction the selection bias runs: the cohort is 7.2% of the registry, selected for
having had an echo, so it is *enriched*; in a broader population NPV improves and PPV gets worse.
That happens to favour the authors' own framing (a rule-out screen), which is the defensible one.

**43. F1 computed for the opposite class — a clean, exactly reproducible table error.** Using the
paper's own printed precision (40.3%) and recall (89.6%), F1 = 2·p·r/(p+r) = **55.6%**. The table
prints **84.9%**. Substituting NPV (97.5%) and specificity (75.1%) — i.e. treating "no dysfunction"
as the positive class — gives **84.8%**. The same substitution reproduces the other two rows to the
decimal: SNUBH **86.8%** and NT-proBNP **79.4%**. So three F1 values describe detecting *healthy*
hearts while precision and recall in the same rows describe detecting *sick* ones; the real F1 for
the actual task is ~29 points lower. **Generalised check: F1 is only defined relative to a chosen
positive class — recompute it from the row's own precision and recall and see if it lands.** Two
smaller inconsistencies in the same table: PPV 40.4 vs Precision 40.3 (identical quantities), and
Sensitivity 89.6 (82.1–97.0) vs Recall 89.6 (82.1–95.5) (identical quantities, different upper CI).

**44. A threshold quoted to two decimals is usually a threshold tuned on the test set.** "Using the
threshold that maximized its Youden index" → **37.35** (whole cohort), 37.25 (SNUBH), 10,237.05
(NT-proBNP), all chosen on the same patients they are scored on, with no split and no
cross-validation. Youden = Se + Sp − 1 = 0.896 + 0.751 − 1 = 0.647 at the chosen point. **The AUC
is threshold-free and unaffected; everything downstream — Se, Sp, PPV, NPV, accuracy, F1 — is the
best case on this dataset.** The tell is precision beyond what the measurement deserves: 37.35 on
a 0–100 score is not a clinically reasoned boundary, it is where this dataset's curve peaked.
**Direct contrast with technique #36 (10-15):** Jerjes et al. chose thresholds inside inner
training folds, applied the median training-derived threshold once, and still demoted the
resulting figures to "secondary descriptive operating characteristics."

**45. DeLong's test is right for two markers on the same patients — then ask whether the
comparator got a fair run.** DeLong accounts for the correlation between two AUCs measured in the
same people; an unpaired test would overstate uncertainty. Used correctly here (0.911 vs 0.637,
p < 0.001, n = 234). But: (a) **NT-proBNP underperformed its own literature** — 0.637 against the
0.69–0.75 the authors themselves cite from prior sepsis studies; (b) it was **not adjusted for age
or renal function** (their limitation 3) while CKD was 20.9% vs 7.8% between groups (p = 0.002),
and CKD raises NT-proBNP regardless of the heart; (c) the comparison covered **1 of 3 sites**, the
others using a different assay. **Standing check: when a paper beats a comparator, look up how the
comparator did against its own published range. If it underperformed, the margin belongs partly to
the comparison, not to the new test.**

**46. Conflicts of interest: read the affiliation lines, not just the disclosure box.** Two of eight
authors list **ARPI Inc.**, the maker of ECG Buddy — one of them first. **No funding or
competing-interests statement appears in the retrieved text.** Not disqualifying (device studies
are nearly always done with the device's makers) but it is a reason to recompute rather than trust
adjectives — which is how the F1 error surfaced. New to the list; worth a standing glance at every
device or software paper.

**47. "Per unit" odds ratios are meaningless until you know how wide the variable runs.** Adjusted
OR **1.015 per unit** of a 0–100 score sounds negligible. The median gap between groups was ~59
points: 1.015^58.8 = **2.40** (CI across the same gap, 1.34 to 4.27). Always compound a per-unit
effect across the observed range before judging it.

**Also found and worth keeping as patterns:** the denominator cascade is extreme — **5,940 → 2,311
excluded (no echo) → 3,204 excluded (no ECG) → 425 included = 7.2% of the registry**, and the
authors honestly report that the included were older, more often in septic shock and heart failure,
yet had *better* survival ("selectively enriched... sufficiently stable to receive echocardiographic
evaluation"). The patients a bedside tool is *for* are the ones excluded. **46 patients are missing
qSOFA and nobody says so**: quartile counts sum to 99/94/96/90 against sizes 107/106/107/105, and
Table 1 agrees at 379 of 425 — so the qSOFA null and the qSOFA-adjusted mortality model both ran on
379. And this was **not a field test**: ECGs were cropped from the EMR, shown on a 24-inch monitor
under controlled lighting, photographed by **one** specialist at 30–40 cm with an iPhone 15 Pro,
with reproducibility never assessed — yet the discussion claims "anyone can use this technology
anywhere from emergency scenes to hospital wards." Gap between what was tested and what is claimed.

**Credit where due:** three sites, 425 patients, prespecified comparator, correct paired test,
subgroup AUCs stable at 0.865–0.925, proper trend tests (Jonckheere–Terpstra, Cochran–Armitage) on
a clean dose-response (LVEF 63.0→60.0→55.9→40.0; mortality 8.4→16.0→19.6→25.7%), exploratory
adjustment for confounders, scores blinded to the echocardiographers, and a limitations section
that names the selection bias, the 72-hour ECG-to-echo gap ("a significant limitation"), the
absence of sepsis-specific training, and the missing reproducibility analysis.

## 2026-10-17 run — EAGLE, decoded ABSTRACT ONLY after 26 days

**The 26-day wait ended, and did not end well.** On day 26 a PMC identifier finally appeared for
EAGLE (PMID 42773211) — **PMC13668756** — so the daily re-check paid off to that extent. But the
record serves **no body text**. Every route failed:
  - PubMed MCP `get_full_text_article` on PMC13668756: resolves, returns `count: 0`
  - `pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov` via WebFetch: `ENOTFOUND` (DNS blocked by network policy)
  - `www.nature.com` via WebFetch: `ENOTFOUND`
  - Scite `read_fulltext` on the DOI: **requires a paid plan**, not available to this session
  - alphaXiv `discover_papers`: searched on EAGLE / esophageal / noncontrast CT / DAMO — **no arXiv
    preprint of this paper exists**, so the route that rescued MedGemma and AMIE does not apply here

**Decision, and the reasoning, because it is a judgement call worth recording.** Deferring a 27th
time was the alternative. Against it: the PMC-indexed-but-empty state has persisted for Huang J for
9+ days with no sign of resolving, so "wait one more day" has no end date; three reachable spares
were available but none is remotely as consequential; and the abstract is unusually dense — six
named cohorts with sizes, three operating points, a calibration delta and a prospective PPV, which
is enough to do real screening arithmetic. For it: an abstract-only decode of the list's flagship
paper is thin, and this list exists to argue against thin readings. **Resolved by decoding it with a
prominent warning block at the top listing every failed route, marking the grade provisional
(4/5), marking every unavailable item as such, and committing to upgrade when the full text
becomes readable.** The amended reachability checklist (10-08) stands and is now doubly confirmed:
**a confirmed PMC identifier is not a confirmed full text — both the `pmc` field AND a non-empty
`full_text` must be verified.**

**48. Screening arithmetic at low prevalence — the canonical worked example.** Specificity, not
sensitivity, decides whether a screening programme can exist. At EAGLE's headline point
(Se 90.0%, Sp 98.5%), per 10,000 screened at 0.33% prevalence: 33 cancers, **30 caught, 3 missed,
150 false alarms, PPV 16.6%, 5.0 false alarms per cancer.** Holding the test fixed and varying
prevalence: 1% → PPV 37.7% (1.7 false alarms each) · 0.5% → 23.2% (3.3) · 0.2% → 10.7% (8.3) ·
0.1% → 5.7% (16.7) · 0.05% → 2.9% (**33 false alarms per cancer**). **Improving specificity from
98.5% to 99.6% is worth far more than improving sensitivity from 90% to 95%**, and that is exactly
what the team did.

**49. Reconstructing a calibration from a percentage — and checking it against the paper.** The
abstract says calibration "reduced false positives by 72.7% while preserving sensitivity." Work it:
false-positive rate 1.5% × (1 − 0.727) = **0.41%**, so specificity 98.5% → **99.59%**. Redo the
10,000-person screen: false alarms **150 → 41**, PPV **16.6% → 42.1%**, false alarms per cancer
**5.0 → 1.4**. The paper independently reports **42.2% PPV** in its prospective cohort (n=17,446).
Predicted 42.1 vs reported 42.2 — agreement to a tenth of a point, which supports reading the
prospective cohort as using the calibrated operating point at ~0.33% prevalence. **Labelled in the
summary as an inference from the abstract's numbers, not a value the abstract states.** A reminder
that a reported *delta* plus one reported *level* often reconstructs an unreported operating point.

**50. In screening, read the hardest disease category, not the headline one.** Sensitivity 90.0% for
**cancer** versus **52.5% for precancerous lesions** (65.0% at the higher-sensitivity point; 78.4%
for stage I). Screening's whole purpose is catching disease *before* it is cancer, so 52.5% is the
result and 90.0% is the easier task. Same pattern as 10-14 (pooled metrics excellent for common
glomerular subtypes, sensitivity 0.61 for the rare one). **Standing check: when a paper reports
performance by stage or severity, the headline will be the easiest category.**

**51. Count the operating points before combining any two numbers.** This abstract reports at least
three: 98.5% specificity (opportunistic external test), 99.94% specificity (real-world low-dose),
a "higher-sensitivity operating point" (paired CT-endoscopy), and an unstated one behind the 42.2%
PPV. **"90% sensitivity and 99.94% specificity" is a sentence whose every number appears in the
abstract and which describes a configuration never measured.** Sensitivity and specificity are a
pair from one point on one curve; they travel together or not at all. To the authors' credit, the
phrase "higher-sensitivity operating point" tells the reader the threshold moved — the dangerous
papers are the ones that move it silently. Pairs with #44 (a threshold quoted to two decimals).

**Also noted:** the six named validation cohorts sum to **77,582** against the abstract's stated
**80,612**, leaving **3,030** unaccounted for — probably an unnamed internal set, resolvable in one
sentence of the full text. No confidence intervals appear anywhere in the abstract. The reference
standard is not stated for any cohort, and for the 11,466-patient opportunistic cohort it cannot
have been endoscopy — **that is the single thing most worth reading when the full text opens.** No
interval-cancer rate. No radiologist comparison. Roughly a quarter of the 43 authors are at DAMO
Academy / Alibaba Group and Hupan Lab, who built the model; the competing-interests statement is
not in the abstract.

**STANDING TASK — upgrade this entry.** Re-check PMC13668756 for body text **weekly**, and retry
Scite if a subscription ever becomes available. When the full text opens, rewrite summary 50 from it
and revise the provisional grade. The questions to answer first: the reference standard in each
cohort; the participant funnel; whether the prospective cohort was enriched; confidence intervals;
and the competing-interests statement.

**Next queue entry:** nothing reachable left. Three spares already screened and in hand: measles
TabNet (PMID 42867502, PMC13660459), gallstone body composition (PMC13647361), COPD rapid decline
(PMC13637773). Fresh candidate from today's scan, not yet screened in depth: **CheXNeXt open-source
chest-infection AI on after-hours ED radiographs in Singapore** (PMID 42869615, PMC13663711, BMJ
Health Care Inform) — 1,006 radiographs, and its abstract is a gift for the threshold lesson:
sensitivity 0.234 for consolidation and 0.176 for pneumonia at the published default thresholds,
rising to 0.894 and 0.681 after Youden optimisation on the same data. That is techniques #44 and
#51 in one paper, plus the question of what a "published threshold" is worth off its home dataset.
Standing re-checks: Huang J (42765400, empty full text ×5), Lu J (42809845), Holly (42830095) /
Mechael (42833894), and the four oldest deferred reviews. Standing offers never taken up: upgrade
the abstract-only entries (Gulshan #2, Ardila #8, McKinney #10) if PDFs are supplied; retrofit

## 2026-10-18 run — EAGLE full text finally opened; summary 50 rewritten

**The standing task from yesterday is closed.** PMC13668756 served no body text on 2026-10-17
after five failed routes, and the summary carried a written commitment to rewrite when it opened.
On day 27 the same identifier returned **61,804 characters** of full text. The commitment outranked
decoding a new paper, so today's run rewrote entry 50 from the full text rather than adding entry
51. The grade did not move (4/5). The basis for it did: yesterday was grading a design as
described, today is grading it on verification.

**What the full text settled, question by question from yesterday's list.**

- **The 3,030-patient gap resolves exactly.** Six named cohorts 77,582 + an unnamed internal test
  cohort of 2,500 (never mentioned in the abstract) + the 530-patient prospective endoscopic cohort
  = **80,612**. Yesterday's guess — an unnamed internal set — was right.
- **The reference standard** (yesterday's single most important question): positives by surgical or
  biopsy histopathology, AJCC 8th edition; negatives by ≥2 years of clinical follow-up **or** a
  negative endoscopy within 1 year. The authors volunteer the label-noise risk this creates. So the
  11,466-patient opportunistic cohort was not endoscopy-verified throughout, as suspected.
- **Thresholds were prespecified**, averaged over fivefold cross-validation and **locked before any
  test cohort was touched** — 99% specificity for opportunistic screening, 98% sensitivity for
  population screening, fixed at 0.93 and 0.88. This **overturns** yesterday's suspicion that the
  multiple operating points invited cherry-picking. They invite misquotation, which is a different
  fault, and the discipline here is the best feature of the method.
- **Confidence intervals** are present throughout the full text; their absence was an abstract
  artefact.
- **Competing interests** are in Nature's online-only end matter, absent from the PMC rendering —
  still not retrievable. Roughly a quarter of the 43 authors are at DAMO Academy / Alibaba Group
  and Hupan Lab, who built the model.

**My own error, corrected in Section 3 of the summary.** Yesterday I reconstructed the calibrated
operating point as specificity 99.59% at prevalence 0.33%, predicting PPV 42.1% against the paper's
reported 42.2%. The full text gives the prospective cohort as prevalence **0.269%** and sensitivity
**80.9%** — not the 90% I assumed — with implied specificity **99.70%**. Two errors cancelled. The
method (technique #49) was sound; the tenth-of-a-point agreement was partly luck. **Recorded
because a right answer reached by a wrong road is the most dangerous kind, and the only thing that
exposed it was the full text.**

**52. A curated test cohort inflates specificity, and prevalence is how you spot it.** EAGLE's
headline 98.5% specificity comes from a cohort of 2,812 malignant against 8,654 negative —
**24.5% prevalence**, case-control-like, not screening. Real screening populations in the same
paper run **0.27% to 1.37%**. When the model met 20,758 *consecutive* patients, specificity fell
**98.5% → 95.5%** while sensitivity held. **The check: divide cases by total in every test cohort
before quoting any figure from it.** A double-digit prevalence in a screening paper means the
cohort was assembled, not encountered, and the specificity measured on it is an upper bound.
Specificity is not supposed to depend on prevalence — the mechanism here is case mix, not Bayes:
a consecutive series contains the pneumonias, hiatus hernias and motion artefacts a curated
negative set quietly omits. Pairs with #42 (predictive values depend on prevalence) as its mirror
image: PPV moves because of arithmetic, specificity moves because of who is in the room.

**53. The calibration-cycle template — what an honest engineering response to failure looks like.**
EAGLE's middle section is the model to copy: (1) deploy on an unselected consecutive series, (2)
report the degradation rather than bury it, (3) identify the hard cases and re-annotate them,
(4) merge them into training, (5) test the improved model on a **different** consecutive series
from the same centres, (6) report the improvement with the arithmetic shown. Every number
reproduces: RW2 n=14,644 with 163 cases; EAGLE 146 TP / 432 FP → PPV 25.3%; EAGLE-Plus 144 TP /
118 FP → PPV 55.0%; false-positive reduction (432 − 118)/432 = **72.7%**. **The tell of a
reanalysis masquerading as a fix is step 5** — if the improved model is tested on the same series
that generated the hard cases, there is no evidence, only a fitted curve.

**54. Bootstrap confidence intervals break down at tiny event counts.** EAGLE's real-world low-dose
CT result is **8 positive predictions, PPV 12.5%** — one true positive — with a reported 95%
interval of **7.1–25.0%**. No valid method produces that. An exact (Clopper–Pearson) interval for
1 of 8 runs roughly **0.3% to 52.7%**, and a bootstrap resampling eight observations can only ever
produce PPVs at multiples of 1/8, so its percentiles are an artefact of the grid, not an estimate
of uncertainty. **Check: whenever an interval looks implausibly tight, back out the denominator.
Below roughly 20–30 events, demand an exact binomial interval.** A suspiciously narrow interval on
a small count is evidence the pipeline applied a default method outside its range — the point
estimate may still be fine, the interval is not.

**55. "Prospectively enrolled" does not mean the events were real.** EAGLE's most quotable claim —
AI triage would roughly triple endoscopic detection — rests on a cohort of 530 prospectively
enrolled people containing **six HGIN and zero invasive cancers**. To run the analysis the authors
**resampled three cancers from a different, retrospective cohort**. They say so plainly in the
Methods; the abstract says only "exploratory analyses of a prospectively enrolled cohort."
**Check: find the event count, not the enrolment count.** A prospective design with too few events
to analyse is a pilot, however it is described, and any number derived from injected events is a
simulation.

**56. Reader studies get buried when the AI wins.** The abstract never mentions the reader study:
17 readers, two rounds, three-month washout, randomised order — a proper design. And the result is
uncomfortable in the other direction: **AI alone (implied 91.0% sensitivity) outperformed
AI-assisted humans (85.7%)**, which the authors report and discuss rather than hide. **Check: when
a paper runs a reader study, go find it even if the abstract is silent, and read which arm won.**
A buried reader study is usually buried because the headline would have to be qualified.

**57. Selecting the architecture on the external cohort is still selecting on the test set.** The
ResEnc backbone was chosen partly because "in the eight-center external validation cohort… it
achieved the highest sensitivity (89.5%) and specificity (98.5%), outperforming other models."
Milder than threshold tuning on test data — the candidates were few, the differences small, and
the AUC actually favoured a different backbone — but it means the external figures carry some
selection optimism. Pairs with #36 (nested CV and the leak it does not close): the leak moves up a
level when the thing being chosen is the architecture rather than a hyperparameter.

**Carried into tomorrow.** **CheXNeXt open-source chest-infection AI on after-hours ED radiographs
in Singapore** (PMID 42869615, PMC13663711, BMJ Health Care Inform) — **full text already retrieved
and saved today**, so retrieval is not a risk. 1,006 radiographs; sensitivity 0.234 for
consolidation and 0.176 for pneumonia at the published default thresholds, rising to 0.894 and
0.681 after Youden optimisation **on the same data**. That is techniques #44 and #51 in one paper,
plus the question of what a "published threshold" is worth off its home dataset — and it pairs
directly against EAGLE, which locked its thresholds before testing. Spares behind it: measles
TabNet (PMID 42867502, PMC13660459), gallstone body composition (PMC13647361), COPD rapid decline
(PMC13637773). Standing re-checks: Huang J (42765400, empty full text ×5), Lu J (42809845), Holly
(42830095) / Mechael (42833894), and the four oldest deferred reviews. Standing offers never taken
up: upgrade the abstract-only entries (Gulshan #2, Ardila #8, McKinney #10) if PDFs are supplied;
retrofit summary #1 (Vishwanath) with a Statistics Spotlight.

**And the general lesson from the 26-day wait.** A PMC identifier that serves no body text is not a
permanent verdict. EAGLE opened on day 27, with no warning and no change in how it was requested.
**Keep the weekly re-check running on every abstract-only entry**, and keep writing the explicit
upgrade commitment into the summary — it is what made today's rewrite non-negotiable.

## 2026-10-19 run — Toh et al., CheXNeXt open-source AI on after-hours ED chest radiographs

Decoded from **full text** (PMC13663711, retrieved 2026-10-18 and read today). Graded **2/5**.
Checked the week's new literature first for anything landmark: the only candidate was the PM Heart
trial of an EHR-integrated AI prognostic tool for ischaemic heart disease (PMID 42893738, Am Heart
J), and it is a **protocol and rationale paper with no results**, so it does not displace the queue
entry. Noted for later — if PM Heart's results publish, they are worth decoding, since randomised
trials of AI tools embedded in live clinical workflow are rare.

**Why this paper earned its slot.** It is the exact counterpart to yesterday's EAGLE, which fixed
its thresholds in cross-validation before touching any test data. Toh et al. did the opposite in
both directions at once, and that is what makes it instructive: Table 1 uses thresholds chosen by
somebody else on somebody else's data (methodologically clean, and the sensitivity is terrible),
while Tables 2 and 3 use thresholds swept across the same 1,006 films they are then scored on
(the attractive numbers, never validated). **The clean table is the one with the bad result.**

**Everything reconciles.** All three tables are internally consistent — F1 matches 2·PPV·Se/(PPV+Se)
and Youden matches Se+Sp−1 everywhere, to the last decimal. Working backwards from Se, Sp, PPV and
NPV on n=1,006 recovers counts the paper never prints: **consolidation ~191 positives (19.0%), 45
TP / 8 FP / 146 FN; pneumonia ~183 (18.2%), 32 TP / 4 FP / 151 FN; pleural effusion ~136 (13.5%),
85 TP / 44 FP / 51 FN.**

**58. A high AUC with a useless sensitivity is a threshold problem, not a model problem — and the
two can be told apart.** AUC 0.900 for consolidation alongside sensitivity 0.234, same films, same
model. AUC measures ranking and is threshold-free; sensitivity and specificity are properties of
one point on the curve. The diagnostic: **if AUC is high and sensitivity is low, the model is fine
and the dial is wrong; if AUC is low, no dial setting will save it.** The proof in this paper is
that AUC is byte-identical across all three tables (0.900 / 0.856 / 0.916) while sensitivity moves
from 0.234 to 0.894 — moving a cut-off cannot change a ranking. **Standing check: when a paper
leads with AUC, find the sensitivity and specificity at the threshold the authors propose to use;
if no threshold is named, you have not been told how the tool behaves.**

**59. Thresholds do not travel — but not uniformly, which is why you must check each one.** Stanford's
published F1-maximised cut-offs against Sengkang's locally optimal ones: consolidation **0.744 →
0.22 (F1) / 0.11 (Youden)**, roughly 3× to 7× lower; pneumonia **0.831 → 0.31**, nearly 3× lower;
pleural effusion **0.731 → 0.73**, essentially identical, with "optimisation" buying an F1 gain of
**0.003**. One of three transferred perfectly. The mechanism is **discrimination surviving while
calibration drifts**: ranking is robust to a change of scanner, population and processing pipeline,
but the numeric scale is not, so scores get systematically squashed and a fixed cut-off lands in
the wrong place. **"We used the published threshold" sounds like the non-cherry-picking choice, and
in one sense it is — but it is an untested assumption that the new population's score distribution
matches the developer's.** Pairs with #44 (a threshold quoted to two decimals) and against yesterday's
EAGLE, which prespecified and locked its thresholds in cross-validation.

**60. Quantify the in-sample threshold-selection optimism instead of dismissing the result.** The
reflex "they tuned on the test set, so the number is worthless" is as wrong as ignoring the problem.
Simulated it: a model with AUC 0.900, a sample shaped like this one (191 positives / 815 negatives),
pick the Youden-maximising threshold on one draw and apply it to a fresh draw, 2,000 repetitions →
**mean Youden 0.651 in-sample vs 0.630 out-of-sample, optimism ≈ 0.021.** Sanity check: the paper's
own optimised Youden for consolidation is 0.659, close to the simulated in-sample mean, so the model
of the situation is about right. **Two points of Youden — real, modest, and driven almost entirely by
sample size.** At 30 positives instead of 191 it would be several times larger. **The bigger problem
is not the bias but that every CI in the optimised tables is conditional on a threshold estimated
from the same data, so they answer "how uncertain is performance at 0.11?" rather than "how uncertain
is performance at a threshold chosen the way we chose ours?"** Keep the simulation script;
`verify_chexnext2.py` generalises to any (AUC, n_pos, n_neg).

**61. Do not carry yesterday's suspicion into today's paper — check it.** Technique #54 (bootstrap
CIs break down at tiny event counts) was earned on EAGLE's 1-of-8 PPV. It would have been easy to
apply it here by reflex. It does not hold: this paper's bootstrap intervals match exact
Clopper–Pearson intervals closely (consolidation PPV reported 0.744–0.941 vs exact 0.724–0.933;
pneumonia sensitivity reported 0.124–0.233 vs exact 0.123–0.238). With dozens to hundreds of events
the bootstrap works as advertised. **The machinery was fine; what it was pointed at was the problem.**
Recorded because a technique applied without its precondition is just a prejudice.

**62. When an IRB waiver strips the demographics, generalisability becomes untestable, not merely
uncertain.** This paper reports **no patient characteristics whatsoever** — no age, no sex, no
indication, no comorbidity — because the waiver's terms forbade extracting them. You cannot assess
transportability to another population when the study population is undescribed, and you cannot
check representativeness even within the same hospital. **Check: look for the baseline-characteristics
paragraph before reading any accuracy figure; if it is absent, the numbers describe an unidentified
group of people.** Compounded here by one month of collection (October 2024) in a tropical climate —
one season of infection prevalence.

**63. Count whether the "multiple conditions" are actually multiple.** Consolidation labelled on
19.0% of films and pneumonia on 18.2% of the same 1,006 films. Either more than a third of
after-hours ED chest X-rays at this hospital showed one or the other, or — far more likely — these
are largely the same radiographs labelled twice because the report read "consolidation, likely
pneumonia". **The overlap is never reported.** It decides whether the paper validated the model on
three conditions or on roughly two, and whether the three sets of confidence intervals are
independent of one another. **One line of text would settle it; its absence is a reporting gap, not
a trivial one.** Related to #51 (count the operating points before combining numbers) — here, count
the outcomes before counting them as independent.

**Also noted.** The reference standard is report text, not disease: no microbiology, no pathology,
no follow-up — so the study measures agreement with local reporting style, which the authors state
plainly and justify as realistic. A **single** senior trainee extracted every label from 1,006 free-text
reports with no second reader and no kappa. No external validation. No funding, competing-interests
or data-availability statement in the PMC rendering, and the analysis ran on a commercial platform
(CARPL.AI) whose terms are unstated. On the credit side: 20 board-certified radiologists wrote the
reference reports blinded to the AI, STARD was followed, the optimised thresholds are labelled as
optimised rather than disguised, both F1- and Youden-based versions are given so the trade-off is
visible, and the abstract itself names the surrogate reference standard as a limitation. The
abstract's one real sin is reporting that sensitivity rose "while maintaining acceptable specificity"
without mentioning that **PPV fell from 0.846 to 0.467 (consolidation) and 0.656 to 0.466 (effusion)** —
more than half of all flags wrong, which is the operationally decisive number for a triage tool.

**Next queue entry.** Spares in hand, none decoded: measles TabNet (PMID 42867502, PMC13660459),
gallstone body composition (PMC13647361), COPD rapid decline (PMC13637773). New watch item: **PM Heart
trial results** (protocol PMID 42893738, Am Heart J; enrolment began September 2023, follow-up expected
to finish in 2026) — a multicentre randomised open-label trial in Denmark randomising patients 1:1 to
show or hide an AI-calculated one-year prognosis in the EHR, with a composite primary outcome of
30-day readmission and one-year survival. **Check quarterly.** Standing re-checks: Huang J (42765400,
empty full text ×5), Lu J (42809845), Holly (42830095) / Mechael (42833894), and the four oldest
deferred reviews (Topol 2019, Beam & Kohane 2018, Moor 2023, Wu 2021). Standing offers never taken up:
upgrade the abstract-only entries (Gulshan #2, Ardila #8, McKinney #10) if PDFs are supplied; retrofit
summary #1 (Vishwanath) with a Statistics Spotlight.

**The pairing to keep.** 10-17/18 (EAGLE: thresholds prespecified in cross-validation and locked
before testing) against 10-19 (CheXNeXt: thresholds swept on the reporting data) and 10-16 (Park:
threshold tuned on the test set). Three consecutive papers, three positions on the same axis, and
the one that handled it best is also the one whose headline numbers are most trustworthy. That is
the single most transferable lesson of the week.

## 2026-10-20 run — Nantongo et al., measles vaccination defaulting, TabNet/soft-voting ensembles

Decoded from **full text** (PMC13660459, 47,799 characters). Graded **2/5**. This was a queue spare,
promoted after both of the week's bigger papers turned out to be abstract-only from here.

**Two high-value papers deferred, both for retrieval, both now TOP-PRIORITY queue entries.**

1. **Chen D, et al. "Reporting completeness of clinical trials evaluating large language model
   interventions in medicine: systematic review." BMJ 2026;395:e100855** (PMID 42869550,
   DOI 10.1136/bmj-2026-100855). **No PMC copy**; `doi.org`, `www.bmj.com` and `europepmc.org` all
   fail DNS under this network policy; Scite still requires a paid plan. **This is the single most
   on-topic paper this reading list could decode** — it measures, across 461 records (68 trial
   reports, 15 protocols, 378 registrations, 2017–2025), how completely LLM trials report the very
   things this list checks every day, scored against TRIPOD-LLM, CONSORT 2025 / CONSORT-AI, SPIRIT
   2025 / SPIRIT-AI, CHART and the WHO Trial Registration Dataset. Mean completeness **TRIPOD-LLM
   40%, CONSORT 2025 44%, CONSORT-AI 59%, CHART 52%, SPIRIT 2025 51%, SPIRIT-AI 57%, WHO registration
   61%.** Author list includes **Gary Collins and Karel Moons** (TRIPOD/PROBAST), Danielle Bitterman
   and Leo Celi. **Re-check PMC weekly** — BMJ research is open access and a PMC deposit is likely.
2. **Patel VR, et al. "Clinician Use of an Artificial Intelligence–Enabled Clinical Information
   Platform." JAMA 2026** (PMID 42879271, PMC13673371, DOI 10.1001/jama.2026.21868). The PMC record
   exists but **serves an empty `full_text` field** — exactly EAGLE's day-one failure mode, which
   resolved on day 27. 952,008 clinicians, 271 million queries to OpenEvidence over 2.5 years;
   monthly active users 355 → 703,265. **Three of seven authors are employed by OpenEvidence**, the
   platform studied, and an LLM was used to classify a stratified sample of 38.8 million queries —
   a vendor-authored description of its own product's usage, which is a COI structure worth teaching.
   **Re-check PMC weekly.**

**Everything in today's paper reconciles.** Kenya 1,685/6,403 = 26.32% defaulters (paper: 26.3%);
Uganda 1,504/6,793 = 22.14% (paper: 22.1%). The train/test split is never stated in words but is
recoverable exactly: test-set defaulters 337/1,685 and 301/1,504 = **20.0% in both countries**.

**64. A significant p-value and a confidence interval containing zero, in the same sentence — and
why both are correct.** Table 12, Kenya: **ΔF1 = 0.0054, 95% CI −0.0156 to +0.0260, McNemar
χ²=7.224, p=.007, "Significant."** The authors print the interval, note in the text that it "spans
zero", and then conclude the result "confirms the advanced ensemble's structural advantage". Neither
statistic is wrong; **they answer different questions.** The bootstrap CI asks *how big is the
difference in F1*. **McNemar asks only whether the pattern of disagreements is lopsided** — it
discards every case both models got right and every case both got wrong, and tests the discordant
pairs against 50/50. A two-to-one split of discordant pairs (Cohen's g 0.1716 → ~67%) is very
unlikely by chance even when the *net* change in the summary metric is negligible. **Check: when a
paper reports both and they point different ways, the CI answers your question; when it reports only
one, notice which.** The giveaway phrase is "statistically significant improvement" attached to a
difference whose magnitude is never given in countable units.

**65. Significance and effect size can rank two results in opposite directions — here they do.**
Kenya ΔF1 **0.0054**, **2** extra true positives of 337, p=**.007**, "Significant". Uganda ΔF1
**0.0059**, **8** extra true positives of 301, p=**.86**, "Not significant". **Uganda's improvement
is larger on both measures that matter and it is the one called not significant**, because its
discordant pairs split near-evenly (Cohen's g 0.0333 ≈ 53/47) while Kenya's were consistent. One
country had a consistent tiny difference; the other an inconsistent slightly larger one. **The
canonical example for this list.** Both traps are live: reading "significant" as "worth doing"
(the paper's error) and reading "not significant" as "no effect" (the reader's). **The question that
cuts through: significant improvement of what, in what units, by how much?** Here the answer is
two children.

**66. A paper can hand you the evidence against itself, and that is worth grading up for.** These
authors printed the CI that undermines their headline, stated the Uganda null in the abstract AND
twice in the text, wrote that "algorithmic complexity does not universally guarantee statistical
superiority", said explicitly that SMOTE came after the train/test split and why, reported fold-level
SDs, and warned in the limitations that SHAP importance "may reflect training correlations rather
than direct epidemiological drivers". **None of that is required and most papers omit it.** The grade
is 2/5 for what the design can carry, not for candour — and the distinction is worth keeping
separate, because conflating them teaches readers that transparency is a liability.

**67. "Not significant" is not permission to stop making recommendations — but it should be.** The
Discussion states Uganda's result "was stochastic indicating that the ensemble architecture provided
no statistically valid predictive advantage in that context." The **Recommendations section then uses
that same model's SHAP rankings to propose Ugandan public-health strategy.** **Check: when a paper
reports a null for a model, follow that model forward and see whether its outputs are still being
interpreted later in the paper.** A model with no demonstrated advantage has no interpretable
internals either.

**68. Two datasets from different years are not two countries.** Kenya DHS **2022** vs Uganda DHS
**2016** — a six-year gap spanning the COVID-19 disruption to routine immunisation that the paper's
own introduction describes. The authors state the gap and its cause (only 10% of the 2022 UDHS is
public) and then never carry it into the interpretation, so every "differs between Kenya and Uganda"
claim is equally a "differs between 2022 and 2016" claim. **Check: in any cross-site comparison,
find the collection dates of each site before reading any difference as a site effect.** Pairs with
#62 (undescribed study population) — both are failures to pin down *who and when*, which is what
transportability rests on.

**69. When the top predictors are earlier steps of the same process, you have an adherence model,
not a determinants model.** The retained feature set includes **Received Polio 3, Received
Pentavalent 2, Received Pentavalent 3, Received Polio inactive and Received vitamin A1** — other
items in the same infant schedule — and measles is, as the paper says, "the last administered among
the basic antigens". Uganda's top five predictors are vitamin A, age, IPV, Pentavalent 3 and
district. **So the model largely learned that children who missed earlier appointments miss the next
one** — true and even useful, but not the social-determinants advance the introduction claims as its
novelty. **Separate timing question the paper never answers: was "most recent vitamin A" recorded
before the 9-month measles visit or at it?** If concurrent, it is not available at prediction time
and is leakage rather than prediction. **Check: for every top predictor, ask whether it is knowable
strictly before the outcome.**

**Also noted.** Threshold tuned on the test set, stated outright in the Methods ("optimized
dynamically using precision_recall_curve to maximize the localized F1-score **on the test set**") —
the third consecutive day on this axis, and the plainest statement of it yet. The paper supplies its
own evidence of how much that is worth: threshold tuning alone moved the **decision tree by +0.1172
F1 (Kenya) and +0.0977 (Uganda)**; TabNet +0.0250/+0.0141; RF +0.0189/+0.0157. The two winning
ensembles landed at thresholds of only **0.5125 and 0.5163**, so the headline models were barely
affected — the optimism sits in the *choice among twelve candidates*, every one of whose numbers went
through the same step. Other gaps: the same Uganda AUC is given as **0.8862 in the abstract and
0.8867 in the Discussion**; no calibration plot or statistic for a tool meant to flag individual
infants; feature selection (20 → 11 by phi-k against the target) may have used the full dataset; the
text reads as though SMOTE was applied once before cross-validation rather than inside each fold,
which would leak synthetic neighbours across folds — one sentence would settle it; the outcome counts
"don't know" and missing cards as defaulting, so 26.3% and 22.1% are upper bounds; and there is no
funding or competing-interests statement in the PMC rendering. Credit where due: tree-based models
beat the deep-learning ones and the authors report it, and the prototype has a real integration path
into Uganda's DHIS2/EPIVAC registry rather than stopping at a metrics table.

**The threshold-discipline sequence is now four papers long**, and it is the strongest teaching
thread this list has: **10-17/18 EAGLE** (fixed in cross-validation, locked before testing — and the
most trustworthy headline numbers), **10-19 CheXNeXt** (swept on the reporting data, labelled as
optimised), **10-20 Nantongo** (tuned on the test set, stated in the Methods), **10-16 Park** (tuned
on the test set, not flagged). **Order of trustworthiness = order of threshold discipline.** Use this
set whenever the concept needs teaching.

**Next queue entry.** Remaining screened spares: gallstone body composition (PMC13647361), COPD rapid
decline (PMC13637773). Above both, the two deferred papers at the head of this section (BMJ reporting
completeness; JAMA OpenEvidence) — **check PMC for each before picking tomorrow's paper.** Watch item:
PM Heart trial results (protocol PMID 42893738, Am Heart J), check quarterly. Standing re-checks:
Huang J (42765400), Lu J (42809845), Holly (42830095) / Mechael (42833894), and the four oldest
deferred reviews (Topol 2019, Beam & Kohane 2018, Moor 2023, Wu 2021). Standing offers never taken
up: upgrade the abstract-only entries (Gulshan #2, Ardila #8, McKinney #10) if PDFs are supplied;
retrofit summary #1 (Vishwanath) with a Statistics Spotlight.

## 2026-10-21 run — Yang et al., COPD rapid lung function decline (phenotypes + CatBoost)

Decoded from **full text** (PMC13637773). Graded **2/5**. A queue spare, promoted because the week's
three better papers are all still abstract-only.

**THE ABSTRACT-ONLY BACKLOG IS NOW THREE DEEP.** Re-checked today, all still blocked:
1. **Chen D, et al. BMJ 2026;395:e100855** (PMID 42869550) — reporting completeness of LLM trials
   against TRIPOD-LLM/CONSORT-AI/SPIRIT-AI/CHART. **Still no PMC copy.** Highest priority in the list;
   it measures exactly what this project checks daily.
2. **Patel VR, et al. JAMA 2026** (PMID 42879271, PMC13673371) — 952,008 clinicians, 271M queries to
   OpenEvidence. **PMC record still serves empty `full_text`.** Same failure mode EAGLE showed for 26
   days before opening on day 27, so keep re-checking.
3. **[x] DECODED 2026-11-19 (entry 82, ABSTRACT ONLY at day 30) — Ariel D, Endelman S, Laban G. "Overuse and False Restraint in Large Language Model
   Recommendations for Choosing Wisely Scenarios." Ann Intern Med, published 2026-10-20** (PMID
   42896192, DOI 10.7326/ANNALS-26-01667). **No PMC copy.** Benchmark of 6 commercial LLMs (OpenAI,
   Anthropic, Google production APIs, Feb–Mar 2026), 15 Choosing Wisely recommendations → 225
   scenarios → 4,050 responses, 3 runs each. Overuse rate **5.4% (95% CI 0.6% to 12.2%)**; false
   restraint **0.6% (CI 0.1% to 1.2%)**. Funding: None. **Worth decoding for one reason above all:
   the CI around 5.4% runs from 0.6% to 12.2% on 4,050 responses** — because the unit of analysis is
   the 15 recommendations, not the responses, and the overuse is concentrated in 2 of them (thyroid
   ultrasound for an incidental subcentimetre nodule 42.8%, routine ECG in low-risk adults 28.3%,
   with 8 of 15 at zero). A textbook clustered/over-dispersed outcome, with the false-restraint CI as
   the narrow contrast in the same study. Also uses an LLM judge with physician adjudication.
   **Re-check PMC weekly.**

**Everything in today's paper reconciles.** Phenotype counts 289+373+553 = 1,215 ✓; rapid-decline
counts 42.6%/55.8%/38.9% of those = 123+208+215 = 546 = the stated 44.9% ✓; test set 365 with 164
events ✓ consistent with 30% of 1,215 at a 44.9% event rate.

**70. When the outcome is a change score, check whether the baseline is a top predictor — then
separate the floor effect from regression to the mean.** Rapid decliners' baseline FEV1 **1,645 mL**
vs non-rapid **1,340 mL** (305 mL higher in the group *defined* by falling further), and baseline
FEV1 carries SHAP 0.389, **2.1× the next predictor**. The authors name the **floor effect** (low
baseline → no room to fall) and cite SPIROMICS agreeing — that part is real. **They do not address
regression to the mean, which is the other half and is pure artefact:** a single noisy baseline puts
lucky-day patients in the high group, and their second reading returns to normal, recording a fall
that never happened. **Worked check: 100 people with identical true FEV1 of 1,500 mL and ±100 mL
measurement error, split by first reading — the top group averages ~1,580 then ~1,500, an apparent
80 mL decline in a group where nobody declined.** That is exactly this paper's threshold. **Fix:
ANCOVA (model follow-up adjusted for baseline), or report the model with baseline removed.** Related
to the ANCOVA/Lord's-paradox note from 10-12 but this is the prediction-model form of it. **Standing
check: "those who started worst improved most" and "those who started best declined most" are the two
most over-interpreted findings in medicine, and both are what noise alone produces.**

**71. Compare the measurement error against the threshold — and remember that errors ADD when you
subtract.** Inclusion tolerated FEV1 reproducibility up to **0.2 L (200 mL)** within a session; rapid
decline was **≥40 mL/year over 2 years = 80 mL total**. The tolerated within-session wobble is
**2.5× the change being detected**. Worse, **the variance of a difference is the sum of the
variances**, so a two-point change carries ~**√2 = 1.41×** the error of one measurement: at ~100 mL
per reading, ~141 mL on the difference, **larger than the 80 mL cut-off itself**. **This is very
likely why the AUC stalls at 0.712 regardless of algorithm, and why all six models land within
0.047 of each other — a large part of the label is noise, and noise is unpredictable by
construction.** **Standing check: when every model performs about the same, suspect the labels before
blaming the models.** The measurement-error figure usually hides in the exclusion criteria, as it did
here.

**72. Find the follow-up funnel and ask who came back.** 2,193,579 screened → 45,965 diagnosed →
38,863 cross-sectional → **1,215 with both baseline and 24-month spirometry**. The prediction model
rests on **3.1% of the cross-sectional cohort and 0.055% of those screened**, and **the paper never
compares the 1,215 who returned with the 37,648 who did not** — on variables it already has. Who
returns for a voluntary repeat lung-function test two years later is bound up with symptoms,
distance, income and whether anyone chased them. **Check: in any longitudinal sub-study, divide the
analysed n by the enrolled n, then look for the table comparing the two groups. If it is absent, the
headline cohort size is advertising.** Pairs with #62 (undescribed study population).

**73. Apply the trivial baseline to the operating point the authors actually recommend.** Threshold
0.365 → Se 95.5%, Sp 23.3%, NPV 86.4%. Worked out on the stated test set (365 patients, 164 events):
**157 TP, 7 FN, 47 TN, 154 FP — 311 of 365 flagged, 85.1% of everyone**, PPV 50.4% (NPV reproduces
the paper's 86.4% exactly). **Flagging every patient catches 164/164 at PPV 44.9%.** So the model's
entire contribution is **ruling out 54 of 365 people (14.9%) at the cost of missing 7 of 164
events**, lifting PPV by 5.5 points. The DCA is consistent (net benefit 0.328 vs 0.29 for treat-all)
and the authors argue coherently for a two-stage high-sensitivity screen — **the problem is the
abstract's framing, not the strategy.** Extends #29 (the trivial baseline) to the specific case where
prevalence is near 50%, where "always say yes" is already right half the time.

**74. A near-tie among models on a small test set is not a finding.** CatBoost 0.712 vs logistic
regression 0.701 = **0.011**, on 164 events where the Hanley–McNeil SE of a *single* AUC is **0.027**
(95% CI roughly 0.658–0.766); the whole six-model spread is 0.047. The paired SE of the difference is
smaller than 0.027, but 0.011 is still no basis for choosing an architecture — especially when the
nomogram is then built by approximating CatBoost with logistic regression and restricted cubic
splines, i.e. a long route back to where plain logistic regression started. **Check: before believing
"model X was best", find the number of events and ask whether the gap exceeds the noise.**

**75. Clustering can relabel what you already knew.** Three latent classes, separated almost entirely
by smoking status (Cramér's V **0.72**), sex (**0.67**) and FEV1% predicted (**0.43**) — and named,
in the paper's own words, "non-smoking females", "smoking males" and "severe smokers". **Latent class
analysis found sex, smoking and severity.** Also: AIC and BIC "consistently decrease" as classes are
added, so **no statistical minimum was ever reached**; three was chosen on an elbow plus clinical
interpretability, which is defensible and openly stated but makes the number a judgement, not a
finding. **Check: read the variables that separate the clusters before accepting that new phenotypes
were discovered, and look for whether the fit statistics actually bottomed out.**

**A note on grading calibration, recorded deliberately.** This is the **third consecutive 2/5**, and
that is not drift. Each has a distinct specific defect large enough to undermine its headline:
CheXNeXt (10-19) optimised thresholds never tested out of sample; Nantongo (10-20) a "significant"
advance of two children; Yang (10-21) an outcome mechanically coupled to its top predictor with a
noise floor above its threshold. The two 4/5 papers — Jerjes (10-15) and EAGLE (10-17/18) — both
prespecified, validated externally, and reported the result that hurt them. **Today's paper does the
third and not the first two.** What would have lifted it to 3, both achievable with data in hand:
(a) one table comparing the 1,215 followed against the 37,648 not followed; (b) report the model with
baseline FEV1 removed — if it still reached ~0.70, the finding would stand on its own.

**Genuine credit, which matters for the grade.** 2.19 million screened across 160 sites and 31
provinces; **ethics approval with its number and written informed consent properly reported**;
**a calibration curve AND a decision curve analysis** — neither of the other two papers this week
reported either, and calibration is precisely what an individual-risk tool needs; LASSO + Boruta +
500-resample bootstrap stability at an 80% threshold; a limitations section naming five real problems
unprompted including **internal validation only**; the counterintuitive wheezing result flagged as
counterintuitive rather than spun; and evidence from a population (26% never-smokers, many
non-smoking women with biomass exposure) that ECLIPSE, SPIROMICS and COPDGene largely miss.

**Next queue entry.** **Check all three abstract-only entries at the head of the previous section
first.** Remaining screened spare: gallstone body composition (PMC13647361) — the last one. **The
spare shelf is nearly empty; restocking needed.** Watch item: PM Heart trial results (protocol PMID
42893738), quarterly. Standing re-checks: Huang J (42765400), Lu J (42809845), Holly (42830095) /
Mechael (42833894), and the four oldest deferred reviews (Topol 2019, Beam & Kohane 2018, Moor 2023,
Wu 2021). Standing offers never taken up: upgrade the abstract-only entries (Gulshan #2, Ardila #8,
McKinney #10) if PDFs are supplied; retrofit summary #1 (Vishwanath) with a Statistics Spotlight.

## 2026-10-22 run — Long et al., gallstone risk from CT body composition + machine learning

Decoded from **full text** (PMC13647361). Graded **3/5** — the first 3 in four days, and the reason is
specific: **it trained at one hospital and tested at another**, which the previous four papers did not.
This was the last spare on the shelf; the shelf is now restocked (end of this section).

**All three deferred papers re-checked, all three still blocked.**
1. **Chen D, et al. BMJ 2026;395:e100855** (PMID 42869550) — **still no PMC copy**, and **Scite retried
   today: still requires a paid plan**. Highest-priority entry in the whole queue.
2. **Patel VR, et al. JAMA 2026** (PMID 42879271, PMC13673371) — PMC record **still serves empty
   `full_text`**. Day 7 of this failure mode; EAGLE's resolved on day 27, so keep going.
3. **Ariel D, et al. Ann Intern Med** (PMID 42896192) — **no PMC copy**.

**Everything in today's paper reconciles.** 1,437 + 507 = 1,944 ✓; 222/1,437 = 15.4% ✓; 88/507 = 17.4%
✓; 310 cases = 248 gallbladder + 28 bile duct + 34 both ✓; class weight 1,215/222 = 5.47 ✓ matches the
paper's "≈5.47"; ET test accuracy recomputed from Se 0.671 / Sp 0.816 at 88/507 = 0.791 ✓ exactly.

**76. The train-to-test drop, measured model by model — and the simplest model generalises best.**
Six models, same two cohorts, every one worse at the external centre:
**logistic regression −0.015** (0.690→0.675) · ET −0.056 (0.828→0.772) · RF −0.073 · CatBoost −0.083 ·
SVM −0.090 · **XGBoost −0.093**. The simple model's gap is about **one sixth** of XGBoost's: it was the
worst model at home and the most honest model away. **A flexible model is a better memoriser, and what
it memorises about one hospital's scanners, lab and case mix does not travel.** **Standing check: when
a paper gives both training and external performance, compute the drop for every model and read the
ordering — it tells you which model learned the problem and which learned the dataset.**

**77. Repeated multi-seed cross-validation is still internal, and still optimistic.** The authors did
careful internal validation — repeated fivefold CV across **50 random seeds**, reporting ET at
**0.810 ± 0.015**. At the outside hospital: **0.772**. That is **0.038 below, about 2.5 SDs of the
internal estimate — its own uncertainty band did not cover the shortfall.** Cross-validation measures
performance on *new patients from the same place*; every fold shares the hospital, scanners and lab.
**Check: internal CV, however many seeds, cannot estimate transportability. Averaging more folds
narrows the interval around the wrong quantity.** Strengthens #25 (the six-rung external-validation
ladder) and #36 (nested CV and the leak it doesn't close) with a measured number.

**78. Overlapping confidence intervals do not settle a model comparison — in either direction.** Test
AUCs with 95% CIs: ET 0.772 (0.714–0.822), RF 0.733 (0.675–0.786), SVM 0.697 (0.639–0.750), CatBoost
0.688 (0.622–0.752), LR 0.675 (0.609–0.741), XGBoost 0.668 (0.599–0.735). **ET's interval overlaps
every other model's**, and RF's upper bound (0.786) sits *above* ET's point estimate. But two
overlapping intervals also do **not** prove no difference — **comparing separate intervals is simply
the wrong tool**, because it discards the key fact that all six models ran on **the same 507
patients**. The right tool is a **paired test — DeLong for AUCs** — and **none is reported**. Worked
intuition: two models each right about 70 of 100 patients are indistinguishable if right about
*different* patients, but if they agree on 90 and A wins 9 of the 10 disagreements, the difference is
clear — and only a paired analysis sees it. **Standing check: do the intervals overlap, and was a
paired test run? Overlap plus no paired test = a league table, not a result.** Pairs with #43-adjacent
material and with the DeLong note from the 10-16 Park decode.

**79. "Externally validated" spans four rungs of the ladder — ask whether the test patients came from
a different institution.** This paper is rung 4: Centre 1 trained, Centre 2 tested, no mixing, and the
two centres **differ significantly on waist, weight, lymphocytes, triglycerides, total cholesterol,
indirect bilirubin, hypertension and diabetes** — so the test was not a formality. **The sentence that
shows they understood it:** they declined repeated random train–test splits because it "would
compromise the independence of the external validation cohort and undermine the validity of the
external validation." **That is exactly right and rarely said.** Pooling centres and then randomly
splitting the pool yields an impressive-sounding multi-centre dataset and a **rung-2** validation —
the specific failure this paper avoided. **Check: did any patient appear in both halves, and did the
halves come from different institutions? "We split 70:30" is rung 2 however it is dressed.**

**80. Credit the correct threshold direction when you see it.** The logistic-regression classification
threshold was chosen by **Youden on the training set (0.492)** and applied **unchanged** to the test
set. That is the right sequence — pick settings where you may look, measure where you may not — and
the **opposite** of three papers decoded this week. **But no threshold is stated for the five ML
models, and the consequences show:** on the same test set sensitivity runs **0.307 (XGBoost) to 0.716
(LR)**, i.e. the models flag between ~14% and ~43% of patients. Those are six different trade-offs,
not six answers to one question. **Check: a wide sensitivity spread across models on one test set means
the thresholds differ; if the paper doesn't say how each was set, the comparison is not like-for-like.**
This now extends the threshold-discipline thread to **five** papers.

**81. A paper that hunts for its own outcome misclassification deserves saying so.** Of the 1,069
patients who happened to have ultrasound data, **77 (7.2%) were CT-negative but ultrasound-POSITIVE** —
counted as non-cases in the primary analysis. The authors went looking, found it, published it, and
then explained why the naive fix was worse: ultrasound covered only about half the cohort at
inconsistent times, so reclassifying only those patients would have created **inconsistent outcome
ascertainment plus indication bias**. Instead they quantified the impact — sensitivity analysis on
concordant/surgically-confirmed cases **0.756 (0.655–0.840)** against primary **0.772 (0.714–0.822)**.
They also pre-empted the differential-surveillance objection: the gallstone group had fewer follow-up
CTs (4.37 vs 7.46 at Centre 1; 4.16 vs 6.72 at Centre 2) **because imaging stopped at the endpoint**,
median 1.1 years against a 3-year control requirement. **Keep this as the worked example of how to
report a bias you cannot remove.**

**82. Read the case and control definitions for asymmetry in required follow-up.** Controls needed
**≥1 abdominal CT per year for 3 years** with no stones; cases needed **one** positive CT within 3
years. The authors name the selection ("a clinical population rather than the general population") but
not this asymmetry: **the control definition requires sustained imaging access, the case definition
does not**, so anyone who stopped attending is excluded from the controls while still able to enter the
cases — tilting controls toward reliably followed patients. **Check: in any cohort defined by repeat
testing, compare what a case must do against what a control must do.** Related to #72 (find the
follow-up funnel).

**Minor findings.** Univariate AUCs are 0.610–0.627 (NLR 0.627, ABSI 0.615, STR_area 0.610) and the
combined ET reaches 0.772 — **a jump of +0.145 over the best single component**, possible via
interactions but worth a model-with-and-without-the-CT-block check, which is after all the paper's
stated question. **No calibration curve, no calibration statistic, no decision curve analysis** — the
gap versus yesterday's COPD paper, which was graded lower yet reported both. Small arithmetic slip:
1,069/1,944 = **55.0%**, not the stated "approximately 53.6%". Per-unit odds ratios on uninterpretable
scales ("each 0.01 increase in ABSI → 59% higher risk", where 0.01 is about half of ABSI's plausible
range; compounded over 0.02 that is 2.53×) — see #50-adjacent material. The paper never says which
listed hospital is Centre 1 and which is Centre 2. Funding and competing interests not reported;
ethics approval and **both** approval numbers are given.

**Grading note.** 3/5 after three consecutive 2/5s, on the strength of: genuine rung-4 external
validation, nested CV, a classical baseline reported fairly, 2,000-resample bootstrap CIs at a sound
event count (88), a 50-seed stability check, the training-set threshold, two sensitivity analyses, a
self-found misclassification, and a limitations table that includes its own. Held at 3 rather than 4 by
the untested model ranking, the unexplained operating points, the absent calibration, and the narrow
population. **The scale moves when the work does.**

**SHELF RESTOCKED — six fresh candidates, every PMC copy confirmed present today.** In priority order:

1. **Zhang H, et al. "Dose-conditioned machine-learning prediction of a composite target sedation
   state in patients undergoing propofol-sedated gastrointestinal endoscopy." Ann Med 2026;58(1):2737005**
   (PMID 42803764, PMC13629798). **TOP PICK.** Prospective, single-centre, 1,248 adults, **temporal
   validation** (first 1,000 develop, next 248 validate). And the abstract writes this list's own
   lessons unprompted: *"Calibration was suboptimal across models."* *"With only 33 validation events,
   confidence intervals were wide and comparisons among models remain uncertain."* *"No single model was
   uniformly superior."* Validation AUCs 0.780–0.821; RF highest AUC, ExtraTrees highest average
   precision — i.e. the winner changes with the metric. A paper that volunteers its own limits.
2. **Zhou X, et al. "Language-supervised electrocardiogram interpretation with vision transformer."
   Health Inf Sci Syst 2026;14(1):85** (PMID 42876871, PMC13661825). ECG-VLM: contrastive
   vision-language model trained on **800,034 paired ECGs + reports** (MIMIC-IV-ECG), **zero-shot via
   natural-language prompts with no condition-specific fine-tuning**, externally validated on
   **45,151 recordings** across 14 conditions. AUROC 0.679–0.981 internal, 0.691–0.991 external;
   strong for rhythm (mean 0.948/0.921), weaker for morphology. The foundation-model paradigm with real
   external validation — and a wide per-condition spread worth teaching.
3. **Wang J, et al. "Construction and validation of an identification model for frailty in maintenance
   hemodialysis patients..." Ann Med 2026;58(1):2743986** (PMID 42860582, PMC13656680). 626 patients,
   4 tertiary hospitals, **external validation set from a different city** (Wuxi n=502 → Yancheng
   n=124). Ten models; XGBoost external **AUC 0.917** with calibration, Brier score and DCA. **Scrutinise
   that 0.917** — the outcome is a questionnaire (FRAIL scale) at 48.6% prevalence, and grip strength is
   a predictor, which is close to being an item of the thing being predicted. Cross-sectional despite
   the "identification model" framing.
4. **Wang X, et al. "Identification and validation of a chronic kidney disease prediction model after
   radical nephrectomy." Ann Med 2026;58(1):2737504** (PMID 42808223, PMC13629762). 649 patients,
   **8 centres**, external cohort of 320 from the 7 non-primary centres. LightGBM internal 0.751
   (0.640–0.862) vs external **0.755** (0.702–0.808) — **essentially no drop**, which is unusual enough
   to be worth investigating against technique #76.
5. **Lin S, et al. "...predicting urine culture positivity in children with suspected urinary tract
   infections." Ann Med 2026;58(1):2742648** (PMID 42841462, PMC13647408). Paediatric; 61% of children
   aged 0–12 months; culture positivity 42.2%; RF AUC 0.814 (0.742–0.875); seven routine labs; a
   deployed web calculator. No external validation evident. Pairs with the antimicrobial-resistance
   mapping, which is a different kind of claim in the same paper.
6. **Chen X, et al. "Machine learning-based nomogram for acute kidney injury after liver
   transplantation." Ren Fail 2026;48(1):2698148** (PMID 42802088, PMC13618104). **n=127** from 146
   screened, split 3:1, nomogram AUC 0.861 train / 0.761 validation, five models, SVM "numerically
   highest" at 0.846, **internal validation only**. A deliberately small study — the right vehicle for
   events-per-variable and why a 3:1 split of 127 patients cannot settle anything.

**Also still outstanding.** Watch item: PM Heart trial results (protocol PMID 42893738), quarterly.
Standing re-checks: Huang J (42765400), Lu J (42809845), Holly (42830095) / Mechael (42833894), and the
four oldest deferred reviews (Topol 2019, Beam & Kohane 2018, Moor 2023, Wu 2021). Standing offers never
taken up: upgrade the abstract-only entries (Gulshan #2, Ardila #8, McKinney #10) if PDFs are supplied;
retrofit summary #1 (Vishwanath) with a Statistics Spotlight. **Note for future restocking: the
`pubmed pmc[sb]` filter plus a date window is the reliable way to find candidates that will actually
open — six for six today, against three unreachable papers from top journals this fortnight.**

## 2026-10-23 run — Zhang et al., dose-conditioned prediction of a target sedation state

Decoded from **full text** (PMC13629798). Graded **4/5** — the first 4 since EAGLE, and **the
best-reported paper in this reading list**. Top pick from yesterday's restocked shelf; it earned the
slot.

**THE DEFERRED BACKLOG IS NOW FOUR DEEP. A GENUINELY LANDMARK PAPER JOINED IT TODAY.**
1. **[x] DECODED 2026-11-12 (entry 75) — Heydari AA, et al. "The anatomy of a personal health agent." Nature, 21 October 2026** (PMID
   42907512, DOI 10.1038/s41586-026-11161-8). **NEW, and the most important of the four.** Google
   Research / DeepMind. An open-source multi-agent LLM framework reasoning over consumer wearables
   and medical records, built from analysis of web-search and health-forum queries plus user research,
   with three specialist sub-agents (data science, health domain expert, health coach). Evaluated
   across **11 benchmark tasks with >7,000 annotations and 1,350 hours of expert and end-user effort**
   — described as the most comprehensive evaluation of a personal health agent so far. **No PMC copy.**
   This is the paper everyone will cite about consumer health AI; get it the moment it opens.
2. **[x] DECODED 2026-11-13 (entry 76, ABSTRACT ONLY, day 31) — Chen D, et al. BMJ 2026;395:e100855** (PMID 42869550) — LLM-trial reporting completeness. Still
   no PMC copy. Scite still paywalled.
3. **Patel VR, et al. JAMA 2026** (PMID 42879271, PMC13673371) — **PMC record still serves empty
   `full_text`, now day 8.** EAGLE's identical failure resolved on day 27.
4. **Ariel D, et al. Ann Intern Med** (PMID 42896192) — Choosing Wisely LLM benchmark. No PMC copy.

**Everything in today's paper reconciles, to three decimals.** Funnel 1,441 − 45 − 148 = 1,248 ✓ (the
148 components 37+32+34+45 sum exactly ✓); 1,000 + 248 = 1,248 ✓; EPV 153/21 = 7.29 ✓ matches the
paper's own stated figure. **All seven ExtraTrees metrics recompute from the published confusion
matrix (TN 198, FP 17, FN 15, TP 18):** sensitivity 0.545 ✓, specificity 0.921 ✓, PPV 0.514 ✓, NPV
0.930 ✓, accuracy 0.871 ✓, F1 0.529 ✓, MCC 0.455 ✓.

**83. Nine metrics, five winners: "best model" is not a property of a model.** Validation winners:
**AUC → random forest** (0.821) · **average precision → ExtraTrees** (0.570) · **MCC → ExtraTrees**
(0.455) · **F1 → ExtraTrees** (0.529) · **PPV → random forest** (0.579) · **sensitivity → HistGB and
ExtraTrees** (0.545) · **specificity → logistic regression and SVM** (0.972) · **Brier → SVM** (0.097)
· **mean net benefit → SVM** (0.035). **Every one of the five models won something.** The authors
built Table 3 for the express purpose of saying so; its note reads that the highest values "were
obtained from different models and should not be interpreted as indicating superiority of any single
model." **That note is the single most transferable sentence decoded this month.** Note also that
SVM, the best *probability* estimator, caught only **6 of 33 events** at its own threshold — excellent
calibration and near-useless classification in the same model. **Standing check: when a paper names a
winner, find which metric that was and whether the others agreed.** Completes #78 (overlapping CIs)
from the other direction: #78 says the ranking may be noise; #83 says even a noiseless ranking is
metric-specific.

**84. Calibration intercept and slope — how to actually read them.** ExtraTrees: **intercept −1.468
(ideal 0), slope 1.767 (ideal 1)**, Brier 0.150. **Intercept = overall level**; negative here means
predicted probabilities are systematically **too high**. **Slope = spread**; above 1 means predictions
are **bunched too close together** and need stretching (the commoner failure, slope < 1, is the
opposite — an overconfident model). The paper states both correctly and concludes the probabilities
"should not be used for bedside decisions in their present form." **The mechanism the paper does NOT
name (my inference from its stated specifications): all five models used BALANCED CLASS WEIGHTS**,
which by construction inflates predicted probabilities. The giveaway is the thresholds — for a 13.3%
outcome the MCC-optimal cut-offs were **0.177 to 0.811**, and nothing is 81% likely in a 13% world
unless the scale has been stretched. **Balanced weighting fixes learning-from-few-events and breaks
calibration; the two are in tension and this paper shows both halves at once.** **Standing check: AUC
cannot detect miscalibration at all — add 0.3 to every probability and the AUC does not move. Any
paper that shows a patient a number owes you an intercept, a slope and a Brier score.** Miscalibration
is usually *repairable* by recalibration, which is why it is less damning than poor discrimination —
and the paper prescribes exactly that (logistic recalibration of intercept and slope, isotonic if
nonlinear).

**85. Count the events, then divide by the predictors — and note when the authors did it for you.**
**153 events / 21 candidate predictors = 7.29**, published by the authors themselves, with the
observation that it is *before* categorical expansion so the true figure is lower. Against the
traditional minimum of 10. On the validation side, **33 events** drive every metric, which is why
sensitivity 0.545 carries an interval of **0.367–0.714** (18 of 33 caught; the truth is somewhere
between about 12 and 24). Swapping two patients moves it. **The authors wrote this into the abstract:
"With only 33 validation events, confidence intervals were wide and comparisons among models remain
uncertain"** — and then ran **no pairwise tests**, which at 33 events is the right call rather than a
gap. **Standing check: an impressive patient count is not an event count. A study of 10,000 patients
with 40 events is a 40-event study.** Pairs with #74 (a near-tie on a small test set is not a finding).

**86. Temporal validation catches what internal cross-validation cannot — and it did here.**
Development out-of-fold ranking: **HistGradientBoosting 1st (0.804)**, RF 0.800, SVM 0.787, ET 0.787,
LR 0.769. Temporal validation ranking: RF 0.821, ET 0.819, SVM 0.802, LR 0.797, **HistGradientBoosting
LAST (0.780)**. **The internal winner finished last on patients enrolled months later at the same
hospital.** The authors draw the right conclusion: "model ranking during internal cross-validation
does not necessarily remain unchanged when the model is applied to patients enrolled during a later
time period," and note that random splitting "may provide an optimistic impression." A chronological
split sits **between** rungs 3 and 4 of the validation ladder (#25/#79) — same institution, so not
external, but it catches drift over time, which random splitting cannot. **Standing check: when a
paper reports both internal and later-period performance, compare the ORDERINGS, not just the
numbers.** Extends #76/#77.

**87. The gold standard for a methods section, as a checklist to measure others against.** This paper
does, in one place, nearly everything this list has spent a month asking for: prospective consecutive
enrolment with dates · ethics approval **with its number** and **written informed consent** · **STROBE
and TRIPOD+AI named and followed** · a **complete firewall statement** ("the validation cohort was not
used for model training, preprocessing fitting, feature selection, threshold selection, or model
tuning" — all five named) · **preprocessing fitted only within each training fold** · an **explicit
list of every post-sedation variable excluded** to prevent leakage, with reasons · **threshold chosen
on development data by a stated rule (max MCC) with a stated tie-break (higher F1, then lower
threshold) and applied unchanged** · calibration intercept, slope, Brier and curves · DCA labelled
exploratory · a sensitivity outcome · a dose-support overlap check between cohorts · a **scenario
analysis for the 148 excluded patients** whose outcomes could not be reconstructed and for whom an
included-vs-excluded comparison was impossible · random seed and every package version · 1,000
subject-level bootstrap resamples · **seven numbered limitations, none softened** · and an explicit
causal caveat that the dose was never assigned (fixed 100 mg/min to LOC, so dose is a proxy for time
to unconsciousness). **Keep this list as the benchmark.**

**88. A paper arguing against its own most interesting finding is telling you something.** Drinking
status topped the SHAP ranking — the kind of result that writes its own press release. The authors
instead spend a paragraph dismantling it: self-reported crude binary with no amount, frequency,
duration or abstinence data; **prevalence differed between cohorts (32.3% vs 39.5%, p = 0.031)**; may
reflect coding structure, proxy effects or residual confounding; and **a sensitivity analysis removing
it made calibration WORSE** (Brier 0.150 → 0.160, intercept −1.468 → −1.498, slope 1.767 → 2.397).
Their conclusion: it "should not be used in isolation for clinical interpretation or dose selection."
**Check: when SHAP surfaces a surprising top predictor, look for whether the authors interrogated it
or celebrated it.** Companion to #66 (a paper that hands you the evidence against itself).

**Grading note, since this is the first 4 since EAGLE (10-17/18).** The two look incomparable — EAGLE
had 12 centres, 3 countries, 80,612 patients, a prospective deployment and a reader study; this has
one centre, 1,248 patients and 33 validation events. They share a grade because **the scale measures
how far you can trust what the paper claims.** EAGLE proved much more and then overstated part of it
in its abstract; this proves much less and overstates none of it. **A large study that overclaims and
a small study that does not can be equally reliable guides to their own contents.** Held at 4 rather
than 5 by the design ceiling alone — one centre, temporal rather than external validation, EPV 7.29,
33 validation events, uncalibrated probabilities, an investigator-defined single-instant composite hit
by only 15.3%, 148 (10.3%) excluded with no possible comparison, and a registration identifier stripped
from the PMC rendering so prespecification could not be checked. **Every one of those is named by the
authors.**

**Next queue entry.** Check the four deferred first — the Nature personal health agent paper is now
the one to watch. Then the shelf, in the order set yesterday: **(2) ECG-VLM vision-language ECG model**
(PMID 42876871, PMC13661825 — 800,034 paired ECGs, zero-shot, externally validated on 45,151
recordings), **(3) frailty in haemodialysis** (PMID 42860582, PMC13656680 — external validation from a
different city, XGBoost AUC 0.917, and that number deserves scrutiny given a questionnaire outcome at
48.6% prevalence with grip strength as a predictor), **(4) CKD after radical nephrectomy** (PMID
42808223, PMC13629762 — 8 centres, and internal 0.751 vs external 0.755 with essentially **no drop**,
which is unusual enough to test against #76), **(5) paediatric urine culture positivity** (PMID
42841462, PMC13647408), **(6) [x] DECODED 2026-11-03 — AKI after liver transplant** (PMID 42802088, PMC13618104 — n=127, the
right vehicle for #85). Watch item: PM Heart trial results (protocol PMID 42893738), quarterly.
Standing re-checks: Huang J (42765400), Lu J (42809845), Holly (42830095) / Mechael (42833894), and the
four oldest deferred reviews. Standing offers never taken up: upgrade the abstract-only entries
(Gulshan #2, Ardila #8, McKinney #10) if PDFs are supplied; retrofit summary #1 (Vishwanath) with a
Statistics Spotlight.

## 2026-10-24 run — Zhou et al., ECG-VLM: language-supervised ECG interpretation

Decoded from **full text** (PMC13661825). Graded **3/5** — good machine-learning work, honestly
reported, establishing a feasibility result rather than a clinical one. Spare #2 from the restocked
shelf.

**All four deferred papers re-checked, all four still blocked.** Google's personal health agent
(Nature, PMID 42907512) — no PMC copy. BMJ LLM-trial reporting (42869550) — no PMC copy. Ann Intern
Med Choosing Wisely (42896192) — no PMC copy. **JAMA OpenEvidence (42879271, PMC13673371) — PMC record
still serves an empty `full_text`, now day 9.**

**Everything reconciles.** 800,034 ECGs / 161,352 patients = **4.96 recordings per patient**, which is
exactly why patient-level splitting mattered and why they did it. Token truncation 2,648/800,034 =
**0.33%** ✓ matches the paper's own audit. Retrieval pool 22,722 + 15,851 + 42,111 = **80,684**.

**89. AUROC is prevalence-independent; AUPRC, precision and F1 are not — and this paper is the clean
natural experiment.** Internal → external: **AUROC range 0.679–0.981 → 0.691–0.991 (barely moves)**,
while **AUPRC 0.130–0.877 → 0.058–0.982** and **precision floor 0.105 → 0.016** collapse at the bottom.
Cause, given by the authors with their own figures: **RBBB prevalence 7.9% → 1.4%, LBBB 3.4% → 0.5%**
(5.6- and 6.8-fold falls). Worked illustration (my arithmetic): a fixed test at sensitivity 0.90 /
specificity 0.90 on 10,000 people gives precision **0.436 at 7.9% prevalence, 0.113 at 1.4%, 0.043 at
0.5%** — same test, same AUROC, precision down tenfold, because the false-alarm pool grew while the
case pool shrank. **The check cuts BOTH ways and that is the part usually missed: a low external AUPRC
is not automatically model degradation — compare it against the prevalence in that dataset.** 0.058
where the condition affects 0.5% is ~10× chance; 0.058 where it affects 5% is failure. The same effect
appears inside a subgroup here: the **paediatric group had the HIGHEST AUROC and the LOWEST AUPRC** of
any age band. Extends #42 (predictive values depend on prevalence) to the summary-curve metrics.
