100%** — a genuine result, unlike entry 61's 1/1 (CI 2.5–100%); the errors are **15 false positives and
zero false negatives**, PPV 62/77 with exact CI 69.9–88.7%; and by subtraction **Wuxi held 66 events of
570**, so the training cohort had ~46 and the internal validation cohort ~**20**, which is why the
internal AUC's interval is ~0.22 wide. **EPV: 46/2 = 23 by the usual accounting, 46/18 = 2.56 against
candidates screened (#115)** — mitigated, genuinely, by #142.

**Also noted — what earns the 3.** **Prospective design with written informed consent.** **Genuine
external validation at a separate city**, 586 participants, not a held-out slice. **Loss to follow-up
2.5% (30/1186), enumerated by reason** (14 uncontactable, 7 relocated, 4 died unrelated, 5 withdrew)
**and the lost compared against the retained** with no differences found — almost nobody runs that
comparison. **A multivariable confounding check** on both selected markers adjusted for eight
covariates, with the non-significant result (p=0.076) reported rather than buried. **Nasal sampling
standardised at the step that matters** — the sponge's residual volume fixed at a 4 mL mark — which is
what makes concentrations comparable between people. **Final model chosen on generalisation, not
apparent performance**: the text notes KNN and RF had higher training AUCs and XGB was preferred for
consistency across validation. **Seven real limitations named**, including that both cohorts came from
one province in one epidemic period, that assays used JN.1 spike only, and that baseline-only markers
miss trajectories.

**Against that.** The 0.996 is reported as the model's best result and partly justifies the model choice,
with no interrogation (#138/#139). **No funding statement, no competing-interests statement, no
reporting guideline** in the retrieved text (TRIPOD+AI fits and most items are met). The calculator's URL
was stripped by the extraction and **was not guessed at**. Supplementary tables S1–S7 and figures S1–S3
unretrievable, which matters more than usual here: S3 (per-cohort baselines), S4 (full Boruta stability),
S6 (six-model metrics), S7 (nested CV per model) and S2 (joint feature-space plots) would settle most of
the above. **Note for the record: the abstract reports the internal 0.835 and describes the external
result in words only** — a reader of the abstract alone gets the more defensible figure.

---

## 2026-11-01 — Okken A et al., AI detection of cardiac-intervention complications (Neth Heart J; PMID 42905390) — graded 3/5

**The first audit/case-finding tool in this list rather than a prediction model**, and the **first paper
in 64 to report what it cost.** All 6,564 consecutive PVI/PTCA/TAVI procedures at St. Antonius Hospital
2017–2022, temporal split (2,999 develop → 3,565 validate), four NHR-defined complications, structured +
unstructured EHR data, deliberately tuned for 100% sensitivity. Sensitivity **94.4–100%**, specificity
**99.4–100%**, **PPV 47.5–81%**, F 0.64–0.87. **127 records reviewed instead of 6,500+ to find all 79
complications.**

**147. A low PPV is a catastrophe for a test and a feature for a filter — what a false positive
TRIGGERS decides.** This list has flagged low PPV as fatal several times; here it is the design working.
CVA: prevalence **19/3,565 = 0.533%**, sensitivity 100%, specificity 99.41% → **21 false alarms beside 19
real cases**, PPV **47.5%**. Why: 0.59% of 3,546 non-cases ≈ 21, the same order as 19. **At a prevalence
under 1%, even 99.4% specificity yields roughly one false alarm per real case — that is arithmetic, not a
flaw.** Cost of "fixing" it, holding sensitivity at 100%: specificity 99.0% → PPV 34.9%; **99.4% → 47.2%**;
99.9% → 84.3%; **99.94% → 89.9%**. So moving PPV from 47.5% to 90% needs specificity from 99.41% to
99.94%, a **tenfold cut in false positives, to save ~19 file-reads.** Nobody should spend that. **Standing
check: ask what happens to a false positive before judging a PPV.** Treatment, scan or phone call →
demand high PPV. "A human opens a file" → 50% at a 28-fold workload cut is an excellent trade. Mirror
image of #89 and the entry-56 precision collapse; same arithmetic, opposite verdict.

**148. When a paper reports RANGES instead of per-group results, mine the Discussion for a worked
example.** Main text gives only "94.4–100%", "99.4–100%", "47.5–81%", "0.64–0.87" across four
complications; per-complication tables are in unretrievable supplements. But one Discussion sentence —
*"the algorithm will produce a shortlist of 40 records for manual review. This shortlist contained all 19
CVA cases"* — plus n=3,565 pins the whole table: **TP 19 · FN 0 · FP 21 · TN 3,525.** Verify against
every reported figure: sensitivity 1.000 (=100% top), specificity 3,525/3,546 = **0.9941** (=99.4%
bottom), PPV 19/40 = **0.4750** (=47.5%), F 38/59 = **0.644** (=0.64). **Four independent confirmations —
and it reveals that every LOW end of all four ranges is the CVA model**, which the paper never says. The
bleeding model yields the same way from *"an F-score of 0.87, albeit with two false negative cases out of
36"*: TP 34, FN 2, solve F=2TP/(2TP+FP+FN)=0.87 → **FP 8**, TN 3,521; sensitivity 0.9444 (=94.4%), PPV
34/42 = **0.8095** (=81% high end), F 0.8718. **The high ends are the bleeding model.** Standing check:
authors illustrate tools with one concrete case; a concrete case plus one metric usually pins the matrix,
and three or four cross-checks confirm it. Extends #121 and #146 to prose rather than percentages.

**149. A negative random-sample check is only as informative as its size — compute the probability of a
null result arising anyway.** The gold standard contains only cases the pattern statements flagged, so
"100% sensitivity" means 100% *of what the screen found*. **The authors saw this and checked** — "a
random sample of non-selected files, as well as a cross-check with existing registry data, did not
identify additional complication cases" — which is exactly the right move, **and the sample size is never
stated.** At 0.533% prevalence, P(zero cases found | missed at the full base rate) = 0.99467ⁿ:
n=50 → **76.6%**; n=100 → **58.6%**; n=200 → 34.3%; n=320 → 18.1%; n=1,000 → 0.5%. **So checking 100
records and finding nothing would have happened more often than not regardless; ~300 are needed for 80%
power to surface one case.** Jellybean analogy: one black bean in 200, pull ten and see none — you have
learned nothing. **Standing check: whenever a paper says it "audited a subset and found no
discrepancies," find n and compute. Reassuring negative audits of rare things are very cheap to obtain by
accident.**

**150. A case-finding tool is not a prediction model, and the yardsticks differ.** The complication has
already happened and is already documented; the model is **retrieving**, not forecasting. High
sensitivity is far more achievable, AUC comparisons against prediction models are category errors, and
**calibration is legitimately irrelevant** when the output is a binary triage filter — the first paper
here where skipping calibration is the right call rather than an omission (a sentence saying so would
have been worth more than a calibration curve). The paper scopes itself correctly and says prediction
"remains beyond the scope of the current project." **Standing check: establish whether the outcome
precedes or follows the data the model reads, then pick the yardstick.** Companion to #127 (match the
denominator to the claim).

**151. Credit the deliberate removal of a leakage proxy, with its reason stated — the clearest example
in 64 papers.** Step 3 of the ten-step process assessed "whether the identified patterns were clinically
plausible, removing spurious signals." Worked example given: **the model found fluoroscopic contrast
administration predictive of CVA, and the team deleted it, because contrast is given for the CT scan that
DIAGNOSES the CVA.** A variable *caused by* the diagnosis, masquerading as a predictor of it. Removing it
cost accuracy and bought validity. **Standing habit: for every top predictor, ask whether it could be a
consequence of making the diagnosis rather than a cause of the condition.** Reusable template; sharper
and more concrete than #119 (measurement-window overlap) because the mechanism is named.

**152. Watch for the screening rule that is ALSO a model input.** The pattern statements did two jobs:
they selected the candidate pool from which the gold standard was built, **and** they are one of three
inputs to the final ensemble ("the criteria-based approach as input features"). So a complication
documented in a way no statement anticipated is both absent from the labels and invisible to a third of
the model — **it can never appear as a false negative.** The paper does not name this circularity, though
#149's check is the right attempt to bound it. Standing check: trace whether the thing that generated
the labels also feeds the model.

**153. A component that is not a model should not be reported inside the model's performance range.**
Phrenic nerve palsy: **2 cases**, and the paper states plainly it was "not feasible to develop a
meaningful AI model for it. This model used selected criteria only" — a hand-written rule. **Its
sensitivity and specificity nonetheless sit inside the abstract's "94.4–100%" and "99.4–100%".**
Sensitivity 2/2 = 100% has an exact 95% CI of **15.8% to 100%**. Credit for disclosing it in the
limitations; the abstract does not. Standing check: for every component inside a reported range, ask how
many events it rests on.

**154. PROBAST+AI is an APPRAISAL instrument, not a reporting guideline — TRIPOD+AI is.** The paper
states "Model development and validation were reported in accordance with the PROBAST-AI guidelines."
PROBAST and its AI extension are risk-of-bias tools for readers, reviewers and systematic reviewers;
TRIPOD and TRIPOD+AI are the author-side reporting checklists. Self-appraising against PROBAST+AI is
sensible and naming something beats naming nothing (two of the last five named nothing) — but it does not
ask for the items TRIPOD+AI asks for, and **the items it omits are exactly the ones this paper is thin
on**: per-outcome performance tables, calibration, complete participant flow.

**155. Ask what the tool costs and how much work it saves — and credit the paper that says.**
**€20,000–30,000 to implement, €10,000/year recurring**, against **100 hours/year** of existing manual
review, with **127 records to read instead of "over 6,500"** — a 28-fold reduction against the 3,565
validation set. **This is the first cost figure in 64 papers**, and it moves the conversation from "does
it work" to "is it worth it," which is the question a hospital actually asks. Caveat recorded: the
post-implementation review *time* is never stated, so the hours saved are implied rather than measured.
**Standing demand for any deployed tool: build cost, running cost, and the workload delta.**

**Also noted — what earns the 3.** **A blinded reference standard**: two independent reviewers, consensus
adjudication by a third, and explicitly "**No model predictions or outputs were available to the
reviewers during the labeling process**" — entry 61 had to list unblinding as its first limitation; this
paper simply did it right. **All 6,564 consecutive procedures, no exclusions**, with the rationale stated
("to reflect routine clinical practice"). **A genuine temporal split.** **The design objective declared
in advance** — "The primary objective was to achieve 100% sensitivity… accepting a lower specificity
and—consequently—false positives" — which is what makes every subsequent number interpretable.
**Outcome definitions from a national registry (NHR)** rather than invented, with the one extension
(clinically relevant bleeding) specified. **Tuning confined to the training set**, ensemble combiner
cross-validated five-fold. **Data standardised to OMOP CDM and ZIB with portability as the stated
rationale** — a design decision aimed at the problem most papers here ignore. **Named model and version
strings** (`all-MiniLM-L6-v2`, scikit-learn 1.2.2, Optuna). **Honest about the bleeding misses** (2 of 36)
and why a continuum outcome is hard. **Tool is actually deployed.**

**Against that.** **No external validation, and it is not among the stated limitations**, for a tool in
clinical use — the OMOP/ZIB work prepares for portability but does not test it. #149's unquantified
check. #152's circularity. #153's 2-case rule inside the ranges. #154's wrong instrument. **No funding
statement and no competing-interests statement** in the retrieved text, while **two of the nine authors
are at Parnas, IT-Services and Consultancy** — disclosed on the author line, not in a declaration.
**Only ranges in the main text**; per-complication tables in unretrievable supplements. **The journal's
"What's new?" box cites "near 100% sensitivity and specificity" and omits PPV** — the metric the entire
value proposition rests on. **The 2.2% incidence and the 79 complications both describe the 3,565
validation set** (79/6,564 = 1.20%; 79/3,565 = 2.22%) and the text names no denominator. **"Prospective
validation set" is a temporal split of retrospectively extracted records** — the design deserves credit,
the word does not.

---

## 2026-11-02 — Li T et al., habitat-informed 3D deep learning for ESCC immunochemotherapy response (iScience; PMID 42920785) — graded 3/5

277 locally advanced ESCC patients, three Chongqing centres. Voxel-level K-means splits each tumour into
**3 habitats** (Calinski–Harabasz peaked at K=3); a three-channel 3D ResNet-50 takes image + mask +
habitat map. **External test at Centre C: 60 patients, 40 responders / 20 non-responders, and a later
non-overlapping time window.** ResNet-50 external AUC **0.927**, habitat-radiomics XGBoost **0.869**,
**DeLong p = 0.343.**

**156. Check whether a confidence interval is centred on its own point estimate.** The headline is
**0.927 (95% CI 0.850–0.951)**: **0.077 below, 0.024 above — a 3.21 ratio.** Measure all nine intervals
in the paper and the other eight run **0.92 to 1.25** (essentially symmetric). And the odd one's midpoint
is **(0.850+0.951)/2 = 0.9005**, half-width 0.0505 — **a symmetric interval around 0.9005, not around
0.927.** Cross-check with Hanley–McNeil at n=40/20: SE at AUC 0.927 ≈ 0.033 → symmetric interval
≈ 0.863–0.991, so **the reported lower bound is plausible and the upper bound is nowhere near it**; SE at
AUC 0.900 ≈ 0.039 → 0.823–0.977. **The CI method is never named anywhere in the paper, and the same
interval appears in the published abstract**, so this is not a PMC rendering artefact. **Standing check:
subtract both ways and compare. For a bounded statistic near its ceiling expect the lower arm longer —
but expect it CONSISTENTLY across the paper. A single interval wildly out of line with its neighbours is
a reporting error, not a property of the statistic.** Do not assert a typo; state that one number does
not belong to the other. Companion to #122 (an interval ending at exactly 100% is a broken method).

**157. Post hoc power is circular — and the tell is WHICH NULL it was tested against.** Verbatim: "no
sample-size calculation was performed at the design stage. A post hoc statistical power was estimated by
Hanley–McNeil variance approximation… the observed AUC of 0.927 yielded power >99.9% against a null AUC
of 0.5 and >99.9% against a clinically meaningful threshold of 0.75." Two problems. **(a) Power computed
from the effect you observed is a deterministic restatement of your p-value** — significant results
always yield high "observed power," non-significant ones always low; the confidence interval already
carries the information without needing a hypothesis. **(b) The nulls tested are not the ones in
dispute.** The claim at stake is ResNet-50 (0.927) vs habitat XGBoost (0.869), reported at **p = 0.343**.
Recover the real power from that p-value alone: two-sided p 0.343 → **z = 0.948**; observed difference
0.058 → **SE_diff = 0.058/0.948 = 0.0612**; power = Φ(0.948 − 1.96) = **15.6%**. **So the study had ~16%
power for its own headline while reporting >99.9%.** For 80% power you need SE_diff = 0.0207, and since
SE scales as 1/√n, **n ≈ 60 × (0.0612/0.0207)² ≈ 524 patients** — a far more useful sentence, computable
from numbers already printed. Coin analogy: six heads in ten flips, then announcing enormous power to
detect a two-headed coin. **Standing check: when you see post hoc or "observed" power, especially against
a null of chance, read it as a flag; then find the comparison the paper rests on and work its power back
from its p-value.**

**158. Count the uncorrected tests and compute the expected false positives.** **321** habitat radiomics
features screened by univariate t/Mann–Whitney at **p < 0.05 "without multiple hypothesis testing
correction"** — stated twice, honestly, which does not fix it. **321 × 0.05 = 16.05 expected by chance,
against 41 actually retained** at that step: up to ~39% of the survivors could be noise before Spearman
(|ρ|≥0.9) and LASSO run. Bonferroni threshold would have been **1.56 × 10⁻⁴**. Pipeline 321 → 41 → 24 →
**9**. Events per feature against the 61 non-responders in development: **61/9 = 6.8** for the final set,
**61/321 = 0.19** against the screen (#115). Standing check: find the number of features tested and
multiply by alpha.

**159. ICC and Dice measure different kinds of agreement — a summary statistic can flatter what a
voxel-wise one exposes.** The paper measures its own segmentation reproducibility (a first in this list):
30 patients re-delineated independently by two blinded surgeons, giving **ICC on tumour volume 0.95
(0.92–0.97)** and **Dice on voxel overlap 0.88 (0.85–0.91)**. **ICC ≈ between-subject variance ÷
(between-subject variance + measurement error)**, so like κ it is inflated by a wide case mix — tumour
volumes vary by an order of magnitude, so a few cm³ of disagreement barely dents the ratio, and ICC only
needs the readers to **rank** tumours the same way. **Dice = 2×(voxels in both)÷(voxels in A + voxels in
B)** has no such escape hatch: it demands voxel-by-voxel agreement within each tumour. Two readers can
agree on volume to 2% while their outlines sit in different places. **Dice 0.88 means ~12% of the
delineated volume is not agreed — and every habitat cluster, all 321 features and the network's entire
input were computed inside those outlines.** Standing check: when agreement is reported on a derived
summary, ask for agreement on the underlying thing; summaries let errors cancel. Extends #123/#124 from
categorical to continuous.

**160. A clinical-only baseline sitting at chance is a FINDING, not a formality.** Eight pretreatment
variables (age, sex, BMI, ECOG, smoking, drinking, cT, cN) in an L2-regularised logistic regression:
external AUC **0.496 (95% CI 0.356–0.648)** — the interval contains 0.5. **There is currently no clinical
way to select patients for this treatment**, which is the entire justification for the imaging work and
the reason the 0.869 from nine hand-crafted features is interesting even without a neural network. The
paper reports it inside a list of baselines and never returns to it. **Standing check: read the
clinical-only or simplest baseline first — it tells you how much room there was to improve on, and
sometimes it is the paper's most important number.**

**161. When the external cohort is the stated basis for choosing an architecture, it has stopped being
fully held out.** Methods say ResNet-18/34/50/101 and DenseNet were all compared. Results give external
AUCs for three — ResNet-50 **0.927**, ResNet-18 **0.833**, ResNet-34 **0.805** — and **the only stated
basis for preferring ResNet-50 is that "its external-test AUC was higher."** ResNet-101 and DenseNet
appear in the Methods and vanish from the Results prose. Contrast **entry 58**, which met the identical
situation and **renamed its own cohort** ("an external evaluation cohort rather than independent
confirmation of a model finalized a priori"); this paper does not raise it. Standing check: count the
architectures/models compared, then ask which dataset chose the winner.

**162. An external cohort that is ALSO temporally later is a stronger test than either split alone.**
Centres A+B ran January 2020 – December 2022; **Centre C ran September 2022 – August 2024** — a different
hospital *and* a non-overlapping later window, so the test covers geographic and temporal shift at once.
**The paper never points this out.** Also reassuring here: response rates 71.7% / 73.1% / 66.7% are
close, so unlike entry 58 (23.7% vs 45.6%) the external cohort is not a different population — which is
part of why the AUCs are comparable at all (#139). Standing habit: check the date ranges of each centre,
not just the names.

**163. Credit a paper that states its own non-significant headline — in the abstract.** ResNet-50 beat
the simple model by 0.058 at **p = 0.343**, and the authors say "numerically higher… not statistically
significant" **five times**: abstract, results, discussion opening, discussion body, and the baseline
comparison section. They also **admit overfitting in their own voice** ("the near-perfect AUC in the
training cohort… is therefore likely optimistic… indicates a generalization gap and suggests potential
overfitting"), and **state the limits of their own ablation** ("does not establish that the AUC
difference is attributable exclusively to that channel"). Companion to #104 and #120. **A paper that
argues against its own headline wants to be understood rather than believed.**

**164. Check what the outcome actually is — here, imaging predicting imaging.** The endpoint is
**iRECIST response at four weeks** read off a post-treatment CT, predicted from a baseline CT. Not
circular in the leakage sense (#119 — different scans, different times), but: **pCR was unavailable**
because the cohort was non-surgical, and **survival follow-up was too immature to analyse**, both stated
honestly. So the outcome inherits iRECIST's own reader variability, and whether responders live longer is
not addressed. Note also that **SHAP was computed for the habitat-radiomics XGBoost, not for the
proposed network** — so the interpretability section explains the comparator and the headline model
remains uninterpreted, which a skimming reader will not notice.

**Also noted — what earns the 3.** **Genuine external validation at a third centre** that contributed no
training or validation data. **Three baselines including a properly specified ablation** — habitat
channel removed with architecture, pretrained initialisation, ROI size (96×96×96), optimiser (Adam, lr
1e−3), batch size and 48-epoch schedule all held identical — each with a DeLong test (vs clinical
p<0.001; vs whole-tumour radiomics p=0.008; vs two-channel CNN p=0.028). **K chosen by an objective
criterion** (CH index maximised at K=3, monotone decline for K≥4). **A described and justified
harmonisation protocol** applied to all 277 scans (300/30 HU window, isotropic 1 mm³ bicubic-spline
resampling, background suppression below −150 HU). **Segmentation by two blinded surgeons with chief-
surgeon review and quantified reproducibility** (#159). **Outcome adjudicated by two independent senior
surgeons with chief-physician arbitration.** **Competing interests declared** ("no competing interests
related to this study"). **Code deposited publicly on GitHub.** **Three ethics committee IDs given**
(KY2025056, BNLLKY-2025–050, 2020MSXM014). Entry 64 managed none of those last three.

**Against that.** #156's interval. #158's uncorrected 321. #157's circular power. #161's apparent
selection peek. **No reporting guideline named** (TRIPOD+AI or CLAIM would fit). Figures and
supplementary tables unretrievable, so **no sensitivity, specificity, PPV, NPV, calibration intercept or
slope could be read for any model** — calibration is asserted as "high concordance" in text only. The
three-habitat biological story (viable / hypoxic-necrotic / immune interface) is a hypothesis the paper
labels as such, with pathological validation acknowledged as unavailable. **Funder names and grant
numbers, and the GitHub URL, were stripped by the text extraction and were not guessed at.**

---

## 2026-11-03 — Kamada H et al., deep learning for cholangioscopy visualisation quality (DEN Open; PMID 42898633) — graded 3/5

**The most honestly reported paper in this list, by a clear margin, and its result is largely negative.**
Kagawa University pilot: 15 patients — **3 development cases** (training + a convergence check) and **12
comparison cases** (gel 5, saline 7), no overlap. ResNet-18 scores endoscopic frames 0–4 for visual
clarity during electrohydraulic lithotripsy. The grade is capped by arithmetic, not candour: twelve
patients cannot produce strong evidence. But **the claim and the evidence are the same size**, and the
authors spent most of their effort proving their own negative.

**165. Verification bias — ask how the cases that got the reference standard were chosen.** The
single most important new technique here, and the first appearance in 66 papers. Expert labels are
expensive, so only 120 of 1,950 frames were scored — and frames were "drawn at random **while cycling
through the model's predicted classes**," which enriched the sample for predicted-usable frames
(**39% of the sample vs 13.8% of all frames**, a 2.8× enrichment). **Because selection depended on the
test's own output, every raw metric is biased.** Reconstruct the 2×2 from sensitivity 34/56 and
specificity 51/64: **TP 34 · FN 22 · FP 13 · TN 51**, predicted-usable = 47/120 = 39.2% ✓. Over-sampling
predicted-usable over-fills **TP relative to FN** (inflating sensitivity) and **FP relative to TN**
(deflating specificity). **The authors caught this themselves, named the correction (Begg–Greenes, 1983),
applied it, and printed both sets of numbers in the abstract.** The fix rests on their own precise
sentence — "per-predicted-class positive predictive values are unbiased" — so you re-weight each
predicted class back to its natural frequency. My binary re-derivation: PPV 34/47 = 0.723 among
predicted-usable, 22/73 = 0.301 among predicted-unusable, mix 13.8%/86.2% → prevalence **0.360**,
sensitivity **27.8%**, specificity **94.0%**; the paper, re-weighting across all five predicted classes,
reports **31.3% / 29.8% / 93.5%**. Same method, same direction. **Result: sensitivity 60.7% → 29.8%
(halved), specificity 79.7% → 93.5%.** Model predicts usable 13.8% of the time, truth ≈31.3% → "specific
but markedly under-sensitive," so HQVR underestimates usable time **in both arms**. School-nurse analogy:
if you only send the children who look ill for a blood test, the misses were never counted. **Standing
check on every diagnostic study: find the sampling sentence in the Methods and ask whether the test's
output could have influenced who got verified.**

**166. Report the human-vs-human agreement first — it establishes whether the ceiling exists.** Four
numbers, arranged the right way: **reader vs reader 5-class weighted κ = 0.98**; **model vs consensus
0.60**; binary Cohen's κ **0.95** vs **0.41**. The human–human figure is the one most papers skip and it
does the most work: a model at 0.60 could be a weak model *or* an inherently subjective task, and only
the readers' 0.98 distinguishes them. **Here the ceiling is near-perfect, so 0.60 is squarely the
model's problem.** Note also the **same model, same frames, 0.60 weighted vs 0.41 binary** — collapsing
an ordered scale discards the partial credit for near-misses, worth **0.19 of κ**. This is the direct
answer to #124 (weighted κ without its weighting scheme is not a reported statistic): report both.
**Standing demand: the human–human κ on the same frames, scored blind. Benchmarks from other studies
will not do — κ depends on case mix (#123).**

**167. With tiny groups, compute the minimum achievable p BEFORE reading the result.** Exact
Mann–Whitney, 5 vs 7: there are **C(12,5) = 792** arrangements, so perfect separation gives one-sided
1/792 and **two-sided p = 0.0025 — the floor.** Reported: HQVR **U = 27, p = 0.15** (exact: 0.1490 ✓);
post-hoc MPQS **U = 34, p = 0.005** (exact: 0.0051 ✓). **U = 34 is one rank-swap from the maximum of 35**,
so p = 0.005 here means *all but one of the five gel values exceeded all seven saline values* — a
near-perfect ordering of twelve numbers, not a large-sample effect. Both readings matter: strong
ordering, twelve observations. **And the floor is a design fact: the primary analysis could never have
reached significance without near-perfect separation, which was knowable in advance — so a
non-significant HQVR was always the likely outcome whatever the gel does. A null from a study that could
barely have produced a non-null is not evidence of no effect.**

**168. A hold-out drawn from the same videos as the training data measures nothing — and this paper
says so about its own 99%.** Hold-out accuracy **99.0%**, sensitivity 100% (16/16), specificity 98.8%
(83/84) — against a best training-validation accuracy of **79.5%** and an independent-validation accuracy
of **70.8%**, a **28.2-point gap**. The paper's verdict on its own number: "the higher hold-out figures
arise because those frames overlap the training videos and do not establish generalizability." **A 99%
sitting in a results section explicitly labelled as meaningless is a thing this list has never seen.**
Standing check: for any hold-out, ask whether it shares *source material* with training — near-duplicate
neighbouring frames, slices of the same scan, repeat visits by the same patient.

**169. Refusing baseline significance tests is correct, and saying why is better.** Verbatim: "No
formal statistical testing was performed for baseline characteristics owing to the small sample and
varying stone burden; **non-significance must not be read as group comparability**." Baseline p-values
conflate chance imbalance with real imbalance and are actively misleading in a non-randomised
comparison; declining to produce them, and explaining, is the right call and almost nobody makes it.

**170. Credit a paper that works out which direction each of its own confounders pushes.** Three,
all stated. **(a)** Gel group had larger stones (16.2 vs 13.3 mm) and longer procedures (928 vs 455 s) —
harder cases, "a confounder that **biases against, rather than toward**, the efficiency rationale for
gel." **(b)** The model scored gel more conservatively (mean signed error −0.40 vs −0.19, permutation
p = 0.34) — also **against** the observed gel advantage. **(c)** Saline cases were earlier and gel later,
allowing an operator learning curve — this one biases **toward** gel, and is disclosed too. **Standing
habit: for every confounder, write down its sign. Papers that do this are telling you they tried to
break their own result.**

**171. Label post hoc analyses as post hoc — in the abstract.** Primary HQVR **p = 0.15, "not
significant"**; MPQS **p = 0.005, "post hoc exploratory"**; both appear in the abstract with their labels
attached, and the Methods state in advance that "MPQS and temporal analyses were not pre-specified."
Caveat worth recording: HQVR, MPQS and longest-usable-interval are three summaries of the same 1,950
model outputs, so they are not independent; the paper names two post hoc measures but does not say
whether others were computed and discarded.

**172. Print the class-imbalance counts and say how imbalance was handled.** Score 0/1/2/3/4 =
**45/964/465/202/56** frames (1,732 total; commonest class 55.7%, rarest 2.6%, ratio **21:1**), handled
with an inverse-frequency weighted random sampler, "**not augmentation**." Exact counts plus the
mechanism, which lets a reader judge whether the rare classes could have been learned at all — and
Table 2 shows they largely weren't: **every per-class precision is below 0.60**, worst in the middle of
the scale (Score 1: 0.25, Score 2: 0.29) where a human most needs help.

**173. List the reporting-guideline items you FAIL.** "Reporting follows applicable CLAIM and
TRIPOD+AI items; unmet items are listed in the Limitations" — and they are: **sample-size
justification, calibration, and a large independent development cohort.** Naming a guideline is common
(#154); naming your own unmet items is not.

**174. Distinguish "this κ validates the SCALE" from "this κ validates the LABELS."** All training
labels came from one expert. Two additional endoscopists scored a **disjoint** subset (weighted κ
0.60–0.79), and the paper states this "**validates the scale rather than the training labels**" — then
lists single-reader labelling as a separate limitation. The κ subset, hold-out and accuracy-evaluation
sets were **mutually disjoint**, which is what makes the distinction checkable.

**Also noted.** Development cases were **prespecified**: "the first three consecutive procedures in which
both viscous gel and saline were used within the same procedure, selected **before the comparison cohort
was assembled** and solely on this mixed-medium criterion." Sampling interval lengthened from 0.2 s to
1.1–10.9 s **explicitly to reduce temporal autocorrelation**, with all comparisons kept at the patient
level. Unrecorded procedural variables disclosed (generator settings, irrigation flow/pressure, gel
volume; injection not protocolised). **Funding "nothing to report"; conflicts "none."** Off-label use of
the gel in the bile duct **disclosed in the consent process**. Nine numbered limitations.

**Against that.** Twelve patients, five versus seven (#167's floor). Three development cases, one
labelling expert. The instrument and the comparison are entangled — the gel result is measured with a
30%-sensitive ruler, defended reasonably by the per-medium check on 50 and 70 frames. **HQVR has no
validated threshold and is linked to no clinical outcome**, which the paper states. And the cholangitis
counts (**4/7 saline vs 1/5 gel**) will read as a safety signal: five events in twelve patients, no test
run (correctly), mechanism unmeasurable because irrigation volume was not recorded.

---

## Shelf restocked — 2026-11-03

Emptied by today's run. Restocked with the `pubmed pmc[sb]` + date-window method (2026/10/20–2026/11/03,
380 hits, screened by metadata). **All six are PMC-confirmed.** Ordered by what each would teach.

**(1) [x] DECODED 2026-11-04 — Deep learning detection of active pulmonary TB from chest radiographs — retrospective AND
prospective multicentre, with a reader study** — PMID 42936410, PMC13697335, *iScience* 2026;29(11):117960,
Tuersun A et al. (Kashi, Xinjiang). 4,613 training radiographs from two centres; **retrospective external
cohort 1,318 patients AND a prospective cohort of 560**; AUC **0.917** and **0.896**; performance "stable
across **prespecified** demographic and scanner subgroups"; and in the prospective cohort **AI assistance
raised radiologist AUCs to 0.897–0.937 across experience levels**. Explicitly: "positive predictions
should not be used alone to initiate treatment." **Top pick — this list has never decoded a prospective
validation with a human-AI reader study**, and several authors are at a commercial AI firm (Deepwise), so
there is a COI angle too.

**(2) [x] DECODED 2026-11-06 — Gestational-age estimation from linked mother–infant EHRs across two health systems** — PMID
42957585, PMC13719529, *Pharmacoepidemiol Drug Saf* 2026;35(11), Bejan CA et al. (Vanderbilt + Michigan).
**54,344 and 34,345 dyads**; the pipeline was **independently reimplemented and retrained at the second
site** (MAE 4.4 → 2.8 days); reports **temporal drift** in EHR elements and performance **stratified by
gestational-age category** (substantially worse for preterm). **Reproducibility and portability as the
stated outcome** — a framing this list has never covered.

**(3) [x] DECODED 2026-11-07 — Bipolar disorder classification from speech and language** — PMID 42967232, PMC13723951, *J Med
Internet Res* 2026;28:e98094, Yamaguchi Y et al. (Tokyo/Keio). 61 patients, 99 controls, **6,373 acoustic
+ 211 linguistic features**, LASSO with **nested CV**, age/sex-matched undersampling, **permutation
testing**, medication sensitivity analyses. BP vs HC AUC **0.916** — but the interesting part is the
failures the authors report: DS vs ES 0.760 (p=.03), **MS/MX vs ES 0.601 (p=.20)**, **DS vs MS/MX 0.569
(p=.30)**. An extreme multiplicity case (6,373 features on 160 people) — direct extension of #158.

**(4) [x] DECODED 2026-11-08 — NOTIFY-DISCUSS: patient and clinician perceptions of a DEPLOYED AI screening tool** — PMID
42965008, PMC13720997, *Am Heart J Plus* 2026;72:100989, Keamy-Minor E et al. (Stanford). Semi-structured
interviews and focus groups, **11 patients and 9 clinicians**, thematic analysis in NVivo. Themes include
consent to AI use, data rights and security, the time burden of contextualising incidental findings,
potential for unintended harm. **This list has never decoded a qualitative study**, and this one is about
a tool already in clinical use.

**(5) [x] DECODED 2026-11-09 — AI-driven biomarker discovery for precision psychiatry (review)** — PMID 42947584, PMC13720497,
*Clin Psychopharmacol Neurosci* 2026;24(4):675–691, Serretti A. Proposes a **six-stage framework** linking
intended use, measurement validity, model evaluation, comparative utility and monitored deployment. Two
sharp reusable observations: "discrimination is supported more strongly in development data than in
independent evaluation," and **"many reported metrics also address a simpler task than the intended
decision: diagnosis instead of differential diagnosis, general prognosis instead of differential
treatment benefit."**

**(6) [x] DECODED 2026-11-10 — Explainable AI in prostate and bladder cancer (review of 70 studies)** — PMID 42960345,
PMC13719121, *Curr Urol* 2026;20(6):332–348, Diamantidis D et al. Distinguishes **post hoc explanation
from interpretable-by-design** and concludes "**prospective evidence that explainable AI affects clinical
decisions is lacking**." A good companion to the accumulated SHAP lessons (#96, #106, #143, #164).

---

## 2026-11-04 — Tuersun A et al., deep learning for active vs non-active pulmonary TB on chest radiographs (iScience; PMID 42936410) — graded 4/5

**The first 4/5 in nine days, and the first paper here with a genuine PROSPECTIVE validation cohort.**
Five hospitals in Kashi Prefecture, Xinjiang: training 4,613 (KCH 2,872 + JCH 1,741), retrospective
external 1,318 (SCH 1,011 + YCH 307), **prospective 560 (MCH, written consent)**. Total **6,491**.
ShuffleNetV2 — 2.3 M parameters, 0.9 s/case. AUC **0.926 / 0.917 / 0.896**. **Every arithmetic breakdown
reconciles exactly** — by cohort, activity status, sex and centre.

**175. PPV belongs to ONE prevalence and travels nowhere — recompute it for your own setting.** The
paper prints its prospective confusion matrix: **TP 137 · FN 22 · FP 68 · TN 333** (n=560), giving
sensitivity 0.862, specificity 0.830, **PPV 0.668**, NPV 0.938. Freeze Se and Sp and vary only
prevalence: **52.2% → PPV 84.7%; 46.5% → 81.5%; 28.4% → 66.8%; 10% → 36.1%; 2% → 9.4%.** **The same
model has PPV 81.5% where it was built and 66.8% where it was tested — a 14.7-point drop from nothing but
base rate.** Mechanism: false positives come out of the healthy pool, which grows as prevalence falls —
at 28.4%, 17% of 401 non-active patients is 68, the same order as the 137 real cases. One-line formula to
run every time: **PPV = (Se × prev) ÷ (Se × prev + (1 − Sp) × (1 − prev))**. **And copy what these authors
did with it:** they computed it, stated that "33.2% of positive predictions were false positives," named
the harms (isolation, extra procedures, distress, resources, inappropriate multidrug therapy), concluded
triage-only, said the threshold must be re-evaluated per population, and put **"positive predictions
should not be used alone to initiate treatment" in the abstract.** Direct application of #147 by the
authors themselves.

**176. When discrimination survives a move and calibration does not, compute the prevalence ratio — the
direction AND size of the error are predictable.** The list has described this since entry 58; here is
the arithmetic. Active TB ran at **44.8%** (training), **52.2%** (retrospective external), **28.4%**
(prospective). Trained at ~46.5% pooled, deployed at 28.4%. Convert to **odds**: training 0.465/0.535 =
**0.869**; deployment 0.284/0.716 = **0.397**. **Correction factor on the odds = 0.397/0.869 = 0.456 —
roughly halve every predicted odds.** Worked: model says 20% → **10.2%**; 30% → **16.4%**; 50% →
**31.3%**; 70% → **51.6%**; 90% → **80.4%**. Every number falls and **the middle of the range moves most
— exactly where the paper says its calibration curve deviated** ("particularly in the low-to-intermediate
predicted-probability ranges"). Intuition: a doctor trained where half of coughing patients had TB will
over-call it in a clinic where a quarter do — their judgement of *which* patients look worse is intact,
their sense of *how common* it is was formed elsewhere. **Watch-out: this uses one number and assumes
nothing else changed; real shifts also move case mix, severity and equipment, so a prior adjustment is a
first approximation. The remedy the paper correctly prescribes is site-specific recalibration on local
data plus ongoing monitoring** — which needs outcome data from the new site, and is why so few deployed
models have it.

**177. Check whether the reference standard includes the PREDICTOR'S OWN MODALITY.** Active TB required
(i) microbiological confirmation, (ii) symptoms, **and (iii) imaging findings** (consolidation, nodules,
cavities, ground-glass, tree-in-bud); non-active required negative microbiology **and imaging features**
(calcified/sclerotic lesions, clean cavities, pleural thickening). **The model's input is the chest
radiograph, so part of what it learned to reproduce is a radiologist's reading of the same image** — an
easier and less useful task than predicting bacteriology. The microbiological criterion anchors it, so
this is partial rather than full circularity, and **the paper never raises it.** The right analysis is a
sensitivity analysis against microbiology alone. **Also a genuine ambiguity: the Methods say "patients
with microbiologically confirmed TB were included" AND that non-active cases were "defined by negative
pathogenetic confirmation" — both cannot be true as written.** The likely reading (history of confirmed
TB, activity re-adjudicated) is not stated, and it determines which population the model applies to.
Sharper than #119 because the overlap is in the *measurement modality*, not the timing.

**178. A crossover reader study with AI always second, on the same images, with no washout, cannot
separate AI benefit from re-reading.** Nine radiologists read unassisted (3 senior ≥10 y, 3 intermediate
5–10 y, 3 junior <5 y): **0.648 (Junior_3) to 0.786 (Senior_3)**, juniors clustered 0.648–0.700, **all
nine below the model's 0.896**. Then **one randomly selected reader per tier** re-read **the same 560
images** with AI output live: Senior_A **0.937**, Intermediate_A **0.902**, Junior_A **0.897**. So the
assisted arm is **n = 3**, not 9; order was not randomised; no washout is stated; and we are not told
*which* reader per tier was selected, so the assisted figures cannot be matched to their own unassisted
baselines. Three mechanisms could produce the gain — AI helped, readers remembered the cases, or those
three were the stronger readers — and the design separates none. **The limitations section covers
international cohorts, clinical parameters, atypical immunocompetent cases and the architecture benchmark
— and never mentions the reader study.**

**179. An assisted reader whose AUC lands ON the model's is a sign of wholesale adoption, not synergy.**
**Junior_A assisted 0.897 vs the model's 0.896 — a difference of 0.001.** **Senior_A assisted 0.937 —
0.041 ABOVE the model.** The natural reading: the junior adopted the model's call more or less wholesale
(**automation bias**), while the senior combined it with their own judgement and beat it. That has direct
implications for who should be given such a tool and with what training. **The paper prints both numbers
and does not comment.** Standing check in any human-AI study: compare each assisted reader's score with
the model's, not just with their own baseline — landing on the model is a red flag, exceeding it is the
goal.

**180. An architecture chosen for efficiency is a legitimate choice — say so, and don't call a 0.001
lead superiority.** Prospective AUCs: ShuffleNetV2 **0.896**, ViT-Base/16 **0.895**, ResNet50 0.882,
DenseNet121 0.848, EfficientNet-B0 0.687. Retrospective external: ResNet50 **0.930**, DenseNet121
**0.927**, ViT **0.927**, **ShuffleNetV2 0.917** (fourth of five), EfficientNet-B0 0.817. Hanley–McNeil
SE at n=159/401 for AUC 0.896 is **0.0173** (implying 0.862–0.930, and the paper reports 0.860–0.932, so
its intervals are sound) — **so the 0.001 lead over ViT is 0.06 standard errors**, and **no DeLong test
between architectures is reported** (they ran DeLong on the subgroups but not on the comparison that
chose the design). **The paper's real argument is a good one and it makes it: 2.3 M parameters vs 86 M
(37×) and 0.9 s vs 6.7 s (7.4×)** for a tool meant for county hospitals with few radiologists. Its phrase
"superior or equivalent" blends an untested lead with the honest word; the honest word is equivalent.
**Standing check: find the standard error before accepting any ranking — and note what the extra 84
million parameters bought.**

**181. Augmentation that produces anatomically impossible images, described as dataset expansion.**
Verbatim: random horizontal and vertical flips "effectively quadrupled the training dataset from 4,613 to
approximately 18,452 images (4,613 original images × 4 augmentation variants: original, horizontal flip,
vertical flip, and both flips)." **A vertically flipped chest radiograph puts the diaphragm above the lung
apices — an image that cannot exist in a human body**; horizontal flipping at least yields a situs-inversus-
like mirror. Such flips can act as useful regularisation noise (the network learns not to rely on absolute
position) but they are **not realistic variation**, and **four copies are not 18,452 independent patients
— the effective sample size is still 4,613.** Standing check: read the augmentation list and ask whether
each transformation could occur in the real imaging of a real body.

**182. "All subgroup p > 0.05" cannot establish invariance.** Five dimensions (age, gender, occupation,
residence, scanner type) compared by DeLong in a cohort with **159 active cases**. Split 159 events
across age bands or scanner models and a non-significant DeLong is close to guaranteed whether or not
real differences exist. **Absence of a detected difference is not demonstrated fairness**, and the
abstract's "performance was stable across prespecified demographic and scanner subgroups" reads as the
latter. Credit for prespecifying them; the inference still needs bounding.

**183. Credit the paper that computes its own PPV, names the harms, and writes "do not treat on this
alone" into the abstract.** Also credited here: **a proper one-factor-at-a-time ablation** (resolution
384², width 0.5/2.0, no augmentation, **no pretraining — the largest drop**, focal loss) ; **benchmarking
that admits three of four rivals won externally**; **error analysis with named anatomical causes** (an
active case missed where the right clavicle overlapped an apical abnormality; a non-active case over-called
where pleural calcification and chronic post-TB change were read as active); **consent handled separately
for retrospective (waived) and prospective (written) arms** with an IRB number; **timing reported** (model
0.9 s; seniors 16.9 s; intermediates 35.3 s; juniors **87.1 s** — juniors took 5.2× longer than seniors
*and* scored lower); and an unusually careful **comparison with prior CXR-TB studies**, noting that
reported 96–99% accuracies came from distinguishing TB from normal — "a different and generally less
clinically specific task" — and that "latent tuberculosis is not directly equivalent to the non-active
pulmonary tuberculosis category used in our study."

**184. Two disclosure notes worth recording.** **(a)** "The authors declare no competing interests" sits
alongside **four authors affiliated to Beijing Deepwise & League of PHD Technology Co., Ltd**, a
commercial medical-AI company, including the author credited with formal analysis and interpretation. The
affiliations are disclosed on the author line, which matters most; the formal declaration is in tension
with them and a reader should know both. **(b) Credit: the first generative-AI-use declaration in this
list** — "the authors used ChatGPT (OpenAI) for language-editing and document-formatting assistance,"
with full responsibility retained. Also: **code address published** (`https://github.com/xujingxu/DL_APT`
as printed; not fetched here), data available on request under a data-use agreement.

**Also noted — minor internal inconsistencies.** The Results give the prospective cohort's mean age as
**60.73 ± 16.32** — which is the figure for all 6,491 patients — while the participant-details section
gives **61.51 ± 17.40**. And the reader paragraph says the unassisted range ran "to 0.786 for Senior_3"
then that seniors reached **0.786–0.811**. **No reporting guideline named** (TRIPOD+AI, CLAIM or STARD
would fit). All five centres in one prefecture, no international validation (acknowledged). Figures and
supplementary tables unretrievable, so **Brier scores, calibration intercept/slope, the full ablation
table, per-architecture sensitivity/specificity and the subgroup table could not be read**; the
prospective confusion matrix is in the body text and was fully verified.

**Next queue entry.** **JAMA reaches day 21 on 2026-11-05 — the standing instruction fires: decode
PMID 42879271 abstract-only and say so explicitly, unless its PMC body has filled.** Re-check the other
three deferred first. After that, continue the restocked shelf at **(2) gestational-age estimation across
two health systems** (PMID 42957585, PMC13719529 — 54,344 + 34,345 dyads, pipeline **independently
reimplemented and retrained** at the second site, MAE 4.4 → 2.8 days, with **temporal drift** analysis and
performance **stratified by gestational-age category**, substantially worse for preterm; reproducibility
and portability as the stated outcome). Then (3) bipolar speech/NLP with **6,373 features on 160 people**
and honestly-reported interstate failures (42967232), (4) the NOTIFY-DISCUSS **qualitative** study of a
deployed AI tool (42965008), (5) the precision-psychiatry six-stage framework review (42947584), (6) the
explainable-AI urology review (42960345). Watch item: PM Heart trial results (protocol PMID 42893738),
quarterly. Standing re-checks: Huang J (42765400), Lu J (42809845), Holly (42830095) / Mechael
(42833894), and the four oldest deferred reviews. Standing offers never taken up: upgrade the
abstract-only entries (Gulshan #2, Ardila #8, McKinney #10) if PDFs are supplied; retrofit summary #1
(Vishwanath) with a Statistics Spotlight.

## From entry 68 — Patel et al., clinician use of an AI clinical information platform (JAMA, 2026; decoded 2026-11-05, ABSTRACT ONLY)

**The first abstract-only decode since entry 10**, and only after twenty-one consecutive days of an empty PMC body, a paywalled Scite route and DNS-blocked direct fetching. The techniques below are all derived from the abstract's own published numbers — which is itself the lesson: **a well-reported abstract contains enough arithmetic to audit several of its own claims.**

**185. A use ratio is a closed system, and its workforce-weighted mean is forced to be exactly 1.0.**

A use ratio is a share divided by a share: group *i*'s share of the queries, `q_i`, over its share of the workforce, `w_i`. So `R_i = q_i / w_i`. Both sets of shares sum to 1, which means

    sum over i of (w_i * R_i) = sum over i of q_i = 1

exactly, by definition, in every possible dataset. **There is no study in which these ratios do not average to 1.**

Consequence for reading: once you learn that some large groups sit well above 1, you have already learned that others must sit below 1 without being told anything about them. In entry 68, internal medicine (1.88) and family medicine (1.80) are two of the largest physician specialties. Using approximate workforce shares, the six named groups hold about 37% of the workforce and contribute about 0.557 of the required total, so **the unnamed 63% must average about 0.70.** "Lower use in procedural and diagnostic specialties" was therefore partly fixed before any data arrived.

**What is not fixed is the magnitude.** Ophthalmology at 0.23 is far below the 0.70 the constraint merely requires, and *that* gap is the finding. **The sign is arithmetic; the size is evidence.** Report how far below, never just which side.

Applies to every share-over-share metric: standardised morbidity and mortality ratios, observed-to-expected ratios, representation indices, location quotients, lift in a marketing or risk model. Always ask what the weighted average is pinned at.

**186. When the outcome variable is produced by a model, sample size buys you nothing.**

Split error into two parts. **Sampling error** is the luck of which units you observed; it shrinks as 1/sqrt(n). **Measurement error** is the systematic gap between instrument and truth; **it does not shrink with n at all.** More measurements through a bent ruler give a very precise estimate of the wrong length.

Entry 68's classified subset is 38.8 million queries. For the reported 51.9%:

    SE = sqrt(0.519 * 0.481 / 38,800,000) = 0.000080  ->  0.0080 percentage points

A 95% interval of roughly 51.884% to 51.916%. For 33.1% it is 0.0076 points; for 13.9%, 0.0056 points. **Sampling uncertainty is about one hundredth of a percentage point. It is invisible and irrelevant.**

Now let the classifier be 90% accurate — respectable for free-text clinical classification. With symmetric confusion between two categories of true share *t* and *d*:

    observed t = 0.9t + 0.1d
    observed d = 0.9d + 0.1t

True shares of 35% and 12% would be observed as 32.7% and 14.3% — close to the published 33.1% and 13.9%. **A 10% symmetric classifier error moves these numbers by two percentage points, which is 250 times the sampling uncertainty.** Asymmetric error moves them further, and in one direction.

**The reading rule: when n is enormous, stop reading the confidence intervals and start interrogating the instrument.** At this scale the intervals are theatre. The only question that determines whether 33.1% means anything is how a query was assigned to its category and how often that assignment was right.

**This is the mirror image of technique 157** (post hoc power is circular). There, n was too small and power was the whole question. Here n is vast, power and precision are solved, and **validity is the whole question.** A huge sample makes a study *precise*, not *strong*. Conflating the two is how a reader comes to believe a classifier they have never seen.

**187. The total divided by the final period recovers the shape of a growth curve when only endpoints are published.**

Entry 68's abstract gives two points and a total, and no trajectory. The total constrains the middle.

    Jan 2024 monthly volume:  355 x 18.0        =      6,390
    Jun 2026 monthly volume:  703,265 x 39.5    = 27,778,968

Define **effective final-volume periods** = total / final-period volume:

    271,000,000 / 27,778,968 = 9.76

So all 30 months hold as much volume as 9.76 months at the final rate. Now compare with pure exponential growth between the same endpoints. The constant monthly multiplier is

    r = (27,778,968 / 6,390) ^ (1/29) = 1.3349   (33.5% per month)

and the geometric sum is

    6,390 * (1.3349^30 - 1) / 0.3349 = 110.7 million  ->  3.99 effective periods

**Observed 9.76 against exponential 3.99.** The real curve accumulated two and a half times more volume than smooth exponential growth between the same endpoints. Only one shape does that: **growth was front-loaded and then flattened.** The platform neared its final scale well before the end rather than climbing steeply to it. S-shaped, not exponential.

Why it matters: an exponential reading forecasts 30x next year; a flattening reading says acquisition is mostly done and the 2.19-fold rise in intensity per user is now the live part of the story. **The abstract does not say which. Its own three numbers do.** Works on any growth report giving a start, an end and a cumulative total — revenue, enrolment, case counts, adoption of anything.

**188. Nested categories and the false "whereas".**

When several percentages appear in one sentence, add the small ones and compare to the large one. In entry 68: clinical decision-making 51.9%, "particularly" treatment and management 33.1%, "whereas" diagnosis 13.9%. Since 33.1 + 13.9 = 47.0 fits inside 51.9 with 4.9 points to spare, **treatment and diagnosis are both children of clinical decision-making** — the contrast is between two siblings.

The sentence structure invites reading 13.9% as small against 51.9%, which compares a part to its own whole. The honest comparison is **33.1 against 13.9, a 2.4-fold ratio.** Mis-reading a part as a peer of its parent makes small categories look smaller than they are, and is one of the most common ways a correctly computed percentage misleads.

**189. Registered, active, and people are three different denominators.**

Entry 68: 271 million queries / 952,008 accounts / 30 months = **9.5 queries per account-month**. The paper reports **39.5 per *active* user-month**. The 4.2-fold gap is not a contradiction — it *is* the inactivity rate, recoverable by division. Separately, 703,265 active in the final month is **73.9% of every account ever created**, which simultaneously indicates high retention and tells you most accounts are recent.

Whenever a paper quotes a per-user rate, ask which users: everyone who ever registered, everyone who did something this period, or everyone who exists in the world. These can differ by an order of magnitude, **and the most flattering one is the one most often quoted.**

**190. Composition checks: predict a reported distribution from a known mix.**

Entry 68 reports 58.5% female across a population that is 58.4% physicians and 41.6% nurse practitioners, physician assistants and others. Taking physicians as roughly 38-40% female and NP/PA as roughly 85%:

    0.40 * 0.584 + 0.85 * 0.416 = 0.587   ->  predicted 58.7%, reported 58.5%

**The check passes**, which is weak positive evidence the population is as described. Run this on any paper reporting a demographic breakdown alongside a composition: it is free, takes thirty seconds, and **when it fails it fails loudly.** Entry 68 is the first paper in this list where this check was run and passed cleanly.

**191. Survivorship in a rapidly growing cohort, and why the fix is cohort analysis.**

When a population grows 1,981-fold, the people present at the start have had 30 months to leave and those present at the end have had none. So any average over "all users" is dominated by recent arrivals, and any *trend* in per-user behaviour is confounded by who survived.

Entry 68's intensity rose 18.0 to 39.5 queries per active user-month. Three explanations fit equally: the product improved; users learned to lean on it more; **or the people who stayed are the ones who liked it.** A server log cannot distinguish a behaviour change *within* people from a composition change *between* people.

**The fix is cohort analysis** — track the January 2024 joiners separately from the January 2025 joiners and see whether each cohort's own intensity rises. Telemetry can do this trivially; abstracts rarely report it. When a paper reports a rising per-user average in a growing population and does not break it out by joining cohort, **treat the rise as unexplained rather than as improvement.**

**192. A definitional artifact masquerading as a finding: check that a measure means the same thing at both ends of its range.**

Entry 68 reports out-of-specialty queries from 35% (oncology/haematology) to 88% (emergency medicine). The 88% is the most eye-catching number and probably the least meaningful: **emergency medicine is the specialty of undifferentiated presentation**, so any topic map assigning "chest pain in a diabetic" to cardiology or endocrinology will label nearly everything an emergency physician asks as out-of-specialty. The number partly measures the taxonomy's inability to represent generalists.

The 35% at the oncology end is interpretable, because oncology has a genuinely bounded topic domain. **A between-group range is only interpretable if the measure is comparable at both ends.** Before believing a range, ask whether the construct even exists in the group at the extreme.

**193. Sponsor-as-author plus non-reproducible data: grade each number by its distance from the raw instrumentation.**

Entry 68 has three of seven authors employed by the company whose product is studied, and the data are proprietary server logs that nobody can re-run. This does not mean the paper is wrong. It means **the error-correction mechanism is absent**, so you weight findings by how hard they would be to get wrong.

**Hard to get wrong:** server-side counts of accounts and requests. They come from the same instrumentation the company bills and reports on, and are visible to engineers with no stake in the paper. Believe 952,008 and 271 million.

**Easy to get wrong:** model-generated interpretive categories. They require judgement calls about taxonomy and prompt, are invisible to anyone outside, and have no published accuracy. Hold 51.9% and 88% loosely.

**The general rule: trustworthiness tracks distance from the raw instrumentation, not the reputation of the journal.** This is the correct way to read any paper whose data cannot be independently obtained — rank the numbers by how many layers of judgement sit between the sensor and the sentence.

**Process note — the 21-day rule, and what it cost.** This list's standing rule has been: attempt full text daily; if a PMC record exists but serves an empty body for twenty-one consecutive days and no second route opens, decode abstract-only and state the reason explicitly. Entry 68 is the first time it fired. **It worked**, in the sense that nine verifiable arithmetic findings came out of a 350-word abstract — but the grade was capped at 2/5 and the single question that decides whether the paper's percentages mean anything (classifier accuracy) is unanswerable from here. **Abstract-only is a real loss, not a formality**, and the honest form of it is to say exactly which judgements are suspended and which way each would move.

**Shelf and deferred plan set on 2026-11-05.** Next run takes shelf spare (2), gestational-age estimation across two health systems (PMID 42957585, PMC13719529), which has a confirmed PMC body. **The BMJ reporting-completeness review (PMID 42869550) reaches its own day 21 on 2026-11-12** — if still without a PMC body then, decode abstract-only under the same rule; its abstract is unusually rich and it is the most relevant paper on the shelf to this list's purpose. The Nature personal-health-agent paper (42907512) and the *Annals* *Choosing Wisely* benchmark (42896192) move to **weekly** re-checks, next 2026-11-12, with 42896192's own abstract-only date set at 2026-11-19.

## From entry 69 — Bejan et al., machine-learning gestational-age estimation across two health systems (Pharmacoepidemiol Drug Saf, 2026; decoded 2026-11-06, FULL TEXT)

Back to full text after yesterday's abstract-only entry, and a useful contrast: entry 68 had 271 million observations and almost nothing checkable; this one has 88,689 and reconciles to the last digit. **The first three techniques here are the most transferable material this list has added in a fortnight.**

**194. Reproducibility of a pipeline, portability of a workflow, and transportability of a model are three different claims.**

When a paper involves a second site, there are three distinct things it might have shown.

**Transportability of a fitted model** — the *trained* model, with its learned split points, run unchanged on the second site's patients. Asks: *do the learned relationships still hold elsewhere?* Hardest test, most clinically relevant, because it is what happens when a hospital installs software.

**Reproducibility of a development process** — a different team takes the *method description* and rebuilds it, training on their own patients. Asks: *is the recipe followable, and does the approach work on another population?* Says nothing about whether the original fitted model travels, because the model is refit from scratch.

**Portability of an analytic workflow** — the narrowest: does the code or procedure *run* at all in another institution's schema and vocabulary?

Entry 69 did the second and third, and says so in exactly those words: "because the machine learning models were retrained at UMich, this analysis evaluated cross-site reproducibility of the development process and portability of the analytic workflow, rather than transportability of the VUMC-fitted models."

**Why the distinction decides what you may conclude.** Technique 138/139 (entry 55) says an external result beating the internal one is a red flag — a fitted model doing better on unseen data usually means leakage or an easier case mix. Here Michigan (MAE 2.8 days) beats Vanderbilt (4.4 days), and **it is not that red flag**, precisely because the model was refit: Michigan's number is a fresh internal result on a different population. **Read "the second site did better" as a red flag only when the same fitted model was used at both.** Get this backwards and you will either dismiss a sound paper or trust an unsound one.

**The analogy.** Transportability is handing someone your sourdough starter and seeing whether their loaf rises. Reproducibility of the process is handing over the written recipe and seeing whether they can bake well with their own starter, flour and oven. Portability is whether the instructions even parse in their kitchen.

**Watch out for:** a paper describing site-specific retraining in the Methods and then writing "externally validated" or "validated in an independent cohort" in the abstract. The tell is one Methods sentence about retraining or refitting. If it is there, **no fitted model was tested anywhere**, and a reader planning to deploy has learned nothing.

**195. Minimising MAE on a lopsided outcome pulls predictions toward the mode, so the rare tail fails by construction.**

Entry 69's outcome: median **274 days**, IQR **265-280** — half of all pregnancies inside a 15-day window; full and early term together **77.2%** of the cohort; preterm **14.3%**, with the range reaching down to 127 days.

Now ask what an error-minimising model does with an uncertain case. The honest answer is "I don't know." The *loss-minimising* answer is **274**, because that is where the mass sits. Guessing near the mode is nearly free: the penalty for being wrong about a rare case is small in the average, and the reward for being right about the common case is large.

    Suppose 85% of pregnancies are 260-290 days and 15% are 200-259.
    Guess 274  -> small error 85% of the time, large error 15% of the time.
    Guess 250  -> moderate error almost always.
    Mean absolute error is LOWER for the first strategy.

So predictions compress toward the centre: too high for short pregnancies, too low for long ones. **That is regression toward the mean, and it is not an algorithm bug — it is the loss function doing what it was asked.**

**How to spot it in any paper:** find the predicted-versus-actual scatterplot and check whether the cloud's slope is visibly **flatter than the 45-degree line**. If it is, the tails are being shrunk inward. Note that correlation is blind to this: entry 69's Pearson r of 0.86-0.95 is fully compatible with heavy compression, because r measures ordering, not magnitude.

**Watch out for:** reporting only an overall MAE or R² on a skewed outcome and then being surprised the clinically important tail fails. **If your decision depends on the tail, the tail is your outcome** — and the fix is to change the loss (upweight rare cases, or model the tail separately), not to collect more of the same data. Entry 69 does the honest interim thing: reports the stratum and warns you off it.

**196. Bootstrap confidence intervals: uncertainty for a statistic with no formula.**

Entry 69 puts a 95% interval on every metric in its results table, by **resampling the test set 1,000 times with replacement, recomputing the metric each time, and taking the 2.5th and 97.5th percentiles.**

**Why no formula would do.** "Proportion within 7 days" is a proportion, so the binomial formula is tempting — but it treats the model as fixed and sampling as the only uncertainty, and it offers nothing at all for **MAE**, a mean of absolute differences with no standard textbook interval. The bootstrap covers both without needing a formula for either.

**The intuition.** Your test set is one draw from a population and you cannot get another. So simulate one: draw the same number of cases *from your own test set, with replacement*, so some appear twice and some not at all. That is a plausible alternative test set. Compute the metric. Repeat 1,000 times; the middle 95% of the results is the interval.

    10 patients, 8 within a week = 80%.
    Resample: maybe 7 of the 8 successes and 3 draws of the 2 failures -> 70%.
    Again -> 90%. Again -> 80%. After 1,000 rounds, read off the 2.5th and 97.5th percentiles.
    At n=10 the spread is enormous; at n=10,869 entry 69's interval is 84.4-85.8%, ~1.4 points wide.

**What the intervals bought here.** Ensemble 85.1% (84.4-85.8) against gradient boosting 84.0% (83.3-84.6): barely overlapping, so the edge is probably real and small. Either against the median baseline 51.7% (50.8-52.6): nowhere close. **The intervals make the comparison that matters unambiguous.**

**Watch out for two things.** First, **the bootstrap quantifies sampling variability only.** It says nothing about a wrong reference standard, an unrepresentative population, or a predictor imputed into uselessness. Technique 186 made this point at n = 38.8 million; it holds at 10,869. **Entry 69's intervals are honest, narrow, and address none of its real limitations.** Second, **resampling does not make the test set bigger** — the interval is conditional on the one dataset you have, and an unrepresentative dataset yields a tight interval around the wrong answer.

**197. Always report what guessing would have achieved.**

Entry 69's best single reporting choice. Constant median prediction: **51.7%** within a week, MAE **11.4 days**. Ensemble: **85.1%** and **4.4 days** — **+33.4 percentage points, 61% error reduction.** Without the baseline, "85% within one week" is unreadable, because an outcome with a 15-day interquartile range can be predicted decently by a ruler. **When a paper reports accuracy on a narrow or imbalanced outcome with no constant-prediction comparator, compute it yourself from the reported distribution before believing the headline.**

**198. Proportion-within-tolerance beats correlation for a continuous prediction used at a threshold.**

Entry 69's Pearson r values (0.86-0.95) are its least informative numbers: r measures *ordering*, is inflated by a wide outcome spread, and is blind to systematic compression — a model predicting exactly half the true deviation from the mean every time still shows near-perfect r. The ±1 week and ±2 week bands answer the user's actual question: **how often is this close enough to act on?** The paper leads with the bands and relegates r to a figure caption, which is the right ordering. This is Bland-Altman logic in a different costume: **correlation is not agreement.**

**199. Median imputation when most values are missing turns a predictor into a constant — exactly where it mattered.**

Entry 69: **birth weight available for 25% of pregnancies**, 5-minute APGAR for 42%, both median-imputed. Since median gestational age is 274 days, the imputed birth weight is a **full-term** weight — so the single most informative signal about prematurity is replaced by a term-looking constant in three cases out of four. **Before accepting median imputation, ask whether the value is missing at random or missing for the same reason it would have been informative.** The paper's own proposed fix is correct: multiple imputation from other predictors available at prediction time, excluding the outcome.

**200. A feature-extraction window derived from the outcome's average is biased at the tails.**

Counting diagnosis codes "during pregnancy" requires knowing when pregnancy began — which requires the gestational age being predicted. Using the dyad's own value is leakage, so entry 69 correctly uses the **training-set median (274 days)** for every dyad. But a 274-day window on a 210-day pregnancy reaches about **64 days before conception**, importing unrelated diagnoses; on a 295-day pregnancy it misses three weeks. **The careful anti-leakage choice introduces a tail-dependent feature error.** When a lookback window is defined by a population average of the target, the window is wrong by roughly the amount your prediction error will be — **check the tails.**

Note that this and technique 199 are **two independent mechanisms both degrading the preterm stratum**, and entry 69 reports the preterm failure and the imputation separately without connecting them. Reading the Methods for mechanisms behind a reported subgroup failure is often more productive than reading the Discussion.

**201. When the reference standard is itself an estimate, "accuracy" means agreement — and sometimes agreement is the right target.**

Entry 69's outcome is "the birth attendant's final assessment," and the paper states that **the underlying clinical dating method could not be determined at either institution.** The usual reading is that this caps the claim: MAE 4.4 days is distance from a note, not from truth.

**But the purpose decides.** The tool exists to fill blank gestational-age fields so EHR research can proceed, so what you want to recover is *what would have been recorded*. Against that goal, agreement with the clinical estimate is not a compromise — it is the specification. **Ask what the number will be used for before deciding whether a soft reference standard is a flaw or the requirement.** Entry 69 never makes this argument for itself; it is the strongest defence of its own design.

**202. Race as a predictor without subgroup performance evaluation.**

Entry 69 used maternal and infant race and ethnicity as inputs, and reported mean gestational age highest among Asian mothers and lowest among Black mothers (**271.7 vs 267.2 days** at VUMC; **273.0 vs 268.1** at UMich — 4.5 and 4.9 days, both of which reproduce exactly). That is a documented disparity driven by social and structural factors, not biology, so **feeding it in encodes a social pattern into something that reads as a clinical measurement.**

The magnitude offers partial reassurance: 4.5 days is about the size of the model's whole mean error, so race can only be doing modest work in any single prediction. **"Modest" is not "none", and the only way to know which way it cuts is a per-group error table** — which the paper acknowledges, in plain words, that it did not produce. **Any model with race as an input owes its readers performance by group.**

**203. A coding-system transition is a free natural experiment in data drift — and a confounder for site comparisons.**

The US ICD-9-CM to ICD-10-CM switch on **1 October 2015** gives a clean date on which the meaning of every diagnosis-derived feature changed. Entry 69 exploits it by prespecifying a before/after stratification, and finds better performance afterwards — a concrete demonstration of temporal drift in EHR features.

**But it also confounds the site comparison.** VUMC spans 2005-2025 (~52% of its calendar span in the ICD-9 era); UMich spans 2012-2024 (~29%). So the **4.4-versus-2.8-day gap is substantially a calendar-era effect, not a site or implementation effect** — and the paper says as much, attributing UMich's edge to "reduced reliance on ICD-9-CM predictors" and adding that earlier years also had noisier reference standards from "more limited ultrasound technology." **When two cohorts cover different calendar spans and the features are administrative codes, era is a confounder before site is.** The fix is to compare within a common window. The per-stratum numbers here are in a supporting table, so the effect size cannot be checked from the retrieved text.

**Reporting-quality note.** Entry 69 is the **third paper in this list to name TRIPOD+AI and the first to deposit the completed checklist** as supporting information. It also gives two IRB approval numbers, grant numbers for every funded author, and itemised industry disclosures held separately from its no-conflicts statement. Against that: **no code or data availability statement appears anywhere** — not "unavailable", simply unaddressed — in a paper whose thesis is reproducibility. That juxtaposition is the sharpest thing in it, and it is the exact behaviour the deferred BMJ reporting-completeness review is built to measure.

**Plan set on 2026-11-06.** Next run takes shelf spare (3), bipolar-disorder classification from speech and language (PMID 42967232, PMC13723951): **6,373 acoustic and linguistic features on 160 people.** After today's entry that is a deliberate contrast — 40 features per participant against entry 69's handful across 88,689 dyads — and the natural place to teach the curse of dimensionality, feature selection inside versus outside the cross-validation loop (techniques 123 and the nested-CV discussion from entry 63), and why a constant baseline matters even more when p >> n. **The BMJ review (PMID 42869550) reaches day 21 on 2026-11-12: decode abstract-only then if its PMC body is still absent.** Nature 42907512 and Annals 42896192 stay on weekly re-checks, next 2026-11-12, with 42896192's abstract-only date at 2026-11-19.

## From entry 70 — Yamaguchi et al., bipolar classification from speech and language (J Med Internet Res, 2026; decoded 2026-11-07, FULL TEXT)

**The most self-critical paper in this list.** 3,684 candidate features on 61 patients — 60 features per patient — and a limitations section that pre-empts nearly every criticism, including the two that destroy its own headline. Graded 3/5 because the grade tracks what a reader can conclude, not how well the authors behaved; the authors behaved extremely well. **The first three techniques below are the load-bearing ones, and 204 is the single most reusable piece of arithmetic added to this list.**

**204. A null result means nothing until you compute the minimum detectable effect. Here is how.**

Entry 70's three interstate comparisons returned p = .03, .20 and .30. The two nulls will be cited as "voice cannot distinguish manic from depressive states." They do not show that.

Before believing any null, ask how big an effect the study could have caught 80% of the time. Effects below that floor were invisible, so finding nothing says nothing about them.
