The paper's own power framework converts an AUC to Cohen's d and runs a two-sample t test, so invert it. For 80% power at two-sided alpha = .05, the required noncentrality is about **delta = 2.80**, and

    delta = d * sqrt(n1*n2/(n1+n2))

so **d_min = 2.80 / sqrt(n1*n2/N)**, and convert back with the relation the paper itself uses, **AUC = Phi(d/sqrt(2))**.

    DS vs ES      n=23v17: sqrt(391/40)=3.126 -> d_min=0.896 -> AUC_min=0.737; observed 0.760 (p=.03) CLEARED
    MS/MX vs ES   n=15v21: sqrt(315/36)=2.958 -> d_min=0.947 -> AUC_min=0.748; observed 0.601 (p=.20) BELOW
    MS/MX vs DS   n=15v27: sqrt(405/42)=3.105 -> d_min=0.902 -> AUC_min=0.738; observed 0.569 (p=.30) BELOW

**All three were blind to anything under roughly AUC 0.74.** A clinically interesting AUC of 0.70 would have been missed most of the time in all three. The two nulls are **uninformative, not negative** — and DS vs ES only just cleared the floor, which is why its p is .03 rather than anything smaller.

**The analogy.** You weigh yourself before and after a diet on a scale marked only in 5-kilogram steps, and the needle does not move. You have not learned you lost no weight; you have learned you lost less than 5 kilograms. **A sample size is an instrument's resolution.**

**Watch out for:** "no significant difference" presented as equivalence. Those are different claims, and the second needs an equivalence or non-inferiority design with a prespecified margin. The honest form of a small-sample null is "we could not detect a difference smaller than X, and we did not detect one." **Entry 70 writes its own version: "nonsignificant results indicate that an effect was undetected rather than absent."** Hold every paper to that sentence, and run the four lines of arithmetic above whenever one does not.

**205. Permutation testing as a multiplicity correction: why it beats Bonferroni when features are correlated.**

Entry 70: **3,684 candidate features, 61 patients.** At an uncorrected p<.05 you would expect ~184 "discoveries" from noise alone.

**Why not Bonferroni.** .05/3684 = **0.0000136**, brutally strict — and it assumes independent tests. These features are nothing like independent: openSMILE builds 6,373 features by applying a few dozen summary functions to a few dozen base signals, so MFCC 8's mean, variance, flatness and rising-slope are four views of one thing. With heavily correlated tests Bonferroni is far too conservative: you discard real findings to control an error rate that was never that high.

**What permutation does instead.** Shuffle the labels so any real association dies but **the correlation structure among features survives untouched.** Re-run the whole selection procedure. Record how often each feature is selected. Repeat. You now have an empirical null — "how often does this get picked when labels are meaningless?" — that **inherits your data's real dependence structure for free.**

    20 coins, best one gets 9 heads in 10 flips. Impressive?
    Bonferroni: compute p for 9-of-10, divide alpha by 20.
    Permutation: simulate 20 FAIR coins x 10 flips, record the BEST, repeat 1,000x,
                 see how often the best of 20 fair coins reaches 9. (~1 in 6 runs.)
    => 9-of-10 as the winner of 20 is unremarkable. The permutation answered the
       question actually asked - "is the best of my set surprising?"

Entry 70 does this **twice**: for feature selection (95th percentile of the permutation null of selection frequency) and for model performance (100 label shuffles). The model-level test is what makes p = .03/.20/.30 meaningful at all, and labels were shuffled **at the participant level** — the right unit, since each participant contributes one aggregated point. Shuffling at the observation level would leak and give an over-optimistic null.

**Watch out for the gap between per-feature and family-wise.** A 95th-percentile threshold on each feature's *own* null controls the per-feature rate at 5% — so across 3,684 features you still expect **~184 false positives.** Family-wise control instead takes the 95th percentile of the **maximum** statistic across all features per shuffle, a far higher bar. This matters concretely in entry 70: it reports **178, 121, 108 and 125** significant features against a ~184 per-feature chance expectation, so if the null were per-feature those counts are consistent with noise *at the feature level* even though the model-level test proves real signal exists. The paper says "family-wise error correction" in one sentence (implying the max-statistic) and "permutation-based thresholding... to account for multiple testing" in another; the definition is in supplementary section 7, not in the retrieved text. **When you read a permutation correction, find out which null it is.**

**206. Comparing feature modalities fairly: match the search space, or use a nested comparison.**

Entry 70 ran acoustic-only (**3,473 features, AUC 0.910**), linguistic-only (**211 features, AUC 0.651**) and combined (**3,684, AUC 0.911**).

**The trap.** The acoustic model searched a set **16x larger.** More chances to fit means more apparent performance, some real and some sample noise. So the 0.910-vs-0.651 gap **cannot be cleanly attributed to the modality.** The paper flags this itself.

**The escape.** Compare **combined (0.911) against acoustic-only (0.910).** The combined set *contains* the acoustic set, so this is nested: adding features can only expand what the model can fit. If adding all 211 linguistic features moves the AUC by **0.001**, they genuinely carry no information beyond the acoustic ones. **Airtight, and wholly independent of dimensionality.** The paper: combined-vs-acoustic "is a nested comparison and is unaffected by this asymmetry."

**The analogy.** Two candidates, one interviewed three hours and one ten minutes, and the first seems better — you have learned you had more chances to find something good, not that they are better. **That is the unmatched comparison.** Now: you have already hired the first, and you ask whether adding the second improves output. If it does not, the second adds nothing *given* the first. **That is the nested comparison, and it answers a question you can answer.**

**The fix for the unmatched version**, if you need it: give each modality the same number of candidates by random subsampling the larger set, repeated many times; or compare each against its own permutation null (entry 70 reports both modalities beat chance at p<=.02).

**A corollary worth keeping.** Entry 70's "acoustic features were 96-98% of the significant features" is near-vacuous, because **94.27% of the candidates were acoustic before any analysis ran.** My binomial tests on the enrichment: BP z=+2.00 (p=.046), DS z=+1.54 (p=.124), MS/MX z=+0.91 (p=.365), ES z=+1.99 (p=.047) — two marginal, two null. **The conclusion survives only via the nested route.** Generally: **before reading anything into the composition of a selected set, compute its composition under the candidate pool's base rate.**

**207. When an asymptomatic group classifies better than a symptomatic one, suspect the treatment.**

Entry 70's euthymic group (minimal symptoms, HAMD-17 <=7 and YMRS <=7) hit **AUC 0.874** against the depressed group's **0.805**. Read charitably: a stable trait marker, detectable in remission — the paper's most publishable claim.

Then check the obvious rival. **72.1% (44/61) of patients were on antipsychotics; zero controls were on anything.** The ES model's diagnostic probability was higher in antipsychotic users at **Cohen d = 1.17 (p=.005)**, and the primary marker tracked total medication burden even after adjusting for HAMD-17 (**partial rho = -0.45, p=.02**). Antipsychotics affect arousal, psychomotor activity and articulation — exactly what MFCCs measure.

**Whenever a model performs best in the group with the LEAST disease activity, ask what else is uniform in that group and absent from the controls:** medication, care setting, time spent in clinic, the recording environment, who did the interview. Entry 70 ran this check and **printed the answer against its own interest, in its conclusion.** Also note the operating-point cost: ES specificity was the lowest of the four comparisons at 0.711, so **28 of 97 healthy controls were flagged** to reach sensitivity 0.962.

**208. Feature-selection stability, and what a Jaccard index of 0.48 means concretely.**

Entry 70 reports a mean of **168 (SD 119)** features selected per resample, mean pairwise **Jaccard 0.48**, Nogueira 0.56. Jaccard is intersection over union, so for two sets of ~168:

    I / (2*168 - I) = 0.48  ->  I = 0.48*336/1.48 = 109

So two resamples agree on about **109 of 168** features and each picks ~59 the other missed — consistent with the paper's "116 selected in at least half of resamples." For the mood-state comparisons Jaccard falls to **0.18-0.29**: the selected sets barely overlap.

**An SD of 119 on a mean of 168 is itself the warning.** The right response is the one the paper takes: base feature-level inference on **selection frequency and coefficient-sign direction**, never on one fit's list. **Never print a top-ten feature table from a single model fit** — and when a paper does, ask what its selection stability was.

**209. A sensitivity below 0.5 is a specific, reportable finding.** Entry 70's MS/MX vs DS: sensitivity **0.467 (7/15)**, accuracy 0.548, AUC 0.569. Below a coin flip for the positive class. Not an error — what weak discrimination plus an unlucky threshold produces, with a huge CI at n=15. **Report it plainly rather than retreating to the AUC**, which entry 70 does. Note the framing: the paper's own introduction says treatment "differ[s] markedly between depressive and manic or hypomanic phases," so **the study's own case makes this the decision that matters, and this is the comparison that fails.**

**210. AUC -> Cohen's d for an a priori power analysis: good practice, two caveats.** Entry 70's chain — prior AUCs 0.71-0.76, Rice & Harris conversion, d = 0.78-1.0, G*Power t test, 17-27 per group — is rare and welcome, and the paper names which comparisons failed it. Caveats: **the conversion assumes both groups' scores are normal with equal variance**, so skew distorts the implied d; and **a t-test power analysis only approximates AUC power** — Obuchowski or Hanley-McNeil variance formulas are the proper tools. Neither undermines the exercise here, because the conclusion (interstate subgroups underpowered) is nowhere near the line.

**211. Several comparisons sharing one control group are not independent results.** Entry 70's four HC comparisons drew on the same ~97 controls, and **19 patients appear in more than one mood-state group.** So the four AUCs are correlated, and "four out of four beat chance" is not four independent confirmations. **Observations are independent WITHIN each comparison — what the model needs — but the comparisons are not independent of EACH OTHER, which is what a reader counting successes needs.** Entry 70 flags this explicitly.

**212. A threshold chosen on the data you report is optimistic.** Entry 70's sensitivity/specificity pairs come from the **Youden-index cut-point** (max of sensitivity + specificity - 1) selected on the pooled out-of-fold probabilities — tuned on the same numbers being reported. The **AUC is unaffected** (it averages over all thresholds); the pairs are not. The paper flags this and supplies **0.5-threshold metrics** in its supplement, which is the correct remedy. **When a paper gives only Youden-point metrics with no prespecified-threshold alternative, treat the pair as a best case.**

**213. "Not significant" and "no effect" are different claims.** Technique 204 is the arithmetic; this is the principle, and entry 70 supplies the best phrasing in this list: **"nonsignificant results indicate that an effect was undetected rather than absent."** It appears in the medication analysis, where subgroups of 20-60 patients returned nulls. Absence of evidence is not evidence of absence, and this is the most common error in reading clinical research.

**Reporting note, and a contrast with entry 69.** Entry 70 names and deposits **STROBE** — correct for an observational study — but **does not name TRIPOD+AI or TRIPOD-LLM**, despite developing and evaluating a diagnostic prediction model, which is TRIPOD+AI's exact scope. Entry 69 named TRIPOD+AI and deposited the checklist. **Entry 70 is in most respects the more rigorous study and is held to the less relevant standard.** That mismatch — between checklist adherence and actual methodological care — is precisely what the deferred BMJ reporting-completeness review (PMID 42869550) quantifies, and it found mean TRIPOD-LLM completeness of 40%. The two entries together are the argument for reading methods rather than counting checklists.

**Extraction note.** No funding, competing-interests or data-availability statement appeared anywhere in the retrieved PMC text. JMIR requires all three, so this is almost certainly an **extraction gap rather than an omission by the authors** — recorded here so the distinction is not lost later.

**Plan set on 2026-11-07.** Next run takes shelf spare (4), **NOTIFY-DISCUSS** (PMID 42965008): patient and clinician perceptions of an AI screening tool **actually deployed in clinical use**. It will be the **first qualitative study in this list**, and after seventy quantitative entries that is a deliberate change of instrument — the natural place to teach what qualitative evidence can and cannot establish, how saturation and thematic analysis work, why reflexivity and an audit trail are the qualitative analogues of preregistration and a seed, and above all why **"n=20" means something entirely different in a qualitative design than it did in today's interstate comparisons**. **The BMJ review reaches day 21 on 2026-11-12: decode abstract-only then if its PMC body is still absent.** Nature 42907512 and Annals 42896192 stay on weekly re-checks, next 2026-11-12, with 42896192's abstract-only date at 2026-11-19.

## From entry 71 — Keamy-Minor et al., NOTIFY-DISCUSS (Am Heart J Plus, 2026; decoded 2026-11-08, FULL TEXT)

**The first qualitative study in this list**, after seventy quantitative entries, and about an AI tool **already in clinical use**. None of the AUC, calibration, power or permutation machinery applies. **Techniques 214-216 are the load-bearing ones and together they are how to read qualitative evidence at all.**

**214. What qualitative evidence can and cannot establish: the existence proof.**

The instinct trained by seventy quantitative papers is "twenty people — what can that show?" The answer is a different kind of thing, unobtainable any other way.

Quantitative work answers **how often** and **how much**, and needs a sample whose proportion resembles the population's. Qualitative work answers **what is going on, in whose words, and what did nobody anticipate**, and needs a sample deep enough that people say things they had not planned to say.

**So the logical form of a qualitative finding is EXISTENCE, not frequency.** Entry 71's most valuable finding is that patients raised **discrimination in health insurance, employment and admission to assisted living** on the basis of an incidental finding. That claim needs no denominator. **One person is enough to establish that a harm pathway is real**, and a system deploying the tool must now decide deliberately what goes into the chart and who can see it — whether it was 1 patient in 11 or 11 in 11.

**The analogy.** To know whether a bridge is safe: a survey of a thousand drivers' confidence tells you about confidence; **one engineer finding one cracked weld tells you the bridge has a cracked weld**, and no amount of survey confidence rebuts it. Now flip it — that engineer cannot tell you what **fraction** of welds are cracked. **Two instruments, two questions, each nearly useless for the other's job.**

What entry 71 CAN support: that seven named concerns exist among people with first-hand experience; that patients and clinicians hold **conflicting** preferences about who delivers results; that years of delay produce confusion and distrust; that insurance discrimination occurred to patients unprompted. **Every one is a design requirement and none needs a percentage.**

What it CANNOT support: that most patients approve; that concerns are rare; that either group worries more than the other. **All frequency claims — and the paper makes versions of the first two, in its abstract.**

**Watch out for** a qualitative abstract that reads like a survey (the tell is most/many/some with no counts) — and for the mirror error, which is more common and worse: **dismissing a qualitative finding because the sample is small.** If a finding's form is existence, sample size is nearly irrelevant to its validity.

**215. Counting in qualitative research, and CQR's general/typical/variant convention.**

The tension: qualitative work is not about frequencies, yet readers need to know whether a theme came from nine people or one, because that changes what you do. Both instincts are right, and the traditions have an answer.

**Consensual Qualitative Research — which entry 71 names in its own Methods — resolves it with counted frequency labels.** As conventionally formulated, each theme is tagged by case count:

    general  = all cases, or all but one
    typical  = more than half
    variant  = two or three cases, up to half

Applied to entry 71's 20 participants that machinery yields something precise:

    general (patients)    = 10-11 of 11      typical (patients)    = 6-9 of 11
    general (clinicians)  =  8-9 of  9       typical (clinicians)  = 5-7 of  9

**That is the whole fix and it costs nothing.** "Concern about consent was *variant* among patients (3 of 11)" is actionable; "some patients felt strongly that consent should be obtained" is not, because *some* spans 2 to 10 of 11. Entry 71 says *"For multiple clinicians, their initial response... was exasperation"* — **"multiple" could be 2 or 8, which are different decisions**: one you note, the other you redesign the workflow for before launch.

**The analogy.** Comment cards say "several diners mentioned the room was cold." Two of two hundred, or two of five? **One is noise, one is a broken thermostat, and "several" cannot distinguish them.** Counting doesn't turn comment cards into a temperature survey — it tells the manager whether to check the thermostat.

**Watch out for** a paper that names a specific qualitative methodology and then skips its characteristic reporting devices. CQR means consensus coding by multiple judges, an auditor, **and** counted frequency labels. Entry 71 has the auditor, a thinner consensus, and no labels. **A named methodology is a claim about procedure — check whether its machinery actually ran.**

**216. Lead time bias: why a sincere "it saved my life" cannot establish that screening saves lives.**

A patient in entry 71 compared her incidental coronary-calcium finding to the mammogram that caught her early breast cancer; several said versions of *"I wouldn't have known until probably it was too late."* Honest statements that feel like evidence. **They are not, and this is the most important idea in all of screening.**

    Take a disease that will kill a person at age 70, and suppose treatment changes NOTHING.
      Found by symptoms at 65  -> survival from diagnosis = 5 years
      Found by screening  at 60  -> survival from diagnosis = 10 years
    Survival from diagnosis DOUBLED. Age at death did not move by one day.

The extra five years is **lead time**: time spent *knowing about* the disease, not time *gained*. **Every screening programme generates it automatically, whether or not the treatment works** — find things earlier and survival-from-diagnosis always rises, so that number can never evidence that finding earlier helped.

It is worse than neutral: those five years are five years of medication, follow-up scans, insurance questions and the ordinary weight of carrying a diagnosis. **Lead time has a cost even when it has no benefit.**

**The only measure immune to it** is **deaths** compared between a screened and an unscreened group over the **same calendar window** — mortality in a population over a fixed period, not survival from diagnosis. That design cannot be fooled by moving the diagnosis date, because the date of death does not care when you learned.

Entry 71's own limitations name it: "prospective studies linking incidental AI-CAC to mortality outcomes are lacking. Further work is needed to assess whether such programs contribute to lead time bias." **A qualitative paper naming lead time bias in its own limitations is unusual and is the best single quality signal in it.** The honest asymmetry: statins for coronary calcium have a real evidence base, so the inference here is far more plausible than for many screening programmes. **Plausible is not demonstrated.**

**Watch out for** five-year survival, median survival from diagnosis, or "caught early" testimony offered as evidence for any screening programme. **All three move when you shift the diagnosis date and change nothing else. Ask for mortality, over a fixed window, against an unscreened comparison.**

**217. Response rate matters differently in qualitative work — but it still matters.** Entry 71 reached **11/129 patients (8.5%)** and **9/63 clinicians (14.3%)** — **20/192, 10.4%.** For a survey estimating a proportion, 8.5% is fatal. **For a qualitative study the themes survive**, because a concern voiced by one person still exists; **the frequency claims do not**, so the damage lands exactly on the most/many/some sentences. Note the awkward feature the paper identifies correctly: participants "may have had strong opinions to share," and **self-selection on strength of opinion inflates praise and complaint alike** — it does not bias in a direction you can correct for.

**218. Single coder, double coder, and the audit trail.** The strongest designs have **two or more analysts code independently**, compare, report agreement, then reconcile by consensus. Entry 71 describes **one coder (CB) with review by one expert (CBJ)** and no agreement statistic — while listing "multiple team perspectives on transcript coding" among its own CQR principles. A reviewer catches errors but **cannot establish that the coding scheme was reproducible**, because reproducibility means two people reaching it separately. **Ask of any qualitative paper: how many coded, independently or sequentially, and was any agreement figure reported?**

**219. Reflexivity, and the specific question of who builds the codebook.** Reflexivity is the qualitative counterpart of preregistration: the analyst is the instrument, so the analyst's position must be declared. The concrete version is **who defined the categories.** Entry 71's codebook was built "in conversation with the project leadership and implementation team (FR, RD)" — **authors of the NOTIFY-1 intervention being evaluated**, FR being this paper's senior author. **The rule: when the people who built a thing help define the categories through which reactions to it are read, the categories are not independent of the thing.** The mitigation is not to exclude them (they hold the context) but to state it as a limitation and describe the guard. Entry 71 states it in the Methods — to its credit — and **does not list it among the limitations.**

**220. COREQ and SRQR exist, and their absence is informative.** COREQ is a **32-item** checklist for interview and focus-group studies (research team and reflexivity; study design; analysis and reporting); SRQR is the broader alternative. **Entry 71 names neither.** A named checklist is not a quality signal — see the sequence note below — **but an unnamed one tells you to check the items yourself**, and here the open items are exactly coder count, agreement, saturation, participant characteristics and reflexivity.

**221. Outcome-dependent recall.** Entry 71: patients who received a new statin prescription "appeared to have particularly positive views." **Evaluations collected after an action, from people who took it, are conditioned on having taken it** — via commitment to the decision, the reasons constructed to justify it, and simply having had a better experience. The fix is **deliberate sampling across outcomes**: people who declined the statin, never opened the portal message, or were annoyed. **Inviting everyone and getting the responders is not sampling across outcomes**, which is why the positivity here is uninterpretable as approval.

**222. An existence proof needs no denominator — and the corollary for harms.** Technique 214 gives the form; the corollary deserves its own number. **For a potential harm, the existence proof is usually enough to act on.** One patient in eleven raising insurance discrimination settles that chart contents and access must be designed deliberately; you do not need the prevalence first. **Prevalence governs how much to spend on a fix. Existence governs whether to think about it at all.** Most harms in deployed systems are found by someone saying something nobody thought to ask about.

**223. Separate a behavioural outcome from a perceptual one — they can move in opposite directions.** Entry 71's notification delay (scans a median of >2 years old) **damaged the experience badly** — confusion, distrust of the old result, a requested repeat scan, clinician liability worry — and, per the pooled analysis of NOTIFY-1 and NOTIFY-PICTURE that the paper cites, **cost nothing in effect size**: timely and delayed notification raised statin prescribing equally. **Both true, and a health system must attend to both**, because an intervention that works while eroding trust is spending down an asset absent from its outcome measure. **Whenever a paper says something "worked," ask what it cost in the experience of the people it worked on** — and note you generally need a qualitative study to find out.

**Reporting-guideline sequence note — three consecutive entries, and the lesson.** Entry 69 (2026-11-06) named **TRIPOD+AI** and deposited the completed checklist. Entry 70 (2026-11-07) named **STROBE** and deposited it, while being arguably the most methodologically careful paper in this list. Entry 71 (today) names **no qualitative reporting guideline at all.** **Three levels of checklist adherence across three days, with the methodological care running in close to the opposite order.** That is precisely the gap the deferred BMJ review (PMID 42869550, mean TRIPOD-LLM completeness **40%**) quantifies — and these three entries are the worked argument both for having that number and for refusing to read it as a quality ranking. **Read the methods, not the checklist name.**

**Disclosure note, and a closed loop.** Entry 71 declares using **OpenEvidence** for aspects of its literature review and **Claude Sonnet 3.5** (enterprise, with data protections) for proofreading and structural suggestions. **OpenEvidence was entry 68 in this list, decoded three days earlier** — the study of 271 million clinician queries. So a paper on clinicians' and patients' experience of a clinical AI tool was written with the help of two clinical AI tools, and says so. Properly declared; also a marker of how fast this became ordinary. Separately, the senior author's COI spans **Novartis, Esperion, Amgen and Arrowhead** (lipid-lowering therapies — and the intervention increases statin prescribing) plus **HeartFlow and Cleerly Health** (coronary-imaging AI — the intervention's own product category). Fully disclosed, directly germane, worth the reader's attention.

**Plan set on 2026-11-08, with the shelf reordered.** A new landmark candidate was assessed and **shelved rather than decoded**: the *Nature Medicine* **gait foundation model** (PMID 42995231, Gabet A et al., Weizmann; 3,328 adults, 3D skeletal motion from a single depth camera, learned embeddings beating engineered gait features for age r=0.69, BMI r=0.91, visceral fat r=0.83, and **1,887 of 3,210 tested phenotypes called significant**). It is biomarker discovery with no clinical decision or deployment, so it did not displace today's first-qualitative-study pick — but **it goes to the front of the shelf**, because 3,210 tests with 1,887 significant is **the largest multiplicity problem this list will have faced**, and the natural extension of techniques 205 (permutation correction for correlated tests) and 186 (precision versus validity at large n). **The first question to ask of it: what false-discovery-rate procedure ran across 3,210 tests, and how many of the 1,887 would chance alone have produced?** **Next run takes the gait paper** if a full text is reachable; otherwise spare (5), the precision-psychiatry framework review. **The BMJ review reaches day 21 on 2026-11-12: decode abstract-only then if its PMC body is still absent.** Nature 42907512 and Annals 42896192 stay on weekly re-checks, next 2026-11-12, with 42896192's abstract-only date at 2026-11-19.

## From entry 72 — Serretti, AI-driven biomarker discovery for precision psychiatry (Clin Psychopharmacol Neurosci, 2026; decoded 2026-11-09, FULL TEXT)

**The first narrative review decoded here, and the paper closest to this list's own purpose.** Its thesis in one line: *"Producing candidates is becoming cheap while qualifying them is not."* **Technique 224 is a piece of arithmetic worth memorising**, and 225-226 are the two failure modes that account for most overclaiming in clinical prediction.

**224. Converting explained variance to AUC — and using it as an optimism detector.**

Entry 72 supplies a calibration figure it does not fully exploit: **reproducible treatment-response prediction in independent data typically explains about 5 to 15% of outcome variance**, and more than that in development data *"usually reflects optimism."* To use it you need to translate between variance and the AUCs papers actually report:

    r = sqrt(R^2)        d = 2r / sqrt(1 - r^2)        AUC = Phi(d / sqrt(2))

Forwards:

    R^2 =  5%  ->  AUC 0.627        R^2 = 20%  ->  AUC 0.760
    R^2 = 10%  ->  AUC 0.681        R^2 = 30%  ->  AUC 0.823
    R^2 = 15%  ->  AUC 0.724        R^2 = 40%  ->  AUC 0.876

**So the review's "reproducible" band is roughly AUC 0.63 to 0.73.** Backwards, on entry 72's own treatment-response numbers:

    AUC 0.650  antidepressant selection      -> R^2 ~  6.9%   INSIDE the band
    AUC 0.850  EEG treatment response pooled -> R^2 ~ 34.9%   (review says it overstates - agrees)
    AUC 0.851  schizophrenia multimodal EXT  -> R^2 ~ 35.1%   (review cites approvingly - UNRECONCILED)
    AUC 0.874  same, discovery               -> R^2 ~ 39.6%
    AUC 0.942  metabolic subtype-specific    -> R^2 ~ 55.3%

**That last line is the technique's whole value.** An AUC of 0.94 for a psychiatric outcome claims to explain **over half the variance** in who gets better — implausible on its face, and you reached that judgement from the reported number alone without reading the methods.

**The analogy.** Someone claims AUC 0.95 predicting height from shoe size: shoe size and height correlate at maybe r = 0.7, about 50% of variance, so it is in the right neighbourhood and you believe it. Now someone claims AUC 0.95 for which of two antidepressants will work better. **That implies a correlation with outcome stronger than shoe size with height, for a decision expert psychiatrists make barely better than chance.** The number is the claim, and the claim is extraordinary.

**Watch out for two things.** The conversion **assumes roughly normal, equal-variance distributions**, so it is a smell test, not an estimator. And **the 5-15% band is specifically for TREATMENT-RESPONSE prediction** — diagnosis against healthy controls can legitimately go much higher. **Always ask: variance in what?** Applying a treatment-response benchmark to a diagnostic AUC is the exact mistake technique 225 is about.

**225. The two substitutions, and what they cost in positive predictive value.**

Entry 72's central methodological finding: reported metrics routinely answer an easier question than the clinical one, in two specific ways.

**Substitution one: diagnosis for DIFFERENTIAL diagnosis.** The example, with a number attached. A schizophrenia classifier kept external **sensitivity 0.75** while **misclassifying 59% of bipolar and 55% of depression cases** — so against the conditions that actually present, its specificity is **0.41** and **0.45**, barely better than a coin.

    Illustrative clinic mix (MY assumption): 20% schizophrenia, 20% bipolar, 40% depression, 20% other
      true positives                    = 0.20 x 0.75 = 0.1500
      false positives from bipolar      = 0.20 x 0.59 = 0.1180
      false positives from depression   = 0.40 x 0.55 = 0.2200
      false positives from other (0.20) = 0.20 x 0.20 = 0.0400
      PPV = 0.1500 / 0.5280 = 28.4%   -> about 7 in 10 positive calls wrong

    Same sensitivity vs HEALTHY controls, specificity 0.85, same 20% prevalence:
      PPV = 55.6%

**The substitution roughly halves the positive predictive value.** The headline was measured on the easy comparison; the clinic supplies the hard one.

**Substitution two: prognosis for DIFFERENTIAL TREATMENT BENEFIT.** *"General outcome prediction identifies who is likely to improve. Treatment selection requires an estimate of how outcomes differ between options for the same patient."* Of **19** comparative antidepressant studies, only **7 (37%)** used external evaluation and **only 1 (5%)** directly ranked treatments.

**The analogy.** You want to know which of two routes to work is faster for you. A model predicting "you will arrive on time today" is a *prognosis* — mostly reading the weather and the hour. It can be highly accurate and tell you nothing about which route to take. **For that you need the difference between two predictions for the same person on the same day**, and accuracy on the first question never delivers the second.

**Watch out for** any AUC whose comparison group is healthy controls when the clinical decision involves distinguishing two diseases. **Ask what the model was asked to separate, and whether that is what you need separated.** Usually one line of the methods; rarely the abstract.

**226. Nonspecific prediction: a marker built without a control arm predicts recovery, not benefit.**

The structural version of 225, with the cleanest example the literature offers. **Anterior cingulate theta activity**, described in entry 72 as **among the most replicated** markers in psychiatry, predicts response to antidepressants — **and to rTMS, and to ECT, and to placebo.**

A marker predicting improvement under *everything, placebo included*, measures **a property of the patient's prognosis**, not of the match between patient and treatment. Useful for enriching a trial; useless for choosing a drug.

**And entry 72 names why this keeps happening:** *"Most treatment-response markers in psychiatry were defined in open-label single-arm data and never tested against a control arm, so their specificity to a treatment is unknown."* **If everyone in your development set got drug A, a marker correlating with improvement cannot be distinguished from a marker correlating with improving anyway. The design makes them indistinguishable — no sample size or model fixes it.**

**The analogy.** Students who sit at the front get better grades, so front-row seating helps? You only ever observed one class. You cannot tell whether the seat helps or whether the kind of student who sits at the front would have done better anywhere. **To find out you have to assign seats.** That is what a control arm is.

**Watch out for** the phrase "treatment-response biomarker" on a marker developed in single-arm or open-label data. **The term asserts a specificity the design could not have tested.**

**227. PPV and alert burden at a realistic base rate.** Entry 72's best example: a wearable study, **144 people over 90,281 person-days**, week-ahead depressive-episode AUC **0.772 (0.75-0.794)**, base rate **5.3%**, PPV **16.7%**, burden **20 alerts per 100 patient-days.** I verified all three are mutually consistent — solving PPV for the false-positive rate, `(1-spec) = sens * prev(1-PPV) / (PPV(1-prev))`, gives **20.6 alerts per 100 patient-days at sensitivity 0.65 and specificity 0.82**, reproducing the reported figure. **PPV 16.7% is one in six: five of every six alerts are false.** A 100-patient service absorbs ~20 alerts a day to catch about **3** real events. **An AUC in the high 0.7s sounds deployable; this is what it looks like in a clinic.** Always convert to alerts-per-day and true-alerts-per-day. Note entry 72 also catches the denominator trap: **144 people, not 90,281** — *"the repeated person-days do not constitute independent participants."*

**228. Predictive versus prognostic markers, and why you need marker-negative participants.** A **prognostic** marker says what will happen; a **predictive** marker says what will happen *differently* under treatment A versus B. Entry 72 grounds this in the **2019 FDA enrichment guidance** and draws the consequence exactly: **a trial conducted only in marker-positive participants can establish efficacy in that group but cannot show the marker is predictive rather than prognostic.** The reason is that evidence for prediction is an **interaction** between marker status and treatment, **and you cannot estimate an interaction from one level of a factor.** Entry 72's ANK3/liafensine example is the failure: placebo-controlled efficacy shown in carriers, **no result reported in non-carriers.** Technique 226 is the mechanism; this is the design consequence.

**229. Standalone model performance versus the model-user pair.** Entry 72's most striking relayed finding, from a preregistered randomised study: **1,298 members of the public, language models alone identified the relevant condition in 94.9% of general medical scenarios, and assisted participants did no better than controls.** **A 94.9% standalone score produced zero measured benefit once a human was in the loop.** Entry 72's conclusion is right: *"performance must be evaluated in the intended model-user pair."* Same lesson as technique 194 in a different register: **what you deploy is not a model, it is a model plus a person plus a workflow, and the only valid test is of the whole assembly.** Caveat entry 72 states itself — the public rather than clinicians, general medical rather than psychiatric scenarios. Indirect, and still the most actionable number in the paper.

**230. The six stages as a two-minute reading checklist.** **(1)** Which decision, which population, which comparator? **(2)** Is the measurement reliable, and does the label represent the clinical state? **(3)** Was the whole pipeline evaluated internally **against a simple baseline**? **(4)** Was a **locked** model tested under a shift in site, time, device or population? **(5)** Has **prospective** testing shown model-guided use improves decisions or outcomes? **(6)** Is deployment monitored for drift and subgroup fairness? Entry 72's own shortcut is two questions: **what was the model compared against, and does the reported metric answer the clinical question** — which catch most weak studies. One caution it supplies: *"development may return to earlier stages when the population, assay or model changes."* **A stage is a property of a claim, not a permanent rank** — and a single "readiness" number is exactly the kind of summary that gets pulled into a slide and compared across rows.

**231. Reporting standards, appraisal instruments and early-evaluation standards do three different jobs.** Entry 72 gets this right where almost nothing does. **TRIPOD+AI** says how a prediction-model study should be *reported* and *"does not prescribe model development or establish low risk of bias."* **PROBAST+AI** *assesses* quality, risk of bias and applicability. **DECIDE-AI** covers reporting of early-stage *live clinical* evaluation. **So a paper can follow TRIPOD+AI perfectly and still be a bad study**, because *"complete reporting enables appraisal, but does not by itself establish validity or utility."* Entries 69, 70 and 71 demonstrated precisely that across three consecutive days, with checklist adherence running counter to methodological care. **Never read a named checklist as a quality signal** — and note entry 72 is the first paper here to state the principle outright rather than illustrate it accidentally.

**232. Overall discrimination conceals subgroup error rates.** Entry 72's example: aggression prediction in acute psychiatric care, **17,703 patients over 42,719 observation-days**, overall AUC **0.81**, while **error rates differed by race or ethnicity, gender, admission mode, citizenship and housing.** An AUC is an average over everyone, and averages hide where errors concentrate. **In a model whose false positives trigger restriction or restraint, which group absorbs the false positives is the whole ethical question.** Demand subgroup performance, subgroup calibration, and what a false decision costs in each group — the same demand technique 202 made of entry 69.

**233. A narrative review that refuses to report frequencies is stronger for refusing.** Entry 72, verbatim: *"It compares method families and evaluation designs across studies too heterogeneous to pool, so it reports no field-wide frequencies and none should be inferred from the examples cited."* The standard failure of a selective review is letting a handful of examples imply a rate across a field. **By declining, it keeps its claims inside what its method can carry**, and what remains is a set of existence proofs about failure modes plus a framework for organising them — the correct product of a narrative review. **Contrast entry 71 the previous day, which made frequency claims from a 10.4% response rate.** Two consecutive entries, opposite discipline, same underlying constraint. **When a narrative review does quote a percentage of the literature, check whether its search was systematic enough to produce one.**

**What caps entry 72 at 4/5, recorded so the grade stays auditable.** **Single author** — no second screener, no inter-rater reliability for study selection, and the six-stage assignments are self-described *"qualitative author judgements."* The instruments it correctly praises (TRIPOD+AI, PROBAST+AI, DECIDE-AI) were built by multi-disciplinary consensus panels precisely because appraisal criteria built by one mind inherit that mind's blind spots. **The framework is also unvalidated in the sense that matters for an instrument**: nobody has shown two readers applying it agree, or that a stage assignment predicts anything. **Fifty-three purposively selected records**, enriched by prespecified criteria for *"large, multicentre, longitudinal, randomised or externally evaluated studies"* — so its central finding (frequent internal evaluation, fewer independent tests, little prospective evidence) is if anything **an optimistic picture of the field**. And **every table is unreachable, including the full six-stage specification**, so the review's central deliverable was graded from its prose.

**One more number worth keeping.** Entry 72's largest cited study: a trans-ancestry depression meta-analysis of **688,808 cases and 4,364,225 controls across 29 countries**, finding **697 associations at 635 loci**, with polygenic scores explaining **at most 5.8% of liability** — which technique 224 converts to **AUC ~0.637**. **Five million participants and 635 real loci produce a predictor barely better than a weighted coin. Sample size bought discovery; it did not buy a clinical test.** Those are different wins, and the distinction should govern where the next grant goes.

**Plan set on 2026-11-09.** **The gait foundation model (PMID 42995231) could not be retrieved** — converting the PMID returns **no PMC identifier**, and Scite returned its paid-plan error — so it **joins the deferred list at day 1 with an abstract-only date of 2026-11-30**, rather than being decoded abstract-only three days after publication. It remains the highest-value outstanding item (**1,887 of 3,210 phenotypes called significant**), and today's technique 224 sharpens the first question for it: its **r = 0.91 for BMI** implies ~83% of variance, which is plausible for a learned embedding predicting a physical measurement, so **the scrutiny belongs on its clinical-diagnosis and medication-use predictions**, not its anthropometrics. **Shelf is down to one spare** — (6) explainable-AI review of 70 prostate and bladder cancer studies (PMID 42960345). **Next run: restock the shelf first** with a `pubmed pmc[sb]` date-window search (six-for-six on 2026-10-28 and again on 2026-11-03), then decode. **The BMJ review (PMID 42869550) hits day 21 on 2026-11-12 and takes priority that day** under the standing rule if its PMC body is still empty.

## From entry 73 — Diamantidis et al., explainable AI in prostate and bladder cancer (Curr Urol, 2026; decoded 2026-11-10, FULL TEXT)

A review about a problem one layer up from the usual: **not whether a model is accurate, but whether its explanation can be trusted.** 70 studies, 4 domains. Graded 3/5 — the framework is sharper than the review's own accounting. **Techniques 234-236 are what the accumulated SHAP lessons (#96, #106, #143, #164) add up to.**

**234. Post hoc explanation and interpretable-by-design are different KINDS of evidence.**

An **interpretable-by-design** model has its reasoning in its structure. A generalised additive model fits a readable curve per predictor, so "PSA density mattered most" is **read directly off the fitted model** — a fact about the model, as reliable as its own arithmetic.

A **post hoc** explanation is a second procedure run against a finished black box. SHAP perturbs inputs and watches outputs to **estimate** each feature's contribution; Grad-CAM reads gradients to **estimate** which pixels mattered. Both are **approximations** with their own error, assumptions and failure modes.

Entry 73's formulation is the cleanest available: the distinction "determines whether an explanation is an auditable property of the model or an external approximation that may not faithfully represent the model's actual reasoning." Practically: whether a report saying "PSA density was the dominant predictor" came from a glass-box or a post hoc method **"materially affects the extent to which that output should guide a clinical decision."**

**The analogy.** Why is a bakery's bread good? **Glass-box:** they hand you the recipe — readable, checkable, arguable. **Post hoc:** you cannot enter the kitchen, so you buy a hundred loaves, vary the weather and the hour, and infer that humidity matters most. Your inference may be right. It is an **estimate about a process you never observed**, and could be wholly wrong while fitting every loaf.

**Watch out for** papers presenting SHAP output in the same register as a regression coefficient table. **A coefficient is a parameter of the fitted model; a SHAP value is the output of a separate estimation procedure applied to it.** When a paper says "the model used feature X," ask whether that is a readout or an inference.

**235. A plausible saliency map is not a correct one — and the three tests that tell them apart.**

Entry 73, verbatim: **"saliency maps can appear anatomically plausible while remaining weakly coupled to the model's true decision process. These maps can also be unstable under minor input perturbations."** Therefore **"visual agreement between a heatmap and anatomical expectations should not, by itself, be considered confirmation that the model is reasoning correctly."**

**Why plausibility is near-worthless as evidence.** A saliency map shows where the image is **locally sensitive** — change these pixels and the output moves. The tumour is the highest-contrast, most unusual structure present, so it is where almost any image model is locally sensitive, **whether or not it drove the classification.** A model keyed on the scanner's noise signature, or on cancer cases having been scanned on the older machine, still produces a heatmap centred on the lesion. **The map is compatible with correct and incorrect reasoning alike, so observing it barely discriminates between them.**

**The three tests entry 73 names, and what each catches:**

    perturbation test            nudge the input; a faithful explanation should be about as
                                 stable as the prediction. If a one-pixel shift rewrites the
                                 heatmap, the heatmap was not describing the model.
    prediction-saliency coupling occlude the highlighted region. If the prediction barely
                                 moves, the model did not use it. THE DIRECT TEST, rarely run.
    expert-annotation concordance compare the map against radiologist contours QUANTITATIVELY.
                                 Overlap can be measured; "looks right" cannot.

For structured data the analogue is **stability across resampling and retraining** — exactly what entry 70's Jaccard and Nogueira indices measured, on a model whose selected feature set changed substantially between resamples while performance held.

**The analogy.** A detective says the butler did it and points at the butler. The butler is the most suspicious-looking person present, so **any** detective would point there, competent or not — you have learned almost nothing about the reasoning. To learn something you remove the butler and see whether the accusation changes. **That is the occlusion test.**

**Watch out for** a figure of heatmaps captioned as "confirming the model focuses on clinically relevant regions," with no quantitative fidelity measure. Entry 73 reports this is how saliency was **most often** presented across its 70 studies. **Treat such a figure as decoration until a coupling or perturbation number appears.**

**236. SHAP attributes association within the model's learned representation — not causation, and not biology.**

Entry 73's formulation is worth memorising: SHAP "values quantify associations between features and predictions **within a model's learned representation.** They do not establish causality and inherit any biases or confounders embedded in the training data."

**Three layers of distance from biology.** SHAP measures how **the model's output** changes with a feature. The output depends on **what the model learned.** What it learned depends on **the training data, including its confounders, coding conventions and collection artefacts.** A SHAP ranking is therefore at three removes from the disease.

Entry 73's own example is uncomfortable and exact. Across bladder registry models, SHAP consistently tops out at T stage, N stage, grade, lymphovascular invasion and carcinoma in situ — which "likely reflect genuine biology, data structure, and overlap between these sources of signal, **which attribution analysis cannot disentangle.**" Three readings fit identically: those features drive outcome; those features are the most reliably **coded** registry fields; or they are mutually correlated so attribution smears arbitrarily. **SHAP cannot choose**, hence the restriction "to hypothesis generation rather than mechanistic inference."

    Worked example of the correlation problem:
      tumour size and T stage nearly perfectly correlated; outcome truly depends on size.
      The model can use either, or split credit arbitrarily.
      SHAP faithfully reports whatever split THIS fit landed on - which depends on
      initialisation, regularisation and seed.
      Retrain -> the ranking can flip, with the biology unchanged.
    Not a SHAP defect. A defect in reading a SHAP ranking as a claim about disease.

**The analogy.** Which ingredient makes a cake rise? You may only vary ingredients in a recipe that always uses self-raising flour **and** baking powder together. Remove either and it flops, so both look essential. **The attribution is correct about your recipe and tells you nothing about baking.**

**Watch out for** SHAP rankings presented as discovery — "the model revealed that X drives outcome." Entry 73's standard is right: **report them as hypotheses, and validate with perturbation tests, localisation metrics or independent biological endpoints before treating them as findings.**

**237. Confirmation by explanation is not validation.** Entry 73 notes explainability often "functions as a post hoc illustration that confirms expected findings." **The consequence it leaves implicit: an explanation that recovers your prior carries almost no information.** Information is surprise. If you expected the heatmap on the tumour and T stage atop the SHAP ranking, observing both has barely updated you — both were near-certain under a correct model **and** under several incorrect ones. **A genuinely informative explanation is one that could have come out wrong**, which is why the three fidelity tests outrank any number of plausible figures. **For designers: an explanation that cannot surprise you is not an audit, it is a reassurance.**

**238. Explanation utility and explanation existence are different claims.** Of entry 73's 70 studies, **1 (1.4%)** ran a reader study and **0** ran a prospective or randomised comparison of explainable against conventional workflows. The one that looked found explanation **design** — not its presence — determined whether clinicians acted, and that overlays let non-expert radiologists reach expert-level accuracy on equivocal **PI-RADS 3** lesions while **halving reading time**. **So this is a measurement gap, not a null result.** Generalising: *does an explanation exist* is an engineering property; *does it improve a decision* is a clinical outcome needing a comparative design. **This is technique 229 applied to explanations rather than predictions — you deploy an explanation plus a clinician plus an interface, and only the assembly can be tested.**

**239. Publication bias specific to explainability, with a known direction.** Entry 73, verbatim: **"Studies with negative or equivocal XAI findings are less likely to be published and are therefore underrepresented."** Consider what that selects on: a team generates saliency maps, looks at them, and if they look wrong, investigates further or drops the figure. **So every heatmap in the published literature has already passed an informal visual screen on the exact property under study — plausibility.** The published distribution of "explanations that look sensible" is biased upward by construction, and **unlike entry 71's opinion-strength selection, which inflated praise and complaint alike, this biases in one known direction.** Remedy as for any publication bias: weight unvalidated plausible-looking explanations near zero, and seek the studies that ran a fidelity test and reported the number either way.

**240. A review's numerator needs a denominator.** Entry 73 reports **70 included studies** with no record counts, no screening numbers, no exclusion tally, no PRISMA-style flow. **70 of 90 is a near-census of a small field; 70 of 900 is a selection — and selection IS the method of a narrative review.** Without the flow you cannot calibrate how much the synthesis depended on invisible choices. **Ask for it; if absent, read the conclusions as the authors' considered opinion informed by 70 studies** — a different and weaker object than a synthesis of a field.

**241. Frequency language without counts, in a review that charted the counts.** Entry 73 makes five frequency claims about 70 studies — "the vast majority", "a small minority", "the most widely used", "most often presented as qualitative overlays", "most included studies were retrospective and single-center" — **with zero numbers**, having extracted explainability method **and scope** and validation type for every study into five tables. **The arithmetic was one column-count away.** Note the three-entry sequence: entry 71 made frequency claims from a 10.4% sample; entry 72 **explicitly forbade** frequency inference from its own corpus; entry 73 asserts frequencies it charted and never reports. **The best of the three is the one that refused.** (I attempted the tally from the flattened PMC tables and **declined to report a split**, finding only 48 performance-bearing cells of an expected 70 with validation wording in 25 of those — stating what cannot be counted is the honest option.)

**242. Match your databases to where each claim's evidence lives.** Entry 73 searched **PubMed/MEDLINE only**, plus reference tracking. Its strongest, most load-bearing claims — saliency unfaithfulness, attention not being explanation, attribution instability under perturbation — originate in the machine-learning literature indexed by IEEE Xplore, the ACM Digital Library, MICCAI proceedings and arXiv. **So its clinical-application evidence is searched and its methodological evidence is merely cited.** The review lists selection bias as a limitation but does not identify single-database search as its likely largest source. **When you cannot cover every relevant database, say which claims rest on the thinner search.**

**243. Foundation models break post hoc explanation, and entry 73 says so.** With imaging, text and genomics fused through cross-modal attention trained at scale, "tracing that output through cross-modal attention layers ... is categorically different from attributing a gradient-boosted tree prediction to its input features," and token- or patch-level attribution "cannot verify whether the model has learned clinically valid reasoning or merely statistical co-occurrence in the training data." **For the newest and most capable models, the honest answer to "why did it say that?" is currently that nobody knows.** Two consequences: **the gap is widening**, since capability scales faster than explanation methods; and **regulation is advancing faster than the methodology meant to support it** (entry 73's own observation), so "explainable" may become a box ticked on a submission before anyone can tick it truthfully.

**Credit where due, recorded so the 3/5 stays auditable.** Entry 73 prints **both Boolean search strings verbatim** with a cut-off date, so anyone can re-run them. Its exclusion of "studies in which explainability was mentioned superficially without a specific method and corresponding results" is exactly the filter that stops a review of explainability becoming a review of the word. It reports the 1-of-70 figure that undercuts its own field. It notes that **generalised additive models with pairwise interactions can match black-box accuracy on structured clinical data while staying visualisable**, and that a logistic-regression interpretability layer reached **AUC 0.83** — which together imply that **on structured clinical data transparency is roughly free, so the dominance of post hoc explanation there is a habit rather than a trade-off** (a stronger claim than the review makes, supported by its own two examples). And its best buried idea, in a sub-section on causability: **"a high-dimensional SHAP plot containing dozens of unlabeled features may provide less usable guidance than a simpler risk summary that maps onto established urological decision frameworks."** An explanation is an interface; an unusable interface explains nothing.

**Disclosure note — third AI-use declaration in this list.** Entry 73 declares **ChatGPT (GPT-5.2 and GPT-5.5, OpenAI) and Grammarly** for language editing and wording refinement. The sequence so far: **entry 67** ChatGPT (language editing); **entry 71** OpenEvidence for literature review plus Claude Sonnet 3.5 for proofreading; **entry 73** ChatGPT GPT-5.2/5.5 plus Grammarly. Three different wordings, all appropriate, inside a fortnight. Funding "None", no conflicts, acknowledgments "None" — the full disclosure block present, in contrast with entry 72 where the whole block was absent from the extraction.

## SHELF RESTOCK — 2026-11-10

The shelf emptied with entry 73, and was restocked using the `pubmed pmc[sb]` date-window method (six-for-six on 2026-10-28 and again on 2026-11-03). Search: AI/ML/DL/LLM in the title AND validation/external/prospective/multicenter/calibration in title/abstract, PMC-indexed, entered 2026-11-01 to 2026-11-10. **189 records returned; 10 screened; 6 kept.** Rejected as outside clinical medicine: a tea-metabolite study (42997676), a livestock-genetics review (42998995), a remote-sensing parasite-risk model (42999529). **All six below have confirmed PMC IDs.**

**(A) [x] DECODED 2026-11-11 — Late prostate radiotherapy toxicity, REQUITE cohort** — PMID 42999685, PMC13744349, *Clin Transl Radiat Oncol* 2026;62:101305, Jordan K et al. **229 men.** **The most valuable item here, because it is a near-null study reported properly.** GI toxicity AUCs **0.573 / 0.571 / 0.587** (L1, Elastic Net, XGBoost) with permutation p = **0.036 / 0.047 / 0.022** — *statistically significant discrimination that is clinically almost worthless*, the cleanest possible teaching case for **significant is not useful**. And **GU discrimination was BELOW chance with no evidence beyond the null distribution.** Repeated nested stratified cross-validation, bootstrap CIs, outcome-label permutation testing, SHAP stability across repeats, rectal EUD the most consistent GI predictor. **This list has almost no honestly reported negative studies. NEXT RUN TAKES THIS ONE.**

**(B) [x] DECODED 2026-11-16 (entry 79) — Bone tumour multimodal imaging + AI** — PMID 42997475, PMC13742044, *J Bone Oncol* 2026;61:100808, Liu X & Liu L. A review advancing **one argument**: the binding constraint is **the stability of the reference standard, not algorithmic capability** — with numbers. Biopsy-vs-resection grade agreement in central chondrosarcoma falls from **weighted kappa 0.63 in long bones to 0.12 in the pelvis**; interobserver kappa for grading cartilaginous tumours **0.44 among MSK pathologists, 0.35 among radiologists**; molecular reclassification means older datasets "contain classes that no longer exist as defined"; histology does not reliably predict behaviour. **And the inference that inverts technique 194: "apparent failure on external validation may reflect label divergence between institutions rather than model deficiency."** Genuinely new territory for this list.

**(C) [x] DECODED 2026-11-14 (entry 77) — Diabetic retinopathy from routine labs** — PMID 42974596, PMC13727297, *Smart Health* 2026;42:100724, Laoh E et al. **97,786 patients, 16,865 cases (17.25%)**; eight-variable XGBoost reporting **AUROC 97.24%**, sensitivity 91.17%, specificity 96.22% on a 19,558-patient untouched test set; SMOTE on training only, test retaining observed prevalence. **Shelved for a sceptical reading:** technique 224 converts AUROC 0.972 to roughly **80% of outcome variance**, and two of the eight predictors are **nephropathy and neuropathy** — the other two microvascular complications of diabetes, routinely coded alongside retinopathy. A near-textbook **label-leakage** candidate, and the paper's own framing is about trustworthiness.

**(D) [x] DECODED 2026-11-15 (entry 78) — Glaucoma visual-field prediction from baseline OCT** — PMID 42997014, PMC13740841, *Ophthalmol Sci* 2026;6(12):101419, Chaurasia AK et al. **1,792 eyes from 1,610 patients** for development, with **genuine external validation at two further centres on two different OCT devices** (Zeiss Cirrus 150 eyes; Heidelberg Spectralis 280 eyes); MAE with 95% CIs throughout; follow-up 4.74 ± 2.59 years; vision transformer; saliency maps implicating inner and outer RNFL. **Multi-device external validation is rare and this list has decoded almost none** — a natural companion to technique 194.

**(E) [x] DECODED 2026-11-17 (entry 80) — MIBC pathological complete response after chemoradiotherapy** — PMID 42997524, PMC13741961, *Clin Transl Radiat Oncol* 2026;62:101320, Fujiwara M et al. **285 patients**, **102 machine-learning models evaluated in the training cohort** with the best-in-training carried forward — a textbook **winner's-curse** setup — reaching test AUC **0.829 (0.706-0.932)**. And a **calibration slope of 0.856 with 95% CI 0.469 to 1.639**, the authors stating themselves that "the wide CI indicated uncertainty in calibration." Honest, and the right vehicle for teaching the calibration slope.

**(F) [x] DECODED 2026-11-18 (entry 81) — Bitewing radiograph positioning errors** — PMID 42981213, PMC13734376, *Clin Exp Dent Res* 2026;12(6), Rabelo KA et al. **403 radiographs, acquired entirely from dental mannequins.** Accuracies from 99.0% (side) down to **73.2% (incorrect receptor placement)** and 79.3% (multi-class cone-cutting severity), with the authors stating that "a validation study using patient-acquired bitewing radiographs from clinical settings is required before clinical deployment." A clean, low-stakes case for **distribution shift: a mannequin is not a patient.**

**SHELF RESTOCK — 2026-11-18.** (F) was the last spare, so a restock screen was run with the proven method (`pubmed pmc[sb]` plus a two-week entry-date window): **347 records retrieved, the newest 10 screened in detail, 5 kept and 5 rejected.** Rejected: a bibliometric analysis of AI in myocardial infarction (43024324, not a performance study), a microRNA scoping review (43021123, molecular rather than clinical-AI appraisal), a cryo-electron-tomography quality classifier (43017487, structural biology), a wildfire review (43017078, not medical), and a computer-aided drug-discovery review (43016799, not clinical prediction). **All five kept items are PMC-confirmed.**

**(G) [x] DECODED 2026-11-20 (entry 83) — SOFA score recalibration by ML-guided optimisation** — PMID 43024933, PMC13766654, *JMIR Med Inform* 2026. **TOP PICK, and the next decode after tomorrow's owed *Annals* abstract.** eICU Collaborative Research Database, **208 US hospitals, 14,848 adult first ICU admissions.** Three reasons it goes first. It **learns integer weights and thresholds for a legacy point-based score rather than replacing it with a black box** — the simple-score-versus-ensemble question entry 80's four-factor count raised (309) and entry 81 raised again, here tested deliberately rather than incidentally. It uses **10-fold nested cross-validation GROUPED BY HOSPITAL** — grouped CV done properly, which is exactly the check entry 81 did not run (319), and the first entry in this run to do it at site level. And it reports that **133,684 of 148,532 encounters (90%) were excluded for at least one missing determination** — the largest exclusion fraction this list will have examined, several times entry 78's 44.4%. **First questions: what does a 90% complete-case exclusion do to who is left (and is the remaining 10% a sicker, better-monitored population?), and does the recalibrated integer score beat the original SOFA by more than the selection noise?**

**(H) AKI prediction in critical pulmonary embolism** — PMID 43024281, PMC13773385, *Medicine (Baltimore)* 2026. **MIMIC-IV for development and internal validation, eICU for EXTERNAL validation** — a genuine cross-database transportability test (284), which this list has decoded only twice; eight ML models with LASSO plus multivariate logistic selection, and **a web calculator already deployed.** **First question: how much performance was lost between MIMIC-IV and eICU, and was the calculator deployed before or after that was known?** Pairs naturally with entry 78, where the two external sets accidentally isolated the device effect.

**(I) Complete renal response in lupus nephritis** — PMID 43015004, PMC13757897, *Ren Fail* 2026. **A PROSPECTIVE cohort of 400** followed 12 months, four ML models, reporting **AUC, calibration AND decision-curve analysis plus SHAP** — a fuller reporting set than almost anything in this run, and prospective rather than a retrospective chart trawl, which is rare enough here to be worth decoding on design alone. The stated hook is that it "leverages the 3-month treatment milestone." **First question: does the 3-month milestone add anything over baseline variables alone — i.e. is this predicting CHANGE or re-predicting a LEVEL (287)?**

**(J) Facial phenotyping in sickle cell disease** — PMID 43019377, PMC13759072, *PLOS Digit Health* 2026. **308 smartphone photographs**, 154 electrophoresis-confirmed cases and 154 age- and sex-matched controls aged 5 months to 19 years, recruited in **Kinshasa, DRC**; interpretable **linear SVM with recursive feature elimination under leave-one-out cross-validation.** A global-health study on low-cost hardware, and the first entry here with a genuinely low-resource deployment setting. **First question: RFE inside LOOCV on 308 images is a classic optimism trap — was the feature selection nested inside the resampling, or run once on all 308 first (306, 311)?** Also: a matched design means the control group is constructed, so the prevalence is 50% by fiat and PPV in any real population is a separate calculation (275).

**(K) Next-day alcohol craving forecasting** — PMID 43013873, PMC13754260, *J Med Internet Res* 2026. Prospective longitudinal, **multimodal — EEG, heart-rate variability and smartphone digital phenotypes** — with a "tiny recurrent neural network." Forecasting a next-day **subjective self-reported state**, which puts the reference-standard question (294, 317) in a new form: the label is not a noisy measurement of an objective fact but a person's own report, which is the only thing it could be and still has test-retest properties nobody usually examines. **First question: what is the comparator — does it beat yesterday's craving score (286)?**

**Also noted, not shelved.** **"Mass Surveillance with Artificial Intelligence — A New Determinant of Health?"** — PMID 43001754, *NEJM*, 7 November 2026, Peoples N, Hoops K, Fleegler EW. A Perspective with no abstract, so not a primary study. Recorded because it is adjacent to **entry 71's** finding that patients spontaneously raised **insurance eligibility, employment and assisted-living admission** as harms of an incidental AI finding. **If a primary study on surveillance-derived health data appears, it goes straight to the shelf.**

**Deferred status, 2026-11-10.** Gait foundation model (42995231) **day 2**, no PMC copy, abstract-only date **2026-11-30**. Nature personal health agent (42907512) **day 21**, weekly. **BMJ LLM-trial reporting review (42869550) day 28 — its twenty-one-day threshold fires 2026-11-12, and it takes priority that day** if the PMC body is still empty; today's entry sharpens it again, since entry 73 states that **"standardized XAI reporting guidelines analogous to TRIPOD do not yet exist"** and calls for a checklist extending TRIPOD-AI to cover explainability depth and validation — precisely the gap the BMJ review quantifies at 40% mean TRIPOD-LLM completeness. *Annals* *Choosing Wisely* (42896192) **day 21**, weekly, abstract-only date 2026-11-19.

## From entry 74 — Jordan et al., late prostate radiotherapy toxicity, REQUITE (Clin Transl Radiat Oncol, 2026; decoded 2026-11-11, FULL TEXT)

**A study that found essentially nothing, and one of the best papers in this list.** 4/5 — the first 4 awarded to a near-null result. Execution at 5 level; a design ceiling (n=229 complete-case, internal validation only, no calibration) holds the grade. **Techniques 244-246 are the load-bearing ones, and 249 is the answer to entry 73.**

**244. A statistically significant AUC can be clinically worthless — convert it before believing it.**

Entry 74's GI models were **significantly** above chance (permutation p = 0.036, 0.047, 0.022). Stop at the p-value and you conclude the model works. **Convert instead, in three steps.**

**Step one, what AUC means.** The probability the model scores a random future-toxicity patient above a random non-toxicity patient. Coin = 0.5. **Best model here = 0.587**, i.e. right 58.7% of the time instead of 50%.

**Step two, the only terms that matter.** At the cohort's 46.7% GI prevalence, taking the best corner of the reported ranges (sens 0.658, spec 0.518):

    PPV = (0.658 x 0.467) / (0.658 x 0.467 + 0.482 x 0.533) = 0.3073 / 0.5642 = 54.5%
    NPV = (0.518 x 0.533) / (0.518 x 0.533 + 0.342 x 0.467) = 0.2761 / 0.4358 = 63.3%

**A positive result takes a man from a 47% chance to 55%. A negative takes him from 53% to 63%** (i.e. 37% chance of toxicity instead of 53%). **Nothing you would do changes at either number.** Note too: specificity ~0.50 means the model **flags half the men who will never develop toxicity.**

**Step three, the sanity check.** Technique 224: **AUC 0.587 is about 2.4% of the variance.** The other 97.6% is elsewhere.

**The analogy.** A weather service announces it can now predict rain 58.7% of the time instead of 50%. Real, measurable, published. **You take the umbrella exactly as often as before**, because no decision turns on 47% versus 55%. **Significance says the effect exists; magnitude says whether to care.**

**Watch out for** a paper reporting a p-value for discrimination and no PPV. **Always convert an AUC to pre-test-to-post-test movement at the actual prevalence before deciding a model is useful.**

**245. A below-chance AUC: noise, or a signal with the wrong sign? The two-sided permutation test tells you.**

Entry 74's three GU models came in **below 0.5** (0.465, 0.455, 0.466). Two genuinely different explanations, and distinguishing them matters:

**(a) Sampling noise.** If the truth is exactly 0.5, roughly half your estimates land below it. Nothing wrong, nothing there.
**(b) A real inverted relationship.** The model found genuine signal with the wrong sign. **That would be a POSITIVE finding** — flip it and use it.

One-sided tests answer only "is it above chance?", so entry 74 added **two-sided** tests "to determine whether performance differed from chance in either direction and to assess whether the observed inverse discrimination was greater than expected from sampling variation alone." Result: **p = 0.150-0.412.** Explanation (a).

**Verify it yourself.** Under the null, an AUC's sampling SD is approximately

    SE = sqrt((n1 + n2 + 1) / (12 * n1 * n2))          [Hanley-McNeil]

With 126 GU events and 103 non-events that is **0.0384**, so the observed 0.455 sits **1.17 SD** below 0.5, two-sided p ~ **0.24** — inside the reported range. **The arithmetic agrees: ordinary fluctuation.**

**The analogy.** 8 heads in 20 flips — biased toward tails? Maybe, or maybe that is what fair coins do one time in four. **You cannot tell from the direction; you have to ask how far a fair coin wanders.**

**Watch out for** a below-chance AUC reported with no two-sided test — and the opposite error, treating one as an inverted predictor without testing whether the inversion exceeds noise.

**246. Permutation testing that re-runs the ENTIRE pipeline, hyperparameter search included.**

**The shortcut:** permute labels, refit your already-tuned model, record the AUC, repeat. Fast, and **wrong in a known direction.**

**Why wrong.** Your reported AUC is not one model's output — it is **a procedure's** output: a grid search, an inner-CV selection, then evaluation. That search is itself a source of optimism. **A null built without the search is too narrow**, because it omits the very variance your real estimate contains. Narrow null, inflated significance.

Entry 74 ran **1,000** permutations in which "the complete repeated nested cross-validation pipeline was then repeated, **including inner-loop hyperparameter optimisation**, model fitting and generation of OOF predictions."

**And here is the verification.** Against the asymptotic null SE of 0.0383 (which assumes a single fixed model):

    L1           AUC 0.573 -> 1.91 SD -> asymptotic p 0.028  | reported 0.036
    Elastic Net  AUC 0.571 -> 1.85 SD -> asymptotic p 0.032  | reported 0.047
    XGBoost      AUC 0.587 -> 2.27 SD -> asymptotic p 0.012  | reported 0.022

**Every reported p is LARGER than the formula predicts — precisely the signature of a null correctly widened by model-selection variance.** Had they come in *smaller* than asymptotic, I would suspect the shortcut. **This is positive evidence of honest implementation**, and it is a check worth running on any paper that reports permutation p-values.

**The analogy.** Does your talent-scouting system beat chance? **Shortcut:** give the scout you already picked random player ratings. **Honest:** give random ratings to the whole process — hiring twelve scouts, auditioning them, picking the best. The second null is wider, because part of your real success was the luck of picking a scout who looked good on the audition data.

**Watch out for** "permutation testing confirmed above-chance performance" with no statement of what was permuted and what was re-run. **Ask: was the hyperparameter search inside the permutation loop?**

**247. Select the operating threshold on training data only.** Entry 74 computed Youden's J from inner-CV predictions on the outer **training** set, fixed it, then applied it to the unseen outer test fold. **Entry 70 selected its Youden point on the pooled out-of-fold predictions it then reported** — tuning the operating point on the numbers used to report performance, which inflates the sensitivity/specificity pair while leaving the AUC untouched. **This is the fix for technique 212.** When a paper reports Youden-optimal sensitivity and specificity, **ask which data chose the threshold.**

**248. Out-of-fold SHAP.** We insist performance be measured on held-out data; **the same applies to explanations.** SHAP computed on training data tells you what the model leaned on *in data it had already seen*, which can be driven by the same overfitting the held-out evaluation exists to detect. Entry 74 calculated SHAP "within each outer validation fold" and retained values **only for patients in the corresponding unseen test fold.** **A feature-importance analysis computed on the full dataset or the training set is the explanation equivalent of reporting training accuracy.** Ask which it was.

**249. A stable, correct, biologically true explanation can sit on a useless model.**

Entry 74's rectal EUD: **ranked FIRST in every one of 25 repeats, across all three models**, with the correct sign, for a mechanism established in radiobiology. **By every available measure that is a perfect explanation.** The model carrying it has an AUC of 0.587 — worth about eight percentage points of PPV.

The paper, verbatim: *"These findings also highlight the distinction between identifying clinically plausible predictors and developing a clinically useful prediction model. Models may repeatedly identify rectal dose or patient and treatment characteristics as influential while providing insufficient discrimination for individual risk stratification and limit clinical utility."*

**Entry 73 the previous day argued that explanation plausibility is not validation. THIS IS THE DEMONSTRATION.** **Explanation quality and model utility are independent dimensions** — a convincing feature-importance plot is not evidence a model discriminates, and a model that discriminates well need not have a tidy explanation. **Check them separately, always.** Cite this entry whenever someone offers a stable SHAP ranking as evidence a model works.

**250. Check reported p-values against the asymptotic null, and check what the study could have detected.** Two cheap moves. The Hanley-McNeil SE (technique 245) tells you whether a reported p is plausible for the sample size, **and in which direction it deviates, which is diagnostic** (technique 246).
