And minimum detectable effect (technique 204): at these sample sizes, 80% power at one-sided alpha=.05 needs **AUC >= 0.595**. Observed GI = **0.587**, just below — **which is exactly why its p-values are 0.022-0.047 rather than anything smaller.** **The crucial contrast with entry 70:** there, three underpowered nulls risked being miscited as "no effect." **Here the study's own conclusion also says "limited / no useful discrimination" — precisely what a study powered to 0.60 may say on finding 0.587 and 0.465. Claim and power match; no overclaim in either direction.**

**251. Equivalent uniform dose as a principled answer to collinearity.** V50, V60, V70, mean dose and max dose are different summaries of one curve and are heavily inter-correlated; feed them all in and attribution smears arbitrarily (technique 236). Entry 74 collapsed them into **organ-specific EUD** via the Niemierko formulation with literature-derived parameters, "reducing correlation between individual dose-volume histogram parameters while retaining information on the uniform dose distribution." **General lesson: when several predictors are transformations of one underlying measurement, combine them using domain knowledge BEFORE modelling** — rather than leaving regularisation to pick arbitrarily afterwards.

**252. Baseline-corrected endpoints: what they fix and what they cost.** Defining toxicity as a **one-grade increase from each patient's own baseline** stops pre-existing symptoms being counted as treatment harm — the right correction, rarely made. **The cost: the endpoint becomes sensitive to mild change**, giving event rates of **46.7% and 55.0%** here against far lower conventional Grade >=2 rates. **So entry 74's null is about predicting ANY worsening, not the severe toxicity a clinician would change a plan for.** Both are legitimate endpoints and they are different questions; a near-null on the first does not establish a near-null on the second. **Note the flip side, which strengthens the finding:** ~50/50 classes are the EASIEST setting for a classifier — no imbalance penalty, maximum information per patient. Combined with prespecified features and zero missing data, **the study had near-ideal conditions and still found 2.4%.**

**253. Complete-case analysis without a parent denominator.** Entry 74: 229 men "with a complete record" of baseline, treatment, dosimetric and longitudinal toxicity data, drawn from a prospective multicentre cohort **whose size is never stated.** **Zero missing data is a real analytic advantage — no imputation assumptions enter at all — purchased by a selection you cannot inspect.** Complete dosimetry plus four follow-up visits across 48 months plausibly favours better-resourced centres and healthier attenders; direction and magnitude unknown. **The fix is one sentence: how many enrolled, how many analysed, how did they differ?** This is technique 240 arriving in a primary study rather than a review.

**What caps entry 74 at 4/5, recorded so the grade stays auditable.** **n=229 is a complete-case subset with no parent denominator** (technique 253). **Internal validation only**, stated. **No calibration assessed anywhere** — discrimination start to finish, which matters less for a model this weak but is conspicuous given the care taken elsewhere, and is one of the few places a TRIPOD+AI checklist would have changed the paper. **No reporting guideline named.** And a **one-patient abstract/body discrepancy**: the abstract says 125 (54.6%) GU events, the Methods and Results both say 126 (55%); each is internally consistent with its own percentage, so it is a transcription slip that changes no conclusion — reported because checking is the job, and it is the only arithmetic inconsistency in the paper.

**Reporting-guideline pattern, now four entries deep and consistent enough to state as a rule.** Entry 69 named TRIPOD+AI and deposited the checklist. Entry 70 named STROBE while being arguably more careful. Entry 71 named none. Entry 73 named none and called for an explainability checklist that does not yet exist. **Entry 74 names none and does more of what TRIPOD+AI asks than any of them.** **The checklist is not the quality signal — read the methods.** This is exactly what the deferred BMJ review (PMID 42869550, mean TRIPOD-LLM completeness 40%) quantifies, and it reaches day 21 tomorrow.

**Plan set on 2026-11-11.** **Next run takes shelf item (C)**, the diabetic retinopathy model (PMID 42974596, PMC13727297), **because today's entry is the perfect contrast.** Entry 74 reports **AUC 0.587 / ~2.4% of variance** after validating as hard as anything in this list; (C) reports **AUROC 0.972**, which technique 224 converts to roughly **80% of variance**, from **eight routine laboratory values** — and two of those eight are **nephropathy and neuropathy**, the other two microvascular complications of diabetes, routinely coded alongside retinopathy. **Read side by side the pair is the clearest available lesson in what validation rigour does to a reported number**, and the first question for (C) is whether its headline survives "could the label be inside the inputs?" **Shelf after today: five spares — (B) bone-tumour reference-standard review, (C) next, (D) glaucoma OCT with two-device external validation, (E) MIBC pCR with 102 models screened, (F) bitewing mannequin-only.** **Tomorrow, 2026-11-12, the BMJ review hits day 21 and takes priority** under the standing rule if its PMC body is still empty; the three weekly deferred re-checks (Nature 42907512, Annals 42896192) also fall due.

**Landmark note.** The week's one new record was **"Deep learning-based gait analysis predicts multisystem health phenotypes"** (PMID 43003904, *Nature Medicine*, 9 Nov 2026) — **no abstract, no author list, and a DOI one digit from the gait foundation model already deferred** (`...04725-8` vs `...04724-9`), which identifies it as the journal's accompanying commentary rather than a primary study. **Does not displace the queue**, but it shows the journal is featuring the gait paper, strengthening the case for decoding the primary article once reachable.

---

## From entry 75 — Heydari et al., the anatomy of a personal health agent (Nature, 2026; decoded 2026-11-12, FULL TEXT)

**Deferred for 22 days and then unblocked.** PMID 42907512 served no PMC body on every check from 2026-10-22. On 2026-11-12 the conversion returned **PMC13736220** and the full text retrieved cleanly. **Second instance in this list of a PMC body appearing well after indexing** — the counter-case is entry 68, which served an empty body for twenty-one consecutive days and never filled.

**A deviation from the standing rule, recorded verbatim from the 2026-11-12 reasoning so the decision stays auditable.** Today was the day the twenty-one-day rule fired on the BMJ review (42869550, day 29, still no PMC body). It did not take priority. The reasoning at the time: **"The deferred Nature paper just became available — PMID 42907512 now has PMC13736220 after 22 days with no copy. The BMJ review still has no PMC body. Since the trigger puts full text ahead of abstract-only, I'll decode the Nature paper today and fire the BMJ abstract-only decode tomorrow."** **The BMJ abstract-only decode is therefore owed on 2026-11-13, ahead of shelf item (C).** The BMJ decode is identical whenever it runs; this one would not have been.

**254. When experts and users disagree about how much better something is, the user's number predicts whether it works.**

This is the most portable finding in the entry, and it was measured **twice, independently**:

- Coaching agent: **experts perceived a 10.2 ± 1.2% advantage; end users perceived 3.5 ± 2.5%.** Ratio **2.9×**.
- Integrated system: **expert deltas averaged 17.1%** (range 5.7–39.0%); **end-user deltas averaged 4.6%** (range 1.6–15.4%). Ratio **3.7×**.
- On **user empowerment**: experts preferred the new system **69.9%** of the time; end users **48.4%** — a coin flip. **An outright reversal, not a smaller version of the same effect.**

**Why the gap is real rather than noise.** Experts are trained to see what users cannot: whether a coaching turn actually used motivational interviewing technique, whether a goal was properly specified, whether an analysis handled missingness. The paper says it directly — experts "identified major quality differences that, although not immediately apparent to end users, are fundamental to providing safe and robust guidance."

**And the consequence the paper does not draw.** A consumer health product changes someone's health only if that person **uses it, trusts it, and acts on it** — all three governed by what the *user* perceives. **An improvement visible only to experts is a real improvement in craft and a near-invisible one in effect.** 17.1% says the engineering got better; **4.6% says how much of that reached the person.**

**The everyday version.** A sommelier reliably distinguishes a £15 wine from a £60 one and will tell you the gap is large. Most dinner guests cannot. Both reports are accurate. **If the goal is for the guests to enjoy the evening more, £45 has bought something they will not notice** — and you need the guests' judgement, not the sommelier's, to learn that.

**Watch out for** a paper reporting expert ratings prominently and end-user ratings in a supplement, or pooling the two. **Ask which group's judgement determines real-world benefit, and look up that group's number.** For clinician-facing tools the clinician is both user and expert, so the gap does not arise; **for consumer tools it is the central measurement problem.** This is technique 229 and entry 68's zero-benefit finding arriving from a different direction: **the deployed thing is the system plus the user, and the user's perception is part of the system.**

**255. A harm rate is a rate. Multiply it by the deployment.**

Five clinicians reviewed the agent's health summaries for 30 real participants: **83% no or minimal potential harm, 15% mild, 2% moderate-to-severe.** The authors call the figures "non-trivial" and carry the concern into the Discussion.

83% reads as reassuring. **Rates do not stay percentages when a product ships.** Carrying the reported rates forward:

- **1,000 summaries**: **20** moderate-to-severe, **150** mild
- **100,000**: **2,000** moderate-to-severe, **15,000** mild
- **1,000,000**: **20,000** moderate-to-severe, **150,000** mild

For calibration on what consumer scale means here, **entry 68 analysed a clinical AI platform that served 271 million queries in 30 months.**

**Two caveats, stated and not weakening the point.** This is **not a deployment estimate** — a shipped product has different prompts, guardrails and users. And **harm means potential harm from a text summary as judged by a reviewing clinician**, not a documented injury. Neither changes the shape: **a 2% moderate-to-severe rate is tolerable in a clinician-reviewed prototype and intolerable in a product reaching a million people unsupervised.** The authors understood this, which is why their Ethical risks section proposes high-risk query detection and clinician handoff.

**The everyday version.** A process that spoils 2% of batches sounds fine. At one batch a week it is one bad batch a year. **At ten thousand batches a day it is two hundred before lunch.** The rate did not change; the exposure did.

**Watch out for** a safety or error rate reported as a percentage with no statement of intended scale. **Multiply it out yourself at the target scale and ask whether the absolute number is acceptable.** Note the incentive: **this is the one calculation a developer has no reason to publish**, which is why running the review at all earns the credit this paper gets.

**256. Multiplicity across many rubrics: the two-layer approach, and how to find which claims it decides.**

Rating three systems on seventeen quality dimensions is a lot of tests. Naive Bonferroni — α/17 = **0.0029** — is too strict, because the rubrics are correlated (a system good at natural conversation tends to be good at empowerment) and each was prespecified as a separate question.

**What this paper did, which is the right answer.** Each rubric was "treated as a confirmatory test of a pre-specified, conceptually distinct quality dimension," so no correction *within* the primary analysis, justified by prespecification. **Then, to check that framing was not understating the error rate, an across-rubric Holm–Bonferroni robustness check was additionally run, with its outcome reported in each result table's caption.** Around it: **GLMM with a log link, system as fixed effect and rater (plus conversation script) as random effects** — so repeated ratings of one transcript by one person are not treated as independent; **Tukey's HSD** for pairwise system contrasts; **Friedman then Wilcoxon with Holm–Bonferroni** for rankings; effect sizes and CIs throughout; all tests two-sided.

**This is the best multiplicity treatment in this reading list.** The layering is the part to copy: **prespecify, analyse per-outcome, then show the work under a stricter correction so a reader can see which findings depend on the framing.**

**How to use it as a reader.** Compare the p-values against the strict threshold yourself:

- personalised coaching, **p = 0.001** → clears 0.0029
- natural conversation, **p = 0.002** → clears 0.0029
- data science vs parallel, **p = 0.016** → does not
- user empowerment, **p = 0.032** → does not
- overall quality, **p = 0.044** → does not

**Three of five end-user findings are the ones a cross-rubric correction puts in question — and "overall quality" is among them.** The check exists, which is to the paper's credit; its result lives in supplementary captions I could not retrieve. **The honest position: the check was run, I cannot verify its outcome, and these three claims are exactly the ones it adjudicates.**

**Why it matters, worked.** Rate two *identical* systems on 17 rubrics by pure noise: the chance at least one shows p<0.05 is 1 − 0.95¹⁷ = **58%.** More likely than not you will find a "significant" difference between two things that are the same. **Watch out for** many rubrics, several marginal p-values and no statement of multiplicity handling — **and its mirror**, a crushing Bonferroni applied to correlated prespecified outcomes that declares nothing significant. **Neither extreme is right; this paper's two layers are.**

**257. An odds ratio on paired data is not a percentage improvement.** Factuality here: **83.6% against 81.8%**, McNemar χ² = 9.51, p = 0.002, **OR = 1.67.** The accuracy gain is **1.8 percentage points.** The odds ratio describes the ratio of **discordant pairs** — items where exactly one system was right — so 1.67 means about five wins for every three losses *among the disagreements*. **It is the correct statistic for paired data and reads like a far bigger effect than it is.** Whenever an OR comes from a paired test, **find the two raw accuracies** — and note the significance here comes from n > 2,000, not from the size of the difference.

**258. Read a top-one accuracy as its complement.** **Top-one diagnostic accuracy 46.1%** means **the right condition was not the first suggestion 53.9% of the time** — on common, self-reported symptoms. The paper's framing (+4.7 points over prior state of the art, p<0.001) is fair for a methods comparison and is not what a person typing symptoms into a box needs. **The same move applies to top-five and top-ten figures, which sound impressive while silently conceding that the earlier answers were wrong.**

**259. Validating an autorater, and what ICC = 0.838 licenses.** Human evaluation at this scale is unaffordable, so the paper built an LLM autorater and **validated it against 354 annotations from 10 expert data scientists, reporting ICC(3,1) = 0.838.** The intraclass correlation measures how much of the score variation reflects real differences between the things scored rather than rater disagreement; by convention **0.75–0.90 is good**, above 0.90 excellent. **So 0.838 licenses using the autorater to rank systems in aggregate, and does not license treating any single autorated score as a human judgement.** The paper's own guard is correct — autorater conclusions "should remain cross-checked against blinded human judgements as models and prompts change." **Watch out for** an LLM-judged benchmark with no human validation and no agreement statistic: that was precisely entry 68's gap, and this paper does not have it.

**260. Role-played personalisation measures whether output reads as personal, not whether it is.** The live studies used **condition-stratified matching to WEAR-ME personas**, so participants judged responses built on **a stranger's wearable and blood data.** The paper names the problem — **"perspective-taking bias"** — and prescribes the fix: longitudinal studies should "recruit people **responding as themselves**." **The rule: when the capability under test is personalisation, the participant must be the person being personalised to.** Otherwise you measure the *plausibility* of personalisation, a different and easier thing. **This is technique 249 transplanted into user research** — a convincing-looking personal touch is not evidence the personalisation is correct, exactly as a stable SHAP ranking is not evidence a model discriminates. And it is the limitation that bears on this paper's own distinctive claim: **trustworthiness 96.9%, personalisation preference 71.9% and personalised coaching p<0.001 were all produced by people reasoning about someone else's data.**

**261. With three or more arms, check which comparison the paper leads with.** Latency: single agent **36.4 ± 1.6 s**, the orchestrated system **205.3 ± 15.0 s**, parallel multi-agent **244.4 ± 11.4 s**. The Results section reports the system as faster and cheaper "than the parallel multi-agent baseline" and headlines a "relative computational cost advantage" — **true, by 1.19×.** Against the single agent it is **5.64× slower with 2.17× the LLM calls**, and end users ranked that single agent only 22 points behind it. Both comparisons are in the paper; **only the flattering one is in the Results heading, and the Discussion is straight about the rest.** **Look up the absolute value: 205 seconds is a product decision, "19% faster" is not.**

**262. A baseline that wins on one dimension, then gets "addressed" between studies.** The simple baseline beat the coaching agent on **progress measurement**, per both end users *and* experts, and the paper says it "explicitly address[es] this in the integrated PHA design." **Honest reporting — and it means the coaching study and the integrated study evaluate different artefacts.** When a weakness found in study one is fixed before study two, **the two sets of numbers are not comparable on that dimension**, and a reader should check whether any headline claim rests on the fix.

**263. Capability evaluation and outcome evaluation answer different questions.** This paper measures **whether the conversations are good** — 11 tasks, >7,000 annotations, expert and user rubrics, preference rankings. It does not measure **whether anyone's health changed**, and says so: "evidence of conversational quality and short-term task efficacy, **not sustained behavioural change or improvement in longitudinal health outcomes**." **Both are legitimate; only one is a health claim.** The design that answers the second is a multi-session deployment measuring engagement, adherence and behaviour over time, **with people using their own data** (technique 260). **When a health-AI paper reports preference rankings and rubric scores, it is a capability paper and the health question is still open.**

**What caps entry 75 at 3/5, recorded so the grade stays auditable.** **No outcome or longitudinal data of any kind** — the authors state it. **The live claims rest on 17, 31 and 45 end users, and the paper's strongest single result (experts rating the integrated system 72.8 ± 1.4% against 64.3 ± 1.3% and 54.4 ± 1.4%, all pairwise p<0.001) comes from three health experts**; a synthetic replication added five more and found consistent patterns, which helps. **Participants role-played other people's data** (technique 260). **Google built it, Google's Gemini runs it, Google evaluated it** — no independent evaluation, with the open-source release as the right partial remedy, though its repository address sits in an availability statement I could not retrieve. And **three of five end-user significance claims sit below the threshold the paper's own cross-rubric check adjudicates** (technique 256).

**What would have earned a 4 or 5**, and it is a short list because the execution is already at that level: **a multi-session deployment with participants using their own wearable and blood data**, reporting engagement and any behavioural measure; **more than three experts on the headline comparison**; and the supplementary captions in reach so the robustness-check outcomes could be read. Nothing about the conduct needs fixing — **the ceiling here is the design, not the craft.**

**Positive practice worth naming, because this list is quicker to find faults.** **1,350 hours split 651 end-user / 699 expert** — most papers in this field use one group or neither. **An autorater validated before use with a reported ICC** (technique 259). **Model-agnosticism checked rather than assumed.** **Three numbered IRB protocols** (Advarra #Pro 00074093, Advarra #GH-SCD-001, WCG #20244970) with informed consent throughout. **A safety review run and its bad half published.** **An ethical-risks section that is specific rather than ritual** — curated retrieval restricted to WHO, PubMed, NICE and CDC, provenance shown beside each recommendation, high-risk query detection, explicit clinician handoff. **And the paragraph that cost the authors something:** rather than claim a triage capability, "simply deferring such questions to clinicians may not address the delays that often motivate people to seek artificial intelligence support," followed by a concrete alternative — help users record symptoms, spot patterns, prepare questions, navigate care, **without diagnostic claims.** That is a harder position than either "it can triage" or "ask your doctor."

**Reporting-guideline pattern, now FIVE entries deep.** Entry 69 named TRIPOD+AI and deposited the checklist. Entry 70 named STROBE while arguably being more careful. Entry 71 named none. Entry 73 named none and called for a checklist that does not exist. Entry 74 named none and did more of what TRIPOD+AI asks than any of them. **Entry 75 names none — only Nature's own Reporting Summary — while running the best multiplicity treatment in the list.** **DECIDE-AI exists for exactly this kind of early-stage live evaluation and is not mentioned.** Five consecutive entries, and **the correlation between naming a checklist and following good practice is now reliably near zero. Read the methods.** This is what the deferred BMJ review (42869550, mean TRIPOD-LLM completeness 40%) quantifies — **and that review is tomorrow's pick.**

**What could not be verified, stated so the gaps are on the record.** Supplementary Sections A–G with **Tables 1–45 and Figures 1–7**; all main and Extended Data figures; **the data-availability, code-availability and competing-interests statements** (only the "Online content" pointer was extracted), hence **no funding or COI reportable and no repository address for the stated open-source release**; the **Holm–Bonferroni robustness-check outcomes** in the table captions; and whether the 2% moderate-to-severe figure is a proportion of summaries or of ratings. **Nothing was reproduced that I had not seen.**

**Internal consistency checks run on this paper, all passed.** 651 + 699 = 1,350 hours. 83 + 15 + 2 = 100% in the safety review. 50 personas × 3 systems = 150 synthesised conversations. **All four reported ranking triples sum to 100%** (46.7/28.9/24.4; 44.4/31.1/24.4; 66.7/20.7/12.6; and the expert best-system split). **No arithmetic inconsistency found anywhere in the paper** — the first entry in some weeks with none.

**Deferred status, 2026-11-12.** **Nature personal health agent (42907512) — RESOLVED after 22 days, decoded today as entry 75.** **BMJ LLM-trial reporting review (42869550) — day 29, still no PMC body, and it is TOMORROW'S PICK as an abstract-only decode**, owed under the twenty-one-day rule that fired today and slipped one day for full text. Gait foundation model (42995231) — **day 4**, no PMC copy, abstract-only date **2026-11-30**. *Annals* *Choosing Wisely* (42896192) — **day 23**, weekly re-check, abstract-only date **2026-11-19**; standing note that its 5.4% / 0.6–12.2% CI is a clustering signature computed across 15 recommendations rather than 4,050 responses.

**Landmark check, 2026-11-05 → 11-12.** Four records, all previously assessed: the NEJM mass-surveillance Perspective (43001754), the *Nature Medicine* gait commentary (43003904), the gait foundation model itself (42995231), and the Nature structRFM RNA model (42983082). **No new candidates; none displaces the queue.**

**Plan set on 2026-11-12.** **Next run takes the BMJ reporting-completeness review (42869550), abstract-only**, as owed. It is the natural successor to today's entry: entry 75 names no reporting guideline while running the best statistics in the list, **and the BMJ review is the paper that quantifies exactly that disconnect** — mean completeness of 40% against TRIPOD-LLM, 44% against CONSORT 2025, 59% CONSORT-AI, 52% CHART, 51% SPIRIT 2025, 57% SPIRIT-AI, 61% against the WHO dataset. **After the BMJ decode, shelf item (C)** — the diabetic retinopathy model at AUROC 0.972 from eight routine labs (42974596, PMC13727297) — as the contrast with entry 74 that yesterday's plan set out, with the first question unchanged: **could the label be inside the inputs?** **Shelf unchanged at five spares — (B) bone-tumour reference-standard review, (C) next after the BMJ, (D) glaucoma OCT two-device external validation, (E) MIBC pCR with 102 models screened, (F) bitewing mannequin-only.**

## From entry 76 — Chen et al., reporting completeness of LLM trials (BMJ, 2026; decoded 2026-11-13, ABSTRACT ONLY)

**The abstract-only fallback, and every attempt that justified it.** PMID 42869550 was first deferred on **2026-10-14** and re-checked daily. **Today is day 31.** Attempts today, in order: PMID-to-PMCID conversion returned **no PMC identifier**; **Scite** `read_fulltext` returned a paid-plan error, as on every prior attempt; the publisher host **`www.bmj.com` failed DNS resolution** under this session's network policy — the same block that affects `doi.org`, and per the session's own guidance no further hosts were tried; a **Consensus** semantic search returned the record with the identical abstract and **no open-access or preprint full text**. **Genuinely inaccessible, declared in the summary header, in Section 3, and at every claim that depends on it.**

**A log correction, recorded rather than overwritten.** The 2026-11-10 entry recorded the BMJ review at **day 28**. The 2026-11-12 entry recorded **day 29**, repeating 2026-11-11's count instead of advancing to day 30. Anchoring on day 28 = 2026-11-10 puts day 1 at **2026-10-14** and today at **day 31**. **The slip changed no decision** — the twenty-one-day threshold had passed more than a week earlier — but the count is corrected here and in the summary, and the error is left on the record because an audit trail that silently self-repairs is not an audit trail.

**264. A completeness score measures the checklist as much as the paper.**

This is the entry's load-bearing lesson and the paper supplies an unusually clean demonstration of it. **The same 68 trial reports, scored against four different instruments, came out at 40%, 44%, 52% and 59%** — a **19-percentage-point spread with the documents held constant.**

**Three choices are buried inside any "reporting completeness" percentage**, and each one moves the number:

- **Which items.** TRIPOD-LLM carries **19 main items and 50 subitems** and is explicitly **modular** (14 main / 32 subitems apply across all categories); CONSORT-AI is an extension bolted onto a general trial checklist. Different questions cannot share an answer.
- **What counts as reported.** A paper says it used "GPT-4." Strict rater: the item asks which version, so **no**. Lenient rater: a model was named, so **yes**. **One judgement call across 50 subitems and 68 papers moves the headline by double digits.**
- **What the denominator is.** Score modular items only when applicable and every paper has its own denominator, so the "mean completeness" is an average of percentages with different bases — a legitimate choice, and **a different quantity** from "share of all items met across the corpus." Which one this paper reports is not visible from the abstract.

**The external proof that this is not hypothetical.** According to PubMed, **He Z et al.** (*npj Digital Medicine* 2026;9(1), PMID 42230743, PMC13530203) scored **mean CONSORT-AI adherence at 78.8%** across 55 LLM-intervention studies (Jan 2022–Jun 2025). **Entry 76 scored CONSORT-AI at 59%** across 68 reports from a window containing almost all of He's. **Nearly 20 points apart on the same named instrument.** The literature did not move that far in months; **the scoring rule did** — and neither paper's abstract publishes it.

**The everyday version.** Two inspectors grade one kitchen. One uses a 20-point sheet and passes an item if the practice exists: **78%**. The other uses a 50-point sheet and passes only if the practice is written down, dated and followed: **59%**. Neither is lying and neither score is wrong, but they are answers to two different questions. **Seeing only one score, you cannot tell which question you were answered.**

**Watch out for** any adherence, completeness or quality percentage quoted without the **instrument, its version, and the scoring rule**. **Three questions:** which checklist and version; what counted as reported for a partially reported item; was the denominator identical across papers. **Without all three the score is not comparable to any other score** — including a later one from the same field, which kills the use such scores are most often put to: showing improvement over time.

**265. A score with no comparator is not yet a finding.**

Entry 76 calls 40–61% "modest." **Modest against what?** The implied referent is 100%, which no medical literature has reached on any checklist — a standard against which every field fails and the word carries no information.

**The real comparator, retrieved separately.** According to PubMed, **Srinivasan A et al.** (*JAMA Netw Open* 2025;8(8):e2529418, PMID 40875232, PMC12395317) audited **21,041 randomised trials** across 30 disciplines: mean CONSORT compliance **57.0% (95% CI 56.8–57.2%)** for 2010–2024, up from **27.3%** in 1966–1990, ranging from **35.2% (pharmacology) to 63.4% (urology)**; within those trials **allocation concealment reported in 16.1%** and **external validity discussed in 1.6%**.

**All seven of entry 76's means — 40, 44, 51, 52, 57, 59, 61 — fall inside that ordinary 35.2–63.4% inter-speciality range.** On generic trial reporting, LLM trials look like medicine.

**Two caveats that make this a calibration, not a verdict, and they are the point.** CONSORT 2025 is an **expanded** instrument versus the CONSORT 2010 those 21,041 were scored against, so a lower score is partly expected; and those 21,041 were scored by **a language model** (91.7% agreement with experts on a 70-article subset, macro F1 0.86), not human raters. **Different ruler, different rater — exactly technique 264's problem.** Which is why the comparison needed to come from **inside** the paper: one contemporaneous sample of non-LLM trials, same three raters, same rules. **That single addition converts "LLM trials report modestly" into either "unusually opaque, by this much" or "trial reporting is weak everywhere and the LLM-specific items are the exception."**

**The everyday version.** A student scored 44%. Alarming — until you learn the class average was 45% and the test was built so nobody finishes. **The score told you nothing; the distribution told you everything.**

**Watch out for** evaluative words — *modest, suboptimal, poor, inadequate* — attached to a percentage with no reference distribution. **Ask what a good score would be and what comparable work scores.** If the paper does not say, find a baseline before repeating the number, because the adjective is doing work the data has not done.

**266. When the measurement is a human judgement, the reliability of that judgement IS the precision.**

Every figure in entry 76 came from **three calibrated raters** deciding, item by item, whether a document reported a thing. Respectable method, specific weak point — **and the weak point is not sample size.**

**Two sources of error, not one.** With a blood test the instrument is fixed and uncertainty is sampling: more patients, tighter interval. With human raters scoring subjective items there is also **disagreement between raters**, and **that does not shrink when you add documents.** If two raters would differ on a third of items, the 40% carries an irreducible wobble no number of extra papers removes — **you have measured an unreliable quantity very precisely.**

**How it is normally reported:** **Cohen's or Fleiss' kappa** (agreement corrected for chance) and the **ICC** (share of score variation reflecting real differences between documents rather than rater disagreement). Conventions: **ICC 0.75–0.90 good, >0.90 excellent**; kappa >0.8 strong, 0.6–0.8 substantial, <0.4 poor. **Entry 75 the previous day reported ICC(3,1) = 0.838 before trusting its autorater, and Fleiss' κ ≥ 0.684 for its human panels** — that is the practice done properly (technique 259).

**Neither statistic appears in entry 76's abstract.** "Calibrated" says the raters were trained together; **it does not say they then agreed.** The full text may report it — flagged as unverifiable, not absent. **But from the abstract, the precision of all seven headline numbers is unknown.**

**And ordinary precision is missing too.** No confidence interval on any of the seven, and **two of them rest on 15 protocols.** Arithmetic: at a per-document SD of 10–20 points, the SE on **68 reports** is **1.2–2.4 points** (95% interval ±2 to ±5); on **15 protocols** it is **2.6–5.2 points** (95% interval **±5 to ±10**). **So SPIRIT 2025 at 51% and SPIRIT-AI at 57% are six points apart with heavily overlapping intervals** — not a supportable difference from fifteen documents.

**The everyday version.** Three judges score a dive. If they agree, the average means something and more dives sharpen it. **If one marks three points harder than another, the average is a number about the judges as much as the divers**, and a hundred more dives will not fix it. **Before trusting the leaderboard, ask how far apart the judges are.**

**Watch out for** any study whose primary outcome is a human rating — chart review, image reading, checklist scoring, qualitative coding, quality appraisal — described as "trained," "calibrated" or "independent" **with no kappa or ICC**. Those words describe the process; the statistic describes the result. **And a point estimate with no interval, on a sample in the dozens, is a number with the uncertainty edited out.** Insist hardest when the paper's own purpose is to hold others to a reporting standard.

**267. The registration-to-report ratio reads a field's pipeline, and its publication bias.** Entry 76: **378 registrations against 68 reports = 5.6 to 1**, with trial reports only **14.8%** of all 461 records. Some is ordinary lag — a trial registered in 2025 cannot have reported. But a ratio that size says most of what has been promised has not been delivered, **and the gap is exactly where publication bias lives**: a trial that found nothing is the one that quietly never reports. The abstract uses 378 as a denominator for the WHO completeness score. **It is also the paper's best single piece of evidence about the state of the field.** **The actionable move:** when someone cites this literature, ask what became of the other five sixths.

**268. Nominal search window versus effective window.** Entry 76 searched **1 Jan 2017 to 6 Dec 2025 — 8.9 years** — but usable LLMs postdate late 2022 (**3.0 years**) and the authors state reports were predominantly published **2024–2025**. So roughly seven of the nine years contributed almost nothing, at about **22 reports per year** at most. **Casting back to 2017 is the correct conservative choice and not a flaw.** The consequence is: **about two years of literature cannot show whether reporting is improving**, so the question a reader most wants answered — are the new guidelines working? — is unanswerable by this design. **Check whether a review's stated window matches the window that actually contains its data before reading any trend into it.**

**269. A mean of per-study percentages is not the share of items met.** Entry 76 reports "mean reporting completeness." Against a **modular** instrument like TRIPOD-LLM, where only some items apply to a given study type, each paper plausibly carries its own denominator — so the headline is an **average of ratios with different bases**, not the proportion of all applicable items satisfied across the corpus. **The two can differ substantially**, and which is reported is not visible from the abstract. Mechanically, 40% on 19 main / 50 subitems corresponds to roughly **8 of 19 main items and 20 of 50 subitems** satisfied by a typical report — **about 11 main items and 30 subitems left unreported** — but only if the denominator was constant, which cannot be assumed. **Whenever a score averages percentages, ask what each percentage was out of.**

**270. When base guideline and AI extension move in opposite directions to expectation, say so rather than smooth it.** On the same documents, **CONSORT-AI (59%) beat CONSORT 2025 (44%) by 15 points**, and **SPIRIT-AI (57%) beat SPIRIT 2025 (51%) by 6** — while **TRIPOD-LLM, the most LLM-specific instrument, came lowest at 40%.** If LLM-specific reporting were simply the weak spot, the extensions should score *worse* than their bases, and two of three comparisons run the other way. Candidate explanations are easy to list and untestable from an abstract: the extensions have fewer or more easily satisfied items; the 2025 base revisions added demanding new requirements; the extensions' items happen to overlap what authors include anyway. **Recorded as an unresolved inconsistency in the paper's own numbers, not adjudicated.** **The reader's move: notice that the direction of the effect reverses between instruments, and ask the full text why** — it is the clearest available hint that technique 264's ruler problem is operating inside this paper too.

**271. Guideline authors grading adherence to their own guideline.** Verified from PubMed: **five of entry 76's twelve authors — Afshar, Celi, Moons, Collins and Bitterman — are also authors of TRIPOD-LLM** (Gallifant J et al.), Bitterman as entry 76's corresponding author, with Collins and Moons additionally the originators of TRIPOD itself. **The case for it is real:** item-level scoring against a modular 19-item / 50-subitem instrument needs people who know what each item means, and naive raters would produce noise (technique 266). **The case against is equally real:** the instrument scoring lowest, and therefore carrying the paper's strongest claim, is the instrument five authors wrote — nobody is better placed to apply it, and nobody has more at stake in it mattering. **Not an allegation of anything undisclosed** — this is precisely what a competing-interests statement covers, and that statement sits in the part of the paper that could not be retrieved. **The general rule: when a paper measures compliance with a standard, check whether its authors wrote the standard, and look for at least some raters with no stake in any instrument used.**

**272. Meta-research is still research, and the same standards apply to it.** Entry 76 documents that others omit methodological detail, and **presents its own seven primary outcomes as bare point estimates with no confidence intervals and no inter-rater reliability.** Stated without labouring it, and with the caveat that the full text may carry both. **The broader point is not irony but symmetry:** a study about reporting quality is appraised by the same questions as any other — what was the measurement, how reliable was it, how precise, compared with what. **A paper's subject matter earns it no exemption from its own thesis.** Note also what entry 76 is *not* scored on here: PRISMA and a PROSPERO registration are absent from its abstract, and **scoring it on that would be cheap and unfair**, since abstracts routinely omit both.

**What caps entry 76 at 3/5, recorded so the grade stays auditable.** **Abstract-only access** — the Methods are invisible, so **the one thing that makes or breaks a study of this kind, how an item was scored, cannot be verified**, and technique 264 shows that choice can move the headline by twenty points. **No inter-rater reliability and no confidence intervals** in what can be read (technique 266), with two of seven means resting on 15 documents. **No comparator arm** (technique 265), which leaves "modest" without a referent. **Five-author overlap with one of the scored instruments** (technique 271). **Why not 2:** the design is genuinely sound — three databases plus two registries, strict human-participant and health-outcome eligibility that correctly excludes the vast exam-question literature, instruments matched to document type rather than applied blindly, dual independent screening, registrations and protocols included rather than published reports alone, and a conclusion calibrated to what the evidence supports (*"modest adherence"* with gaps named, **not** a claim that the trials are wrong). The abstract-only entry in this list graded 2 had problems deeper than invisibility — an unvalidated classifier generating every percentage, and a metric that was arithmetically forced. **Why not 4: it is a 4 if the full text reports an ICC above roughly 0.75, publishes its scoring rule, and gives confidence intervals** — and the entry will be upgraded if a PMC body appears.

**The finding inside this paper that is stronger than its own headline.** Look at where the floor sits. The **generic** instruments come in at 44, 52, 59, 61 — unremarkable against the 35.2–63.4% medical baseline (technique 265). **TRIPOD-LLM, the only instrument written specifically for language models, comes lowest at 40%.** So: **the field is roughly normal at the reporting it inherited and worst at the reporting that is new to it** — model versioning, inference parameters, prompt and preprocessing detail, access modality. **The authors' own narrative says exactly this; their flat list of seven percentages buries it.** That reframing is the single most useful thing to carry out of this entry.

**Reporting-guideline pattern — the six-entry sequence now has a number attached.** Entry 69 named TRIPOD+AI and deposited the checklist. Entry 70 named STROBE while arguably being more careful. Entry 71 named none. Entry 73 named none and called for a checklist that does not exist. Entry 74 named none while doing more of what TRIPOD+AI asks than any of them. Entry 75 named none while running the best multiplicity treatment in this list. **Entry 76 is the paper that measures the whole phenomenon, and it quantifies it: 40% mean completeness against the instrument built for the job.** **That number IS the mechanism behind this list's standing rule.** A checklist filled in at 40% **cannot discriminate careful work from careless work** — which is precisely why entries 74 and 75 could do excellent work while naming nothing, and why naming TRIPOD+AI did not make entry 69 the most careful paper here. **The checklist is not the quality signal. Read the methods.** Cite this entry whenever someone offers guideline adherence as evidence of study quality.

**Pairing worth keeping.** Entry 75 was a consumer health agent whose own clinician panel judged **2% of its outputs capable of moderate-to-severe harm**, and it was unusual precisely because it ran that safety review and published the bad half (techniques 254–255). **Entry 76's finding is that most trials in this space reliably do neither:** *"Harms planning, monitoring, and reporting were often incompletely reported, limiting the critical appraisal of the safety of LLM interventions in healthcare."* **Read the two together** — one shows what honest safety reporting looks like, the other shows how rare it is.

**Arithmetic verified today, all passing.** Record counts close exactly (**68 + 15 + 378 = 461**, no residual bucket). Registrations per report **5.56**; reports **14.8%** of records. Nominal window **8.9 years**, post-ChatGPT **3.0 years**, bulk 2024–2025, ~**22 reports/year**. Report-level instrument spread **40–59% = 19 points on identical documents**. Base-to-extension gaps **+15** (CONSORT) and **+6** (SPIRIT), both against TRIPOD-LLM's lowest-of-all **40%**. **All seven** means inside the external **35.2–63.4%** range. Precision arithmetic on n=15 and n=68 as in technique 266. **No arithmetic inconsistency found in the abstract** — the only correction made today was to this list's own day count, recorded above.

**What could not be verified, stated so the gaps are on the record.** The entire Methods and Results beyond the structured abstract; any confidence interval on any of the seven means; any inter-rater reliability statistic; the scoring rule and the denominator used; the per-item percentages behind every narrative claim about provenance, versioning and harms; whether any non-LLM comparator was scored; PROSPERO registration and this review's own protocol, data and code availability; **funding and competing interests, including the five-author TRIPOD-LLM overlap.** **Nothing was reproduced that was not seen, and no full-text link is given because none could be verified.**

**Deferred status, 2026-11-13.** **BMJ LLM-trial reporting review (42869550) — RESOLVED, decoded today as entry 76, abstract-only at day 31.** ***Annals* *Choosing Wisely* (42896192) — day 24**, re-checked today, **still no PMC copy**, weekly, **abstract-only date 2026-11-19** (six days out); standing note that its 5.4% / 0.6–12.2% CI is a clustering signature computed across 15 recommendations rather than 4,050 responses. **Gait foundation model (42995231) — day 5**, re-checked today, **still no PMC copy**, abstract-only date **2026-11-30**; it carries the largest multiplicity problem this list will have faced (**1,887 of 3,210 tested phenotypes called significant**) and the first question for it is what false-discovery-rate procedure ran across those 3,210 tests.

**Landmark check, 2026-11-06 → 11-13.** Three records, all previously assessed: the NEJM mass-surveillance Perspective (43001754), the *Nature Medicine* gait commentary (43003904), and the gait foundation model itself (42995231). **No new candidates; none displaces the queue.**

**Plan set on 2026-11-13.** **Next run takes shelf item (C)**, the diabetic retinopathy model reporting **AUROC 0.972 from eight routine laboratory values** (PMID 42974596, PMC13727297) — owed since 2026-11-11 and twice postponed, once for entry 75's unblocking and once for today's abstract-only debt. It stays the deliberate contrast with entry 74's **AUC 0.587**, and today's entry sharpens the pick: **technique 265 says a score with no comparator is not a finding, and 0.972 badly needs one.** **Two questions now, not one. (1) Could the label be inside the inputs?** — two of the eight predictors are **nephropathy and neuropathy**, the other two microvascular complications of diabetes, routinely coded alongside retinopathy. **(2) Against what baseline is 0.972 being called good?** — what does eight routine labs plus age and diabetes duration achieve in a plain logistic model, and what do published retinopathy risk scores achieve on the same data? **Shelf after today: five spares — (B) bone-tumour reference-standard review, (C) next, (D) glaucoma OCT two-device external validation, (E) MIBC pCR with 102 models screened, (F) bitewing mannequin-only.**

## From entry 77 — Laoh et al., diabetic retinopathy from routine labs (Smart Health, 2026; decoded 2026-11-14, FULL TEXT)

**Both pre-registered questions answered.** This pick was set on 2026-11-11 as the deliberate contrast with entry 74, carrying two questions written down **before** the paper was read. **(1) "Could the label be inside the inputs?"** — yes, and worse than anticipated: the **DR label is an ICD code** (362.0x, E10.31x–E10.35x, E11.31x–E11.35x), **two of the eight features are the other two members of the diabetic microvascular triad** drawn from the same records, three more are nephropathy's lab signature, and the design is **cross-sectional by the authors' own statement** with no index date, no washout and no incident-case definition. **(2) "Against what baseline is 0.972 good?"** — answered by the paper's own §4.4: **logistic regression 0.9713**, within **0.0011**. **Recording that both were posed in advance and both were answered from the paper's own numbers** — the value of writing the question down before reading.

**273. When every model from logistic regression to a transformer ties, the information is in the data, not the method — and that is a leakage detector.**

Entry 77 benchmarked five models on identical patients, identical preprocessing and an identical 0.50 threshold. The whole result:

- **logistic regression (24 var): AUROC 0.9713** → ~64.4% of variance
- **MLP (24 var): 0.9635** → ~61.6%
- **FT-Transformer, attention-based (24 var): 0.9739** → ~65.3%
- **XGBoost (24 var): 0.9750** → ~65.8%
- **proposed XGBoost (8 var): 0.9724** → ~64.8%

**Total spread 1.15 AUROC points.** A linear model with no interactions is **0.0011** from the proposed model and **0.0037** from the best. Via technique 224, **architecture buys about three percentage points of variance** out of sixty-five.

**Why this is diagnostic.** Model families succeed by exploiting different structure — logistic regression only adds weighted inputs; boosting finds thresholds and interactions; a neural net finds smooth non-linearities; attention learns which features to consult given the others. **If all four land in the same place there is almost no interaction structure and almost no non-linearity to find**, so the outcome is close to a weighted sum of a few inputs. **A problem that is trivially easy for every method is usually easy for a reason, and "the answer is partly already in the inputs" is the commonest reason.**

**The everyday version.** Predict umbrella ownership from two inputs: owns a raincoat, owns wellingtons. Logistic regression scores 97%. So do boosting, a neural net and a transformer. The right reaction is not "my simple model is impressively competitive" but **"wet-weather gear comes in sets"** — you have not learned to predict umbrellas, you have learned that these things share a cause.

**Watch out for** a model-comparison table where everything from the simplest baseline to the fanciest architecture lands within a point or two, **presented as evidence that the chosen model is efficient.** It is that, and it is also a signal to audit the inputs. **Two questions: could the label have been produced by the same process that produced any input — same coder, same visit, same chart? And is any input downstream of, or a sibling of, the outcome rather than upstream of it?** **This is the cheapest leakage test available** and needs no extra analysis, since most papers already have the table. **Cite this entry whenever a flat model zoo is offered as a virtue.**

**274. SMOTE calibrates your probabilities to a prevalence that does not exist.**

Entry 77 got the hard part right, and that comes first: **SMOTE was applied only to the training subset, after partitioning**, with the observed 17.25% prevalence preserved in validation and test — stated three separate times. **The catastrophic version (balancing before the split, which copies minority patients across the boundary) was avoided.** Many papers fail exactly there.

**But there is a second consequence and it is unaddressed.** After SMOTE the training set is ~50/50, so **the model learns probabilities for a world where half of diabetics have retinopathy**, then is deployed where **17.25%** do. **Every predicted probability comes out too high.**

**The correction is simple.** Convert to odds, multiply by (true prior odds ÷ training prior odds), convert back. Here (0.1725/0.8275) ÷ 1.0 = **0.2084**:

- model says **0.50** → true risk ~**17%**
- model says **0.70** → true risk ~**33%**
- model says **0.90** → true risk ~**65%**

**Two consequences pulling opposite ways.** The **classification is fine** — the 0.50 cut corresponds to a true risk near 17%, a sensible screening operating point arrived at by accident, so the reported sensitivity and specificity stand. But **the probabilities are unusable as risks**, and entry 77's stated purpose (help a physician "evaluate the risk," set "tailored screening intervals") is a use of the probability, not the label. **No calibration plot, slope, intercept or Brier score appears anywhere**, so none of this is visible.

**The everyday version.** Learn to judge rain in Scotland in November, where it rains half the time, then move to Arizona. Your *ranking* of days still works — the ones you call wettest are the wettest. **Every one of your probabilities is far too high.** Ranking transferred; numbers did not.

**Watch out for** any paper using SMOTE, class weighting or undersampling that reports probabilities or a risk score **without calibration.** **Discrimination (AUROC/AUPRC) is untouched by rebalancing because it depends only on ranking; calibration is destroyed by it.** Two questions: was balancing applied after the split, and **was the output recalibrated to the real prevalence?** **The first is the one everyone asks; the second is the one that matters once the first is answered correctly.** The tell: eight discrimination and classification metrics reported, zero calibration metrics.

**275. PPV is not a property of a model; it is a property of a model plus a population — and entry 77 showed this properly, unprompted.**

This is the practice worth copying. Sensitivity and specificity describe the test; **PPV and NPV describe what a result means for a patient and move with prevalence** even when the test is unchanged. Holding sens 0.9117 and spec 0.9622 fixed, entry 77 reported — and I reproduced all six values to within 0.0003:

- **5% prevalence: PPV 0.5597, NPV 0.9952**
- **10%: PPV 0.7285, NPV 0.9899**
- **17.25% (their cohort): PPV 0.8342, NPV 0.9812**
- **20%: PPV 0.8579, NPV 0.9776**

**Read the first line twice.** At 5% prevalence a positive result is **wrong about 44% of the time** — nearly half of those referred would not have DR. **The model has not changed; the AUROC is still 0.9724. Only the population changed.**

**Why:** as disease gets rarer there are more healthy people available to generate false positives and fewer sick people to generate true ones, so false positives come to dominate the positive column.

**Worked example.** 1,000 patients at 5% prevalence = 50 DR / 950 non-DR. Catch 91.17% of 50 → **46 true positives**; wrongly flag 3.78% of 950 → **36 false positives**; 46 ÷ 82 = **56%**. At 20%: 200 / 800 → 182 TP and 30 FP → 182 ÷ 212 = **86%**. **Same test, same numbers, two very different letters to send a patient.**

**Why it bites here:** entry 77's own motivating statistic is that **only 43% of diabetics get an annual eye exam**, and its 17.25% comes from a diagnosed, coded hospital-network extract. **A primary-care screening population would plausibly have lower recognised-DR prevalence**, pushing PPV toward the 56% end.

**Watch out for** PPV, NPV or "precision" quoted as fixed properties of a model, especially where cohort prevalence differs from the deployment setting. **Ask: what was the test-set prevalence, and what will it be in use?** Then recompute — it needs only sensitivity, specificity and prevalence and takes thirty seconds. **And give entry 77 credit: it ran the recomputation itself, which almost nothing else in this list has done.**

**276. Co-coded comorbidities are not predictors; they are relabelings of the outcome.** Retinopathy, nephropathy and neuropathy are **one disease process in three tissues**, all caused by cumulative exposure to high glucose. Entry 77 predicts an ICD code for the first using **ICD-coded comorbidity variables for the other two**, from the same records, plus **creatinine, albumin and hemoglobin** — the standard laboratory picture of the diabetic kidney disease already included as a code. **Four of eight features are kidney-related, one is nerve-related.** A patient coded with diabetic nephropathy *and* neuropathy is, almost by construction, someone with long-standing poorly-controlled diabetes already worked up by specialists. **The general rule: before accepting a predictor, ask whether it is a cause, a precursor, a sibling, or a consequence of the outcome.** Siblings and consequences inflate discrimination without adding predictive power — a treatment for the disease, a referral the disease generated, another complication of the same cause. **The fix is one line and the authors already have everything needed: report AUROC with and without the two comorbidity codes.** Until that ablation exists, their contribution to 0.9724 is unknown, and it is the first thing a reader should want.

**277. Cross-sectional is not screening; a screening claim needs an index date.** Entry 77's title says "Screening" and its introduction promises to let physicians "establish tailored screening intervals," while its limitations state the work is **"based on static, cross-sectional laboratory measurements."** Those cannot all be right. **A screening tool is defined by temporal order — it must see the patient before the diagnosis does.** Nothing in the retrieved text establishes an **index date**, requires labs to **precede** the DR code, imposes a **washout**, or separates **incident from prevalent** disease. A patient diagnosed in 2019 whose creatinine was drawn in 2023 counts as a correct prediction. **What the study demonstrates is association between concurrent records — a real finding and a different one.** **The design that would answer the intended question:** define an index date, require every input to precede it, exclude anyone with a DR code before it, and report the prediction horizon. **Ask of any "prediction" model: what would it have known at the moment it was supposed to be useful?**

**278. A SHAP direction that contradicts the epidemiology is a finding, not a footnote — and this is the inverse of entry 74.** Entry 77 reports that **higher age lowers predicted risk** and **lower hemoglobin lowers predicted risk.** Both run backwards: DR prevalence rises with age and diabetes duration, and anaemia is an established DR risk factor and a marker of diabetic CKD. **The likely mechanism is in the paper's own introduction** — nearly all type 1 diabetics develop DR after 20 years, and the cohort is capped at **18–65**. So a 28-year-old carrying DR codes is plausibly long-duration type 1 disease, while a 60-year-old in a primary-care extract is more often recently-diagnosed type 2 without retinopathy. **Diabetes duration and type are absent from the model, and age has been recruited as an inverted proxy for them** — a classic omitted-variable signature, and evidence the model learned a cohort artefact that external validation would expose. **Now the pairing that matters.** Entry 73 argued explanation plausibility is not validation; **entry 74 demonstrated it** — rectal EUD ranked first in all 25 repeats, right sign, established biology, on a model with AUC 0.587. **Entry 77 is the mirror image and the more hazardous direction:** superb performance, and two of four leading explanations implausible. **A reader who trusts the SHAP plot because the AUROC is 0.97 has the inference backwards** — the implausible explanation is evidence against the model's construct validity, not a curiosity the performance excuses. **Check explanation and performance separately, in both directions** (technique 249).

**279. A sensitivity analysis that reports the boundary at which your answer survives.** Entry 77 blends literature and data importance as **S = 0.223L + 0.777D**, described as **"a sensitivity boundary, not an optimized hyperparameter"** — specifically **"the most data-dominant configuration examined at which the same eight variables remained within the top-eight selected set."** Read carefully: **push past 77.7% data weighting and the feature set changes**, so **at least 22.3% literature weight is required** to retain these eight features. That is a weaker claim than "literature and data agree," and close to the opposite of how the stability result reads at first pass. **The authors reported the boundary rather than hiding it, which is to their credit.** **The reader's move: when a paper reports the parameter value at which its conclusion still holds, note which side of the boundary the conclusion lives on** — "stable up to X" means "not stable past X."

**280. When your novel component correlates ρ = 0.98 with the simple version, say so — and they did.** TF-RCR's distinguishing ingredient is the Relative Citation Ratio weighting. The authors' own check: **the RCR-weighted ranking and the plain unweighted term-frequency ranking agree at Spearman ρ = 0.98**, which they gloss as RCR "refin[ing] rather than replac[ing]" the frequency signal. **Fair, and it also means the citation weighting is close to decorative** — operationally, "TF-RCR" is counting how often a variable is mentioned. **Reporting it anyway is a genuine credit**; most authors would have omitted a check that undercuts the method in the title. **General practice to copy: run your novel component against the obvious simple alternative and publish the correlation.** **General practice to look for as a reader:** when a paper names a method after a component, check whether that component is doing any work.

**281. A feature-selection method that ranks a list you already had is not knowledge discovery.** Entry 77's TF-RCR candidate vocabulary **was the 24 structured variables already in the dataset.** The 13,002-article corpus (PubMed, Jan 2023, 1990–Jan 2023, `("diabetic retinopathy") AND (predict OR factor OR determine)`) therefore **ordered a predetermined shortlist** — it discovered no variable, proposed none the authors lacked, and ruled none out. A legitimate use, and much weaker than "incorporating medical domain knowledge" suggests. **Compounding it, the authors note TF-RCR measures prominence, not direction, with no negation detection** — so **a variable repeatedly reported as NOT associated with the outcome scores highly.** Stated honestly. **The literature signal is "how much is this discussed," not "how much does this matter."**

**282. An interpretability criterion the final model fails.** Entry 77 defines, from Miller's 7±2, an interpretability score of **1 for ≤7 features and 7÷x above** — then ships **eight** features, scoring **0.875**, a self-imposed 12.5% penalty. Nothing turns on it numerically, and the entire case for eight features over 24 rests on a threshold the model exceeds by one. **A criterion the preferred option does not meet is doing rhetorical rather than analytic work.** Worth noting whenever a paper constructs a bespoke metric and then selects a model that scores below its own ceiling.

**What caps entry 77 at 2/5, recorded so the grade stays auditable.** **The study cannot distinguish predicting DR from detecting that it has already been coded** (techniques 276–277), and the entire clinical case depends on that distinction. **No calibration of any kind, in a paper that used SMOTE** (274). **Internal validation only**, stated. **Two of four leading SHAP directions contradict DR epidemiology, unremarked** (278). **No reporting guideline** — and uniquely in this list, naming one would have changed the paper. **Why not 1:** nothing here is incompetent or evasive. **Why not 3:** the grade tracks what a reader may safely conclude, and "AUROC 0.97 for DR screening from eight routine labs" would be a significant public-health finding if established — while the two things that would establish it (a temporal design, and a model not using the other two microvascular complications) are both absent.

**The ML hygiene exceeds the grade, and that is worth stating plainly.** **Patient-level stratified split** with an explicit guarantee no patient appears in two subsets. **Imputation and scaling fitted on training only.** **Test set held out from all development**, validation carrying model selection. **SMOTE training-only, after partitioning, prevalence preserved downstream** — stated three times. **1,000 patient-level bootstrap resamples** across eight metrics. **Same-cohort benchmarking against four comparators** with identical patients, pipeline and threshold, and an explicit statement that external published comparisons were *retired* to avoid "conflating differences in cohort composition, prevalence, preprocessing, and outcome definition." **AUPRC alongside AUROC. Unprompted prevalence-scenario PPV/NPV.** And hedging in the right direction throughout: **repeated refusals to call XGBoost interpretable**, an explicit definition of what interpretability does and does not mean here, a statement that **clinician comprehension and trust were never evaluated**, a published sensitivity check that **undercuts its own named novelty**, and a declaration that **external validation has not been done** with a dataset being sought. **A team optimising for citations does not write "formal evaluation with clinicians remains necessary" four times.** **The grade turns on clinical design, not on execution** — and concerns 276, 277 and 278 are all **clinical-domain** errors, not statistical ones. Three authors, all industrial engineering, no apparent clinical co-author. **The paper's own stated motivation for mining PubMed was that clinicians are too busy to consult; this entry is a demonstration of what consulting one buys that a literature corpus does not.**

**What would make it a 4, concretely.** Define an **index date** and require every lab to precede it. **Exclude any DR code before the index date** so the outcome is incident. **Drop nephropathy and neuropathy, or report performance with and without them** — the single ablation that would answer the central question, runnable today. **Add diabetes duration and type**, which would likely fix the inverted age effect. **Report calibration** after a prevalence correction for SMOTE. **Validate externally.** Expectation: **the AUROC would fall substantially — and the paper would be far more valuable, because its number would mean what it says.**

**The entry-74 contrast, which is what this pick was for.** Entry 74: **AUROC 0.587, ~2.4% of variance**, predicting a future event from pre-treatment data, with permutation testing that re-ran the complete pipeline 1,000 times. Entry 77: **AUROC 0.9724, ~64.8% of variance**, **27× more**, identifying a concurrent diagnosis code using two closely related diagnosis codes as inputs. **The tempting conclusion is that entry 77 is 27 times the study. The real difference is the question** — one is prediction, the other may be recognition, **and AUROC cannot tell them apart.** This is the clearest demonstration in this list of why a headline discrimination number is not a finding until you know what was predicted, from what, and when. **Read side by side with technique 265 from yesterday: a score with no comparator is not a finding — and entry 77 supplies its own comparator, a logistic regression at 0.9713, which is what makes the diagnosis possible.**

**Reporting-guideline pattern — seven entries, and the first one where the checklist would have helped.** Entry 69 named TRIPOD+AI and deposited the checklist. Entry 70 named STROBE while arguably being more careful. Entry 71 named none. Entry 73 named none and called for a checklist that does not exist. Entry 74 named none while doing more of what TRIPOD+AI asks than any of them. Entry 75 named none while running the best multiplicity treatment in this list. Entry 76 measured the phenomenon and quantified it at **40% mean completeness** against the instrument written for the job. **Entry 77 names none — and TRIPOD+AI requires exactly the two things whose absence drives this grade from a 4 to a 2: a calibration assessment and an explicit prediction horizon.** **So the sequence now has both halves: six entries showing a checklist does not predict quality, and one showing where it would have caught something.** The rule stands and gains a clause: **the checklist is not the quality signal — read the methods; and when something is missing, the checklist tells you what to look for.**

**Arithmetic verified today, every check passing.** Cohort and split counts close exactly (**62,582 + 15,646 + 19,558 = 97,786**; **3,373 + 16,185 = 19,558**), prevalences **17.25 / 17.24 / 17.25%** as stated. **The entire confusion matrix rebuilt from sens/spec/prevalence alone reproduces all four derived metrics** — accuracy 0.95349 vs 0.9535, PPV 0.83407 vs 0.8342, NPV 0.98123 vs 0.9812, F1 0.87116 vs 0.8712. **All six prevalence-scenario PPV/NPV values reproduce** to within 0.0003. The stated 0.0026 gap to the 24-feature XGBoost confirmed. Variance conversions: five models span **61.6–65.8%** vs entry 74's **2.4%**, a **27×** ratio. SMOTE prior-odds factor **0.2084**. HCCL at eight features **0.875**. **No arithmetic error of any kind was found in this paper — its internal consistency is the best in recent entries, which is precisely why the grade turns on design rather than execution.**

**What could not be verified.** **All figures and tables**, costing specifically the **bootstrap 95% CI values** — the procedure is described and the paper says the intervals "were narrow," but **not one interval value is reportable.** Also absent from the retrieved text: any calibration assessment; any temporal separation between labs and diagnosis; the extent of median imputation; whether nephropathy and neuropathy were themselves ICD-coded (stated only as "comorbidity variables"); diabetes duration and type; **IRB or data-use approval** (often genuinely exempt for de-identified commercial EHR data, but normally stated); funding; competing interests; any reporting guideline. **Nothing was reproduced that was not seen.**

**Deferred status, 2026-11-14.** ***Annals* *Choosing Wisely* (42896192) — day 25**, weekly re-check and **abstract-only date both 2026-11-19**, five days out and the next scheduled fallback; standing note that its 5.4% / 0.6–12.2% CI is a clustering signature computed across 15 recommendations rather than 4,050 responses. **Gait foundation model (42995231) — day 6**, abstract-only date **2026-11-30**; largest multiplicity problem this list will have faced (**1,887 of 3,210 tested phenotypes significant**), first question being what false-discovery-rate procedure ran across those 3,210 tests. Both re-checked 2026-11-13 with no PMC copy; **re-checks resume on schedule rather than daily since neither is near threshold.**

**Landmark check, 2026-11-07 → 11-14.** Three records, all previously assessed: NEJM mass-surveillance Perspective (43001754), *Nature Medicine* gait commentary (43003904), gait foundation model (42995231). **No new candidates; none displaces the queue.**

**Plan set on 2026-11-14.** **Next run takes shelf item (D)**, glaucoma visual-field prediction with **external validation on two separate OCT devices** (PMID 42997014, PMC13740841), **because today's entry makes it the sharpest available follow-up.** Entry 77's two decisive weaknesses are **internal validation only** and **a feature set that may contain its own label**; (D) is the one paper on the shelf **validated on data from different hardware**, the strongest form of external validation in imaging-adjacent work, and it directly tests what entry 77 could not. **First question for (D): did performance drop between the two devices, and by how much** — a model holding up across two acquisition platforms has answered the question entry 77 left open, and one that collapses shows why entry 77's 0.9724 should not be assumed to travel. **Carry technique 275 forward too:** check (D)'s reported prevalence and what its PPV would be in a screening population. **Shelf after today: four spares — (B) bone-tumour reference-standard review, (D) next, (E) MIBC pCR with 102 models screened, (F) bitewing mannequin-only.**

---

## From entry 78 — Chaurasia et al., glaucoma visual-field prediction from baseline OCT (Ophthalmol Sci, 2026; decoded 2026-11-15, FULL TEXT)

**The pre-registered question, and its answer.** Set yesterday with one question written down in advance: **"did performance drop between the two devices, and by how much?"** **Answer: yes, substantially — and the paper says otherwise.** Heidelberg MAE increases of **VFI +28.2%, MD +54.0%, PSD +20.2%**, with **non-overlapping 95% CIs for VFI and MD**, and a one-directional mean bias on all three (+1.05/+1.83/+1.13) against near-zero bias (+0.05/+0.17/+0.08) at the same-device external site. **The carried-forward technique 275 instruction does not apply** — this is a regression model on continuous indices, so there is no prevalence and no PPV. Recorded explicitly rather than silently dropped.

**283. Non-overlapping confidence intervals refute "no significant difference" — read the intervals, not the adjective.**

Entry 78 states there was **"no statistically significant difference in model performance across data sets."** It also reports:

- **internal MD: MAE 1.76, 95% CI 1.61–1.93**
- **Heidelberg MD: MAE 2.71, 95% CI 2.51–2.91**

**The intervals do not touch** — a gap from 1.93 to 2.51. Same for VFI: internal 2.91–3.56, Heidelberg 3.82–4.46. **Two of three headline comparisons show no overlap at all**; only PSD overlaps, and barely (1.21 against an upper bound of 1.27).

**The logical point, often got backwards.** When two 95% intervals **do not overlap**, the difference is significant — **and at a level stricter than 0.05.** Each interval already spans about two standard errors either side, so clearing each other entirely requires the estimates to be roughly four standard errors apart, where a conventional test needs about two. **Non-overlap is a conservative criterion: if the intervals miss, the difference is real by any standard.**

**The converse trap, which is the commoner error.** **Overlapping intervals do NOT prove no difference.** Two intervals can overlap substantially while a proper paired test finds a clear difference. "The intervals overlap, so performance is the same" is absence of evidence reported as evidence of absence.

**So memorise the asymmetry: intervals that miss each other → difference established. Intervals that overlap → undetermined, go and run the test.**

**The everyday version.** Two bakeries claim identical delivery times. One says "20 to 24 minutes," the other "31 to 36 minutes." **You need no statistics — the ranges do not touch.** Had the second said "23 to 28," they would overlap and you genuinely would not know. **Entry 78 is the first pair, described in the language of the second.**

**Watch out for** "no significant difference," "comparable," "consistent," "stable" sitting next to a table of intervals the paper never compares. **Do it yourself; ten seconds per row.** **Two red flags:** an author calling a non-degradation result **"surprising"** or **"contrary to the literature"** — that is precisely the moment to check the intervals, because the literature is usually right and the summary sentence is where the error lives; and a paper that reports intervals but runs no between-group test, leaving the reader to assume one was done. **Cite this entry as the clean worked case.**

**284. Site transportability and device transportability are different questions — and entry 78's design separates them.**

Two external sets differing in exactly one respect:

- **Essendon: new site, new patients, new clinicians — SAME scanner brand (Zeiss).** MD 1.92 vs 1.76 internal, bias **+0.17 dB**, all CIs overlap. **The model travelled.**
- **Gladstone Park: new site, new patients, new clinicians — AND a DIFFERENT brand (Heidelberg).** MD 2.71, bias **+1.83 dB**, CIs do not overlap. **It did not.**

**Because one set holds the device constant and the other does not, the difference between them isolates the hardware effect.** That is a controlled comparison, and it is the paper's most valuable property — delivered against its own conclusion, which pools both as "external validation" and averages the message into "robust generalizability."

**Why devices break models when sites do not.** A new site changes the *patients* — ages, severities, referral patterns — and a model that learned real biology survives that. **A new device changes the measurement itself.** Here: a **6 × 6 mm cube scan (200 A-scans × 200 B-scans)** versus a **3.5 mm circular line scan**, different segmentation software, non-equivalent quality gates (**signal strength ≥7 of 10** vs **quality ≥20 of 40**). **Anything the model learned about how the image was made, rather than about the eye, breaks** — and the bias pattern shows exactly that: near-zero offset at the same-device site, one-directional offset on all three measures at the new device. **Standardising brightness (255) and contrast (30) is sensible and cannot make a cube-derived image and a circle scan the same picture.**

**This refines technique 194** (reproducibility ≠ portability ≠ transportability): **a fourth split sits inside transportability — across institutions versus across instruments.** Most papers claiming "external validation" test only the first.

**The everyday version.** You learn to judge a room's temperature from one wall thermometer. Move to another building with the **same model** of thermometer: fine. Move to a **different brand**, mounted and calibrated differently: every reading is off by a couple of degrees **in the same direction.** Your skill did not change; your skill at reading *that instrument* did not transfer. **The giveaway is that the errors all lean the same way.**

**Watch out for** "externally validated" with no statement of what changed. **Ask three questions: new patients? new institution? new equipment or assay?** A model validated across hospitals that all run the same scanner, analyser or assay platform has shown far less than one validated across platforms. **And when a paper does cross devices, look for the BIAS, not just the error** — error that grows with a consistent directional offset is an instrument problem, and that is the most informative pattern available in an external validation.

**285. When degradation is mostly systematic bias, it is fixable — measure bias separately from scatter.**

A **Bland–Altman** analysis reports two distinct things: the **mean bias** (average offset and its direction) and the **limits of agreement** (individual scatter). **Different problems, different fixes**, and a single error number hides which you have.

Entry 78's figures, with bias as a share of the total MAE on Heidelberg:
