- **VFI: bias +1.05, MAE 4.14 → 25% of the average error**
- **MD: bias +1.83, MAE 2.71 → 68%**
- **PSD: bias +1.13, MAE 1.37 → 82%**
- **Zeiss, all three: +0.05 / +0.17 / +0.08 — essentially zero**

**Why the distinction changes what you do.** A **constant offset** is a scale reading two kilograms heavy — **subtract two kilograms**, and the correction can be estimated from a modest sample. **Random scatter** means patient-by-patient unreliability, and no single correction helps. **Bias is cheap to fix; scatter is not.** So the constructive reading of entry 78, which the paper does not state: **recalibrating the intercept on the 280 Heidelberg scans they already hold should recover most of the lost accuracy.** More useful than "performance was stable."

**One honest caution cutting the other way, reported by the authors:** limits of agreement were **"wide across the OCT machines, particularly for VFI and MD, indicating clinically meaningful individual-level variability,"** and the plots **"suggested proportional bias, with prediction errors varying across the range of VF indices."** **Proportional bias is the fingerprint of predictions being pulled toward the middle** — which is what minimising average error does on a compressed outcome, and entry 78's outcome was compressed from **both** ends (advanced glaucoma excluded at one, improving eyes at the other). **Together with the mean-reference comparator coming close, that is three independent signs of one phenomenon** (technique 195).

**The everyday version.** Two archers both miss by six inches on average. The first puts every arrow six inches high, tightly grouped; **the second scatters around the bullseye.** Same average error, completely different problems: **the first adjusts their sight once and is excellent; the second must learn to shoot.** Report only "six inches" and nobody can tell which archer they hired.

**Watch out for** external validation given as a single error number with no bias and no limits of agreement. **Ask for the Bland–Altman, or its two numbers** — "MAE rose from 1.76 to 2.71" and "…of which 1.83 is a constant offset" demand opposite responses (start again vs recalibrate). **And treat proportional bias as a warning, not a footnote.**

**286. The baseline that matters is the cheapest information you already have.** Entry 78 ran three comparators — **mean-reference, Ridge, Random Forest** — all on OCT image features (a 1,166-dim handcrafted vector). **None used the patient's own baseline visual field**, which every eye had (an inclusion criterion) and which appears in the paper's own correlation matrix. For early-to-moderate glaucoma over ~5 years, **"the future field will resemble the current field" is a formidable predictor, and it is free standard-of-care information.** Until the model beats it, "forecasting future VF from a single OCT scan" is not established as clinically additive. **This is technique 273 one level up:** not "does a simpler model of the same inputs match it?" but **"does the information the clinician already holds match it?"** **General rule: the right comparator for a prognostic model is standard of care, not another model of the same data.** TRIPOD+AI asks for exactly this, and entry 78 names no guideline.

**287. Predicting a future LEVEL is not predicting CHANGE.** Entry 78's own limitation, verbatim: the model **"can predict future key VF indices without distinguishing between the impacts of baseline disease severity and true longitudinal changes."** **Future MD = baseline MD + change.** In a cohort restricted to early-to-moderate disease, the change term over five years is small relative to the spread *between* patients — so **a model that merely reads current severity off the scan scores well on the future level while carrying no prognostic content at all.** And the paper's own introduction concedes cross-sectional structure–function mapping from OCT is already established. **So the claimed novelty is forecasting, and the design cannot separate forecasting from the mapping that was already known.** **The fix is one analysis: report error on the change, or against a baseline-carried-forward comparator** (technique 286). **Ask of any "prediction" model: is the reported error on the level or on the change?** The two differ enormously in difficulty and only one is prognostic.

**288. Test–retest variability is the wrong yardstick for prediction error.** Entry 78's Discussion cites Swanson et al — repeat Humphrey testing gives MD variation +2.5 to −1.4 dB (SD 0.9) and PSD 0.9 to 2.1 (SD 0.3) — then notes its MAEs of 1.76 and 1.14 are "comparable to or lower than the inherent test–retest variability," concluding predictions are "statistically robust and clinically meaningful" and that this "enables the identification of eyes requiring treatment escalation from the baseline visit." **Category error. Test–retest variability is the noise in measuring the SAME state twice; the model's MAE is error in predicting a DIFFERENT state 4.74 years later.** Worse, **beating measurement noise can be a symptom rather than an achievement**: if the quantity barely moves over follow-up, then "predict no change" automatically produces an error close to measurement noise. **The right denominator is the magnitude of actual change** — which in entry 78 sits in an unreadable table, so the ratio cannot be computed and is flagged rather than guessed. **Watch out for** "our error is within the measurement noise of the gold standard" offered as evidence of clinical utility.

**289. A 44% exclusion on follow-up interval both selects the cohort and defines the horizon.** Entry 78 went from **3,224 eligible eyes to 1,792**, dropping **1,432 (44.4%)**, to keep only those within one SD of mean follow-up (**2.15–7.33 years**), stated reason "to minimize the influence of outliers arising from a limited data set." **Follow-up duration is not a clinical criterion and it is not random:** very short intervals may mean loss to follow-up, relocation or death; very long ones may mean disease stable enough to be seen rarely. **Selecting on follow-up duration selects on everything correlated with it.** It also **bounds applicability — the validated horizon is 2.15 to 7.33 years and no wider** — which the paper does not state as a deployment constraint. **Check any cohort curation step that is defined by study logistics rather than by clinical definition, and ask what it selects for.**

**290. Excluding the cases that got better truncates the outcome asymmetrically.** Entry 78: **"eyes showing an improvement in VF from baseline, based on the Hodapp–Parrish–Anderson criteria, were excluded to minimize the postcataract surgery effect."** **The motive is legitimate** — cataract surgery brightens the entire field and would masquerade as glaucoma improvement. **The undisclosed cost: the upper tail of CHANGE is removed**, so the cohort is enriched for progression and the outcome range is compressed further, on top of the advanced-glaucoma exclusion at the other end. **The fraction removed by this rule is not reported**, so the magnitude cannot be assessed. **General rule: any exclusion defined by the OUTCOME reshapes the outcome distribution, and deserves a number.** Pair with technique 285's proportional-bias finding — compressing a target from both ends is the regime in which MAE-minimisation most rewards predicting the middle.

**291. Model selection on the set you then report is selection-optimistic — name your held-out set.** Entry 78 screened **135 configurations** (verified: 15 architectures × 3 loss functions × 3 optimizers), 5 epochs each, on a single patient-level **80/20 split** — and **the same 20% is reported as "internal validation."** Picking the winner among 135 candidates on a set and then reporting performance on that set is optimistic by construction; the honest structure is **three-way: train / select / test.** **The mitigation here is real and worth stating: the Zeiss-external result (MD 1.92 vs internal 1.76) is close enough that the optimism appears small — and that reassurance exists ONLY because external validation was done.** Separately unreconciled: screening MAEs quoted at **5.4–7.6** (ViT) and **9.0–10.3** (CNNs) against final reported MAEs of **1.14–3.23**, with no explanation of the scale change; and the **"47% error reduction"** compares the best configuration to the **tenth-best**, not to any baseline.

**292. When an explanation anomaly and a performance drop point at the same subgroup, that is corroboration.** Entry 78 reports, in separate sections, that (a) saliency attention fell partly on the **retinal pigment epithelium and outer retinal layers** — and the RPE is **not** where glaucoma acts, since glaucoma destroys retinal ganglion cells and their axons in the RNFL and inner retina; and (b) off-RNFL attention occurred **"especially in Heidelberg Spectralis OCT device-driven scans"** — **the same device on which performance degraded.** **The two facts are never joined.** Together they suggest the model is reading something device-specific rather than something glaucomatous, which is precisely the mechanism technique 284 predicts. **Full credit to the authors** for reporting both honestly, naming "blood vessel shadows, image quality, differences in scan quality across OCT machine vendors, or artifacts" as candidates, and stating that saliency maps are **"exploratory visual explanations rather than proof"** — **the best saliency caveat in this reading list.** The connection is the only thing missing. **General move: when you find an explanation oddity, check whether it concentrates in the same subgroup as any performance oddity.**

**293. A commercial disclosure you cannot read, in the direction of the overclaim.** Entry 78's abstract carries the journal's flag that **"Proprietary or commercial disclosure may be found in the Footnotes and Disclosures"** — a section absent from the retrieved text. **Verifiable from affiliations: three of eleven authors, including the first author, are at Pandani Solutions Pty Ltd**, and the paper proposes the model "could be integrated into existing OCT systems." Funding not reported in what was retrieved. **This is not an allegation of anything undisclosed — the journal flagged it, which is the system working.** It is recorded because **the overclaim in technique 283 runs in the direction of commercial interest**, and a reader should go and read the statement. **General rule: when a paper's summary sentence is more favourable than its own numbers, check who benefits from the summary.**

**What caps entry 78 at 3/5, recorded so the grade stays auditable.** **The stated conclusion is contradicted by the reported intervals** (283), and the authors flagged the result as "surprising" and contrary to the literature, then explained it rather than re-checked it. **Clinical value over predicting the average is unestablished** — their own mean-reference comparator was "slightly lower," improvement "modest, particularly for VFI and PSD," and the margin is in an unretrievable table. **The comparator that matters most was available and never run** (286). **Test–retest used as the yardstick** (288). **44.4% excluded on follow-up interval** (289) and **improving eyes excluded** (290). **No held-out internal test set** (291). **A commercial disclosure that could not be read** (293). **No reporting guideline.**

**Why not 2, and the distinction is worth keeping.** Entry 77 scored 2 for **missing analyses** — no external validation, no calibration, and an ablation never run. **Entry 78 ran the analyses and reported them; the error is interpretive.** That is a materially better paper, because **a careful reader with the full text reaches the right conclusion using only the paper's own numbers — the data corrects the discussion.** **Why not 4:** the headline claim about the very thing that makes the paper valuable is wrong by its own evidence, and the forecasting claim has no standard-of-care comparator.

**What would make it a 4, concretely.** **Say what the intervals say** — report the Heidelberg degradation with its biases as a finding rather than as consistency. **Add one comparator:** future VF from baseline VF. **Quantify the mean-reference margin in the main text**, not a supplement. **Report error on the change**, not only the level. **Try the intercept recalibration** on Heidelberg, which the bias figures suggest recovers most of the loss. **Four of those five use data the authors already have.**

**Positive practice worth carrying forward, and there is a lot of it.** **External validation at two sites on two devices** — the thing almost nobody does, and the reason this paper was picked. **Bootstrap CIs on every metric**, which is the sole reason the degradation is detectable at all: a paper reporting bare point estimates of 1.76 and 2.71 would leave a reader guessing. **A mean-reference comparator actually run.** **Bland–Altman with mean bias and limits of agreement for both external sets** — a genuine agreement analysis, and the closest thing to calibration available for a regression model; entry 77 had nothing comparable. **Two HREC approvals with committee numbers** (Univ. of Tasmania 29775; Royal Victorian Eye and Ear 24/1597HL), consent waivers at all three sites, Declaration of Helsinki. **A follow-up-interval sensitivity analysis by two methods** (Kruskal–Wallis and Spearman) in both external sets. **An AI-writing-tool disclosure** (Grammarly, readability and grammar). **The best saliency caveat in this list** (292). **And a frank limitation that identifies the paper's own central weakness** (287). **The data needed to correct this paper's central claim is all inside this paper** — which is the most generous and most accurate thing that can be said about it.

**Reporting-guideline pattern — eight entries, and the second running where the checklist would have helped.** 69 named TRIPOD+AI with the checklist; 70 named STROBE while arguably being more careful; 71 none; 73 none, while calling for a checklist that does not exist; 74 none, while doing more of what TRIPOD+AI asks than any of them; 75 none, while running the best multiplicity treatment in this list; **76 measured the phenomenon and quantified it at 40% mean completeness**; 77 none, where TRIPOD+AI would have forced calibration and a prediction horizon; **78 none, and TRIPOD+AI requires reporting against a simple baseline model — which is technique 286 exactly.** **The rule holds with its clause: the checklist is not the quality signal — read the methods; and when something is missing, the checklist tells you what to look for.**

**Arithmetic verified today, every check passing.** Exclusion cascade closes exactly: **4,038 − 23 − 791 = 3,224** as stated; **3,224 − 1,792 = 1,432 = 44.4%** dropped on follow-up interval alone. Follow-up window **4.74 ± 2.59 → 2.15–7.33 years** reproduces. **1,792 eyes / 1,610 patients = 1.11** eyes per patient. Architecture screen **15 × 3 × 3 = 135** confirms the stated count. External retention **150/188 (79.8%)** Zeiss and **280/377 (74.3%)** Heidelberg. CI overlap tested for all six internal-vs-external comparisons: **all three Zeiss overlap; Heidelberg VFI and MD do not** (gaps 3.56→3.82, 1.93→2.51); Heidelberg PSD overlaps narrowly (1.21 vs 1.27). Bias as share of MAE on Heidelberg: **25% / 68% / 82%**. **No arithmetic error was found in the paper — every number is correct; the error is that the prose does not describe them.**

**What could not be verified.** All figures, tables and supplementary material, costing four specific things: **the comparator table**, so the ViT's margin over the **mean-reference model is unquantifiable** — the single most important missing number; **the magnitude of actual VF change** over follow-up, so model error cannot be set against real change (technique 288); **the fraction of eyes removed by the "excluded if improved" rule** (290); and **the Footnotes and Disclosures** (293). Also absent: any **R², correlation or variance-explained** measure for any index; **funding**; any **reporting guideline**. **Nothing was reproduced that was not seen, and no comparator result was estimated.**

**Deferred status, 2026-11-15.** ***Annals* *Choosing Wisely* (42896192) — day 26**, weekly re-check and **abstract-only date both 2026-11-19**, four days out and the next scheduled fallback; standing note that its 5.4% / 0.6–12.2% CI is a clustering signature computed across 15 recommendations rather than 4,050 responses. **Gait foundation model (42995231) — day 7**, abstract-only date **2026-11-30**; largest multiplicity problem this list will have faced (**1,887 of 3,210 phenotypes called significant**), first question being what false-discovery-rate procedure ran across those 3,210 tests. Both last re-checked 2026-11-13 with no PMC copy; **neither near threshold, so re-checks stay on schedule.**

**Landmark check, 2026-11-08 → 11-15.** Three records, all previously assessed: NEJM mass-surveillance Perspective (43001754), *Nature Medicine* gait commentary (43003904), gait foundation model (42995231). **No new candidates; none displaces the queue.**

**Plan set on 2026-11-15.** **Next run takes shelf item (B)**, the bone-tumour review arguing **reference-standard instability is the binding constraint** on diagnostic AI (PMID 42997475, PMC13742044), **because the last three entries have converged on it from three directions and (B) addresses it head-on.** Entry 76 found harms and methods reporting incomplete at **40%** against the instrument written for the job. Entry 77's label was an **ICD code** that may have been co-written with two of its own predictors. **Entry 78's outcome is a visual-field test whose own test–retest noise the paper had to invoke as a yardstick — and whose measurement shifted by +1.83 dB when the device changed.** **All three are failures of the REFERENCE STANDARD, not of the model** — the thing being predicted was itself unstable. **First question for (B): does it quantify reference-standard disagreement, or only argue for it?** — technique 265 says a claim about a binding constraint needs a measured one. **Carry technique 283 forward:** a review will report pooled estimates with intervals, and the habit of checking whether intervals touch applies to every forest plot in it. **Shelf after today: three spares — (B) next, (E) MIBC pCR with 102 models screened, (F) bitewing mannequin-only.**

---

## From entry 79 — Liu & Liu, bone tumour imaging and the reference-standard ceiling (J Bone Oncol, 2026; decoded 2026-11-16, FULL TEXT)

**The pre-registered question, answered.** Set yesterday: **"does it quantify reference-standard disagreement, or only argue for it?"** — because technique 265 holds that a claim about a binding constraint needs a measured one. **It quantifies, and states which grounds can be quantified and which cannot.** Three of four get numbers; the fourth (histology does not predict behaviour) is left unquantified rather than padded. Method disclosed: agreement statistics **"extracted as reported by the primary studies, without recalculation or pooling."**

**The carried-forward instruction failed, informatively.** Yesterday predicted "a review of this kind will report pooled estimates with intervals, and the habit of checking whether intervals touch applies to every forest plot in it." **Wrong: this review pools nothing and reports no intervals at all.** No forest plot, no pooled estimate, nothing for technique 283 to act on — **so the absence became the finding instead, and technique 304 exists because the interval-checking habit found no intervals to check.** Recording the failed prediction rather than quietly dropping it.

**294. A noisy reference standard sets a ceiling on measurable accuracy that no algorithm can lift.**

The cleanest case needs no assumptions. **Biopsy grade agreed with resection grade in 36% of pelvic central chondrosarcomas.** Take resection — the whole tumour, examined completely — as truth. Then **the biopsy label is right ~36% of the time.** A *perfect* model, always predicting the true resection grade, tested against biopsy labels, **measures 36%.** Not because it is bad; because **the answer key is wrong 64% of the time.** In long bones (83% concordance) the same perfect model measures **83%. Same model, same skill, two scores, decided entirely by label quality.**

**The subtler consequence: a model better than its labels measures as worse than it is.** With a ~78%-accurate label and three grades, measured ≈ `p_model·p_label + (1−p_model)(1−p_label)/2`:

- truly **78%** accurate → measures **63%**
- truly **90%** → measures **71%**
- truly **95%** → measures **74%**
- **perfect** → measures **78%**

**Going from a 90% model to a flawless one buys under 7 measured points.** Every improvement is taxed by label noise. The review's phrasing: **"improvements in architecture will not translate into improvements in measurable accuracy beyond a ceiling the algorithm does not control."**

**And the review contains the real-world demonstration, buried in a subordinate clause.** One CT radiomics external test cohort, ACT vs higher-grade chondrosarcoma: **biopsy 64% correct, classifier 75%, radiologist 81%. The reference standard was the WORST of the three.** The model beat its own label by 11 points — and graded against biopsy alone, as most such studies are, it could not have exceeded ~64% **and its superiority to the label would have been invisible.** Only the existence of resection truth made it visible.

**The everyday version.** You sit an exam whose marking sheet is wrong on a third of the answers. A perfect student scores two-thirds. A student who knows more than whoever wrote the sheet is penalised exactly where they are right and the sheet is wrong. **Better textbooks will not lift the class average past two-thirds; only fixing the marking sheet will.**

**Watch out for** any accuracy, AUC or kappa reported against a label that is itself a judgement — a pathologist's grade, a radiologist's read, a chart-review adjudication, a billing code, another model's output. **Two questions: how accurate is the label, and how do you know? And has anyone estimated the ceiling before reporting progress toward it?** **If a field's performance figures plateau at a suspiciously specific number, the plateau may be the label rather than the science.** And the inverse flag: **a model reported as beating expert agreement has probably learned one institution's habits** — the review's own words, "performance above it indicates the model has learned an institution's labelling conventions rather than the underlying biology."

**295. Kappa: what it is, how to undo it, and the paradox in this paper's own numbers.**

**kappa = (observed agreement − chance agreement) ÷ (1 − chance agreement)** — the share of the *available* agreement, beyond luck, that raters actually achieved. Bands: <0.20 poor, 0.21–0.40 fair, 0.41–0.60 moderate, 0.61–0.80 substantial, >0.80 almost perfect. **Weighted** kappa gives partial credit for near misses (grade 1 vs 2 counts less than 1 vs 3).

**Run it backwards to recover raw agreement** — more intuitive, and the conversion is `Po = κ(1−Pe) + Pe`. Assuming roughly even grade distribution (**stated, not hidden**), Pe = 1/2 for two grades or 1/3 for three:

- pathologists **0.443** → ~**72%** agreement (2 grades) / ~**63%** (3)
- radiologists **0.345** → ~**67%** / ~**56%**
- pathologists, **high-risk** lesions **0.236** → ~**62%** / ~**49%**
- radiologists, high-risk **0.206** → ~**60%** / ~**47%**

**Then infer each reader's accuracy.** Two readers measuring one truth with independent errors: `Po = p² + (1−p)²/(k−1)`. Solving: pathologists ~**83%** (2 grades) / ~**78%** (3); radiologists ~**79%** / ~**72%**.

**The convergence worth recording.** From the **radiologists' own κ = 0.345** alone, each radiologist computes to **~79% accuracy.** The review separately reports, empirically, that **a radiograph classifier reached 80% external accuracy and was "statistically indistinguishable" from MSK radiologists.** **Predicted ceiling and observed plateau are one point apart.** **Caveats that matter:** balanced classes and independent errors are assumed, and **the independence assumption fails in a known direction** — real experts share training and stumble on the same hard cases, so errors are *correlated*, which inflates observed agreement and therefore **overstates** p, meaning the true ceiling is likely **lower**. Also the kappa is cartilage grading while the 80% is a radiograph classifier — related, not identical. **Suggestive, not proof — and worth running because it cost two lines.**

**The kappa paradox, visible in their own pair.** Long bone **83% raw / κ 0.63**; pelvis **36% raw / κ 0.12**. Raw agreements differ by 47 points, kappas by 0.51. Back-calculating implied chance agreement: **0.541** long bone vs **0.273** pelvis — because long-bone cartilage lesions are overwhelmingly low grade, so the distribution is lopsided and chance agreement is high, which makes kappa punish that setting harder than raw agreement does. **The same kappa can arise from very different raw agreements and vice versa, depending on class skew.** (These are *weighted* kappas, so the back-calculation is approximate and the implied Pe is indicative only.)

**The everyday version.** Two people predict rain in a desert where it rains five days a year; both say "no" daily. **They agree 98.6% of the time and know nothing.** Kappa is near zero, correctly. Move them to a coin-flip climate and 72% agreement is real skill. **Raw agreement without the class distribution cannot tell those apart — which is what kappa is for, and why you should always ask for the raw agreement too.**

**Watch out for** kappa with no sample size and no interval (**this review's own weakness**); kappa without the class distribution, because of the paradox; and **agreement reported only in aggregate** when the clinically decisive subgroup may be far worse — here it nearly halved.

**296. Report an interval on every agreement statistic, and stratify it by consequence.**

**Kappa is an estimate with a standard error**, depending on cases, raters and class skew. For two raters, 46 cases, ~63% agreement: **SE ≈ 0.107**, so the 95% band around **0.443** runs roughly **0.23 to 0.65** — **spanning "fair" to "substantial."** The underlying nine-rater design carries more information and narrows that; **how much is unrecoverable, because the review prints no interval for any of its agreement figures.**

**The authors have a partial defence** — they extracted "without recalculation or pooling," so if primary studies printed bare kappas, passing them through is faithful. **But primary agreement studies normally do report intervals**, and a review whose thesis rests on six or seven agreement statistics owed its readers the bands; it would have cost one line.

**Which direction does it cut?** Mostly in the review's favour, so this is a criticism of precision not of thesis: **the lower bound is worse than the point estimate.** The argument survives; the apparent exactness does not.

**And stratify.** The most important fact in this paper's agreement data is that **κ fell from 0.443 to 0.236 (pathologists) and 0.345 to 0.206 (radiologists) in lesions later judged high-risk.** An aggregate kappa averages the easy cases, where everyone agrees, with the hard ones, where nobody does — **and a model gets deployed on the hard ones.** So the aggregate systematically **overstates the ceiling where it matters.**

**The everyday version.** A weather service reports 85% annual accuracy — until you learn almost all of it came from a long run of identical sunny days, and on the four hurricane-warning days it was right once. **True, and not the number you want before deciding whether to evacuate.**

**Watch out for** any reliability figure — kappa, ICC, percent agreement — as a bare number. **Ask: how many cases, what interval, and what is it in the subgroup that matters?** When a reliability figure justifies a ceiling, a threshold or a sample size, **plan against the lower end of its interval.**

**297. External-validation failure has at least three candidate causes, and no performance table distinguishes them.** Entry 79's most useful and least tested idea: **"Apparent external validation failure may reflect label divergence rather than model deficiency. Two institutions applying different diagnostic thresholds produce datasets on which no single model can perform well."** **This inverts the standard inference** — the field reads external failure as proof of overfitting. Now read it against **entry 78**, where a model degraded on a different manufacturer's scanner with a one-directional **+1.83 dB** bias and off-target attention concentrated on that same device: **that degradation was measurement, not labels.** So the candidate causes are at least **(1) genuine overfitting, (2) measurement or instrument shift (technique 284), and (3) label divergence** — and a performance table separates none of them. **The review proposes the hypothesis and correctly claims no test.** **The test is straightforward and absent from the literature: have institution A's pathologists re-grade institution B's cases and see how much of the drop disappears.** Until someone runs it, "external validation failed" is a symptom with three differential diagnoses.

**298. Agreement is worst exactly where the stakes are highest.** Pathologist κ **0.443 → 0.236**, radiologist κ **0.345 → 0.206**, in lesions subsequently judged high-risk on clinical course. Undoing the chance correction: **two expert pathologists looking at a consequential lesion agree on its grade roughly half the time.** **The review states this and moves on.** It is the most alarming finding in the paper: it is the subgroup any deployed model would be used on, and it means **the ceiling in the cases that matter is far below the ceiling reported on average.** **Any paper reporting agreement should report it stratified by clinical consequence, and almost none do.**

**299. The most-studied task can be the one with the lowest ceiling.** Entry 79, verbatim: cartilaginous lesion grading comprises the most-studied applications, "and the reason is instructive: **cartilaginous lesion grading is the task on which human interobserver agreement is poorest**, making it both the greatest clinical need and the setting in which the reference standard is least stable." **So the field's research effort has concentrated precisely where measurable progress is most tightly capped** — clinical need and reference-standard instability coincide, because both flow from the task being genuinely hard. **The review notices and leaves the conclusion implicit: this is an argument for redirecting effort** toward outcome-based endpoints or tasks with stabler labels. **General move: before joining a crowded benchmark, check whether the crowd is there because the problem is important or because it is unsolvable as posed — and whether the ceiling has been estimated.**

**300. Label drift: a class that no longer exists.** Molecular criteria have redefined entities within the past decade. **Datasets predating the separation of CIC- and BCOR-rearranged sarcomas from Ewing sarcoma contain those tumours under the former label**, so "a model trained on such data has learned a class that no longer exists as defined." **This is distinct from label noise** — the labels were correct under the taxonomy of their time and are wrong under the current one, which no amount of re-reading the images fixes. **Ask of any historical dataset: has the classification system changed since these labels were assigned?** In oncology, molecular reclassification makes this routine rather than exotic; the same applies anywhere a diagnostic definition has been revised (sepsis, AKI, MI criteria).

**301. Patient-level vs image-level splits, and a leakage mechanism invisible in the metrics.** Entry 79 names the field-specific form: "because patients undergo serial imaging across a treatment course, a dataset partitioned at **image** rather than **patient** level places images of the same lesion on both sides of the split. **This inflates apparent performance and is not detectable from reported metrics.**" **That last clause is the important one** — no accuracy, AUC, interval or calibration plot reveals it; only the splitting code does. **Ask explicitly: was the split at patient level?** Entry 78 did this correctly and said so; entry 77 did too. **Whenever a dataset contains repeated measurements of the same subject — serial imaging, multiple lesions, both eyes, repeat admissions — image-level or record-level splitting silently inflates everything.**

**302. Human comparators handicapped by design.** Entry 79: "Where models are compared against radiologists, the comparison is usually against readers **without access to clinical history or prior imaging**, a condition under which human performance is artificially depressed relative to practice." **This is the mirror of technique 294 and the two distortions compound in the same direction.** The model's score is *capped* by label noise; the human's score is *depressed* by an artificial reading condition — **both make the model look relatively better than it is in practice.** **A reader needs both corrections to interpret any human-versus-AI comparison.** Ask: what did the human have access to, and what would they have in clinic? Prior imaging and clinical context are usually the two things removed, and they are usually the two things a real reader leans on most.

**303. The case-mix double bind in rare disease.** Entry 79: "Any dataset assembled from consecutive presentations is dominated by benign lesions; any dataset enriched for malignancy is unrepresentative of the triage population." Plus: "A model reporting high accuracy on a tertiary sarcoma centre cohort has been evaluated on a case mix in which **malignancy prevalence far exceeds that of the triage population.**" **There is no dataset that is both adequately powered for the rare malignant classes and representative of the screening population.** This is technique 275 with no escape route: **identical sensitivity and specificity give very different predictive value at tertiary-centre versus primary-care prevalence**, and the rarer the disease the sharper the divergence. **The review names the bind and offers no solution, because there is not a clean one** — the honest response is to report prevalence explicitly and recompute predictive values for the intended setting. **High reported accuracy in a rare disease warrants scrutiny of case mix before anything else.**

**304. Epistemic vs aleatoric uncertainty, and why rarity forces the distinction.** Entry 79: Monte Carlo dropout and Bayesian approaches distinguish **epistemic** uncertainty, which "rises where a case falls outside the training distribution," from **aleatoric** uncertainty "inherent to the data." The two call for opposite responses: **epistemic uncertainty says *I have not seen this before, defer*; aleatoric says *this case is genuinely ambiguous, and more training data will not help*.** And the reason it is load-bearing here: **"Rarity guarantees that any deployed bone tumors model will encounter subtypes absent from training, making this the setting where such quantification matters most, and where it is least often reported."** **In rare disease, out-of-distribution cases are not an edge case — they are the expected case.** A model with no uncertainty output will answer confidently on a subtype it has never seen, and nothing in its reported metrics anticipates that. **Ask whether a deployed model can say "I don't know," and which of the two kinds of not-knowing it is reporting.**

**305. Use a reporting guideline as an instrument, not a badge.** Entry 79 names and applies **six** — CLAIM, TRIPOD+AI, CLEAR, IBSI, METRICS, RQS — and cites two formal appraisals (METRICS/RQS on chondrosarcoma radiomics, CLAIM-based on osteosarcoma radiomics) as establishing deficits "quantitatively rather than impressionistically," with common findings being **absent external validation, no calibration analysis, and limited data or code availability.** **This is the first entry in nine to name any guideline, and it is also the best-argued paper in that run** — but the causation does not run from the checklist to the quality. **The distinction worth keeping: a paper that uses guidelines as analytic instruments, to appraise a literature and generate findings, differs from one that names a guideline as a compliance badge.** Entry 69 deposited a TRIPOD+AI checklist and was not the most careful paper in this list; entries 74, 75 and 79 named varying amounts and did careful work. **The rule stands, with its complement: naming a guideline does not predict quality, so read the methods — and when a paper turns a guideline into a measurement, that is a signal about the authors' disposition, not about the guideline.**

**What caps entry 79 at 4/5, recorded so the grade stays auditable.** **No confidence interval on any agreement statistic** (296), with the headline κ from 46 lesions and the unanimity finding from 12 osteosarcomas. **The search cascade has no terminus** — 16,247 → 9,435 screened, then no full-text count and no final included-study count, so claims that deficiencies are "common" or external validation "infrequent" have no denominator in the text. **The ceiling claim's strong form is false** while the review's actual form ("cannot be *shown* to exceed") is correct and the next sentence handles it — a trap for anyone quoting the first clause alone. **Two radiologists, no pathologist co-author**, on a thesis whose load-bearing evidence is pathological. **And a review cannot establish what the primary literature has not done:** the study that would test consequence two — give the same cases to multiple institutions' pathologists, train against each institution's labels, measure how much cross-site drop is label divergence — **does not exist, and the review says so.**

**Why not 5:** in this list a 5 requires evidence that settles something, and no review does; this one argues, quantifies and synthesises without measuring. **Why not 3:** everything that normally drags a review down is absent. It is not a survey, it does not inflate its field, it reports its own limits in a dedicated section, it includes contradicting evidence, it names and applies guidelines, and its declarations are complete. **The defects are of precision, not of honesty.**

**Positive practice worth carrying forward, and there is more of it here than in any previous entry.** **A falsifiable thesis declared as such** — "advances a specific argument rather than surveying available technologies." **Quantification of that thesis**, with explicit statement of which grounds can and cannot be quantified. **Extraction method disclosed**, including the no-pooling limitation. **Counter-evidence included** — the **ICC 0.78** that cuts against the thesis, with the honest gloss "Agreement is not uniformly poor… Instability is therefore concentrated in grading, and in the cases with the greatest clinical consequence." **Negatives reported as negatives** — no study predicts MDM2 amplification or IDH mutation status from imaging ("The molecular associations are established; the imaging inference is not"); no direct comparison of domain-specific vs natural-image pretraining exists. **Six reporting instruments named and applied** (305). **A leakage mechanism identified and flagged as metric-invisible** (301). **Regulatory status stated flatly** — no clearance for bone tumour characterisation, grading or response prediction in either jurisdiction, "Every application is investigational," the field "remains at the first two stages." **A dedicated section on its own methodological limitations**, naming publication bias specifically in "negative or externally non-replicating AI results." **Complete declarations** — no funding, no competing interests, both explicit, and a **generative-AI disclosure of exemplary specificity** (ChatGPT for language, grammar, readability and clarity only; explicitly **not** for literature identification, reference selection, citation placement, figure generation or substantive content; "All citations have been verified against the primary articles"). **And a closing distinction that functions as research-funding guidance:** "methodological rigor and data infrastructure are addressable through coordinated effort, whereas **ground-truth instability is a property of the disease and its evolving classification.**"

**Arithmetic verified today, every check passing.** Search cascade closes exactly: **4,842 + 6,115 + 5,290 = 16,247** and **16,247 − 6,812 = 9,435**, both as stated. All four kappa-to-raw-agreement conversions computed under a stated balanced-class assumption. Reader accuracy solved from `Po = p² + (1−p)²/(k−1)` for two and three grades. Attenuation table computed: ceiling **77.7%** against a 77.7%-accurate label, **6.7 measured points** between a 90% model and a perfect one. Implied chance agreement back-calculated from the paired raw/kappa figures (**0.541** long bone, **0.273** pelvis), flagged approximate because the published kappas are weighted. Kappa SE on 46 cases **0.107** under a two-rater approximation, nine-rater caveat stated. **No arithmetic error was found in the review** — every figure it reports is internally consistent.

**What could not be verified.** **All tables and figures**, including the ground-truth instability table, the evidence-synthesis tables, the molecular-reclassification table and the implementation-pathway figure. Most consequentially, **the comparative performance promised by the title**: the full retrieved text contains **no AUC values at all** (the only "AUC" is in the abbreviation list), so that half of the review lives entirely in unreachable tables and **nothing in entry 79 reports how well any model performs** — stated in the summary header, in concern 4, and here. Also absent: **any confidence interval on any kappa, ICC or concordance figure**, and **the number of studies finally included** plus any full-text screening count. **Nothing was reproduced that was not seen.**

**Reporting-guideline pattern — the streak breaks at eight, instructively.** 71, 73, 74, 75, 77, 78 named none. 69 named TRIPOD+AI with the checklist. 70 named STROBE. **76 measured the phenomenon and quantified it at 40% mean completeness** against the instrument written for the job. **79 names six instruments and is also the best-argued paper in the run.** One case does not overturn the rule, and the rule never said guidelines are useless — it said **naming one does not predict quality, so read the methods.** **The complement entry 79 adds: a paper that uses guidelines as analytic instruments rather than as a compliance badge tends to be a paper that was going to be careful anyway. The guidelines did not make this review good; the same disposition produced both. Rule updated, not abandoned** (305).

**Deferred status, 2026-11-16.** ***Annals* *Choosing Wisely* (42896192) — day 27**, weekly re-check and **abstract-only date both 2026-11-19, three days out and the next scheduled fallback**; standing note that its 5.4% / 0.6–12.2% CI is a clustering signature computed across 15 recommendations rather than 4,050 responses. **Gait foundation model (42995231) — day 8**, abstract-only date **2026-11-30**; largest multiplicity problem this list will have faced (**1,887 of 3,210 tested phenotypes called significant**), first question being what false-discovery-rate procedure ran across those 3,210 tests. **Both due a PMC re-check on 2026-11-19**, when the *Annals* threshold fires.

**Landmark check, 2026-11-09 → 11-16.** Two records, both previously assessed: NEJM mass-surveillance Perspective (43001754) and *Nature Medicine* gait commentary (43003904). **No new candidates; none displaces the queue.**

**Plan set on 2026-11-16.** **Next run takes shelf item (E)**, muscle-invasive bladder cancer pathological complete response with **102 models screened** (PMID 42997524, PMC13741961), **because today's entry supplies exactly the lens it needs.** Entry 79 establishes that measured accuracy is capped by reference-standard quality and that **scoring above the ceiling indicates a model has learned labelling conventions rather than biology** — and **pCR after neoadjuvant therapy is assessed on a resection specimen by a pathologist**, so it is a reference standard of precisely the kind entry 79 analyses. **Two questions, both written down now.** **(1) What is the reported agreement on the pCR reference standard itself, and does the paper acknowledge any?** — technique 294 says the ceiling is set there, and pCR assessment has known interobserver variability. **(2) With 102 models screened and a calibration-slope CI of 0.469–1.639, how was selection among 102 candidates accounted for?** — technique 291 says selection on the reported set is optimistic by construction, and **a calibration slope interval that wide already spans severe overfitting (slope ≪1) and severe underfitting (slope ≫1)**, so the honest reading may be that calibration is simply unestimated at that sample size. **Shelf after today: two spares — (E) next, (F) bitewing mannequin-only.**

---

## From entry 80 — Fujiwara et al., MIBC pathological complete response after chemoradiotherapy (Clin Transl Radiat Oncol, 2026; decoded 2026-11-17, FULL TEXT)

**Both pre-registered questions, answered.** Set yesterday, before reading.

**(1) "What is the reported agreement on the pCR reference standard itself?"** — **Not reported.** No interobserver agreement, no kappa, no duplicate read of ypT0. **But the paper does better than acknowledge the problem: it designs around it.** Its introduction names the exact defect entry 79 quantified — *"most bladder-preservation studies ... usually assess CRT response with imaging and TURBT rather than complete surgical pathology. Imaging and TURBT can miss microscopic residual cancer"* — and its first stated strength is that surgical pathology defined the endpoint. **Entry 79's prescription applied by design, requiring a 24-year institutional protocol to make possible.** **My finding the paper does not address: 148/285 (52%) of specimens are PARTIAL cystectomies covering only the original tumour site** — see technique 310.

**(2) "How was selection among 102 candidates accounted for?"** — **Answered, and reassuringly.** *"Model training, model ranking, and final model selection were completed in the training cohort BEFORE test-cohort performance was examined"*; *"Candidate models were ranked by AUC from INTERNAL VALIDATION within the training cohort."* **So the specific worry — ranking 102 candidates on apparent training fit, which would select the most overfit model — is resolved.** What remains is technique 306. **And the calibration prediction was right**: the interval spans severe overfitting to severe underfitting, which the authors state in their own abstract.

**306. One train/test split is a single draw from a distribution — and this paper shows what happens when you check.**

Entry 80 ran the check almost nobody runs: *"To assess whether performance depended on a single patient split, we performed additional internal validation using **five different train/test splits** of the 285 patients"*, same AutoGluon procedure re-run independently each time. **Result, from the Discussion: "Performance was lower in the additional five-fold internal validation, showing that the result varied across patient splits."** And in limitations: **"Therefore, overfitting cannot be excluded."**

**So the headline 0.829 is the best of six**, and the abstract and Highlights carry only the 0.829.

**Why split-to-split variation is large here.** The test set is **57 people — 36 responders, 21 non-responders.** The AUC rests on 36 × 21 = 756 pairs but only 57 independent patients; move eight or ten borderline cases across the split and the figure moves materially. **The number reported is partly a property of which patients the random seed happened to hold out.**

**The honest summary of a split-dependent result is the distribution, not the maximum** — report mean and range across splits, or better, use repeated cross-validation so every patient takes a turn in a test fold. **Entry 74 did exactly that (25 outer test sets from repeated nested CV), which is why its near-null AUC 0.587 is more trustworthy than many larger numbers.**

**The everyday version.** A student scores 8/10 on a ten-question test. You give five more tests from the same syllabus and they average 6. **The 8 was real and it was the best of six.** Reporting only the 8 is a true fact that misdescribes the student.

**Watch out for** a single train/test split with no indication of split sensitivity, especially under a few hundred patients. **Two questions: was the split repeated, and what was the range? And was the reported split prespecified or chosen?** Here the second answer is reassuring — a fixed random seed set in advance, so the favourable draw was luck not cherry-picking — **and the first is in a supplement, which is the wrong place for it.** **When a paper says performance "varied across splits" without printing the variation, treat the headline as an upper bound.**

**307. A calibration slope with a wide interval means calibration is UNMEASURED — which is not the same as acceptable.**

**Discrimination is ranking; calibration is the numbers.** A model can rank perfectly and still have probabilities that are badly wrong, and for a patient deciding about their bladder the number is what matters.

**The calibration slope: 1.0 is perfect. Below 1 = predictions too extreme** (says 90% when truth is 75%; the signature of overfitting). **Above 1 = predictions too timid**, squashed toward the middle.

Entry 80 reports **0.856 (95% CI 0.469–1.639)**. At **0.469** predictions are grossly too extreme; at **1.639** grossly too conservative. **The interval is 1.17 wide and contains 1.0.**

**Now the distinction routinely fumbled: containing 1.0 means no miscalibration has been DEMONSTRATED. It does not mean calibration has been demonstrated.** The honest conclusion is **unmeasured** — not fine, not broken. **The authors say exactly this, in the abstract** (*"the wide CI indicated uncertainty in calibration"*) and in their final limitation (*"predicted pCR probabilities remain uncertain"*). **Credit: that is the first thing an abstract reader learns about calibration here, where entry 77 three days earlier reported eight discrimination metrics and no calibration at all.**

**Why so wide: the 57 patients.** Estimating a slope needs variation across the whole probability range, and 57 people spread over 0-to-1 leave very few per region. **Discrimination is cheaper to estimate than calibration** — see technique 308.

**The everyday version.** A forecaster ranks days perfectly — the ones they call wettest really are wettest — **but when they say "70% chance" it rains on 40% of those days.** Ranking flawless, numbers useless, and you would wrongly cancel a wedding on the 70%. **Now judge them on six forecasts: you cannot tell, and calling it "seems fine" reports your ignorance as reassurance.**

**Watch out for** a prediction model reported on discrimination only. **Ask for the calibration slope AND its interval, then read the interval, not the point estimate.** Three traps: a slope near 1.0 with a wide interval called "well calibrated"; calibration shown only as a plot with no slope, so precision is invisible; **and any model whose deliverable is a percentage, validated only on ranking.**

**308. Minimum detectable effect — and "big enough for which claim?"** The fair counterweight. **A small sample is not uniformly fatal; it depends what you are detecting.** For an AUC, effective sample size is `n1·n2/(n1+n2)` = **36 × 21 / 57 = 13.26**; at 80% power and one-sided α = 0.05, `d_min = 2.80/√13.26 = 0.769`, giving a **minimum detectable AUC of ~0.707.** **Observed 0.829 clears it, so the discrimination claim IS adequately powered** — unlike entry 70's underpowered nulls. **Meanwhile the calibration slope on the same 57 patients came out with a 1.17-wide interval.** **Same study, same patients, one claim supported and one not**, because ranking 756 pairs is a far easier estimation problem than pinning a slope across the full probability range. **Everyone asks whether a study is big enough; far fewer ask "big enough for which claim?" Compute the detectable floor separately for every claim a paper makes.** And note the corollary: **a sample big enough to show that something works is routinely too small to show how well it works or what its numbers mean** — and the latter two are usually what a clinician needs.

**309. A simple count can beat the ensemble — and here it is the paper's most durable output, filed as "exploratory."** Four of the nine variables — **≥cT3, tumour >30 mm, concomitant CIS, multiplicity** — simply counted, give pCR of **87% / 66% / 49% / 29%** across **84 / 88 / 72 / 41** patients on **all 285** (p<0.001), with **concordant 5-year metastasis-free survival of 87.5% / 86.5% / 70.9% / 59.8%** (p<0.001) and OS 86.9 / 85.1 / 72.8 / 66.1 (p=0.041). **I verified those four cells reproduce the study's total of 178 responders** (84×0.87 + 88×0.66 + 72×0.49 + 41×0.29 = 178.3). **A monotone dose-response across four clinical features, on the full cohort, concordant with survival, is far more transportable than a stacked ensemble chosen from 102 candidates and tested on 57 people** — and it is estimated on 285 patients rather than 57. **The paper labels the count exploratory and the ensemble as the main result; the reverse is better supported.** **General move: when a paper offers both a complex model and a simple score, check which one is estimated on the full sample and which has a monotone, mechanistically sensible gradient.** Note the interaction with technique 312: dichotomising every predictor is what makes such a count possible, so the information thrown away buys something back.

**310. An upgraded reference standard still needs checking for completeness.** Entry 80's great strength is surgical pathology instead of imaging plus TURBT. **But 148/285 (52%) are PARTIAL cystectomies, which remove the original tumour site and not the rest of the bladder.** So for half the cohort **ypT0 means "no tumour at the original site in the piece removed," not "no tumour anywhere."** **A partial cystectomy is to the bladder what a needle biopsy is to a tumour** — a sample, chosen on prior information, that can miss disease elsewhere; exactly the defect the introduction criticises in imaging and TURBT, one level up. **Mitigation, stated fairly: partial cystectomy was reserved for patients with a complete CLINICAL response on imaging, cytology and repeat resection, so the remainder was assessed by other means.** **The problem is that the label is heterogeneous across the cohort** — whole-bladder clean in 137, one-region clean in 148 — **and the paper never reports pCR or model performance separately by specimen type**, which is the one table that would license pooling. **General rule: when a study upgrades its reference standard, ask whether the upgrade is complete for every patient or only for some, and whether the label means the same thing in each stratum.**

**311. The winner of a large model screen carries an order statistic, not an estimate.** Entry 80's selected model had an internal-validation AUC of **0.921**, the **highest of 102 candidates**, and a test AUC of **0.829** — a gap of **0.092**. **The maximum of 102 noisy estimates is biased upward by construction**, and a gap of that size is roughly what best-of-100 on 228 patients produces unaided. **The selection procedure here was correct** (internal validation, test set untouched until after), **so this is not an error — it is a reason not to read 0.921 as a performance figure.** **Whenever a paper reports "we screened N models and the best achieved X on internal validation," X is the max order statistic; only the held-out number is an estimate.** The larger N, the larger the upward bias, and reporting both without comment invites a reader to average them.

**312. Dichotomising every predictor: what it costs and what it buys.** Entry 80 made all nine predictors binary — size *>30 mm*, stage *≥cT3*, grade *G3*. **Cutting a continuous variable in two discards information and usually costs predictive power**: a 31 mm and a 70 mm tumour are treated identically. **What it buys is technique 309** — a countable set of yes/no factors a clinician can apply with a pen, and a monotone gradient that transports. **A defensible trade, not an error, and it should be named**, because some of an ensemble's modest margin over simpler models may be the dichotomisation rather than the data. **Ask: were continuous predictors kept continuous, and if not, was the cut-point prespecified or chosen from the data?** Here the cut-points came "based on prior studies," which is the right provenance.

**313. Verification that is almost free: check that subgroup tables decompose to the totals.** Entry 80's four clinical-factor splits: ≥cT3 46/91 vs 96/137; size >30 mm 37/77 vs 105/151; CIS 13/27 vs 129/201; multiplicity 39/85 vs 103/143. **Every one sums to 228 patients and 142 pCR events.** The factor-count table sums to 228 with percentages reproducing 29/32/27/12/0.4. The full-cohort cells sum to 285 and reproduce 178 events. **Six independent reconciliations, all passing, in about two minutes of arithmetic.** **This is the cheapest quality signal available in any paper with subgroup tables** — and when it fails it finds real errors (entry 74's one-patient abstract/body discrepancy; entry 73's missing denominator). **Run it on every paper. A study whose tables all reconcile has been checked by its authors; one whose tables do not has not.**

**314. A long accrual window is an era-confounding question that needs asking even when it cannot be answered.** Entry 80 accrued from **1997 to 2021 — 25 years, ~11 patients a year.** Over that span CT and MRI improved substantially, TNM moved through editions (the 8th applied retrospectively to all), grade used a **1999** WHO instrument and variants a **2016** one, and resection technique and pathology handling both changed. **The authors flag the imaging problem** — *"pretreatment imaging was not standardized, which may have affected clinical T staging and tumor-size measurement"* — **and report no analysis by accrual period.** **With 285 patients over 25 years an era split would be genuinely underpowered, which is a real constraint rather than an excuse.** **The move for a reader: note the window, ask whether the predictors are era-sensitive (staging and size measurement plainly are), and treat a model trained across a quarter-century of changing measurement as carrying unmeasured heterogeneity.** Related to technique 300 (label drift) but distinct: there the classes changed, here the *measurements* did.

**315. The one honest sentence that stops a decade of over-citation.** Entry 80 writes, flatly: **"We do not claim that the multilayer ensemble is superior to simpler models."** It also writes *"We did not directly compare our approach with these MRI-based methods,"* that the biological explanations *"remain hypotheses that require direct testing,"* and — the most consequential — **"Because all patients underwent partial or radical cystectomy after CRT, the survival results cannot be directly applied to patients managed with bladder preservation alone."** **Each costs the authors something and each is correct.** The survival one matters most: the five-year MFS gradient of 87.5% to 59.8% is measured in people whose bladders were removed or partly removed, so **a patient who KEEPS their bladder on this model's advice has a different prognosis from anyone in the cohort** — and the authors say so rather than letting the curves imply otherwise. **Practice to copy: state what you have NOT shown, in the Discussion, in plain words.** **Practice to look for as a reader:** a paper that never says what it has not shown has usually not asked.

**What caps entry 80 at 3/5, recorded so the grade stays auditable.** **Test set of 57 (36 pCR / 21 non-pCR)** — every test-cohort number rests on it, with an AUC interval 0.23 wide. **0.829 is the best of six splits** by the authors' own account, with the other five unreadable (306). **Calibration unestimated** (307). **The winner's 0.921 is an order statistic** (311). **52% of the reference standard is a partial specimen**, with pCR never reported by specimen type (310). **No interobserver agreement on ypT0.** **25-year accrual with no era analysis** (314). **Internal validation only, single centre**, stated. **Funding not reported.** **The regularized logistic regression was among the 102 and its result is in a supplement, not the main text** — entry 77's question computed but unreadable.

**Why not 2:** entry 77 scored 2 for analyses not run and claims not supported. **Here the analyses were run, the unfavourable ones published, and the conclusion correctly bounded** ("This may support decision-making"; "Independent external validation is required"). **Nothing is overclaimed.** **Why not 4:** the grade tracks what a reader may safely take away, and 57 test patients establish that the signal is real, not how strong it is. **Entry 74 earned a 4 on 229 patients with repeated nested CV, 1,000-fold permutation of the complete pipeline, and out-of-fold everything; entry 80 has one prespecified split, a five-split sanity check, and a frank admission the splits disagreed.**

**What would make it a 4, concretely.** **Report the five additional splits' AUCs in the main text** and lead with their range. **Report the regularized logistic regression's performance in the main text.** **Report pCR and model performance separately for partial versus radical specimens.** **Report ypT0 agreement** between two pathologists on a sample. **Validate externally**, which the authors name as the priority. **Four of those five use data the authors already have.**

**Positive practice worth carrying forward — the most in any primary study in this run.** **A deliberately upgraded reference standard** (310). **Correct selection order**, completed before the test cohort was touched. **A split-stability check run, found unfavourable, and published** (306). **Calibration assessed with its uncertainty in the abstract** (307). **2,000-sample stratified bootstrap intervals** on both headline quantities. **Permutation importance quarantined** from training and selection and labelled exploratory. **TRIPOD named with a completed checklist deposited.** **No missing data; no sample-size calculation; an unstratified split — all three stated rather than concealed.** **IRB number M2019-192 and written informed consent from every patient**, possible because these were prospective protocols rather than a chart trawl. **Software versions to the patch level** (AutoGluon Tabular 1.3.1, Python 3.11.13, JMP Pro 17.0, R 4.0.2). **And four sentences of genuine restraint** (315).

**Arithmetic verified today, every check passing.** Cohort cascade: **314 − (6 + 23) = 285**, excluded fractions reproducing 9.2 / 1.9 / 7.3%; **148 + 137 = 285**. Split **228 + 57 = 285**; pCR **142 + 36 = 178**; rates 62.3 / 63.2 / 62.5% against stated 62 / 63 / 62. **All four factor subgroups decompose to 228 and 142**; factor-count table sums to 228 with correct percentages; **full-cohort cells sum to 285 and reproduce 178 events.** Hanley-McNeil on 36/21 gives **0.724–0.934** against their bootstrap **0.706–0.932** — consistent, bootstrap slightly wider on the low side as expected. Minimum detectable AUC **0.707** at 80% power, cleared by 0.829. Variance explained **~31%** (entry 77: 65%; entry 74: 2.4%). **No arithmetic error found anywhere in this paper; internal consistency is the best in the recent run.**

**What could not be verified.** All tables, figures and the entire supplement, costing four specific things: **Supplementary Table 3** (the 102 candidates, hence the logistic regression); **Supplementary Table 4** (the five additional splits, hence the range behind "performance was lower"); **Supplementary Table 1** (the deposited TRIPOD checklist); and the calibration plot and decision curve. Also absent: **funding**; any ypT0 agreement; pCR or performance by specimen type; any accrual-era analysis; and the operating threshold, hence no sensitivity, specificity, PPV or NPV. **Nothing was reproduced that was not seen.**

**Reporting-guideline pattern — two consecutive entries now name one.** 71, 73, 74, 75, 77, 78 named none; 69 named TRIPOD+AI with the checklist; 70 named STROBE; **76 measured the phenomenon at 40% mean completeness**; **79 named six instruments and applied them; 80 names TRIPOD and deposits a completed checklist.** **The rule stands unchanged — naming a guideline does not predict quality, so read the methods — and yesterday's complement holds:** entry 80's candour about split instability, calibration and ensemble non-superiority is not something a checklist extracted from it; the same disposition produced both. **One narrow credit to the instrument: TRIPOD requires a calibration assessment, and entry 80 has one where entry 77, which named no guideline, had none at all.**

**Deferred status, 2026-11-17.** ***Annals* *Choosing Wisely* (42896192) — day 28**, weekly re-check and **abstract-only date both 2026-11-19, two days out**; standing note that its 5.4% / 0.6–12.2% CI is a clustering signature computed across 15 recommendations rather than 4,050 responses. **Gait foundation model (42995231) — day 9**, abstract-only date **2026-11-30**; largest multiplicity problem this list will have faced (**1,887 of 3,210 phenotypes called significant**), first question being what false-discovery-rate procedure ran across those 3,210 tests. **Both due a PMC re-check on 2026-11-19.**

**Landmark check, 2026-11-10 → 11-17.** One record, previously assessed: the *Nature Medicine* gait commentary (43003904). **No new candidates; none displaces the queue.**

**Plan set on 2026-11-17.** **Next run takes shelf item (F)** — the bitewing radiograph positioning-error classifier trained entirely on **mannequins** (PMID 42981213, PMC13734376) — **the LAST spare, so the shelf is then empty and a RESTOCK IS DUE.** **Two questions written down now. (1) Does anything in the paper test transfer from mannequin to patient?** A mannequin has no soft tissue, no motion, no anatomical variation and no pathology, so this is **the most extreme form of the transportability question entry 78 raised across two OCT devices: not a different scanner but a different kind of subject.** **(2) What is the reference standard for a "positioning error," and who defined it?** Entry 79 says the ceiling is set there — and positioning error is a judgement about image geometry that may be far more reliably assessed than a tumour grade, **which would make (F) the first entry in this run with a genuinely stable reference standard**, and therefore the cleanest available test of whether the ceiling argument predicts anything. **Operational note: the 2026-11-18 run should screen for new PMC-confirmed candidates alongside the decode**, as was done on 2026-11-10, since the shelf will be bare afterwards. **Shelf after today: one spare — (F).**

**Standing watch item:** PM Heart trial results (protocol PMID 42893738), quarterly.

## From entry 81 — Rabelo et al., bitewing positioning errors on dental mannequins (Clin Exp Dent Res, 2026; decoded 2026-11-18, FULL TEXT)

**Shelf item (F), the last spare, and the pick that was set up two days in advance to test entry 79's thesis. Both pre-registered questions answered, and the thesis predicted correctly.** 403 bitewing radiographs from **three dental mannequins**, taken by Bachelor of Oral Health and DMD students across 2021–2022 assessment periods; five CNN architectures trained **from scratch**, 200 epochs, **no augmentation**, stratified fivefold cross-validation at image level; six independent classification tasks. **What makes this entry matter is not the dentistry. It is that every label here has a written rule, so the reference-standard ceiling becomes directly observable instead of inferred.**

**316. When every label has a written rule, accuracy tracks the rule's precision — and that is the reference-standard ceiling made visible.** Entry 79 argued that measurable accuracy is capped by how reliably the right answer can be defined, not by algorithmic capability. **The trouble with testing that is that most medical labels are noisy in ways nobody measured.** Entry 81 is the exception: each of six tasks has an **operationally defined** label, so the rules can be ranked by how much judgement they demand and the performance checked against that ranking. **Side, right or left — 99.0%**: "based on the orientation of the image and the anatomical structures visible," essentially a geometric fact. **Cone-cut present or absent — 96.3%**: "a well-defined, radiopaque, non-exposed area," an unmistakable white wedge. **Type, premolar or molar — 95.8%**: one named landmark, the entire mesial half of the first maxillary premolar. **Interproximal overlap — 93.4%**: surfaces superimposed "extending beyond the contact point," with two named exceptions, so a rule with edge cases. **Cone-cut severity, three grades — 79.3%**: count the compromised interproximal areas, with the line at three versus four. **Incorrect receptor placement — 73.2%**: lay a printed template over the image and decide whether the occlusal plane falls in the green band or the yellow. **Five of those six are two-class problems and they span 73.2% to 99.0% — a 25.8-point spread with class count, dataset, architecture family and image quality all held constant.** The only thing varying is how much interpretation the rule requires. **The mechanism, and it is the whole of entry 79's thesis in one sentence: a model can only learn a boundary that its labellers agreed on, and can only be measured against labels as consistent as they are.** **The everyday version**: give three teachers one pile of essays and three tasks — "is this in English or French" (perfect agreement, learnable), "does it contain the word *however*" (near-perfect), and "is the argument *moderately* persuasive rather than *very* or *slightly*" (they will split on half the pile, and any student trying to learn their standard will look incompetent when in fact no single standard exists). **Watch out for** a paper reporting several tasks at very different accuracies and explaining the spread by model capacity, class count or dataset size without once asking how consistently each label was assigned. **Ask, per task: what was the rule, and how reliably can a human apply it?** **The diagnostic pattern: when accuracy orders itself by how judgemental the label is, with everything else constant, the ceiling is in the labels.**

**317. Consensus labelling produces good labels and destroys the evidence about them.** Entry 81's reference standard: one senior DMFR lecturer with >8 years' experience classified every image, two further lecturers assessed them, and *"All images were analyzed until investigators reached a consensus on the presence of each positioning error. This was then considered the ground truth."* **That is a sound way to produce labels — three experts arguing to agreement will usually land closer to the truth than any one alone — and it makes the reliability of those labels permanently unmeasurable.** The mechanism: a kappa needs **independent** judgements, two or more people scoring the same case without seeing each other's answers. **Consensus deliberation yields exactly one answer per case**, so there is no second opinion to compare against; it was merged into the first by discussion. **The disagreement that existed before the conversation is gone and no analysis can recover it.** So entry 81 reports no kappa, no ICC and no pre-consensus disagreement rate — **not withheld, never generated.** **The fix costs an afternoon: have the raters score a sample blinded and independently FIRST, compute the kappa on that, then convene and deliberate to produce the final labels.** You keep the same consensus ground truth and gain a number telling every reader how hard the labels were to assign. **For this paper that number would have settled technique 316 outright** — a much lower kappa for receptor placement than for side would confirm the pattern directly rather than by inference. **The everyday version**: three diving judges confer and announce one agreed score per dive. The scores are probably excellent, and you can never learn whether they were an easy crowd or a divided one, because you only ever see the agreed figure. **Watch out for** "labelled by consensus," "adjudicated by a panel," "reviewed until agreement was reached" — each signalling good labels and no reliability data. **Two questions: were there independent reads before the discussion, and what was the agreement?** Note the asymmetry: **a paper reporting kappa 0.65 has told you something useful; a paper reporting consensus has told you nothing, and the second reads as more authoritative.** Pairs with techniques 266, 295 and 296 (report an interval on every agreement statistic) and now completes them: **296 says report the interval, 317 says make sure you can compute the statistic at all.**

**318. Report the fold-to-fold spread — and notice when the spread itself tracks task difficulty.** Entry 81 reports a standard deviation on **every metric, per class and overall**, across its five folds, and that is where the real information sits. **A mean alone hides how much the five results differed**: 84-85-85-85-86 and 70-78-85-92-100 both average 85%, and they are not the same finding. Converting entry 81's SDs into approximate 95% intervals (SE = SD/√5, t(4) ≈ 2.776): **side 99.0 ± 0.6 → ~98.3–99.7%; cone-cut binary 96.3 ± 1.8 → ~94.1–98.5%; type 95.8 ± 3.0 → ~92.1–99.5%; overlap 93.4 ± 5.2 → ~86.9–100%; three-class 79.3 ± 5.7 → ~72.2–86.4%; receptor placement 73.2 ± 8.3 → ~62.9–83.5%.** **So the headline "73.2%" is really "somewhere between about 63% and 84%" on 332 images** — the difference between a tool worth building and one that is not. **And here is the finding inside the finding: the SDs run 0.6 to 8.3, a FOURTEEN-fold range, ordered exactly as the accuracies are.** Crisp labels give both higher means and tighter folds; judgement-heavy labels give both lower means and wilder folds. **That is a second, independent signature of technique 316** — inconsistent labels make a model worse on average *and* more dependent on which images landed in which fold. **One honest caveat: entry 81 discloses that "Random seeds were not fixed," so part of the fold variation is training randomness rather than data variation, and these intervals are rough.** **The everyday version**: two restaurants average three and a half stars; one has fifty reviews all saying "three or four, perfectly fine," the other twenty-five fives and twenty-five twos. Same average, completely different restaurant — **and the second tells you something inconsistent is happening in the kitchen. A wide spread is not merely imprecision; it is a clue.** **Watch out for** cross-validation reported as a bare mean, still the norm. **Ask for the SD or the per-fold numbers, convert to an interval, and when you have spreads for several tasks, compare them.**

**319. Group-level versus image-level cross-validation, and what happens when the groups are very few.** Entry 81 cross-validated **at the image level** across **three mannequins**, and assures the reader that *"no identical image appeared in both training and validation sets."* **That rules out duplicates. It does not address the grouping problem**, which is that with three anatomies every fold contains images of all three. **For the two anatomical tasks — side and type — a network can learn "this is mannequin two's left molar region" rather than left-versus-right in general**, so 99.0% on three fixed skulls at one fixed exposure is consistent with the task being close to trivial here. This is technique 301 with **n_groups = 3**, the smallest this list has seen. **The paper's own reading of its 99.0% is that transfer learning is unnecessary for small datasets; the simpler reading is that the task is easy on this data.** **In fairness, for the positioning-error tasks the concern is materially weaker** — a cone-cut or a misplaced receptor is operator-induced and varies within mannequin, so those labels are not confounded with anatomy the same way. **The decisive experiment is free: three-fold leave-one-mannequin-out.** **General rule: the unit of cross-validation should be the unit you want to generalise across.** Across patients, split by patient; across sites, split by site; across devices, split by device; **across subjects when there are only three of them, split by subject and accept that you have three folds.** **And "no duplicate images across folds" is not the same claim as "no shared subject across folds" — papers routinely offer the first as if it answered the second.**

**320. Declaring the central limitation five times is a reporting practice worth copying by name.** Entry 81's mannequin limitation appears in the **stated objective** ("in a controlled mannequin-based setting"), in the **abstract's conclusion** (*"A validation study using patient-acquired bitewing radiographs from clinical settings is required before clinical deployment"*), **twice in the Discussion** (*"These results should be viewed in light of the BWs taken from mannequins, which may have contributed to this high overall accuracy"*; *"further investigation in clinical data should be conducted"*), and in the **limitations**. **Five placements, including the two a reader actually reads — title area and abstract.** **This is the best scope declaration in this reading list, and it converts what would otherwise be a fatal gap into a declared boundary on the claim.** Set it against entry 78, whose abstract claimed "strong performance across 2 OCT devices" while its own confidence intervals showed non-overlapping degradation, and against entry 77, which claimed screening from cross-sectional data. **The grading consequence, stated so the rubric stays auditable: a modest result that cannot be misread outranks an impressive one that can.** **Practice to copy: put the limitation that bounds your claim in the abstract, not only in the limitations paragraph.** **Practice to look for as a reader: check whether the abstract's claim and the limitations section describe the same study.** Where they diverge, the abstract is what gets cited.

**321.
