"Random seeds were not fixed" — a one-sentence disclosure most papers omit.** Entry 81 states it plainly. **The consequence is small and specific: the five fold results differ partly because of training randomness, not only because of which images were held out, so the fold SDs of technique 318 are an upper bound on data-driven variation and the intervals are approximate rather than exact.** **The honest version of this is what entry 81 did — say it.** The dishonest version is silence plus a suspiciously tidy single number. **The better version is to fix seeds and report variation across seeds separately from variation across folds**, which decomposes the two sources. **General rule: any reported performance figure carries at least three sources of variation — which data you trained on, which data you tested on, and the randomness of training itself.** Most papers report one number and describe none of the three. **A paper that names even one of them has told you more than a paper that names none.**

**322. A paper's own explanation of its weakest result can be refuted by its own table — check before accepting it.** Entry 81 attributes its poor results to class count: *"It is expected, as we increase the class number and keep the same data set size the accuracy tends to decrease."* **But incorrect receptor placement is a TWO-class task — present 155, absent 177 — and it scored 73.2%, against 99.0% for the two-class side task.** Class count cannot explain a 25.8-point gap between two binary problems; it is at most a partial account of the three-class drop and **no account whatever of the worst result in the paper.** The authors' second explanation — that the region of interest was not defined beforehand — is more plausible and still does not reach receptor placement, which needs no region of interest, only the occlusal plane's position. **The general move, and it costs nothing: when a paper explains a weak result, check the explanation against the paper's own numbers before accepting it.** Authors' explanations of their own failures are the least adversarially tested claims in any paper — nobody reviews a limitation for being too generous. **This is distinct from an arithmetic error (technique 313): the numbers here are all correct. The reasoning laid over them is not.**

**323. The ordinal middle is where a graded label is least reliable, and per-class metrics are the only way to see it.** Entry 81's three-grade cone-cut task: per-class AUC **94.9 for "critical," 94.5 for "minimal," and 72.2 for "SEVERE"** — a 22-point hole in the middle while both extremes sit near 95. **And look at the definitions: "severe" means three or fewer interproximal areas compromised, "critical" means more than three.** **The boundary the model cannot find is a count at three versus four.** The authors see the pattern and attribute it to the undefined region of interest; **the complementary reading is that nothing shows three raters deliberating to agreement placed that 3-versus-4 line consistently — and because of technique 317, nothing can.** **General rule for any ordinal outcome — tumour grade, severity score, Likert response, triage category: the extreme categories are defined by being extreme and are easy; the middle categories are defined by their BOUNDARIES, which is where graders disagree and where models fail.** **This is only visible if per-class metrics are reported.** An overall accuracy of 79.3% conceals a near-ceiling performance on both ends and a near-coin-flip in the middle. **Ask for per-class metrics on every ordinal outcome, and read the middle first.** Connects to entry 79's kappa paradox (295): the same middle-category instability is what drives expert agreement down in exactly the cases that matter.

**324. A phantom isolates the construct and removes the difficulty — both are true, and papers argue only the first half.** Entry 81's defence of mannequins is genuinely good and worth quoting: a phantom *"eliminates patient-related variability, allowing for a more focused evaluation of acquisition-related faults."* **That is correct reasoning. A positioning error is something the OPERATOR does, not something the patient has, so removing patient variability isolates exactly the thing being measured.** **The unargued half: patients are *why* positioning is hard** — gag reflex, shallow palate, crowded or tilted teeth, tori, movement. **A phantom removes the confound and the challenge at once.** So the study measures whether a network can detect operator error **under the easiest possible conditions**, which is a legitimate first step and must not be read as a performance estimate for anything else. **Entry 81 is protected here by technique 320** — saying it five times means nobody reads it that way. **General rule: for any study on simulated, phantom, synthetic or otherwise idealised data, ask both questions separately. Does the simplification isolate the construct? And does it also remove the main source of difficulty?** A paper that argues the first and is silent on the second has made half an argument. **This is the extreme end of the transportability spectrum this list has been building** — technique 194 (new site), 284 (site versus device), and now **subject type**, which is further than either.

**325. Name the asymmetric cost of your errors — and entry 81 does.** From the Discussion: *"False negatives should be identified for critical cone-cutting errors or severe overlap, as this would lead to non-diagnostic images passing unnoticed, while false positives could lead to unnecessary retakes and additional radiation exposure."* **One sentence, and it tells a reader which direction of error to care about and why — which is the information needed to set a threshold.** It also makes the per-class recall figures interpretable: the cone-cut binary task's **"present" recall of 92.7% means 7.3% of cone-cuts are missed**, and under the stated cost structure that is the number to look at rather than the 96.3% overall accuracy. **General rule: an overall accuracy treats both error types as equally bad, which is almost never true in medicine.** **Ask, for every classifier: which error is worse here, by how much, and did the authors say?** A paper that names the asymmetry has thought about deployment; one that reports accuracy alone has not. Pairs with technique 275 (PPV depends on the population) and 258 (read an accuracy as its complement).

**326. Reporting that someone else beat you.** Entry 81 states that a previous study reached **100% on left/right classification using DenseNet-121 pre-trained on ImageNet with fine-tuning, on 1,000 bitewings**, against its own 99.0% from scratch on 403 — and that another reached **97.56%** on inverted-image recognition with ResNet-34. **Putting a competitor's better number in your own Discussion costs something and is correct.** It also lets a reader evaluate the paper's methodological claim honestly: **entry 81 argues its from-scratch result "challenge[s] th[e] assumption" that transfer learning is needed for small datasets, and the reader can see that the comparison is 99.0% on 403 mannequin images against 100% on 1,000 clinical ones** — which is not obviously a win. **General practice to look for: does the Discussion cite prior work that outperformed this study, or only prior work it beats?** A selective comparison set is among the easiest forms of spin to execute and among the hardest to detect without reading the field yourself. **A paper that volunteers its own unfavourable comparison has given you a reason to trust the rest of its reporting** — and entry 81 earns that credit.

**What caps entry 81 at 3/5, recorded so the grade stays auditable.** **No held-out test set** — five architectures plus a learning rate selected per task, with a different winner for each of six tasks, and the reported accuracies are the cross-validation scores on the folds that chose them, so every number is an order statistic (311) with no equivalent of entry 80's 0.829. **No agreement statistic on the reference standard, and none obtainable from this design** (317). **Image-level folds across three mannequins** (319). **The two clinically useful tasks are the two that failed** — severity 79.3%, receptor placement 73.2%, the latter being the second commonest retake cause in the paper's own cited literature. **403 images, with fold intervals as wide as 62.9–83.5%** (318). **No transfer test to patients** — declared five times, so a boundary rather than a hidden flaw (320, 324). **Ethics approval number truncated as "2024/" in both the Methods and the Ethics Statement, so none is reportable.** **No reporting guideline named; CLAIM applies.**

**Why not 2:** entry 77 scored 2 for claiming screening from cross-sectional data with its label possibly inside its inputs — **a mismatch between claim and evidence. Entry 81 has no mismatch at all**: the title says mannequin, the abstract says mannequin, the conclusion says a patient study is required first. Everything claimed is shown and everything not shown is named. **Why not 4:** the grade tracks what a reader may safely take away, and what a reader may take away is that **a CNN can identify bitewing type, side and the presence of a cone-cut on three plastic dummies, and cannot reliably grade severity or detect misplacement** — on 403 images, no held-out set, no label-reliability data, no patient test.

**What would make it a 4, concretely — and all four use data the authors already have.** **One blinded independent round before the consensus discussion, with the kappa reported** (317) — the single highest-value addition, and it would settle technique 316 directly. **A held-out test set, or at minimum the four losing architectures per task** so the selection spread is visible. **Three-fold leave-one-mannequin-out** (319). **And the per-mannequin image counts.**

**Positive practice worth carrying forward.** **The five-fold scope declaration** (320). **Operationally defined labels in writing for every task** — the first genuinely crisp reference standard in this run. **Standard deviations on every metric, per class and overall** (318). **Per-class metrics and a confusion matrix** (323). **Full acquisition and training reporting** — device, focal spot 0.4 mm, 70 kV / 7 mA / 0.10 s, PSP receptors, 1980×1486 native and 512×512 downsampled, RTX 2080 Ti, Adam, loss function, 200 epochs, default initialisation, and explicit statements of no augmentation, no LR scheduling and no extra regularisation. **"Random seeds were not fixed," disclosed** (321). **The asymmetric cost of errors named** (325). **A competitor's better result reported** (326). **Funding explicitly none; conflicts explicitly none.** And the framing contribution — *"The study separates image acquisition quality from diagnostic content"* — a real idea the caries-detection literature needs, given one cited study had **interproximal overlap misclassified as caries.**

**Arithmetic verified today, every check passing.** **All six task tables reconcile exactly**: 208+195=403 (type), 173+230=403 (side), 179+224=403 (cone-cut binary), 70+61+48=179 (three-class, matching the 179 "present" cases), 171+162=333 (overlap), 155+177=332 (receptor placement). **The exclusion chain is fully traceable**: 403 − 70 critical = 333 and 333 − 1 = 332, both as stated. Fold SDs converted to approximate 95% intervals with t(4) = 2.776, giving receptor placement **62.9–83.5%** and the SD range 0.6 to 8.3 — **a fourteen-fold spread tracking the accuracy ordering.** **No arithmetic error was found anywhere in this paper.**

**What could not be verified.** Any inter-rater agreement statistic (and none is obtainable from this design); any held-out test set; the complete ethics approval number; the per-mannequin image counts; any mannequin-level grouped cross-validation; the four non-winning architectures' results per task; any reporting guideline; **all figures, including the confusion matrix and the receptor-placement template images**; and Supporting Files 1 and 2. **Nothing was reproduced that was not seen.**

**Reporting-guideline pattern — the two-entry run ends, and the rule now holds in both directions.** 71, 73, 74, 75, 77, 78 named none; 69 named TRIPOD+AI with the checklist; 70 named STROBE; **76 measured the phenomenon at 40% mean completeness**; **79 named six instruments and applied them; 80 named TRIPOD and deposited a checklist; 81 names none.** **CLAIM exists precisely for medical-imaging AI** and would have demanded the two things most missing here — the reliability of the reference standard, and a validation set separate from the selection set. **The standing rule is unchanged: naming a checklist does not predict quality, so read the methods.** **And entry 81 supplies the counterpart case: a paper scrupulously honest about its scope while omitting exactly the two checks an imaging checklist would have required.** **Honesty about limits and completeness of method are different virtues, and this paper has the first without the second** — which is the more useful failure mode of the two, because a reader can see the gap.

**Both pre-registered questions, answered.** **(1) "Does anything in the paper test transfer from mannequin to patient?" — Nothing does, and the paper says so five separate times** (320), including in the abstract's conclusion, with a genuine argument for the phantom that gets half-credit (324). **(2) "What is the reference standard for a positioning error, and who defined it?" — three DMFR lecturers by consensus, with operationally defined written rules for every label** — making this the first stable-reference-standard entry in the run, which was the point of the pick — **but with no agreement statistic, and none obtainable** (317). **And the pick paid off: entry 79's ceiling argument, formed on noisy labels, predicted correctly on crisp ones** (316).

**Deferred status, 2026-11-18, both re-checked today.** ***Annals* *Choosing Wisely* (42896192) — day 29, STILL NO PMC COPY, and its abstract-only date is TOMORROW, 2026-11-19, so it is tomorrow's pick as an ABSTRACT-ONLY decode** under the standing twenty-one-day rule; standing note that its 5.4% / 0.6–12.2% CI is a clustering signature computed across 15 recommendations rather than 4,050 responses. **Gait foundation model (42995231) — day 10, still no PMC copy**, abstract-only date **2026-11-30**; largest multiplicity problem this list will have faced (**1,887 of 3,210 tested phenotypes called significant**), first question being what false-discovery-rate procedure ran across those 3,210 tests.

**Landmark check, 2026-11-11 → 11-18.** **One new record, the first in over a week: PMID 43045766, *Nature* 659(8141):852, "Should we move from artificial intelligence to artificial life?" (Petrovic O)** — article type **"News"**, single author, one page, **no abstract**. An opinion piece, not a primary study. **Assessed; does not displace the queue.** Also re-confirmed: the *Nature Medicine* gait commentary (43003904), previously assessed.

**Plan set on 2026-11-18.** **Tomorrow, 2026-11-19, takes the *Annals* *Choosing Wisely* benchmark (42896192) as an ABSTRACT-ONLY decode**, as owed — its threshold date is tomorrow and it still has no PMC body after 29 days. **After that, shelf item (G)**, the SOFA recalibration study, for three reasons: it **learns integer weights for a legacy point score rather than replacing it with a black box** (the simple-versus-ensemble theme entry 80's four-factor count raised, tested deliberately); it uses **10-fold nested cross-validation grouped by hospital**, which is exactly the check entry 81 did not run (319); and it excludes **133,684 of 148,532 encounters (90%) for at least one missing measurement**, the largest exclusion fraction this list will have examined. **Shelf after today: five spares — (G), (H), (I), (J), (K), all PMC-confirmed, restocked today because (F) was the last one.**

**Standing watch item:** PM Heart trial results (protocol PMID 42893738), quarterly.

## From entry 82 — Ariel et al., LLM overuse and false restraint on Choosing Wisely scenarios (Ann Intern Med, 2026; decoded 2026-11-19, ABSTRACT ONLY)

**The deferred pick owed today**, under the standing twenty-one-day rule — deferral opened 2026-10-28, closed at **day 30** with no PMC copy ever appearing. **Abstract-only, with all four access routes documented**: PMC absent (re-checked across 30 days and again today), Scite refused for want of a paid plan, publisher host `www.acpjournals.org` DNS-blocked exactly as `doi.org` and `www.bmj.com` are, and no preprint or open-access copy found. **The abstract was independently re-confirmed verbatim by a second literature service, so every number below was seen in two places.** 6 commercial LLMs on production APIs Feb–Mar 2026, **15 Choosing Wisely recommendations → 225 scenarios → 4,050 responses**. **And the shelf note written on 2026-10-28 called the clustering read in advance; today's run confirmed it arithmetically, reproducing the reported upper bound to within 0.2 percentage points.**

**327. A big N is not a big study: find the independent unit, then check whether the interval was built on it.** Entry 82 reports **4,050 responses** and an overuse rate of **5.4% with a 95% CI of 0.6% to 12.2%** — a span that looks absurd until you see where it comes from. **The 4,050 decompose as 15 recommendations × 15 scenarios × 6 models × 3 runs.** Three runs of one scenario on one model are nearly the same observation; fifteen scenarios within one recommendation are variations on a single theme, so a model's blind spot about incidental thyroid nodules shows up in all ten restraint scenarios at once. **They rise and fall together, so the independent unit is the recommendation, and there are 15.** **My arithmetic:** a binomial interval on the correct 2,700-response denominator gives **4.5% to 6.3%** (half-width 0.85 pp); the reported upper half-width is **6.8 pp — a ratio of 8.0×**; and a 15-cluster t-interval using t(14) = 2.145 on the fifteen per-recommendation rates gives an upper bound of **12.4% against the reported 12.2%**, reproducing it to within **0.2 pp**. The reported interval is also **asymmetric (−4.8 / +6.8)**, consistent with a cluster bootstrap on a right-skewed distribution. **So the wide interval is not sloppiness — it is the authors refusing an interval eight times tighter, and it deserves credit.** **The everyday version**: to find a country's average height you could measure 4,050 random people, or pick **15 families** and measure 270 members of each. Both give 4,050 numbers and the second tells you far less, because relatives resemble each other — your effective sample size is nearer 15 than 4,050. **Scenarios within a recommendation are a family.** **Watch out for** "we analysed N responses" where N is scenarios × models × repeats. **Two diagnostics, both free. (1) Compute the naive interval yourself from the big N: if the paper's is much wider, they clustered properly and should be credited; if it MATCHES the naive one, the precision may be overstated by a factor of eight.** (2) **A suspiciously narrow interval on a repeated-measures benchmark is the red flag, and a suspiciously wide one may be a mark of quality** — the opposite of most readers' instinct. Pairs with 306 (one split is a single draw) and 319 (group-level versus image-level CV): **clustering your cross-validation by site and clustering your confidence interval by recommendation are the same act.**

**328. An average across categories is not a rate — and on a lopsided distribution it describes nothing.** Entry 82's headline **5.4%** is the **unweighted mean of fifteen per-recommendation rates**, and I verified it: the abstract's partition is complete and non-overlapping (**8 recommendations at zero + 2 named outliers at 42.8% and 28.3% + 5 in the 0.6–3.3% band = 15**), and taking the mean with the five low values averaging **1.98%** — inside the stated range — gives **5.41%**. **So the headline is the average TOPIC, not the average response, and certainly not the chance a clinician gets bad advice.** And the distribution is far too lopsided for a mean: **summing the fifteen rates, the two outliers carry 87.6% of all the overuse in the study.** The accurate summary is a shape, not a rate — **clean on thirteen topics, poor on two** — which is what the paper's own conclusion says while the results line supplies the number that will be quoted. **Second, subtler problem: which weighting answers the question?** An unweighted mean treats all fifteen recommendations as equally important, but **incidental subcentimetre thyroid nodules and routine ECGs in low-risk adults are COMMON situations**, so weighting each recommendation by how often it actually arises would give the two worst performers more weight, not equal weight — **and the overall rate would plausibly come out HIGHER than 5.4%, not lower.** **The reassuring headline rests on a weighting choice that does not match clinical reality, and the abstract never flags it as a choice.** **Worked example**: a hospital with 1 bad outcome per 1,000 cardiology procedures (0.1%) and 30 per 100 neurosurgical ones (30%) has an **unweighted departmental average of 15.05%** and an **actual rate of 31/1,100 = 2.8%** — differing more than fivefold, both correctly called "the error rate." **Watch out for** any headline rate from a study spanning several topics, conditions, sites or tasks. **Ask: is this an average of the groups or a rate over all observations? And if it is an average of groups, is the distribution lopsided enough that the mean describes none of them?** **The tell is usually in the paper itself — when the results say "most of the effect came from two categories," the authors have already undermined their own headline mean.** And always ask the weighting question: **equal weighting is a decision, not a default.**

**329. When the measuring instrument is itself an AI, its reliability is your study's precision — and it is reportable.** Entry 82 reduces to one binary judgement repeated 4,050 times: did this response endorse action or restraint? That judgement was made by an **"LLM judge with physician adjudication"**, and the abstract reports **no kappa, no concordance rate, no count of disagreements, and no statement of how many cases went to adjudication.** **Every number in the paper is downstream of it.** This is technique 266 in a new setting — when the measurement is a judgement, reliability IS the precision — and **entry 81's consensus-labelling gap in a sharper form**, because there the instrument was three humans whose disagreement deliberation destroyed, whereas **here the instrument belongs to the same class of system under evaluation**, raising a correlated-error question: a judge sharing the models' blind spots makes mistakes that are not independent of theirs, which is the hardest kind to detect from outside. **And unlike entry 81, where no kappa was obtainable from the design, here it plainly was**: according to PubMed, **Johnson T & Gaissmaier W, *JMIR AI* 2026;5:e87034 (PMID 42530970, PMC13473853, DOI 10.2196/87034, verified independently) report LLM-rater interrater reliability of κ = 0.8893 and 0.9366** on an almost identical task — scoring whether chatbot answers matched official recommendations — across **35 LLMs and all 142 USPSTF recommendations.** **The statistic is routine, cheap and standard in this exact literature; its absence is a reporting choice.** **And that same paper supplies the reason it matters most here: it found "high rates of LLMs avoiding definitive recommendations."** If models frequently hedge rather than clearly endorsing action or restraint, **sorting their answers into two bins is a hard task full of borderline cases** — and Choosing Wisely scenarios are *built* from borderline cases, since every recommendation carries caveats. **The everyday version**: you test whether six bakers followed a recipe by hiring a judge to rule on each loaf, then report that the bakers failed 5.4% of the time **without ever checking whether your judge can tell** — and if your judge trained at the same school as the bakers, you have a particular problem. **Watch out for** "classified by LLM judge," "scored by GPT-4," "automated evaluation," "adjudicated by the study team." **Ask for the agreement statistic and what it was computed against**, plus two habitual follow-ups: **what fraction went to human adjudication, and what was the disagreement rate on those?** and **was the judge validated against independent human scoring, or simply trusted?** **A paper reporting κ = 0.85 has told you its measurement is sound; a paper naming its judge and reporting nothing about it has not told you its result is wrong — it has told you that you cannot know how precise it is.**

**330. Prespecify BOTH error directions as co-primary outcomes, and build the sampling frame so the second is measurable.** This is entry 82's best feature and the thing most worth copying. The study declared **two** co-primary outcomes — the **overuse rate** (endorsing action when restraint was right) and the **false restraint rate** (withholding an action that was right) — and then **designed the scenario set to support both**, with **10 restraint-appropriate and 5 action-appropriate scenarios per recommendation**. **Most safety benchmarks test one direction and declare victory.** The reason that fails is immediate: **a system that recommended restraint for everything would score a perfect 0% overuse and be caught instantly by the other outcome.** Entry 81 earned credit merely for *naming* the asymmetric cost of false negatives and false positives in its Discussion (325); **entry 82 built both into the design, the sampling frame and the prespecified analysis**, which is a level up. **The cost is visible and worth noting**: splitting 15 scenarios per recommendation 10/5 leaves false restraint resting on **1,350 responses and roughly 8 events**, so the second outcome is measurable but imprecise — which the authors handle correctly by reporting its interval (0.1%–1.2%) rather than the bare 0.6%. **General rule for any evaluation of a decision-making system: name the two ways it can be wrong, prespecify both, and sample enough of each case type that both are estimable.** **Then ask which error is worse and by how much**, since an overall accuracy treats them as equal and almost nothing in medicine is. **And as a reader: when a benchmark reports only one direction, ask what the trivial degenerate strategy would score on it.** If the answer is "perfectly," the benchmark is not measuring safety.

**What caps entry 82 at 3/5, recorded so the grade stays auditable.** **Abstract only**, four routes documented. **No reliability statistic for the LLM judge** (329), where a verified comparator reports 0.89–0.94. **No human comparator** (286), so "is 5.4% good?" is unanswerable — and Choosing Wisely exists precisely because clinicians overuse these tests. **The headline is an unweighted mean over topics** (328), with 87.6% of the overuse in 2 of 15, and the clinically relevant weighting would plausibly raise it. **Six models pooled with no per-model breakdown**, against a verified 43-point between-model spread. **Three runs cannot characterise run-to-run instability** and none is reported, against a verified comparator finding internal consistency as low as **0.60** (Landon S et al., *J Gen Intern Med* 2026;41(5):1286–1291, PMID 41055682, PMC13083678, DOI 10.1007/s11606-025-09888-7). **False restraint rests on ~8 events.** **15 recommendations selected from hundreds with no stated method**, and **the 225 scenarios and their answer key were authored by the same three investigators** — entry 81's lesson transferring directly, since the caveats are the whole difficulty in conditional recommendations. **Six models unnamed and unversioned on continuously-updated production APIs**, so not replicable even in principle. **No reporting guideline visible.**

**Why not 2:** entry 77 scored 2 for a mismatch between claim and evidence. **Entry 82 has none** — the design does what the title says, the interval is honest about the precision, the four stated limitations are the right four, and the conclusion is narrower than the data would have permitted. **Why not 4:** entry 79 earned a 4 by advancing a falsifiable thesis and quantifying it with counter-evidence included; this is a competent benchmark read through an abstract. **It is a 4 if the full text reports the judge's agreement, all fifteen per-recommendation rates, and the six models separately — all three being RE-REPORTS of data already collected.** **Standing upgrade offer applies, as for entries 68 and 76.**

**Positive practice worth carrying forward.** **Co-primary outcomes in both error directions, prespecified, with the sampling frame built to support the second** (330). **A confidence interval computed at the correct clustering level, at visible cost to apparent precision** (327). **The per-recommendation decomposition IS in the abstract**, the only reason the concentration is detectable — **entry 76, also abstract-only, had neither a decomposition nor any interval at all, so this is the better-reported of the two abstracts by a wide margin.** **A conclusion narrower than the data allowed.** **An explicit refusal of the most quotable extension** — *"Findings may not extend to consumer-facing use"* — closing off the chatbots-harming-patients headline on the correct grounds that a clinician prompt was tested. **Providers and dates stated.** **Funding explicitly none.**

**Arithmetic verified today, every check passing.** **Structure reconciles exactly:** 15 × (10 + 5) = **225 scenarios**; 225 × 6 × 3 = **4,050 responses**. **Denominator split the abstract never makes:** overuse 15 × 10 × 18 = **2,700**, false restraint 15 × 5 × 18 = **1,350**, summing to 4,050 — so **"4,050 responses" is the denominator of NEITHER headline rate**; in counts, ~**146 overuse** and ~**8 false-restraint** responses. **5.4% reproduces as the unweighted mean of 15** (partition 8 + 2 + 5 = 15 complete; five low values must average 1.98%, inside the stated 0.6–3.3% range). **Concentration 87.6%** in two recommendations. **Complements: 57.2% and 71.7%** correct restraint on the two outliers. **Interval: naive 4.5–6.3% vs reported upper half-width 8.0× wider; 15-cluster t-interval reproduces 12.2% to within 0.2 pp; reported interval asymmetric −4.8/+6.8.** **No arithmetic error found anywhere in the abstract, and the two reported intervals are mutually consistent with the design.**

**Comparator discipline — a note on provenance, because three of four were used and one was not.** **Used and independently verified against PubMed before citing:** Johnson & Gaissmaier (PMID 42530970) for the judge-reliability statistic and the between-model spread; Landon et al. (PMID 41055682) for run-to-run instability. **Used but explicitly flagged as unverified:** a Findings-of-EMNLP 2026 benchmark (Gourabathina et al.) reporting LLMs more likely than physicians to recommend unnecessary care — **PubMed does not index it, so it is cited as indicative only.** **Found and deliberately NOT used:** an arXiv preprint reporting bimodal acquiescence from 0% to 100% across 19 models with an LLM judge validated at κ = 0.957 — **an unrefereed preprint, and this list does not lean on preprints for comparator numbers.** **Recording the one I declined matters as much as the three I used.**

**What could not be verified.** The entire full text, costing: **any judge-agreement statistic**; **the six models' names and versions**; **any per-model results**; **any measure of variation across the 3 runs**; **the prompt text**; **how the 15 recommendations were selected from several hundred**; **who authored and validated the 225 scenarios and their answer key**; **thirteen of the fifteen per-recommendation rates** (only two outliers and a range for five others are given); **any human comparator**; **the conflict-of-interest statement**; and **whether any reporting guideline was followed.** **Nothing was reproduced that was not seen.**

**Reporting-guideline pattern — and entry 76 predicted this paper's gaps with uncomfortable precision.** 71, 73, 74, 75, 77, 78 named none; 69 named TRIPOD+AI with the checklist; 70 named STROBE; **76 measured the phenomenon at 40% mean TRIPOD-LLM completeness**; 79 named six instruments and applied them; 80 named TRIPOD with a deposited checklist; 81 named none; **82 names none visible in its abstract.** **The standing rule is unchanged — naming a checklist does not predict quality, so read the methods.** **But entry 76 reported that completeness was worst on model versioning and access modality, inference parameters, and output postprocessing — and those are exactly the three things missing here.** **Caveat recorded rather than suppressed: they may well be in the full text; what is verifiable is only that they are absent from the abstract most readers will see.** **This is the first time a prior entry in this list has made a specific, checkable prediction about a later entry's omissions and been right.**

**Deferred status, 2026-11-19.** ***Annals* Choosing Wisely (42896192) — RESOLVED, decoded today as entry 82, abstract-only at day 30**, closing a deferral opened 2026-10-28. **Gait foundation model (42995231) — day 11, re-checked today, still no PMC copy**; abstract-only date **2026-11-30**, eleven days out; largest multiplicity problem this list will have faced (**1,887 of 3,210 tested phenotypes called significant**), first question being what false-discovery-rate procedure ran across those 3,210 tests. **And note the connection formed today: that paper's problem is the mirror image of entry 82's.** Entry 82 has **15 independent units dressed as 4,050**; the gait paper has **3,210 tests whose dependence structure decides whether 1,887 "significant" findings mean anything.** **Both are questions about how many independent things were really examined** — technique 327 and technique 205 are the same question asked from opposite ends.

**Landmark check, 2026-11-12 → 11-19 — three new records, all assessed, none displacing the queue.** **PMID 43052445**, *Nature* 2026-11-18, hypothalamic volume and cell-type changes in ageing and Alzheimer's — a major atlas (458 MRI scans, 614,403 nuclei) in which machine learning is one component inside a neuroscience resource paper, not the object of study. **PMID 43045795**, *Nature Medicine* 2026-11-17, generational gains in cognitive capacity and dementia incidence — **a genuinely important paper** (~783,000 memory assessments from ~219,000 people across five cohorts followed up to 30 years; later-born generations show both higher memory entering old age and slower decline, plausibly accounting for the reported 13%-per-decade fall in dementia incidence), **but lifespan epidemiology rather than medical AI, so it does not displace a medical-AI queue — noted as a candidate if this list ever widens its scope.** **PMID 43018982**, *JAMA* 2026-11-12, "Beyond the Rogue Agent—Preparing Health Systems for AI Failure" — a viewpoint with **no abstract**, not a primary study.

**Plan set on 2026-11-19.** **Next run takes shelf item (G)** — **SOFA score recalibration by ML-guided optimisation**, PMID 43024933, PMC13766654, *JMIR Med Inform* 2026, eICU, 208 US hospitals, 14,848 admissions. **Three reasons, and today reinforced the second.** It **learns integer weights and thresholds for a legacy point score rather than replacing it with a black box**, testing deliberately the simple-versus-complex question entries 80 and 81 both raised incidentally. It uses **10-fold nested cross-validation GROUPED BY HOSPITAL** — the grouped check entry 81 did not run (319), and now also the clustering question entry 82 has made central (327): **grouping your cross-validation by site and clustering your confidence interval by recommendation are the same statistical act, and (G) will be the first entry in this run to do the first of them.** And it excludes **133,684 of 148,532 encounters (90%) for at least one missing determination** — the largest exclusion fraction this list will have examined, several times entry 78's 44.4%. **Two questions written down now, before reading. (1) What does a 90% complete-case exclusion do to who is left — is the remaining 10% a sicker, more heavily monitored population, and does the paper characterise the 90% it dropped?** **(2) Does the recalibrated integer score beat the original SOFA by more than the selection noise** — is the comparison made on data untouched by the recalibration, and is the margin larger than the fold-to-fold spread (318)? **Shelf after today: four spares — (H) AKI in pulmonary embolism with MIMIC-IV → eICU external validation, (I) prospective lupus nephritis with calibration and decision curves, (J) sickle-cell facial phenotyping with LOOCV on 308 smartphone photos, (K) next-day alcohol craving from EEG, HRV and phone sensing.**

**Standing watch item:** PM Heart trial results (protocol PMID 42893738), quarterly.

## From entry 83 — Dueñas-Ruiz et al., interpretable recalibration of a SOFA-derived score (JMIR Med Inform, 2026; decoded 2026-11-20, FULL TEXT)

**Shelf item (G), and the methodological high-water mark of this reading list.** eICU-CRD, 208 US hospitals, **14,848 adult first ICU admissions**, mortality **17.03%**. The paper learns SOFA's thresholds and integer weights from outcome data under monotonicity, integrality and scale-anchoring constraints, keeping an additive point score. **Both pre-registered questions answered, the first more sharply than expected.** What makes this entry worth its space is not the 0.059 AUROC gain — it is that the validation design is correct in every respect the previous 82 entries got wrong somewhere, and that the paper reports three separate findings against its own interest.

**331. Grouped nested cross-validation, and the one sentence that closes the field's commonest loophole.** Entry 83 does all five of the things that make a cross-validated number mean something, and the combination is rare enough to enumerate. **(a) Folds grouped by the unit you want to generalise across** — hospitals, with *"all encounters from a given hospital remained within the same fold and no center contributed to both development and evaluation."* **(b) The grouping extends into the inner loop**: the internal validation split is also by **whole hospitals**, ~25% of development encounters. **(c) Selection happens inside the loop** — bin counts and every hyperparameter chosen on internal validation log loss, refit on the outer development set, **evaluated once** on held-out hospitals — so the reported numbers are **not** selection scores, the defect that capped entries 80 and 81 (311). **(d) One out-of-fold prediction per patient**, so nobody is counted twice. **(e) All comparators on identical outer folds with equivalent tuning**, which is what makes the comparison table a fair fight rather than a shop window. Plus **prespecified convergence criteria** (validation AUROC below 0.70, or >3 predictors with identical weights across all intervals, treated as non-convergent) and **a single prespecified fixed random seed** — the exact inverse of entry 81's disclosed *"Random seeds were not fixed."* **And then the sentence this list had not previously seen: the final published weights were refit on the full cohort, and "This full-cohort model was used only to derive the final thresholds and integer weights; it was not used to estimate predictive performance."** **You want the shipped tool fitted on all the data; you must not let that fit produce your performance number. One sentence states that the loophole was closed.** **Watch out for** cross-validation reported without naming the unit of splitting. **Four questions: were folds grouped by whatever you must generalise across; was the inner split grouped the same way; was every tuning decision inside the loop; and did the final full-data refit contribute any reported number?** This is technique 319 satisfied, and 327's clustering logic applied to splitting rather than to intervals — **grouping your folds by hospital and clustering your confidence interval by recommendation are the same statistical act.**

**332. The paired difference is more stable than the absolute performance — demonstrated, not asserted.** Entry 83's own observation, and the most elegant thing in it: across the 10 hospital-grouped folds the recalibrated score's AUROC ran **0.766 to 0.857, a spread of 0.091**, while the **paired difference** ran **0.036 to 0.067, a spread of only 0.031 — about three times narrower** — and was positive in **all 10 folds**. I verified both spreads against the paper's own statement. **The mechanism: both scores were computed on the same patients in the same fold, so whatever makes one hospital group harder than another raises or lowers both equally and cancels in the difference.** **Consequence for reading: "0.059 better" is a far more trustworthy number than "0.811," and a paper reporting only the absolute figure has thrown away its most reliable result.** **The everyday version**: to test whether a new running shoe is faster, having some runners wear the new one and others the old buries the shoe effect in the much larger variation between runners; having **every runner run both** and looking at each person's own difference cancels fitness, weather and course. **Watch out for** two-arm comparisons where only absolute metrics are reported per arm, with no within-fold or within-subject pairing — and for the reverse error of treating a *paired* interval as if it described the absolute performance. **Ask: was the comparison paired, on identical cases, and is the headline the paired difference or the absolute score?** **Prefer the paired difference.** Related to 283 (non-overlapping CIs refute "no difference") — here the CIs also fail to overlap, with a 0.039 gap, so the pairing and the interval agree.

**333. A 90% complete-case exclusion is a change of population, and standardized mean differences are how you see it.** Entry 83 excluded **133,684 of 148,532 encounters (90%)** for at least one missing day-1 determination. **The cost is not precision; it is who you are studying.** Missingness concentrated in **PaO₂/FiO₂ (76.5%) and bilirubin (64%)** — an **arterial blood gas** and a **liver panel**, neither of which is ordered for a patient who is stable and improving. **So "has complete data" is a proxy for "was sick enough that someone investigated invasively."** The paper measures exactly this: ventilation **64.2% vs 20.2% (SMD 0.995)**, norepinephrine **28.6% vs 8.3% (SMD 0.540)**, **ICU mortality 17.03% vs 4.54% (SMD 0.411) — a 3.75-fold ratio** — while **age was comparable (absolute SMDs <0.03)** and sex similar. **The SMD is the right tool and the p-value is not**: at n=148,532 every difference is "significant," including trivial ones, so what you need is the magnitude in units of the variables' own spread (technique 186). **Above 0.1 is a notable imbalance; 0.995 is roughly a full standard deviation.** **A correction recorded against myself: my first check used a pooled-variance denominator and returned 0.535 for mortality against the paper's 0.411 — the paper was right and my formula was wrong.** The standard SMD for two proportions divides by **sqrt of the average of each group's own variance**, not the pooled variance; **"SMD" names a family of closely related statistics and the denominator matters.** **The everyday version**: measure emergency-department waiting times using only records with a full set of vital signs, and you measure the wait **of patients staff were worried about** — not a noisy estimate of the general wait but a clean estimate of a different question. **Watch out for** "complete-case analysis," "patients with available data," "after excluding records with missing values." **Three questions: what fraction was excluded; how do included and excluded differ in SMDs rather than p-values; and was the missingness plausibly caused by how sick the patient was?** **That last is decisive — missing-because-nobody-ordered-the-test depends on the outcome, and no imputation method fixes it, only a sensitivity analysis bounds it.**

**334. Run the sensitivity analysis that can embarrass you, then publish the number.** Entry 83 prespecified an expansion to encounters missing **no more than one** of five required inputs, filling that one value by **random draw from a range entirely inside the corresponding zero-point SOFA category** (bilirubin 0.2–1.1 mg/dL; creatinine 0.4–1.1; platelets 150–300 ×10⁹/L; MAP 70–95 mm Hg; non-ventilated P/F 400–500), sampled uniformly with a fixed seed and **using no outcome information**. Result on **61,469 encounters from 186 hospitals** (4.1× larger, prevalence **17% → 8%**): the paired AUROC gain fell from **0.059 to 0.037 — a 37.3% relative reduction** — still positive in all 10 folds. **The direction is robust; the magnitude is not, and 0.037 is the better estimate for a less-selected population.** **They published it.** Two further details worth copying. **They refused to impute 5,796 ventilated encounters with missing P/F** because *"assuming normal oxygenation in the presence of mechanical ventilation was not considered clinically defensible"* — **turning down free sample size on a clinical argument.** And they **explained why they did not add missingness indicators as predictors**: doing so *"would introduce a distinct measurement-process signal that may reflect local testing and documentation practices in addition to patient condition, potentially limiting transportability across hospitals"* — a sophisticated choice, argued rather than defaulted into. **General rule: for any analytic decision that could plausibly be driving your result, prespecify the alternative, run it, and report the number even when it is worse.** **And as a reader: a paper whose sensitivity analysis moves its headline by a third and says so has earned far more trust than one whose sensitivity analyses all confirm it.**

**335. Discrimination and calibration are separate claims, and a model can win one without the other — the Brier score will not tell you which.** Entry 83 makes two claims and only one succeeds. **Discrimination** (can it rank?): AUROC **0.811 (0.800–0.819)** vs **0.751 (0.740–0.761)**, **non-overlapping, 0.039 gap** — unambiguous. **Calibration** (are the numbers right?): slope **1.011**, CITL **0.008**, O:E **1.005** — excellent in absolute terms, **but not better than the reference**, and the paper says so in its own results: the recalibrated score **"had a lower Brier score but higher integrated calibration index, E50, E90, and Emax values,"** and *"these findings do not indicate consistently superior calibration relative to the reference score."* **The trap the paper avoided: the Brier score bundles discrimination and calibration into one number**, so a lower Brier is consistent with better ranking and unchanged-or-worse probability accuracy — which is exactly what happened. **Never accept a calibration claim resting on a Brier score alone; ask for the slope, calibration-in-the-large, O:E, and a calibration-curve-based error measure (ICI, E50, E90, Emax).** They also reported that **both** models underestimate mortality in short stays (**O:E 1.400 and 1.507**), i.e. a failure shared rather than hidden. **Why the distinction bites clinically: for triage and trial enrolment you need ranking; for telling a family a number you need calibration.** **A model can be the right choice for one purpose and the wrong one for the other, from the same table.** Extends 307 (read the interval, not the point) and pairs with 336.

**336. A calibration slope's precision scales with sample size — this is what "estimated" looks like.** Entry 83's calibration slope is **1.011 with a 95% interval of 0.970–1.055, a width of 0.085** — the tightest in this reading list — computed on **14,848 out-of-fold predictions**. Set it against entry 80's **0.856 (0.469–1.639), width 1.170** on **57 test patients**: **13.8× wider**, spanning severe overfitting to severe underfitting and containing 1.0, which is why that entry's calibration was recorded as **unestimated rather than acceptable**. **Same statistic, same purpose, two completely different epistemic situations — and only the interval tells them apart.** **The practical rule: a calibration slope on a few dozen outcomes cannot be estimated, no matter how close the point estimate sits to 1.0.** Roughly, the interval narrows with the square root of the number of events, so going from ~21 events to ~2,529 should tighten it by about an order of magnitude — which is what is observed. **Watch out for** a slope reported without an interval (precision invisible), a slope near 1.0 with a wide interval called "well calibrated" (307), and a calibration **plot** offered in place of a slope. **Ask for the number, the interval, and the event count behind it.**

**337. Report stability at every level the method has — cutpoints, points, and patients.** Entry 83 re-ran **the entire pipeline** — automatic discretization, weight optimisation **and** integer projection — **1,000 times** with different seeds for data partitioning and weight initialisation, and reported the result at three levels. **Which cutpoints got chosen**: the first GCS boundary (4.5, IQR 4.5–4.5), first MAP boundary (40.5, IQR 40.5–40.5) and first and fourth creatinine boundaries were highly consistent, while **P/F, bilirubin and platelet boundaries "showed greater variability or were selected in only a subset of replications."** **How much the assigned points moved**: mean interquartile width **0.27 points for platelets, 0.55 MAP, 0.65 P/F, 0.71 GCS, 0.95 creatinine, and 2.29 for bilirubin.** **How much individual patients moved**: median within-patient score IQR **2 points on a 0–62 range**, **63.1% (9,367/14,848)** of patients varying by ≤2 points, and **median pairwise Spearman correlation between replications 0.970 (IQR 0.964–0.975)**. Plus the sentence that forecloses selective reporting: **"All 1000 attempted replications satisfied the prespecified convergence criteria; none was rejected or repeated."** **And the distinction most papers blur, drawn correctly here: patient-level resampling measures OPTIMISATION stability, hospital-grouped CV measures GENERALISATION, and the paper says the 1,000 replications are "distinct from" the primary assessment.** **General rule: an unstable intermediate does not necessarily mean an unstable output — report both.** Here several cutpoints wander while patient rankings barely move, which is the informative finding. **Watch out for** a paper reporting only that its final model is stable, or only that its cutpoints are, and for one that presents resampling stability as if it were evidence of generalisation.

**338. Report the competitor that beats you AND the competitor that ties you — and the second is the more dangerous one to omit.** Entry 83's comparison table, all on identical hospital-grouped folds: reference **0.751**, penalized logistic regression **0.775**, **AutoScore 0.810**, proposed score **0.811**, **XGBoost 0.818**. **Two disclosures most papers would not make.** The black box still wins (**0.818 vs 0.811**), which the paper reports and then slightly overclaims past in its conclusion (see below). **And an existing published interpretable method ties them at 0.810 against 0.811** — a difference of **0.001** with essentially identical intervals — which the paper states plainly: *"AutoScore showed nearly identical discrimination to the proposed approach, indicating that the principal distinction between the 2 interpretable methods lies in model structure rather than predictive performance."* **It then reframes its contribution correctly as structural: AutoScore builds an entirely new score, whereas this framework keeps SOFA's organ-domain skeleton and clinicians' decades of familiarity with it.** **That is a real and defensible contribution — and it is not a performance contribution, which the abstract's framing invites a reader to assume.** **General rule for readers: the comparator that matters most is the best existing method in the paper's own class, not the strawman and not the black box.** A paper comparing only against a weak baseline and a black box has left out the one number that decides whether its method was needed. **Ask: what is the state of the art among methods with the same constraints, and is it in the table?** Entry 81 earned credit for reporting a competitor that beat it (326); **entry 83 goes further by reporting one that tied it, which is the disclosure that actually threatens the paper's novelty.**

**339. Refuse to read your own learned weights as physiology — and say so in the paper.** Entry 83's interpretive discipline is the best in this list, and it takes the form of **four explicit refusals.** The score runs **0 to 71, not 0 to 24**, so *"the learned weights are not on the SOFA scale"* and represent *"relative contributions within the fitted prognostic score rather than revised 0 to 4 organ-domain points."* The data-driven cutpoints *"should not be interpreted as revised physiological definitions of respiratory, coagulation, hepatic, or renal dysfunction."* The whole instrument *"should therefore be interpreted as a mortality risk model rather than as a replacement for the descriptive organ dysfunction grading provided by conventional SOFA."* **And the one that most impressed me: dobutamine received 0 points, and the paper writes that "the zero dobutamine contribution should not be interpreted as evidence of clinical neutrality; it indicates that the fitted additive model assigned no additional prognostic contribution to this binary exposure variable."** **A lesser paper would have written a paragraph about dobutamine being safe.** This is the correct response to the problem entry 77 ran into from the other side — there, SHAP directions contradicted the epidemiology and went unremarked (278). **The general rule: a weight in a fitted prognostic model is a statement about this cohort's outcome associations under this model's constraints, not a statement about biology** — above all for treatment variables, where the weight absorbs confounding by indication. **Watch out for** any paper that converts its model coefficients into clinical claims, and especially for a zero or reversed coefficient on a treatment presented as evidence about that treatment. **Also note the structural admission: because MAP, mechanical ventilation and the four vasoactives enter as SEPARATE predictors rather than composite domains, additivity survives but SOFA's organ sub-scores do not** — so "preserves the bedside-computable form" is true while "preserves the organ-domain structure" is only partly true, and the paper says which.

**340. AUPRC moves with prevalence and AUROC does not — read a precision-recall number against its floor.** Entry 83 supplies an unusually clean demonstration without commenting on it. Between the primary and expanded cohorts, **the same approach produced a HIGHER AUROC (0.811 → 0.853) and a LOWER AUPRC (0.495 → 0.395)**, because **prevalence fell from 17.03% to 8%.** **A useless model's AUPRC is approximately the event rate**, so the achievable floor fell from 0.17 to 0.08. Measured against that floor: **0.495 / 0.1703 = 2.91× in the primary cohort, and 0.395 / 0.08 = 4.94× in the expanded one** — **so relative to what was achievable the precision-recall performance IMPROVED, and the apparent drop is prevalence halving.** **The everyday version**: a metal detector that mostly beeps on gold where 1 in 5 buried objects is gold will produce far more false alarms where 1 in 12 is — **the detector did not get worse, the haystack got bigger.** AUROC measures the detector; AUPRC measures the detector **on that beach.** **Watch out for** AUPRC compared across cohorts, eras or sites with different event rates — a fall may be entirely prevalence, and a rise may be entirely prevalence too. **Always print the event rate beside the AUPRC and compute the ratio.** Conversely, a paper reporting **only** AUROC on a rare outcome is concealing how unusable its alerts would be (275). Entry 83 does report both, with the event rate, in the same sentence — which is why the comparison is checkable at all.

**341. False precision in data-driven cutpoints, and the stability analysis that proves it.** Entry 83 reports creatinine thresholds at **1.085, 1.425 and 1.665 mg/dL** — four significant figures — a P/F threshold at **49.9**, and a neurological threshold at **"GCS ≤4.5"** on a scale whose values are **integers from 3 to 15**, where 4.5 simply means **"4 or less."** **A threshold estimated from data has a sampling distribution, and printing three decimals implies a stability the paper's own replication analysis contradicts for several variables** — bilirubin and platelet boundaries "showed greater variability or were selected in only a subset of replications," and bilirubin's assigned points varied by a mean interquartile width of **2.29**. **The saving grace, and it is substantial: this paper REPORTS that variability, so a reader can discount the decimals.** **General rule: round a learned cutpoint to the precision at which the underlying measurement is actually used clinically, and report the cutpoint's own variability across resamples.** A creatinine threshold is a clinical decision boundary, not a physical constant. **Watch out for** any paper offering data-derived cutpoints to three or four significant figures with no resampling distribution — and for the special case of a non-integer threshold on an integer scale, which signals that the cutpoint was never translated back into the clinical units it will be applied in.

**342. An easy subgroup is an easier task, not better performance.** Entry 83's AUROC by length of stay: **0.924** for stays ≤2 days, **0.855** for ≤7 days, **0.812** for ≤30 days. **The first number is the most quotable and the least informative.** The reason is in the paper's own cohort description: **44.40% of deaths occurred within 2 days and 81.53% within 7 days.** So in the ≤2-day stratum the score is predicting death that happens **tomorrow** from physiology measured **today** — closer to measuring the patient's current state than to forecasting. **The honest headline is the ≤30-day figure of 0.812**, which is essentially the whole cohort. **To the paper's real credit, the PAIRED improvement is nearly identical across strata (+0.059, +0.062, +0.059)**, so the *gain* is not a stratification artefact even though the *absolute* numbers are — which is technique 332 doing useful work again. **General rule: when a paper reports performance by subgroup, ask whether the high-performing subgroup is one where the outcome is nearly concurrent with the predictors.** Short-horizon prediction, prevalent-case detection and sickest-stratum performance all inflate discrimination for the same reason: **the thing being predicted is closer to the thing being measured.** Related to 287 (predicting a future LEVEL is not predicting CHANGE) and 277 (cross-sectional is not screening).

**What caps entry 83 at 4/5, recorded so the grade stays auditable.** **No external validation, one database** however many hospitals — all within one US telehealth network, one data pipeline, one documentation culture; the authors name **MIMIC-IV, AmsterdamUMCdb and HiRID** as the right targets. **The 90% exclusion bounds the population severely** (333): developed and validated on the sickest tenth, selected by having had invasive tests. **The comparator was handicapped** — no urine output, binary rather than dose-graded vasoactives — so part of the 0.059 is plausibly the optimiser recovering information canonical SOFA already uses; the paper's disclaimer is correct and does not enlarge the margin. **An existing method tied it** (AutoScore 0.810 vs 0.811), so the performance gain belongs to data-driven recalibration in general rather than to this framework (338). **The conclusion overreaches by one sentence** — *"the dichotomy between predictive performance and interpretability is false"* is contradicted by its own table, where XGBoost wins by 0.007; the supportable and more interesting claim is that **the integer score captures 89.6% of the reference-to-XGBoost headroom against penalized logistic regression's 35.8%.** **The abstract's "should transfer to other legacy instruments" is untested speculation** — one instrument, one database. **Day-1 static only; mortality only; SOFA 2.0 uncompared** because its variables were not captured. **Cutpoints printed to false precision** (341). **Funding, COI, data- and code-availability all absent from the retrieved body** — JMIR normally carries them, so recorded as **not retrievable rather than absent**, and no funder or disclosure reported that was not seen; **the calculator URL and repository link were stripped and are not guessed.** **And the premise applies to the paper itself: it criticises 19 years of staleness (1996→2015) while shipping a calculator whose weights were estimated from 2014–2015 data and published in 2026, an 11-year-old snapshot** — the *framework* is the durable contribution, which the paper says, but the tool ships the weights.

**Why not 3:** entries 80 and 81 were held at 3 because their headline numbers were selection scores with no held-out estimate, and entry 82 because its measuring instrument's reliability was unreported. **None of those defects is present here** — selection was inside the loop, the reported numbers are out-of-fold, the outcome is death with no rater reliability at issue, and uncertainty is quantified at **metric, fold, replication, cutpoint and patient** level. **Why not 5:** one database, a cohort excluding 9 in 10 eligible patients and keeping the sickest, and a comparator weakened before the comparison began. **What would make it a 5: external validation on MIMIC-IV (free, and already named by the authors); a comparison against canonical SOFA on data where urine output and vasoactive doses exist, to see how much of the 0.059 survives an unhandicapped reference; a head-to-head against SOFA 2.0; and deleting the two overclaiming sentences, which costs nothing.**

**Arithmetic verified today — every check passing except one of my own.** **Cohort:** 148,532 − 133,684 = **14,848**; exclusion **90.0%**; 2,529 + 12,319 = 14,848; mortality **17.03%** exact. **All seven missingness percentages reproduce** within 0.06 pp. **Exclusion split:** 52,417 (39.2%) + 81,267 (60.8%) = **133,684**. **Urine output:** 14,848 − 7,727 = **7,121** as stated. **GCS rule:** 437 = **2.9%**; 437 + 14,411 = 14,848; 111 + 2,418 = **2,529**. **AUPRC paired difference** 0.495 − 0.393 = **0.102** exact. **CIs non-overlapping**, 0.039 gap. **Fold spreads 0.091 and 0.031**, matching the paper. **Headroom capture 35.8% / 88.1% / 89.6%.** **Stability 9,367/14,848 = 63.1%.** **Calibration width ratio 13.8×.** **Sensitivity 37.3% relative reduction.** **AUPRC lift 2.91× vs 4.94× of floor.** **No arithmetic error anywhere in the paper.** **My own error, recorded rather than repaired silently: my first SMD used a pooled-variance denominator and gave 0.535 for mortality against the paper's 0.411 — the paper was right.** Recomputed with the standard two-proportion formula, all three SMDs reproduce exactly (0.995, 0.540, 0.411).

**Reporting-guideline pattern — the sharpest form the running observation has taken.** 71, 73, 74, 75, 77, 78 named none; 69 named TRIPOD+AI with a deposited checklist; 70 named STROBE; **76 measured the phenomenon at 40% mean TRIPOD-LLM completeness**; 79 named six instruments and applied them; 80 named TRIPOD with a checklist; 81 named none; 82 named none visible; **83 names none.** **And entry 83 satisfies more of TRIPOD+AI's substance than entry 69, which named it AND deposited the completed checklist:** participants, predictors and outcome fully specified; missing data described, quantified **and** sensitivity-tested; discrimination **and** calibration with slope, CITL and O:E; the complete model published so anyone can compute it; a simple-baseline comparison; and uncertainty at five levels. **So the standing rule reaches its strongest statement: naming a checklist does not predict quality — the best-reported primary study in this list names none.** **The one counterweight, recorded fairly: the four declaration items a checklist pins down (funding, COI, data availability, code availability) are exactly the four absent from the retrieved body.**

**Landmark check, 2026-11-13 → 11-20 — fifteen records, the largest weekly haul yet, all screened, none displacing the queue.** **Eleven News or Editorial**, including four *Nature* AI news items (**43058281** Anthropic IPO, **43058273** hallucinated references, **43026326** an "AI mechanic" for inspecting neural networks, **43052545** a plagiarism quiz), a *Science* editorial on correcting the record (**43056460**), and commentary on AI accountability and wearables (**43052483**, **43045767**). **Three non-medical primary papers**: twisted-perovskite polar vortices with an ML interatomic potential (**43052434**, materials physics), Amazonian deciduousness from Sentinel-2 plus ML (**43052430**, ecology), and the poxvirus portal complex by cryo-ET (**43039357**, structural virology). **Three already assessed** (**43052445**, **43045795** on 11-19; **43045766** on 11-18). **One genuine medical-AI paper goes to the shelf as (L).**

**SHELF ADDITION — 2026-11-20.**

**(L) Early brain functional connectivity changes induced by antidepressants and placebo** — PMID 43054784, **PMC13781515**, DOI 10.1038/s44220-026-00729-y, *Nature Mental Health* 2026;4(11):1642–1659, Tong X et al. (Stanford, UT Austin, Lehigh, Penn, Children's National). **Two independent cohorts, n=386: 125 placebo, 123 sertraline, 138 escitalopram**; 257 female, 129 male; 18–65; functional connectivity after **1 or 2 weeks** of treatment; **NCT01407094**. **Why it earns a slot: it has a PLACEBO ARM, which almost nothing in this list does**, and it uses predictive and contrastive ML to try to separate placebo-related from drug-specific effects. **The claim needing the hardest look**: drug-specific effects are *"present only in a subset of patients treated with antidepressants"* and **"the responses of those without such changes can be predicted with a placebo response prediction model"** — a subgroup-plus-prediction claim of exactly the shape techniques 311 (is the subgroup the winner of a search?) and 313 (do the subgroups decompose to the totals?) were written for. **First questions: was the responder/non-responder split prespecified or found in the data, and is the placebo-model prediction evaluated out-of-sample?** Not decoded today because it is a mechanistic neuroimaging study rather than a landmark displacing a queued pick, and because it was e-published 2026-09-18 and only now entered the date window.

**Deferred status, 2026-11-20.** **Gait foundation model (42995231) — day 12, re-checked today, still no PMC copy**; abstract-only date **2026-11-30**, ten days out; largest multiplicity problem this list will have faced (**1,887 of 3,210 tested phenotypes called significant**), first question being what false-discovery-rate procedure ran across those 3,210 tests. **And today turns yesterday's two-way connection into a three-way one.** Entry 82 had **15 independent units dressed as 4,050**. The gait paper has **3,210 tests whose dependence structure decides whether 1,887 "significant" findings mean anything.** **Entry 83 is the positive case: it identified its independent unit (the hospital), split on it, and reported a paired difference three times more stable than the absolute measurement.** **All three ask how many independent things were really examined — techniques 205, 327 and 331 are one idea seen from three sides.**

**Plan set on 2026-11-20.** **Next run takes shelf item (H)** — **AKI prediction in critical pulmonary embolism**, PMID 43024281, PMC13773385, *Medicine (Baltimore)* 2026: **MIMIC-IV for development and internal validation, eICU for EXTERNAL validation**, eight ML models with LASSO plus multivariate logistic selection, **and a web calculator already deployed.** **Three reasons it follows today naturally.** It is **the genuine cross-database transportability test (284) that entry 83 explicitly lacks and names as its own priority** — and it runs in the opposite direction, MIMIC-IV → eICU, where entry 83 used eICU alone. It turns the grouped-validation question into an **external** one: entry 83's hospital-grouped folds showed absolute AUROC swinging **0.091** across hospital groups *within* one network, so the first thing to look for is how much is lost **crossing between networks**. And **a calculator is already deployed**, raising the deployment-before-evidence question entry 83 deliberately avoided by stating that external and prospective validation must come first. **Two questions written down now, before reading. (1) How much performance was lost between MIMIC-IV and eICU, and was the web calculator deployed before or after that was known?** **(2) Was the external validation genuinely held out — features selected and hyperparameters fixed on MIMIC-IV before eICU was touched — or does the paper report eICU numbers that informed model choice (311, 331)?** **Shelf after today: four spares — (I) prospective lupus nephritis with calibration and decision curves, (J) sickle-cell facial phenotyping with LOOCV on 308 smartphone photos, (K) next-day alcohol craving from EEG, HRV and phone sensing, (L) antidepressant versus placebo functional connectivity.**

**Standing watch item:** PM Heart trial results (protocol PMID 42893738), quarterly.
